Thupavaṃso [O 0.0001]

The Chronicle of the Stupa

Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa

Homage to the Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One.

Yasmiṃ sayiṃsu jinadhātuvarā samantā

Chabbaṇṇa raṃsi visarehi samujjalantā,

Nimmāya lokahitahetu jinassa rūpaṃ

Taṃ thupamabbhuta tamaṃ sirasā namitvā;

Cakkhāmahaṃ sakala loka hitāvahassa

Thupassa sabba jana naṇdana kāraṇassa,

Vaṃsaṃ surāsura nariṇdavarehi niccaṃ

Sampūjitassa ratanujjala thūpikassa;

Having bowed with my head to that most wondrous stupa, in which the supreme relics of the Conqueror lay all around, shining brightly with spreading rays of six colors, representing the Conqueror’s form for the world’s benefit; I shall recount the lineage of the stupa that bestows benefit on the entire world, that causes joy to all people, which is always honored by gods, asuras, and supreme kings, and whose pinnacle gleams with jewels.

Kiñcāpi so yatijanena purātanena

Atvāya sīhaḷajanassa kato purāpi,

Vākkena sihaḷabhavena’bhisaṅkhamattā

Atthaṃ na sādhahati sabbajanassa sammā;

Although it was made by an ancient ascetic, and was previously composed for the Sinhalese people, yet merely by being composed in the Sinhala language, it does not properly accomplish the welfare of all people.

Yasmā ca māgadha niruttikatopi thūpa-

Vaṃso viruddhanaya sadda samākulo so,

Vattabbameva ca bahumpi yato na vuttaṃ

Tamhā ahaṃ punapi vaṃsami’maṃ vadāmi;

And since the Thūpavaṃsa, even in its Magadhan linguistic formulation, is filled with conflicting principles and discordant words, and since much that should have been said has not been said, therefore I shall again recount this lineage.

Suṇātha sādhavo sabbe paripuṇṇamanākulaṃ

Vuccamānaṃ mayā sādhu vaṃsaṃ thūpassa satthunoti;

Listen, all you virtuous ones, with minds complete and undisturbed, to what I shall well declare concerning the lineage of the Teacher’s stupa.

Tattha thūpassa vaṃsaṃ vakkhāmīti ettha tathāgato arahaṃ sammā sambuddho thūpāraho, paccekabuddho thūpāraho, tathāgatassa sāvako thūpāraho, rājā cakkavatti thūpārahoti vacanato thūpārahānaṃ buddhādīnaṃ dhātuyo patiṭṭhāpetvā kata cetiyaṃ abbhunana taṭṭhena [O 0.0002] thūpoti vuccati, idha pana kañcana mālika mahāthūpo adhippeto, so kassa dhātuyo patiṭṭhāpetvā katoti ce? Yodīpaṅkarādīnaṃ catuvīsatiyā buddhānaṃ santike laddhavyākaraṇo samatiṃsapāramiyo pūretvā paramābhisambodhiṃ patvā dhammacakkappavattanato paṭṭhāya yāva suhadda paribbājaka vinayāna sabba buddhakiccāni niṭṭhāpetvā anupādiyesāya nibbānadhātuyā parinibbuto tassa bhagavato arahato sammā sambuddhassa dhātuyo patiṭṭhāpetvā kato ayamettha saṅkhepo, vitthāro pana veditabbo?

Herein, I shall describe the lineage of the stupa. In this context, the Tathāgata, the Arahant, the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One, is worthy of a stupa; a Paccekabuddha is worthy of a stupa; a disciple of the Tathāgata is worthy of a stupa; a wheel-turning monarch is worthy of a stupa. Thus, because it is understood that a shrine is constructed by establishing the relics of those worthy of a stupa, such as Buddhas and others, and is called a ‘stupa’ due to its elevated mound, here, however, the great golden-wreathed stupa is intended. For whom were the relics enshrined to build it? For one who, having received a prediction in the presence of the twenty-four Buddhas beginning with Dīpaṅkara, fulfilled the thirty perfections, attained supreme enlightenment, and from the turning of the Wheel of Dhamma onward, completed all his Buddha-duties, which extended until the time of the wandering ascetic Subhadda, and then attained final Nibbāna without remainder—the relics of that Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One, were enshrined to build this stupa. This is the summary here; further details are to be elaborated.

2. Ito kira kappasatasahassādhikānaṃ catunnaṃ asaṅkheyyānaṃ matthake amaravatī nāma nagaraṃ ahosi. Tattha sumedho nāma brāhmaṇo paṭivasati. So aññaṃ kammaṃ akatvā brahmaṇasippameva uggaṇhi tassa daharakāleyeva mātāpitaro kālamakaṃsu. Athassa rāsivaḍḍhanako amacco āya potthakaṃ āharitvā suvaṇṇa rajata maṇimuttādi bharite gabbhe vivaritvā ettakaṃ te kumāra mātu santakaṃ, ettakaṃ pitusantakaṃ, ettakaṃ ayyaka payyakānanti yāva sattamā kulaparivaṭṭā dhakaṃ ācikkhitvā etampaṭijaggāhīti āha. So sādhūti sampaṭicchitvā agāraṃ ajjhāvasanto ekadivasaṃ cintesi.

2. It is said that a hundred thousand aeons beyond four incalculables, there was a city named Amaravatī. There lived a brahmin named Sumedha. Without engaging in any other work, he mastered only the brahminical craft. While he was still young, his parents passed away. Then, his wealth-increasing minister brought an account book, and opening treasuries filled with gold, silver, gems, pearls, and so forth, explained: 'So much, young sir, belongs to your mother; so much to your father; so much to your grandparents,' going up to the seventh generation of ancestors. Then he said, 'Take charge of this.' He accepted it respectfully, and while living at home, one day he reflected.

3. Punabbhave paṭisaṇdhigahaṇaṃ tāma dukkhaṃ, tathā nibbatta nibbattaṭṭhāne sarīrabhedanaṃ ahañca jānidhammo jarādhammo vyādhidhammo maraṇadhammo. Evaṃ bhūtena mayā ajāti ajaraṃ avyādhiṃ amaraṇaṃ sukhaṃ sītalaṃ nibbānaṃ gavesituṃ vaṭṭatīti nekkhammakāraṇaṃ cintetvā puna cintesi imaṃ dhanaṃ sabbaṃ mayhaṃ pitu pitāmahādayo paralokaṃ gacchantā eka kahāpaṇampi gahetvā nagatā mayā pana gahetvā gamanakāraṇaṃ kātuṃ vaṭṭatīti nagare bheriṃ carāpetvā mahājanassa dānaṃ datvā himavantassa pavisitvā tāpasa pabbajjaṃ pabbajitvā sattāhabbhantareyeva abhiññā ca samāpattiyo ca nibbattetva samāpattisukhena vītināmesi.

3. Taking rebirth is indeed suffering. Likewise, the dissolution of the body in each and every existence—I am by nature subject to birth, aging, sickness, and death. Since this is so, it is fitting for me to seek the unborn, the unaging, the sickness-free, the deathless, the blissful, cool Nibbāna." Having considered the cause for renunciation, he further reflected: "All this wealth—my father, my paternal grandfather, and others, when departing for the next world, did not take even a single kahapana with them. It is for me, however, to make this the provision for my journey." He had a proclamation made by drum throughout the city, gave alms to the populace, entered the Himalayas, and undertook the ascetic's renunciation. Within seven days, he developed the higher knowledges and meditative attainments and lived enjoying the bliss of attainment.

Tadā dīpaṅkaro nāma satthā parayābhisambodhiṃ patvā satta sattāhaṃ bodhisamīpeceva vitināmetvā sunaṇdārāme dhammacakkaṃ pavattetvā koṭisattānaṃ devamanussānaṃ dhammamataṃ pāyetvā cātuddīpika mahāmegho viya dhammavassaṃ vassento catūhi khīṇāsaya satasahassehi parivuto anupubbena cārakaṃ caramāno ramma nagaraṃ patvā sudassana mahāvihāre paṭivasati tadā rammanagaravāsino sappi phaṇitādīni bhesajjāni gahetvā puppha dhūpa gaṇdhahatthā yena buddho tenapasaṅkamitvā satthāraṃ vanditvā pupphādīhi pūjetvā ekamantaṃ nisīditvā dhammaṃ sutvā svātanāya bhagavantaṃ nimantetvā uṭṭhāyāsanā dasabalaṃ padakkhīnaṃ katvā pakkamiṃsu.

At that time, the Teacher named Dīpaṅkara, having attained supreme enlightenment, spent seven weeks near the Bodhi tree. Then, at the Sunandārāma, he set in motion the Wheel of the Dhamma, causing tens of millions of devas and humans to drink the deathless nectar of the Dhamma. Like a great rain cloud showering the four continents, he poured down the rain of Dhamma. Surrounded by four hundred thousand arahants, he traveled gradually and arrived at the city of Ramma. There, he dwelled in the Sudassana Great Monastery. The residents of Ramma, taking medicines like ghee and molasses, as well as flowers, incense, and perfumes in their hands, approached the Buddha. They paid homage to the Teacher, honored him with flowers and other offerings, sat to one side, listened to the Dhamma, and invited the Blessed One for the next day’s meal. Rising from their seats, they circumambulated the Ten-Powered One and departed.

Te [O 0.0003] puna divase asadisa mahādānaṃ sajjetvā dasabalassa āgamana maggaṃ sodhenti. Tasmiṃ kāle sumedhatāpaso attano assama padato uggantvā rammanagaravāsīnaṃ tesaṃ manussānaṃ uparibhāgena ākāsena gacchantā te haṭṭhapahaṭṭhe maggaṃ sodhente disvā kinnu kho kāraṇanti cintento sabbesaṃ passantānaṃyeva ākāsato oru ekamante ṭhatthāyaha te manusse pucchi.’Hambho kassa pana imaṃ maggaṃ sodhethā’ti te āhaṃsu bhante sumedha tumhe kiṃ naṃ jānātha, dīpaṅkaro nāma satthā paramābhisambodhiṃ patvā pavattavara dhammacakko janapada cārikaṃ caramāno anukkamena amhākaṃ nagaraṃ patvā sudassana mahāvihāre paṭivasati. Mayaṃ taṃ bhagavantaṃ nimantayimha tassa bhagavato āgamanamaggaṃ sodhemāti. Taṃ sutvā sumedhapaṇḍito cintesi. Buddhoti kho panesa ghosopi dullabho, pageva buddhuppādo tena hi mayāpi imehi manussehi saddhiṃ dasabalassa āgamanamaggaṃ sodhetuṃ vaṭṭatīti so te manusse āha, sace bho tumhe imaṃ magga buddhassa sodhetha - sayahampi ekaṃ okāsaṃ sampaṭicchatvā ayaṃ sumedhapaṇḍito mahiddhiko mahānubhāvoti jānantā dubbisodhanaṃ udakasambhinnaṃ ativisamaṃ ekaṃ okāsaṃ sallakkhetvā imaṃ hakāsaṃ tumhe sodhetha. Alaṅkarothāti adaṃsu.

On the following day, preparing an unsurpassed great almsgiving, they cleared the path for the arrival of the Ten-Powered One. At that time, the ascetic Sumedha, having left his hermitage, was traveling through the sky above the people of Rammanagara. He saw those joyful and delighted people clearing the road and, wondering, "What could be the reason?" he descended from the sky in full view of them all, stood to one side, and asked those people, "Friends, for whom are you clearing this road?" They replied, "Venerable Sumedha, do you not know? The Teacher named Dīpaṅkara, having attained supreme enlightenment and set in motion the excellent Wheel of Dhamma, is traveling through the countryside and will gradually arrive at our city, where he will reside in the Sudassana Great Monastery. We have invited that Blessed One and are clearing the path for his arrival." Hearing this, the wise Sumedha thought, "Even the word 'Buddha' is rare to hear, let alone the arising of a Buddha. Therefore, it is fitting for me to clear the path for the Ten-Powered One together with these people." So he said to them, "If you, friends, are clearing this path for the Buddha, then grant me one section as well." Knowing that this wise Sumedha was powerful and mighty, they noted a waterlogged and very uneven section that was difficult to clean, and assigned it to him, saying, "Clear and adorn this section."

Sumedhapaṇḍito buddhārammaṇa pītiṃ uppādetvā cintesi. Ahampanimaṃ okāsaṃ iddhīyā paramadassanīya kātuṃ pahomi evaṃ kato pana maṃ na paritoseti ajja pana mayā kāyaveyyāvaccaṃ kātuṃ vaṭṭatiti paṃsuṃ āharitvā taṃ pahesaṃ pūreni, tassa pana tasmiṃ padese asodhite jayasumana kusuma sadisi vaṇṇaṃ dupaṭṭacīvaraṃ timaṇḍalaṃ paṭicchādetvā nivāsetvā tassupari yuṇṇamāpaṅgena kusumakalāpaṃ parakkhipanto viya vijjullatā sassīrīkaṃ kāyabaṇdhanaṃ baṇdhitvā kanaka girisikhara matthake lākhārasaṃ parisiñcante viya suvaṇṇacetiyaṃ pavāḷajālena parikkhipanto viya suvaṇṇaṅghakaṃ rattakambalena paṭimuñcanto viya sarada samaya rajanikaraṃ rattavalāhakena paṭicchādento viya ca lākhārasena tinna kiṃ sukaksumavaṇṇaṃ rattavara pasukūla cīvaraṃ pāripitvā gaṇdhakūgidva rato kanakaguhāto sīho viya nikkhamitvā jaḷabhiññānaṃyeva catūhi khīṇāsava satasahassehi parivuto amaragaṇa parivuto dasasatanayano viya brahmagaṇaparivuto mahā brahmā viya ca aparimita samaya samupacitāya kusalabalajanitāya anopamayā buddhalīlāya tārāgaṇaparivuto [O 0.0004] sarada samaya rajanikaro viya gaganatalaṃ alaṅgata paṭiyattaṃ maggaṃ paṭipajji.

Sumedha the wise, having aroused joy with the Buddha as its object, reflected: 'I am able to make this place extremely beautiful with my psychic power. Yet, having done so, it would not satisfy me. Today, it is fitting for me to perform bodily service.' Having brought soil, he filled that muddy place. While that spot was still uncleared by him, the Blessed One—having donned a superior red rag-robe, the color of the Kiṃsuka flower, dyed with lac dye, like one pouring lac dye on the peak of a golden mountain, like encircling a golden shrine with a net of coral, like covering a golden greave with a red blanket, and like covering the autumn moon with a red cloud—emerged from the perfumed chamber like a lion from a golden cave. Surrounded by four hundred thousand Arahants possessing the six higher knowledges, like the thousand-eyed one surrounded by the host of immortals, and like the great Brahmā surrounded by the host of Brahmās, with the incomparable grace of a Buddha born from the power of merit accumulated over an immeasurable time, like the autumn moon surrounded by a host of stars, he set out upon the prepared and adorned path.

5. Sumedhatāpasopi tena alaṅkata paṭiyattena maggena āgacchantassa dīpaṅkarassa bhagavato dvattiṃsa varalakkhaṇa patimaṇḍitaṃ asatiyā anubyañjanehi anubyañjitaṃ byāmappahāparikkhepa sassīrīkaṃ iṇdanīlamaṇisaṃkāsoākāse nānappakārā vijjullatā viya chabbaṇṇabuddharaṃsiyo vissajjentaṃ rūpaggappattaṃ attabhāvaṃ oloketvā ajja mayā dasabalassa jīvitapariccāgaṃ kātuṃ vaṭṭati mā bhagavā kalale akkami maṇimayaṃ lakasetuṃ akkamanto viya saddhiṃ catūhi khīṇāsava satasahassehi mama piṭṭhiṃ akkamanto gacchatu taṃ me bhavissati dīgharattaṃ hitāya sukhāyāti kese mocetvā ajinajaṭā vākacīrāni kalela pattharitvā tattheva kalalapiṭṭhe nipajji nipanno ca sace ahaṃ iccheyyaṃ sabbakilese jhāpetvā saṅghanavako hutvā rammanagaraṃ paviseyyaṃ aññātakavesena pana me kilese ṇdhapetvā nibbānapattiyā kiccaṃ natthi, yaṃnūnāhaṃ dīpaṅkara dasabalo viya paramābhisambodhiṃ patvā dhammanāvaṃ āropetvā mahājanaṃ saṃsārasāgarā uttāretvā pacchā parinibbāyeyyaṃ. Idaṃ me patirupanti cintetvā aṭṭhadhamme samodhānetvā buddhabhāvāya abhinīhāraṃ katvā nipajja.

5. The ascetic Sumedha, seeing the bodily form of the Blessed One Dīpaṅkara approaching along that adorned and prepared path—a form embellished with the thirty-two excellent marks and the eighty minor characteristics, resplendent with a fathom-wide aura, like an indranīla gem, emitting six-colored Buddha-rays like various kinds of lightning in the sky, a body that had reached the pinnacle of beauty—thought: "Today, it is fitting for me to sacrifice my life for the Ten-Powered One. Let the Blessed One not tread upon the mud; let Him, as if treading upon a jeweled causeway, pass over my back together with the four hundred thousand Arahants. That will be for my long-term welfare and happiness." He then loosened his hair, spread his antelope skin, matted locks, and bark garments over the mud, and lay down there on the surface of the mud. As he lay there, he reflected: "If I wished, I could burn away all defilements, become a novice in the Sangha, and enter the city of Ramma. But for me, there is no purpose in attaining Nibbāna by extinguishing my defilements in the guise of one unknown. Why should I not, like the Ten-Powered Dīpaṅkara, attain supreme full enlightenment, embark the great multitude upon the ship of the Dhamma, ferry them across the ocean of saṃsāra, and afterward attain final Nibbāna? This would be fitting for me." Having reflected thus, having fulfilled the eight conditions, and having made the aspiration for Buddhahood, he lay down.

Dīpaṅkaropi bhagavā āgattvā sumedhapaṇḍitassa sīsasāge ṭhatvā kalalapiṭṭhe nipannaṃ tāpasaṃ disvā ayaṃ tāpaso buddhatthāya abhinīhāraṃ katvā nipanto, ijjhissati nu kho etassa patthanā udāhu noti upadhārento - anāgate gotamo nāma buddho bhavissatīti ñatvā ṭhitakova parisa majjhe vyākāsi, passatha no tumhe bhikkhave imaṃ uggatapaṃ tāpasaṃ kalalapiṭṭhe nipannanti. Evaṃ bhante, ayaṃ yiddhatthāya abhinīhāraṃ katvā nipanno samijjhissati imassa patthatā kappasata sahassādhikānaṃ catuntaṃ asaṅkheyyānaṃ matthake gotamo nāma buddho bhavissatīti sabbaṃ vyākāsi.

The Blessed One Dīpaṅkara, having arrived and stood at the head of Sumedha the wise, saw the ascetic lying on the surface of the mud. Considering, 'This ascetic is lying here having made an aspiration for the sake of Buddhahood; will his wish succeed or not?' and knowing that in the future he would become a Buddha named Gotama, he stood right there and declared in the midst of the assembly: "Do you see, monks, this ascetic of intense austerity lying on the surface of the mud?" "Yes, venerable sir." He declared everything: "This one is lying here having made an aspiration for the sake of Buddhahood. His wish will succeed. At the end of four incalculable aeons and one hundred thousand kappas, he will become a Buddha named Gotama."

Vuttañhetaṃ buddhavaṃse.

For this was stated in the Buddhavaṃsa.

Dīpaṅkaro lokavidū - āhutīnaṃ paṭiggaho,

Ussīsake maṃ ṭhatvāna - idaṃ vacanamabravi;

Dīpaṅkara, the Knower of the World, the Receiver of Offerings, standing at my head, spoke this utterance:

Passatha imaṃ tāpasaṃ - jaṭila uggatāpasaṃ aparimeyye ito kappe - ayaṃ buddho bhavissati. Ahu kapilavahayā rammā - nikkhamitvā tathāgato. Padhānaṃ padahitvāna - katvā dukkara kārikaṃ

"Behold this ascetic, this matted-haired one of intense austerity! In an immeasurable eon from now, he will become a Buddha. Going forth from the delightful Kapilavatthu, the Tathāgata, having striven the great striving, will perform the difficult practice."

Buddhe loke - keci.

When a Buddha is in the world, some...

Ajapāla [O 0.0005] rukkhamūlasmiṃ - nisīditvā tathāgato,

Tattha pāyāsamaggayha - nerañjaramupeheti;

"At the foot of the Ajapāla tree, the Tathāgata will sit down. There, having accepted the milk-rice, he will approach the Nerañjarā River."

Nerañjarāya tīramhi - pāyāsādāya so jino,

Paṭiyattavaramaggena - bodhimūlañhi ehīti;

"On the bank of the Nerañjarā, that Victor, having eaten the milk-rice, will go by the prepared, excellent path to the foot of the Bodhi tree."

Tato padakkhiṇaṃ katvā - bodhimaṇḍaṃ anuttaro

Assattharākkhamūlamhi - vujjhissati mahāyaso;

"Then, having circumambulated the unsurpassed Bodhi-seat, at the foot of the Assattha tree, the one of great fame will awaken."

Imassajanikā mātā - māyā nāma bhavissati, pitā suddhodano nāma - ayaṃ hessati gotamo. Anāsavā vītarāgā - santacittā samāhitā,

"His birth-giving mother will be named Māyā, his father will be named Suddhodana; this one will be Gotama. Taintless, free from passion, with peaceful minds, and well-composed,"

Kolito upatisso ca - aggā hessanti sāvakā;

Ānaṇdo nāmupaṭṭhāko - upaṭṭhissatī’maṃ jinaṃ;

"Kolita and Upatissa will be the chief disciples; an attendant named Ānanda will attend upon this Victor."

Khemā uppalavaṇṇā ca - aggā hessanti sāvikā;

Anāsavā vītarāgā - santacittā samāhitā;

"Khemā and Uppalavaṇṇā will be the chief female disciples; taintless, free from passion, with peaceful minds, and well-composed."

Bodhi tassa bhagavato assatthoti pavuccatī”ti.

"The Bodhi of that Blessed One is called the Assattha."

Abhinīhāra kathā

The Account of the Aspiration

6. Tato dīpaṅkaro dasabalo bodhisattaṃ pasaṃsitvā aṭṭhahi puppha muṭṭhīhi pūjetvā padakkhiṇaṃ katvā pakkāmi. Tepi catūsata sahassa khīṇāsavā bodhisattaṃ pupphehi ca gaṇdhehi ca pūjetvā padapphaṇaṃ katvā pakkamiṃsu. Devamanussā ca tatheva pūjetvā vanditvā atha kho bodhisatto dasabalassa vyākaraṇaṃ sutvā buddhabhāvaṃ karatalagatamiva maññamāno pamudita hadayo sabbesu paṭikkantesu sayanā vuṭṭhāya puppharāsi matthake pallaṅkaṃ ābhujitvā nisinno buddhakārakadhamme upadhārento kahananu kho buddhakāraka dhammā, kiṃ uddhaṃ adho disāsu vīdīyāsūti anukkamena sakalaṃ dhammadhātuṃ vicinanto porāṇakabodhisattehi āsevita nisevita paṭhamaṃ dānapāramiṃ disvā tattha daḷabhisamādāna katvā evaṃ anukkamena sīla - nekkhamma - paññā - vīriya - khanti - sacca - adhiṭṭhāna - mettā - upekkhā pāramiyoca disvā tatthadaḷha samādānaṃ katvā devatāhi abhitthuto ākāsamababhugganatva hima himavantameva agamāsi.

6. Then Dīpaṅkara, the Ten-Powered One, having praised the Bodhisatta, honored him with eight handfuls of flowers, circumambulated him, and departed. Those four hundred thousand Arahants also honored the Bodhisatta with flowers and perfumes, circumambulated him, and departed. The gods and humans likewise honored and paid homage to him. Then the Bodhisatta, having heard the prediction of the Ten-Powered One, considering the state of Buddhahood as if it were already in the palm of his hand, with a joyful heart, after all had departed, rose from where he lay. Having sat cross-legged atop the heap of flowers, he reflected on the qualities that make a Buddha: "Where, indeed, are the qualities that make a Buddha? Are they above, below, in the cardinal directions, or in the intermediate directions?" Systematically investigating the entire realm of phenomena, he first saw the perfection of giving, practiced and cultivated by the Bodhisattas of old, and made a firm resolution upon it. Thus, in sequence, discerning the perfections of virtue, renunciation, wisdom, energy, patience, truthfulness, determination, loving-kindness, and equanimity, and strengthening his resolve in each, he was praised by the deities. Rising into the sky, he went to the Himalayas.

Dīpaṅkaropi satthā catūhi khīṇāsava satasahassehi parivuto rammanagaravāsīhi pūjīyamāno devatāhi abhinaṇdiyamāno alaṅkata paṭiyattena maggena rammanagaraṃ pavisitvā paññattavarabuddhāsane nisīdi. Bhikkhusaṅghopi attano attano pattāsane nisīdi. Rammanagaravāsinopi upāsakā buddhapamukhassa bhikkhusaṅghassa mahādānaṃ [O 0.0006] datvā bhagavantaṃ bhuttāviṃ onīta pattapāṇiṃ mālāgaṇdhādīhi pūjetvā dānānumodanaṃ sotukāmā nisīdiṃsu. Bhagavāpi tesaṃ anumodanaṃ karonto dānakathaṃ sīlakathaṃ saggikathaṃ kāmānaṃ ādīnavaṃ okāraṃ saṃkilesaṃ nekkhamme ānisaṃsañca pakāsetvā amata pariyosānaṃ dhammakathaṃ kathesi.

The Teacher Dīpaṅkara, surrounded by four hundred thousand Arahants, was honored by the residents of Ramma city and rejoiced at by the deities. He entered Ramma city by the adorned and prepared path and sat upon the appointed, excellent Buddha-seat. The Saṅgha of monks also sat, each on their own appointed seat. The lay devotees of Ramma city, having offered a great alms-giving to the Saṅgha headed by the Buddha, honored the Blessed One—who had finished his meal and withdrawn his hand from his bowl—with garlands, perfumes, and other offerings. Eager to hear the words of rejoicing for the gift, they sat down. The Blessed One, giving his approval to them, expounded a discourse on generosity, a discourse on virtue, a discourse on the heavens, the danger, inferiority, and defilement of sensual pleasures, and the benefits of renunciation, concluding with a Dhamma talk on the deathless.

Evaṃ tassa mahajanassa dhammaṃ desetvā ekacce saraṇesu ekacce pañcasu sīlesu ekacce sotāpattiphale ekacce sakadāgāmi phale ekacce anāgāmī phale ekacce catusupi phalesu ekacce tīsu vijjāsu ekacce chaḷabhiññāsu ekacce aṭṭhasu samāpattisu patiṭṭhāpetvā uṭṭhāyāsanā rammanagarato nikkhamitvā sudassana mahāvihārameva pāvisi.

Having thus taught the Dhamma to that great assembly, and having established some in the refuges, some in the five precepts, some in the fruit of stream-entry, some in the fruit of once-returning, some in the fruit of non-returning, some in all four fruits, some in the three knowledges, some in the six higher knowledges, and some in the eight attainments, he rose from his seat and, leaving Ramma city, entered the Sudassana Great Monastery.

Vuttañhetaṃ

For this was said:

“Tadā te bhojayitvāna - sasaṅghaṃ lokanāyakaṃ,

Uphagachuññaṃ saraṇaṃ tassa - dīpaṅkarassa satthuno,

Saraṇāgamane kañci - nivaseti tathāgato

Kañci pañcasu sīlesu - sīle dasavidhe paraṃ;

Then, having fed the leader of the world along with the Saṅgha, they went for refuge to the Teacher Dīpaṅkara. The Tathāgata establishes some in the taking of the refuges, some in the five precepts, and others in the higher tenfold precepts;

Kassaci deti sāmaññaṃ - caturo phalamuttame,

Kassaci asathe dhamme - deti so paṭisambhidā;

To some he gives the holy life and the four supreme fruits; to some he gives the analytic insights into the unwavering Dhamma;

Kassaci varasamāpattiyo - aṭṭha deti narāsabho,

Tisso kassaci vijjāyo - chaḷabhiñña pavecchati;

To some, the Bull of Men gives the eight supreme attainments; to some he bestows the three knowledges and the six higher knowledges;

Tena yogena janakāyaṃ - ovadeti mahāmuni,

Tena vitthārikaṃ āsī - lokanāthassa sāsanaṃ;

By that means, the Great Sage instructs the populace; through that, the Teaching of the World-Protector became widespread;

Mahāhanūsabhakkhaṇdho - dīpaṅkarasanāmako,

Bahū jane tārayati - parimoceti duggatiṃ;

With great jaws and bull-like shoulders, the one named Dīpaṅkara ferries many people across, freeing them from bad destinations;

Bodhaneyyaṃ janaṃ disvā - satasahassepi yojane

Khaṇena upagantvāna - bodheti taṃ mahāmunī”ti;

Seeing a person fit to be awakened, even a hundred thousand leagues away, the Great Sage approaches in an instant and awakens them.

Iti so dīpaṅkaro satthā vassasata sahassāni ṭhatvā sattānaṃ baṇdhanamokkhaṃ kurumāno sabbabuddhakiccāni niṭṭhāpetvā naṇdārāme anupādisesāya nibbānadhātuyā parinibbāyinaheva dhātuyo tassa - satthuno vikiriṃsu tā. Ṭhitā ekaghanā hutvā - suvaṇṇapaṭimā viya sakalajambudīpavāsino manussā ghanakoṭiṭimasuvaṇṇaṭṭhikāhi evaṃ chattiṃsa yojanikaṃ mahāthūpamakaṃsu nivedasi - keci.

Thus, that Teacher Dīpaṅkara, having dwelt for a hundred thousand years, bringing about the release from bondage for beings and completing all the duties of a Buddha, attained final Nibbāna in the Nandārāma grove with the Nibbāna-element without residue remaining. The relics of that Teacher did not scatter. Remaining as one solid mass, like a golden image, the people of the entire Jambudīpa built a great stupa thirty-six yojanas high. So it is related by some.

Tena [O 0.0007] vuttaṃ

Therefore, it was said:

“Dīpaṅkaro jino satthā - naṇdārāmamhi nibbuto,

Tattheva tassa jinathūpo - chattiṃsubbedha yojano“;

The Conqueror Dīpaṅkara, the Teacher, attained Nibbāna in Nandārāma. Right there his Victor's stupa stood, thirty-six yojanas in height.

“Patta cīvara parikkhāra - paribhogañja satthuno,

Bodhimūle tadā thūpo - tīṇi yojanamuggato”ti;

For the Teacher's bowl, robe, and requisites of use, a stupa then rose at the foot of the Bodhi tree, three yojanas high.

7. Dīpaṅkarassa pana bhagavato aparabhāgo ekaṃ asaṅkheyyaṃ atikkamitvā koṇḍañño nāma satthā udapādi. Tadā bodhisatto vijitāvi nāma cakkavatti hutvā koṭisatasahassa saṅkhassa buddha pamukhassa bhikkhusaṅghassa mahādānaṃ adāyi satthā bodhisattaṃ buddho bhavissatīti vyākaritvā dhammaṃ desesi so satthu dhammaṃkathaṃ sutvā rajji niyyādetvā pabbaji so tīṇi piṭakāni uggahetvā aṭṭha samāpattiyo pañcaabhiññāyoca uppādetvā aparihīnajjhāno brahmaloke nibbatti. Sopi buddho vassasatasahassāni ṭhatvā sabbabuddhakiccāni niṭṭhāpetvā caṇdārāme parinibbāyi tassāpi bhagavato dhātuyā na vikiriṃsu sakalajambudīpavāsino manussā samāgantvā sattayojanikāsattaratanamayaṃ haritālamanosilāyamattikākiccaṃ tela sappīhi udakakiccaṃ katvā cetiyaṃ niṭṭhāpesuṃ

7. After the Blessed One Dīpaṅkara, when one incalculable eon had passed, a Teacher named Koṇḍañña arose. At that time, the Bodhisatta was a wheel-turning monarch named Vijitāvī. He gave a great offering to the Saṅgha of bhikkhus, headed by the Buddha, numbering one hundred thousand koṭis. The Teacher prophesied, "This Bodhisatta will become a Buddha," and then taught the Dhamma. Hearing the Teacher’s Dhamma talk, the Bodhisatta handed over his kingdom and went forth. He mastered the Three Piṭakas, produced the eight meditative attainments and the five higher knowledges, and, with his jhāna undiminished, was reborn in the Brahma world. That Buddha too, having lived for one hundred thousand years and completed all the duties of a Buddha, attained final Nibbāna in Caṇḍārāma. The relics of that Blessed One were not scattered. The people of all Jambudīpa gathered and completed a cetiya seven yojanas high and made of seven kinds of precious stones, performing the work of clay with orpiment and realgar, and the work of water with oil and ghee.

Koṇḍaññe kira sambuddho - caṇdārāme manorame,

Nibbāyi cetiyo tassa - sattayojaniko katoti;

It is said that Koṇḍañña, the Perfectly Enlightened One, attained Nibbāna in the delightful Caṇḍārāma. His cetiya was built seven yojanas high.

Tassa aparabhāge ekaṃ asaṃṅkhayyaṃ atikkamitvā ekasmiṃyeva kappe cattāro buddhā uppajjiṃsu - maṅgalo sumano revato yohitoti. Maṅgalassa pana bhagavato kāle bodhisatto suruci nāma brāhmaṇo hutvā satthāraṃ nimantessāmīti upasaṅkamitvā madhura dhammaṃkataṃ sutvā svātanāya nimantetvā koṭisatasahassa saṅkhassa buddhapamukhassa saṅghassa sattāhaṃ gavapānaṃ nāma dānamadāsi satthā anumodanaṃ karonto mahāpurisaṃ āmantetvā tvaṃ kappasatasahassādhikānaṃ dvannaṃ asaṅkheyyānaṃ matthake gotamo nāma buddho bhavissasīti vyākāsi mahāpuriso vyākaraṇaṃ sutvā ahaṃ kira buddho bhavissāmi ko me gharāvāsena attho pabbajissamīti cintatvā tathārūpaṃ sampattiṃ keḷapiṇḍaṃ vīya pahāya satthu santike pabbajitvā buddhavacanaṃ uggaṇhitvā abhiññā ca samāpattiyo ca nibbattetvā āyupariyosāne brahmaloke nibbatti, tasmimpi buddhe parinibbute dhātuyo na vikiriṃsu, jambudīpavāsino pubbe viya tiṃsayojanakaṃ thūpamakaṃsu.

After him, when one incalculable eon had passed, in a single eon four Buddhas arose: Maṅgala, Sumana, Revata, and Sobhita. During the time of the Blessed One Maṅgala, the Bodhisatta was a brahmin named Suruci. Thinking, "I will invite the Teacher," he approached him, heard the sweet Dhamma talk, and invited him for the next day. For seven days, he gave an offering called Gavapāna to the Saṅgha of bhikkhus, led by the Buddha, numbering one hundred thousand koṭis. The Teacher, giving his approval, addressed the great being: "At the end of two incalculable eons and one hundred thousand more, you will become a Buddha named Gotama." Hearing this prophecy, the great being thought, "It seems I will become a Buddha—what use is household life to me? I shall go forth." Abandoning such wealth as if it were a lump of spittle, he went forth in the Teacher’s presence, learned the Buddha’s word, and developed the higher knowledges and meditative attainments. At the end of his life, he was reborn in the Brahma world. When that Buddha, too, attained final Nibbāna, his relics were not scattered. The people of Jambudīpa, as before, built a stupa thirty yojanas high.

Tena [O 0.0008] vuttaṃ “uyyāne vasabho nāma - buddho nibbāyi maṅgalo, tattheva tassa jinathūpo - tiṃsayojanamuggito”ti.

Therefore it is said: "In the park named Vasabha, the Buddha Maṅgala attained Nibbāna. Right there, his Victor's stupa rose thirty yojanas high."

Tassa aparabhāge sumano nāma satthā udapādi tadā mahāsatto atulo nāma nāgarājā hutvā nibbatti-mahiddhiko mahānubhāvo so buddho uppannoti sutvā ñātisaṅghaparivuto nāgabhavanā nikkhamitvā koṭisatasahassa bhikkhuparivārassa tassa bhagavato dibbaturiyehi upahāraṃ kāretvā mahādānaṃ pavattetvā paccekaṃ dussayugāni datvā saraṇesu patiṭṭhāsi sopi naṃ satthā anāgate buddho bhavissasīti vyākāsi tasmimpi buddhe parinibbute dhātuyo na vikiriṃsu jambudīpavāsano pubbe viya catuyojanikaṃ thūpamakaṃsu.

After him, a Teacher named Sumana arose. At that time, the great being was born as a nāga king named Atula, of great psychic power and great majesty. Hearing that a Buddha had arisen, he left his nāga palace surrounded by his retinue of kin and had an offering of divine musical instruments made to that Blessed One, who was accompanied by a retinue of one hundred thousand koṭis of bhikkhus. He then held a great alms-giving, giving pairs of cloths to each, and was established in the refuges. That Teacher also prophesied to him, "In the future, you will become a Buddha." When that Buddha attained final Nibbāna, his relics did not scatter; as before, the inhabitants of Jambudīpa erected a stupa four yojanas high.

Tena vuttaṃ

Therefore it was said:

“Sumano yasadharo - buddho aggārāmamha nibbuto,

Tattheva tassa jinathūpo - catuyojanamuggato”ti;

Sumana, the glorious Buddha, attained Nibbāna at Aggārāma. Right there, his Victor's stupa rose four yojanas high.

Tassa aparabhāge revato nāma satthā udapādi tadā bodhisatto atidevo nāma brāhmaṇo hutvā satthu dhammadesana sutvā saraṇesu patiṭṭhāya sirasi añjaliṃ paggahetvā tassa satthuno kilesappahāne vaṇṇaṃ vatvā uttarāsaṅghena pūjamakāsi. Sopi naṃ satthā buddho bhavissatiti vyākāsi tasmiṃ pana buddhe parinibbute dhātuyo vikiriṃsu.

After him, a Teacher named Revata arose. At that time, the Bodhisatta was a brahmin named Atideva. Hearing the Teacher’s Dhamma discourse, he was established in the refuges, raised his joined hands to his head, spoke in praise of that Teacher's abandonment of defilements, and made an offering with his upper robe. That Teacher also prophesied to him, "You will become a Buddha." When that Buddha attained final Nibbāna, the relics were scattered.

Tena vuttaṃ

Therefore it was said:

“Revato pavaro buddho - nibbuto so mahāpure,

Dhātu vitthārikaṃ āsī - tesu tesu padesato”ti;

Revata, the foremost Buddha, attained Nibbāna in the great city. His relics became widespread throughout those various regions.

Tassa aparabhāge sobhito nāma satthā udapādi tadā bodhisatto ajito nāma brāhmaṇo hutvā satthu dhammadesanaṃ sutvā saraṇesu patiṭṭāya buddhapamukhassa saṅghassa mahādānaṃ adāsi. Sopi naṃ satthā buddho bhavissasīti vyākāsi tassāpi bhagavato dhātuyo vikiriṃsu.

Subsequently, a Teacher named Sobhita arose. At that time, the Bodhisatta, having become a brahmin named Ajita, heard the Teacher’s Dhamma discourse, established himself in the refuges, and gave a great offering to the Sangha headed by the Buddha. That Teacher also prophesied to him, 'You will become a Buddha.' The relics of that Blessed One were also scattered.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Sobhito varasambuddho - sīharāmamhi nibbuto,

Dhātuvitthārikaṃ āsī - tesu tesu padesato”ti;

“Sobhita, the excellent Perfectly Enlightened One, attained Nibbāna in Sīhārāma. His relics were scattered throughout the various regions.”

Tassa aparabhāge ekamasaṅkheyyaṃ atikkamitvā ekasmiṃyeva kappe tayo buddhā nibbattiṃsu - anomadassi padumo nāradoti. Anomadassissa bhagavato kāle bodhisatto eko

Subsequently, after one incalculable eon had passed, in a single eon three Buddhas arose: Anomadassī, Paduma, and Nārada. During the time of the Blessed One Anomadassī, the Bodhisatta was a certain

1. Yasavaro dhīro - keci.

1. —some say, Yasavaro the wise—

Yakkha [O 0.0009] senāpati ahosi mahiddhiko mahānubhāvo aneka koṭisatasahassānaṃ yakkhānaṃ adhipati. So buddho uppannoti sutvā āgantvā buddhapamukhassa saṅghassa mahādānamadāsi satthāpi taṃ anāgate buddho bhavissasīti vyākāsi. Anomadassimhi pana bhagavati parinibbute dhātuyo na vikiriṃsu jambudīpavāsino pañcavīsayojanakaṃ thūpaṃ kariṃsu

yakkha general, of great psychic power and might, the lord of many hundreds of thousands of crores of yakkhas. Hearing, 'A Buddha has arisen,' he came and gave a great offering to the Sangha headed by the Buddha. The Teacher also prophesied to him, 'In the future, you will become a Buddha.' When the Blessed One Anomadassī attained final Nibbāna, his relics did not scatter. The inhabitants of Jambudīpa built a stupa twenty-five yojanas high.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Anomadassi jino satthā - dhammārāmamhi nibbuto,

Tattheva tassa jinathūpo - ubbedhā paṇṇuvīsatī’ti;

“Anomadassī, the Victor, the Teacher, attained Nibbāna in the Dhammārāma. Right there his Victor's stupa was twenty-five yojanas in height.”

Tassa aparabhāge padumo nāma satthā udapādi tathāgate agāmakāraññe viharentā bodhisatto sīho hutvā satthāraṃ nirodhasamāpattiyā samāpannaṃ disvā pasannacitto vanditvā padakkhiṇaṃ katvā pītisomanassajāto tikkhattuṃ sīhanādaṃ naditvā sattāhaṃ buddhārammaṇaṃ pītiṃ avijahitvā pītisukheneva gocarāya apakkamitvā jivitapariccāgaṃ katvā payirupāsamāno aṭṭhāsi satthā sattāhaccayena nirodhā vuṭṭhito sīhaṃ oloketvā bhikkhusaṅghepi cittaṃ pasādetvā saṅghaṃ vandissatī“ti bhikkhusaṅgho āgacchatūti cintesi bhikkhu tāvadeva āgamiṃsu sīho saṅghe cittaṃ pasādesi. Satthā tassa manaṃ oloketvā anāgate buddho bhavissatīti vyākāsi tassa pana bhagavato dhātuyo vikiriṃsu.

Subsequently, a Teacher named Paduma arose. While the Tathāgata was dwelling in the Agamaka forest, the Bodhisatta, having become a lion, saw the Teacher attained to the attainment of cessation. With a confident mind, he paid homage, circumambulated him, and with rapture and mental joy arisen, he roared a lion's roar three times. For seven days, not abandoning the joy that had the Buddha as its object, and without departing for his feeding ground, he stood in attendance, having made a sacrifice of his life, sustained only by that rapture and happiness. At the end of seven days, the Teacher emerged from cessation. Seeing the lion, he thought, 'He will also inspire confidence in the Sangha of bhikkhus and will pay homage to the Sangha. Let the Sangha of bhikkhus come.' The bhikkhus arrived at that very moment. The lion inspired confidence in the Sangha. The Teacher, perceiving his mind, prophesied, 'In the future, he will become a Buddha.' The relics of that Blessed One, however, were scattered.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Padumo jinavaro satthā - dhammārāmamhi nibbuto,

Dhātu vitthārikaṃ āsi - tesu tesu padesato”ti;

“Paduma, the excellent Victor, the Teacher, attained Nibbāna in the Dhammārāma. His relics were scattered throughout the various regions.”

Tassa aparabhāge nārado nāma satthā ahosi tadā bodhisatto, isipabbajjaṃ pabbajitvā pañcasu abhiññāsu aṭṭhasuca samāpattisuciṇṇavasī hutvā buddhapamukhassa saṅghassa mahādānaṃ datvā lohita caṇdanena pūjamakāsi sopi naṃ anāgato buddho bhavissatīti vyākāsi nārada pana bhagavato dhātuyo ekaghanā ahesuṃ. Sabbe deva manussā sannipatitvā catuyojanikaṃ thūpaṃ kariṃsu.

Subsequently, there was a Teacher named Nārada. At that time, the Bodhisatta, having gone forth into the ascetic life, and having become a master of the five higher knowledges and the eight meditative attainments, gave a great offering to the Sangha headed by the Buddha and made an offering with red sandalwood. He too prophesied to him, 'In the future, you will become a Buddha.' The relics of the Blessed Nārada, however, were a single solid mass. All gods and humans gathered and built a stupa four yojanas high.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Nārado jinavasabho - nibbuto sudassane pure,

Tattheva tassa thūpavaro - catuyojanamuggato”ti;

“Nārada, the Bull of Victors, attained Nibbāna in the city of Sudassana. Right there, his excellent stupa rose four yojanas high.”

Tassa aparabhāge ekamasaṅkheyyamatikkamitvā ito kappasatasahassa matthake ekasmiṃ kappe padumuttaro nāma satthā udapādi. Tadā bodhisatto jaṭilo nāma mahāraṭṭhiko hutvā buddhapamukhassa saṅghassa civaradānamadāsi. Sopi taṃ anāgato buddho bhavissatīti vyākāsi. [O 0.0010] Padumuttarassāpi bhagavato dhātuyo ekaghanā ahesuṃ. Sabbe devamanussā sannipatitvā dvādasa yojanikaṃ mahāthūpamakaṃsu.

Subsequently, after one incalculable eon had passed, one hundred thousand eons ago from the present, in a certain eon, the Teacher named Padumuttara arose. At that time, the Bodhisatta, having become a wealthy landowner named Jaṭila, gave an offering of robes to the Sangha headed by the Buddha. He too prophesied to him, 'In the future, you will become a Buddha.' The relics of the Blessed One Padumuttara were also a single solid mass. All gods and humans gathered and built a great stupa twelve yojanas high.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Padumuttaro jino buddho - naṇdārāmamhi nibbuto,

Tattheva tassa thūpavaro - dvādasubbedhayojano”ti;

“Padumuttara, the Victor, the Buddha, attained Nibbāna in Nandārāma. Right there, his excellent stupa was twelve yojanas in height.”

Tassa aparabhāge tiṃsakappa sahassāni atikkamitvā sumedho sujāto cāti ekasmiṃ kappe dve buddhā nibbattiṃsu sumedhassa pana bhagavato kāle bodhisatto uttaro nāma mānavo hutvā nidahitvā ṭhapitaṃyeva asītikoṭidhanaṃ vissajjetvā buddhapamukhassa saṅghassa mahādānaṃ datvā dhammaṃ sutvā saraṇesu patiṭṭhāya nikkhamitvā pabbaji sopi naṃ anāgate buddho bhavissatīti vyākāsi. Sumdhessa pana bhagavato dhātuyo vikiriṃsu

Subsequently, after thirty thousand eons had passed, two Buddhas arose in one eon: Sumedha and Sujāta. During the time of the Blessed One Sumedha, the Bodhisatta, having become a young man named Uttara, gave away eighty crores of wealth that had been buried and stored. He gave a great offering to the Sangha headed by the Buddha, and having heard the Dhamma and established himself in the refuges, he renounced the world and went forth. He too prophesied to him, 'In the future, you will become a Buddha.' The relics of the Blessed One Sumedha, however, were scattered.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Sumedho jinavaro buddho - medhārāmamhi nibbuto,

Dhātuvitthārikaṃ āsi - tesu tesu padesato”ti;

“Sumedha, the excellent Victor, the Buddha, attained Nibbāna in Medhārāma. His relics were scattered throughout the various regions.”

Tassa aparabhāge sujāto nāma satthā udapādi. Tadā bodhisattocakkavattirājā hutvā buddho uppannoti sutvā upasaṃkamitvā dhammaṃ sutvā buddhapamukhassa saṅghassa saddhiṃ sattahi ratanehi catumahādīpaṃ rajjaṃ datvā satthu santike pabbaji sakalaraṭṭhavāsino raṭṭhuppādaṃ gahetvā ārāmika kiccaṃ sādhentā buddhapamukhassa saṅghassa niccaṃ mahādānamadaṃsu. Sopi naṃ satthā anāgate buddhobhavissatīti vyākāsi sujātassa pana bhagavato dhātuyo ekaghanā ahesuṃ jambudīpavāsino tigāvutaṃ thūpamakaṃsu

Subsequently, the Teacher named Sujāta arose. At that time, the Bodhisatta was a wheel-turning monarch. Hearing that a Buddha had arisen, he approached him, listened to the Dhamma, and gave the kingdom of the four great continents, together with the seven treasures, to the Sangha headed by the Buddha. Then he went forth in the Teacher's presence. The inhabitants of the entire kingdom, taking the produce of the kingdom, performed the duties of monastery attendants, constantly giving great offerings to the Sangha headed by the Buddha. That Teacher also prophesied to him, 'In the future, you will become a Buddha.' The relics of the Blessed One Sujāta, however, remained in a single mass. The inhabitants of Jambudīpa built a stupa three gāvutas high.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Sujāto jinavaro buddho sīlārāmamhi nibbuto,

Tattheva cetiyo tassa - tīṇi gāvutamuggato”ti;

“Sujāta, the excellent Victor, the Buddha, attained Nibbāna in Sīlārāma. There his shrine rose three gāvutas high.”

Tassa aparabhāge aṭṭhārasa kappasatamatthake ekasmiṃ kappe piyadassi atthadassi dhammadassiti tayo buddhā nibbattiṃsu piyadassi buddhakāle bodhisatto kassapo nāma māṇavo tiṇṇaṃ vedānaṃ pāragato hutvā satthu dhammadesanaṃ sutvā koṭisatasahassa dhana pariccāgena saṅghārāmaṃ kāretvā saraṇesu ca sīlesu ca patiṭṭhāsi atha naṃ satthā aṭṭhārasa kappa sataccayena buddho bhavissatīti vyākāsi piyadassissa bhagavatopi dhātuyo ekaghanāva ahesuṃ jambudīpavāsino sannipatitvā tiyojanikaṃ mahāthūpamakaṃsu.

Subsequently, eighteen hundred eons ago, in one eon, three Buddhas arose: Piyadassī, Atthadassī, and Dhammadassī. During the time of Piyadassī Buddha, the Bodhisatta was a young brahmin named Kassapa who had mastered the three Vedas. Having heard the Teacher’s Dhamma discourse, he had a monastery for the Sangha built with a donation of one hundred thousand crores of wealth, and established himself in the refuges and the moral precepts. Then the Teacher prophesied to him, 'After an interval of eighteen hundred eons, you will become a Buddha.' The relics of the Blessed Piyadassī also were a single solid mass. The inhabitants of Jambudīpa gathered and built a great stupa three yojanas high.

Tena [O 0.0011] vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Piyadassi munivaro - salalārāmamhi nibbuto,

Tattheva tassa jinathūpo - tīṇi yojanamuggato”ti;

“Piyadassī, the foremost sage, attained Nibbāna in Salalārāma. There, the Victor's stupa rose three yojanas high.”

Tassa aparabhāge atthadassi nāma bhagavā udapādi tadā bodhisatto mahiddhiko mahānubhāvo susimo nāma tāpaso hutvā bhagavato santike dhammaṃ sutvā pasīditvā dibbāni maṇdārava padumapāricchattakādīni pupphāni āharitvā cātudvīpika mahāmegho viya pupphavassaṃ vassetvā samantato pupphamaṇḍapa pupphaagaghiya toraṇādīni katvā maṇdārava pupphacchattena dasabalaṃ pūjesi sopi naṃ bhagavā anāgato gotamo nāma buddho bhavissatīti vākāsi. Tassa pana bhagavato dhātuyo vikiriṃsu.

Subsequently, the Blessed One named Atthadassī arose. At that time, the Bodhisatta was an ascetic named Susīma, of great psychic power and might. Having heard the Dhamma in the presence of the Blessed One and being filled with confidence, he brought divine flowers such as the mandārava, paduma, and pāricchattaka. Like a great cloud over the four continents, he caused a rain of flowers, and having made flower pavilions, flower houses, arches, and so on all around, he honored the Ten-Powered One with a parasol of mandārava flowers. That Blessed One also prophesied to him, 'In the future, you will become a Buddha named Gotama.' The relics of that Blessed One, however, were scattered.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Atthadassi jinavaro - anomārāmamhi nibbuto,

Dhātuvitthārikaṃ āsi - tesu tesu ca raṭṭhato”ti;

“Atthadassī, the excellent Victor, attained Nibbāna in Anomārāma. His relics were scattered throughout the various kingdoms.”

Tassa aparabhāge dhammadassi nāma satthā udapādi. Tadā bodhisatto sakkā devarājā dibbagaṇdhapupphehi ca dibbaturiyehi ca pūjaṃ akāsi sopi naṃ buddho bhavissatīti vyākāsi. Dhammadassissa pana bhagavato dhātuyo ekaghanā ahesuṃ. Jambudīpavāsino tiyojanikaṃ thūpamakaṃsu.

Subsequently, the Teacher named Dhammadassī arose. At that time, the Bodhisatta, who was then Sakka, king of devas, made an offering of divine fragrant flowers and divine music. He also prophesied concerning him: 'He will become a Buddha.' The relics of the Blessed Dhammadassī, however, remained in a single mass. The inhabitants of Jambudīpa built a stupa three yojanas high.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Dhammadassi mahāvīro-kelāsāramamhi nibbuto,

Tattheva thūpavaro tassa-tīṇi yojanamuggato”ti;

“Dhammadassī, the Great Hero, attained Nibbāna in Kelāsārāma. There his excellent stupa rose three yojanas high.”

Tassa aparabhāge catunavutikappa matthake ekasmiṃ kappe ekova siddhattho nāma satthā udapādi. Tadā bodhisatto uggatejo abhiññābala sampanno maṅgalo nāma tāpaso hutvā mahājambuphalaṃ āharitvā tathāgassa adāsi satthā taṃ phalaṃ paribhuñjitvā catunavuti kappamatthake buddho bhavissatīti vyākāsi tassāpi bhagavato dhātuyo na vikiriṃsu, catuyojanikaṃ ratanamayaṃ thūpamakaṃsu.

Subsequently, at the end of ninety-four eons, in one eon, a single Teacher named Siddhattha arose. At that time, the Bodhisatta, who was then an ascetic named Maṅgala, endowed with intense energy and the power of supernormal knowledge, brought a large rose-apple fruit and gave it to the Tathāgata. The Teacher, having eaten the fruit, prophesied concerning him: 'Ninety-four eons hence, you will become a Buddha.' The relics of that Blessed One also did not scatter; they built a jeweled stupa four yojanas high.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Siddhatto munivaro buddho anomāramamhi nibbuto,

Tattheva tassa thūpavaro-catuyojanamuggato”ti;

“Siddhattha, the foremost sage, the Buddha, attained Nibbāna in Anomārāma. There, his excellent stupa rose four yojanas high.”

Tassa aparabhāge ito dvānavuti kappamatthake tisso phussoti ekasmiṃ kappe deva buddhā nibbattiṃsu tissassa bhagavato kāle bodhisattā mahābhogo mahāyaso sujāto nāma khattiyo hutvā isipabbajjaṃ pabbajitvā mahiddhikabhāvaṃ patvā buddho [O 0.0012] uppannoti sutvā dibbamaṇdārava paduma pāricchatta pupphāni ādāya catuparisamajjhe gacchantaṃ tathāgataṃ pūjesi. Ākāse pupphavitānamiva aṭṭhāsi sopi naṃ satthā ito dvānavuti kappamatthake buddho bhavissatīti vyākāsi. Tassāpi bhagavato dhātuyo na vikiriṃsu dhātuyo gahetvā tiyojanikaṃ thūpamakaṃsu.

Subsequently, ninety-two eons from now, in one eon, two Buddhas named Tissa and Phussa arose. During the time of the Blessed One Tissa, the Bodhisatta, who was then a wealthy and illustrious noble named Sujāta, having renounced the world as a hermit and attained great psychic power, heard that a Buddha had arisen. Taking divine mandārava, lotus, and pāricchatta flowers, he honored the Tathāgata as he was going in the midst of the fourfold assembly. The flowers remained like a canopy in the sky. That Teacher also prophesied concerning him: 'Ninety-two eons hence from this, he will become a Buddha.' The relics of that Blessed One also did not scatter; taking the relics, they built a stupa three yojanas high.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Tisso jinavaro buddho-naṇdārāmamhi nibbuto

Tattheva tassa thūpavaro-tīṇi yojanamussito”ti;

“Tissa, the foremost Victor, the Buddha, attained Nibbāna in Nandārāma. There his excellent stupa rose three yojanas high.”

Tassa aparabhāge phusso nāma buddho udapādi tadā bodhisatto vijitāvi nāma khattiyo hutvā mahārajjaṃ pahāya satthu santike pabbajitvā tīṇi piṭakāni uggahetvā mahājanassa dhammakathaṃ kathetvā sīlapāramiñca pūresi. Sopi naṃ buddho tatheva vyākāsi tassa pana bhagavato dhātuyo vikiriṃsu.

Subsequently, the Buddha named Phussa arose. At that time, the Bodhisatta, who was then a noble named Vijitāvi, having renounced his great kingdom, went forth in the Teacher’s presence, mastered the three Piṭakas, preached the Dhamma to the great multitude, and fulfilled the perfection of virtue. That Buddha likewise prophesied concerning him. The relics of that Blessed One, however, were scattered.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Phusso jinavaro satthā-sunandārāmamhi nibbuto

Dhātuvitthārikaṃ āsi-tesu tesu padesato”ti;

“Phussa, the foremost Victor, the Teacher, attained Nibbāna in Sunandārāma. His relics were scattered in various regions.”

Tassa aparabhāge ito ekanavuti kappamatthake vipassi nāma buddho udapādi tadā bodhisatto mahiddhiko mahānubhāvo atulo nāma nāgarājā hutvā sattaratana khacitaṃ sovaṇṇamahāpīṭhaṃ bhagavato adāsi sopi naṃ ito ekanavutikappamatthake buddho bhavissatīti vyākāsi tassa pana bhagavato dhātuyo na vikiriṃsu sabbe devamanussā santipatitvā dhātuyo gahetvā sattayojanikaṃ thūpamakaṃsu.

Subsequently, ninety-one eons from now, the Buddha named Vipassī arose. At that time, the Bodhisatta, who was then a nāga king named Atula, mighty and of great power, offered the Blessed One a great golden throne studded with the seven jewels. He also prophesied concerning him: 'Ninety-one eons hence from this, he will become a Buddha.' The relics of that Blessed One, however, did not scatter; all devas and humans gathered together, and taking the relics, they built a stupa seven yojanas high.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Vipassi jinavaro vīro-sumittārāmamhi nibbuto,

Tattheva so thūpavaro-sattayojaniko kato”ti;

“Vipassī, the foremost Victor, the hero, attained Nibbāna in Sumittārāma. There, an excellent stupa, seven yojanas high, was made for him.”

Tassa aparabhāge ito ekatiṃsa kappamatthake sikhī vessabhūti dve buddhā nibbattiṃsu sikhissa bhagavato kāle bodhisatto ariṇdamo nāma rājā hutvā buddhapamukhassa saṅghassa sacivaraṃ mahādānaṃ pavattetvā sattaratana patimaṇḍitaṃ hatthiratanaṃ datvā hatthippamāṇaṃ katvā kappiya bhaṇḍamadāsi sopi naṃ ito ekatiṃsa kappe buddho bhavissatīti vyākāsi, sikhissa bhagavato dhātuyo ekaghanā hutvā aṭṭhaṃsu, sakala jambudīpavāsino pana manussā dhātuyo gahetvā tiyojanubbedhaṃ sattaratanamayaṃ himagiri sadisa sobhaṃ thūpamakaṃsu.

Subsequently, thirty-one eons from now, Sikhī and Vessabhū, two Buddhas, arose. During the time of the Blessed One Sikhī, the Bodhisatta, who was then a king named Ariṇḍama, offered a great almsgiving with robes to the Sangha headed by the Buddha, and gave an elephant treasure adorned with the seven jewels, having made a gift of permissible goods equal to an elephant's measure. He also prophesied concerning him: 'Thirty-one eons hence, he will become a Buddha.' The relics of the Blessed One Sikhī remained in a single mass. However, all the inhabitants of Jambudīpa, taking the relics, built a stupa three yojanas high, made of the seven jewels, with a beauty resembling Mount Himavanta.

Sikhī [O 0.0013] munivaro buddho-dussārāmamhi nibbuto,

Tattheva tassa thūpavaro-tīṇi yojanamuggato”ti;

“Sikhī, the foremost sage, the Buddha, attained Nibbāna in Dussārāma. There his excellent stupa rose three yojanas high.”

Tassa aparabhāge vessabhū nāma satthā udapādi. Tadā bodhisatto sudassano nāma rājā hutvā buddhapamukhassa saṅghassa sacīvaramahādānaṃ datvā tassa santike pabbajitvā ācāraguṇasampanno buddharatane citatīkāra pīti bahulo ahosi sepi naṃ satthā ito ekatiṃsakappe buddho bhavissatīti vyākāsi vessabhussa pana bhagavato dhātuyo vikiriṃsu.

Subsequently, the Teacher named Vessabhū arose. At that time, the Bodhisatta, who was then a king named Sudassana, gave a great offering with robes to the Sangha headed by the Buddha. He went forth in his presence and, endowed with virtuous conduct, was full of joy and great reverence for the Buddha-jewel. That Teacher also prophesied concerning him: 'Thirty-one eons hence, he will become a Buddha.' The relics of the Blessed One Vessabhū, however, were scattered.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Vessabhū jinavaro satthā-khemārāmamhi nibbuto;

Dhātuvitthārikaṃ āsi-tesu tesu padesato”ti;

“Vessabhū, the foremost Victor, the Teacher, attained Nibbāna in Khemārāma. His relics were scattered in various regions.”

Tassa aparabhāge imasmiṃ kappe cattāro buddhā nibbattiṃsu kakusaṇdho konāgamano kassapo amhākaṃ bhagavāti katusaṇdhassa pana bhagavato kālo bodhisatto khemo nāma rājā hutvā buddhapamukhassa saṅghassa sapattacīvaraṃ dānañceva añjanādi bhesajjāni ca datvā satthu dhammadesanaṃ sutvā pabbaji sopi naṃ satthā vyākāsi tassa pana bhagavato dhātuyo na vikiriṃsu sabbe sannipatitvā dhātuyo gahetvā gāvutubbedhaṃ thūpamakaṃsu.

Subsequently, in this eon, four Buddhas arose: Kakusandha, Konāgamana, Kassapa, and our Blessed One. Now, during the time of the Blessed One Kakusandha, the Bodhisatta, who was then a king named Khema, gave an offering of bowl and robes, together with medicines such as eye ointment, to the Sangha headed by the Buddha. Having heard the Teacher’s Dhamma discourse, he went forth. That Teacher also prophesied concerning him. The relics of that Blessed One, however, did not scatter; all having come together, they took the relics and built a stupa one gāvuta high.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Kakusaṇdho jinavaro - khemārāmamhi nibbuto,

Tattheva tassa thūpavaro-gāvutaṃ nabhamuggato”ti;

“Kakusandha, the foremost Victor, attained Nibbāna in Khemārāma. There his excellent stupa rose one gāvuta to the sky.”

Tassa aparabhāge konāgamano nāma satthā udapādi tadā bodhisatto pabbato nāma rajā hutvā amaccagaṇa parivuto satthu santike gantvā dhammadesanaṃ sutvā buddhapamukhaṃ bhikkhusaṅghaṃ nimantetvā mahādānaṃ pavattetvā pattuṇṇacīnapaṭṭa koseyya kambala dukulāni ceva suvaṇṇapaṭṭakañca datvā satthu santike pabbaji. Sopi naṃ satthā vyākāsi tassa bhagavato dhātuyo vikiriṃsu

After him, the Teacher named Konāgamana arose. At that time, the Bodhisatta, who was then a king named Pabbata, surrounded by his retinue of ministers, went to the Teacher’s presence. After hearing the Dhamma discourse, he invited the Sangha of monks headed by the Buddha and established a great almsgiving. He offered fine woolen cloth, Chinese silk, Kāsi silk, woolen blankets, and fine linen, as well as golden brocade, and then went forth in the Teacher’s presence. That Teacher also prophesied concerning him. The relics of that Blessed One were scattered.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Konāgamano sambuddho pabbatārāmamhi nibbuto,

Dhātu vitthārikaṃ āsi-tesu tesu padesato”ti;

“Konāgamana, the Perfectly Enlightened One, attained Nibbāna at Pabbatārāma. His relics became widespread in various regions.”

Tassa aparabhāge kassapo nāma satthā udapādi. Tadā bodhisatto jotipālo nāma māṇavo hutvā tiṇṇaṃ vedānaṃ pāragubhūmiyañceva antalikkheva pākaṭo ghaṭīkārassa kumbhakārassa mitto ahosi. So tena saddhiṃ satthāraṃ upasaṅkamitvā dhamma

After him, the Teacher named Kassapa arose. At that time, the Bodhisatta, who was then the brahmin youth Jotipāla, having reached mastery of the three Vedas and being renowned far and wide, was a friend of Ghaṭīkāra, the potter. Together with him, he approached the Teacher to hear the Dhamma.

Kathaṃ [O 0.0014] sutvā pabbajitvā āraddhavīriyo tīṇi piṭakāni uggahetvā vattā vattasampattiyā buddhasāsanaṃ sobhesi. Sopi naṃ satthā vyākāsi. Kassapassa pana satthuno dhātuyo na vikiriṃsu sakala jambudīpavāsino manussā sannipatitvā ekekaṃ suvaṇṇaṭṭhikaṃ koṭi agghanakaṃ ratanavicittaṃ bahi racanatthaṃ ekekaṃ aḍḍhakoṭi agghanakaṃ abbhantara pūraṇatthaṃ manosilāya mattikākiccaṃ telena udakakiccaṃ karontā yojanubbedhaṃ thupamakaṃsu.

Having heard the teaching, he went forth, and with aroused effort, mastered the three Piṭakas. As an expounder and one accomplished in conduct, he glorified the Buddha's dispensation. That Teacher also made a prediction concerning him. However, the relics of the Teacher Kassapa were not scattered. The people dwelling in all of Jambudīpa assembled and built a stupa one yojana in height, using for the external construction golden bricks, each worth a crore and variegated with jewels, and for the interior filling, bricks each worth half a crore, performing the work of clay with red arsenic and the work of water with oil.

Tena vuttaṃ

Thus it was said:

“Mahākassapo jino satthā-setavyāyañhi nibbuto,

Tattheva tassa jinathupo-yojanubbedhamuggato”ti;

“The great Kassapa, the Victorious Teacher, attained final Nibbāna in Setavyā. There, his stupa, one yojana high, arose.”

Ettha ca

And here,

Dīpaṅkaro ca koṇḍañño-maṅgalo sumano tathā,

Anomadassī buddho ca-nārado padumuttaro;

Dīpaṅkara and Koṇḍañña, Maṅgala, and likewise Sumana, Anomadassī the Buddha, and Nārada, Padumuttara;

Sujāto piyadassī ca-dhammadassi naruttamo,

Siddhatthabuddho tisso ca-vipassi ca sikhī tathā;

Sujāta, Piyadassī, and Dhammadassī, the supreme among men, Siddhattha the Buddha, Tissa, and Vipassī, and likewise Sikhī;

Kakusaṇdho kassapo cāti-soḷasete mahesayo,

Thupappamāṇametesaṃ pāliyaṃyeva dassitaṃ;

Kakusandha and Kassapa—these sixteen great seers; the measurements of their stupas are shown in the Pāli text itself.

Yasmā tasmā mayā sādhū-te sabbepi pakāsitā,

Thūpā saddhā janā sādhū-te vandeyyatha sādaraṃ;

Therefore, I have well proclaimed all these; virtuous people of faith should reverently venerate these stupas.

Yesānaṃ pana aṭṭhannaṃ - sugatānaṃ hitesinaṃ

Dhātu vitthārikā āsuṃ - tesu tesu padesatoti;

For eight of those Sugatas, the seekers of welfare, the relics became widespread in various places.

Sādhujana manopasādanatthāya kate thūpavaṃse vijjamānathūpānaṃ buddhānaṃ thūpakathā ceva sabbesaṃ santike abhinihārakatāce. Samattā.

In the Thūpavaṃsa, composed for the purpose of inspiring faith in virtuous people, the account of the stupas of the Buddhas whose stupas are extant, and of the aspirations made in their presence, is concluded.

Kassapassa pana bhagavato aparabhāge ṭhapetvā imaṃ sammā sambuddhaṃ añño buddho nāma natthi. Evaṃ dīpaṅkarādīnaṃ catuvīsatiyā buddhānaṃ santike laddhavyākaraṇo bodhisatto pāramiyo pūretvā vessantarattabhāve ṭhito-

After the Blessed Kassapa, apart from this Perfectly Enlightened One, there was no other Buddha. Thus, the Bodhisatta, having received prophecies from the twenty-four Buddhas beginning with Dīpaṅkara, and having fulfilled the perfections, was in his existence as Vessantara—

“Acetanāyaṃ pathavī-aviññāya sukhaṃ dukhaṃ,

Sāpi dānabalo mayhaṃ-sattakkattuṃ pakampathā”ti;

“This insentient earth, not knowing pleasure or pain, even she trembled seven times by the power of my giving.”

Evaṃ mahāpathavi kampanāni puññāni katvā āyupariyosāne tato cuto tusitabhavane nibbatti tattha aññe deve dasahi ṭhānehi adhigaṇhitvā yāvatāyukaṃ dibbasampattiṃ anubhavanto manussagaṇanāya [O 0.0015] sattahi divasehi āyukkhayaṃ pāpuṇissatiti vatthāni kiḷissanti, mālā milāyanti, kacchehi sedā muccanti, kāyeceva dubbaṇṇiyaṃ okkamati. Devo devāsane nābhiramatīti imesu pañcasu pubbanimittesu uppannesu tāni disvāsuññāvata no saggā bhavissantiti saṃvegajātāhi devatāhi mahāsattassa pāraminaṃ pūritabhāvaṃ ñatvā imasmiṃ idāni aññaṃ devalokaṃ anupagantvā manussaloke uppajjitvā buddhabhāvaṃ patte puññāni katvā cuta cutā devalokaṃ pūressantīti cintetvā.

Thus, having performed meritorious deeds that caused the great earth to tremble, at the end of his life, he passed away from there and was reborn in the Tusita realm. There, surpassing other devas in ten respects, he experienced divine bliss for the full extent of his life. When the five premonitory signs arose—his garments became soiled, his garlands withered, sweat was released from his armpits, discoloration spread over his body, and the deva found no delight in his celestial seat—seeing these, the devas, filled with a sense of urgency, thought: “Alas, our heavens will become empty!” Knowing that the Great Being had fulfilled the perfections, they reflected: “Now, without going to another celestial world, he will arise in the human world and attain Buddhahood. Then, those who perform meritorious deeds will, upon passing away, fill the celestial worlds.”

“Yatohaṃ tusite kāye-santusito nāmahaṃ tadā,

Dasasahassi samāgantvā-yācanti pañjalī mamaṃ;

“When I was in the Tusita realm, named Santusita, then the ten-thousand-fold world system assembled, beseeching me with folded hands;

Kāloyaṃte mahāvīra-uppajja mātukucchiyaṃ,

Sadevakaṃ tārayanto-bujjhassu amataṃ padanti”;

‘O Great Hero, this is your time to arise in a mother’s womb. Liberating the world with its devas, awaken to the deathless state!’”

Evaṃ buddhabhāvatthāya āyācito kālaṃ-dīpaṃ-desaṃ kulaṃ-janettiyā āyuppamāṇanti imāni pañca mahāvilokanāni viloketvā katasanniṭṭhāno tato cuto sakyarājakule paṭisaṇdhiṃgahetvā tattha mahāsampattiyā parihariyamāno anukkamena bhaddaṃ yobbanaṃ anupāpuṇitvā tiṇṇaṃ utūnaṃ anucchavikesu tīsu pāsādesu devalokasiriṃ viya rajjasiriṃ anubhavamāno uyyānakīḷāya gamanasamaye anukkamena jiṇṇa - vyādhi - matasaṅkhāte tayo devadūte disvā saṃjāta saṃvegā nivattitvā catutthe vāre pabbajitaṃ disvā sādhu pabbajjāti pabbajjāya ruciṃ uppādetvā uyyānaṃ gantvā tattha divasaṃ khepetvā maṅgala pokkharaṇitīre nisinno kappaka vesaṃ gahetvā āgatena vissakamma devaputtena alaṅkata paṭiyatto rāhulakumārassa pātasāsanaṃ sutvā puttasinehassa balavabhāvaṃ ñatvā yāva imaṃ baṇdhanaṃ na vaḍḍhati tāvadevanaṃ chiṇdissāmīti cintetvā sāyaṃ nagaraṃ pavisanto-

Thus, having been implored for the sake of Buddhahood, he considered the five great observations—time, continent, country, family, and the mother’s lifespan—and, having made his firm decision, he passed away from there. He then took conception in the Sakyan royal family, where he was nurtured with great prosperity. Gradually attaining a splendid youth, he experienced the glory of kingship, like the glory of the deva world, in three palaces suitable for the three seasons. While going to the pleasure garden for sport, he saw in succession the three divine messengers known as the old, the sick, and the dead, and, a sense of urgency having arisen, he turned back. On the fourth occasion, he saw a renunciate and, developing a liking for renunciation, he thought, “Good is renunciation!” After going to the garden and spending the day there, he sat by the auspicious lotus pond. Adorned and prepared by the devaputta Vissakamma, who had come disguised as a barber, he heard the morning news about Prince Rāhula. Realizing the strength of affection for a son, he thought, “Before this bond grows, I will sever it now.” That evening, as he was entering the city—

“Nibbutā nūna sā mātā - nibbuto nūna so pitā,

Nibbutā nūna sā nārī - yassāyaṃ īdiso pati”ti;

“At peace indeed is that mother, at peace indeed is that father, at peace indeed is that woman who has such a husband.”

Kisāgotamiyā nāma pitucchā dhītāya bhāsitaṃ imaṃ gāthā sutvā ihaṃ imāya nibbutapadaṃ sāvitoti gīvato satasahassagghanakaṃ muttāhāraṃ omuñcitvā tassā pesetvā attano bhavanaṃ pavisitvā sirīsayane nipanno niddāvasa gatānaṃ nāṭakānaṃ vippakāraṃ disvā nibbinnahadayo channaṃ uṭṭhāpetvā kaṇthakaṃ āhārāpetvā taṃ āruyha channasahāyo dasasahassa cakkavāḷa devatāhi kata parivāro mahābhinikkhamanā nikkhamitvā teneva rattāvasesena tīṇi rajjāni atikkamma anomāya nadiyā paratīraṃ patvā assāpiṭṭhito oruyha [O 0.0016] muttarāsi sadise cālikāpuliṇe ṭhatvā channatvaṃ mayhaṃ ābharaṇāni ceva kaṇthakañca ādāya gacchāhi ābharaṇāni ca kaṇthakañca paṭicchāpetvā dakkhiṇa hatthena maṅgalakhaggamādāya vāmahatthena moliyā saddhiṃ cūḷaṃ chiṇditvā sace ahaṃ buddho bhavissāmi ākāse tiṭṭhatu no ce bhūmiyaṃ patatūti ākāse khipi cuḷāmaṇī baṇdhanaṃ yojanappamāṇaṃ ṭhānaṃgantvā ākāse aṭṭhāsi atha sakko devarājā yojanikena ratanacaṅgoṭakena paṭiggahesi.

Having heard this verse spoken by Kisāgotamī, the daughter of his paternal aunt, and thinking, “By this, the word for ‘peace’ has been made known to me,” he removed from his neck a pearl necklace worth a hundred thousand and sent it to her. Then he entered his own dwelling. Lying down on his royal couch, he saw the disarray of the female musicians who had fallen asleep, and his heart became disenchanted. He woke Channa, had Kaṇṭhaka brought, mounted him, and with Channa as his companion, surrounded by deities from the ten-thousand-fold world system, he went forth in the Great Renunciation. In the remainder of that very night, having crossed three kingdoms, he reached the far bank of the Anomā River. Dismounting from the horse, he stood on a sandy bank resembling a heap of pearls and said to Channa, “You take my ornaments and Kaṇṭhaka and go.” Having handed over the ornaments and Kaṇṭhaka, with his right hand he took the auspicious sword, and with his left hand, he cut off his topknot together with the turban. Thinking, “If I am to become a Buddha, let it remain in the sky; if not, let it fall to the ground,” he threw it into the sky. The jeweled topknot, having gone a distance of a yojana, stopped in the air. Then Sakka, the king of the devas, received it with a jeweled casket one yojana in size.

Yathāha

As it is said:

Chetvāna moliṃ varagandhavāsitaṃ

Vehāsayaṃ ukkhipi sakyapuṅgayavo,

Sahassanetto sirasā paṭiggahi

Suvaṇṇa vaṅgoṭavarena vāsavo’ti;

“Having cut off his topknot, perfumed with excellent scents, the bull of the Sakyans tossed it into the sky. The thousand-eyed one received it with his head; Vāsava, with an excellent golden casket.”

Paṭiggahetvā ca pana devalokaṃ netvā suneru muddhani tiyojanappamāṇaṃ iṇdanīlamaṇimayaṃ cuḷāmaṇi cetiyaṃ nāma akāsi atha kassapa buddhakāle porāṇa sahāyako ghaṭikāra mahābrahmā ekaṃ buddhantaraṃ vināvāsabhāvappattena mattabhāvo cittesi. Ajja me sahāyako mahābhinikkhamanaṃ nikkhanto samaṇa parikkhāramassa gahetvā gacchāmīti-

Having received it, he took it to the deva world and built a shrine named the Cūḷāmaṇi Cetiya, three yojanas in extent, made of sapphire, on the summit of Mount Sineru. Then, the great Brahmā Ghaṭīkāra, his former companion in the time of Kassapa Buddha, who had attained the state of a non-returner for one Buddha-interval, reflected: “Today my friend has gone forth in the Great Renunciation. I shall take the requisites of a recluse and go to him.”

“Ticīvarañca patto ca vāsi sūci ca baṇdhanaṃ,

Parissāvana aṭṭhetena-yuttayogassa bhikkhuno”ti;

“The three robes, the bowl, the razor, the needle, the belt, and the water strainer—these eight are for a bhikkhu devoted to the practice.”

Ime samaṇaparikkhāre āharitvā adāsi mahāpuriso arahaddhajaṃ nivāsetvā uttamaṃ pabbajjāvesaṃ gaṇhitvā sāṭakayugalaṃ ākāse khipi taṃ brahmā paṭiggahetvā brahmaloke dvādasayojanikaṃ sabbaratanamayaṃ dussacetiyamakāsi.

Having brought these requisites of a recluse, he gave them. The Great Being, having donned the banner of the Arahants and taken up the supreme appearance of a renunciate, threw the pair of cloths into the sky. Brahmā received them, and in the Brahmā world, he made a twelve-yojana shrine of the cloths, made entirely of jewels.

“Kilesa appahīṇepi=mahāsattassa taṃ khaṇe,

Yassānubhāvato evaṃ dussacūḷā hi pūjitā

Tasmā tamma mahābodhi-sattānaṃ paṭipattiyaṃ,

Na kareyya mahussāhaṃva-ko hi nāma budho jano”ti;

“Even though defilements were not yet abandoned at that moment for the Great Being, by whose power even the cloth-topknot was thus honored, therefore, in the practice of those great Bodhisattas, what wise person would not make great effort?”

Cūḷāmaṇidussa thupadvayakathā

The Story of the Two Stupas: The Cūḷāmaṇi and the Dussa.

9. Bodhisatto pabbajitvā anukkamena rājagahaṃ gantvā tattha piṇḍāya caritvā paṇḍava pabbatapabbhāre nisinno magadharājena rajjena nimantiyamāno taṃ paṭikkhipitvā sabbaññutaṃ patvā tissavijitaṃ āgamanatthāyatenagahita paṭiñño āḷāraṃ-uddakañca upasaṅkamitvā [O 0.0017] tesaṃ santike adhigatavisesena aparituṭṭho chabbassāni mahāpadhānaṃ padahitvā visākha puṇṇama divase senāninigame sujātāya dinnapāyāsaṃ paribhuñjitvā nerañjarāya nadiyā suvaṇṇapātiṃ pavāhetvā nerañjarāya tīre mahāvanasaṇḍe nānāsamāpattīhi divābhāgaṃ vitināmetvā sāyaṇha samaye sotthiyena dinnaṃ tiṇamuṭṭhiṃ gahetvā kāḷena nāgarājena abhitthutaguṇo bodhimaṇḍaṃ āruyha tiṇāni. Sattharitvā na tāvīmaṃ pallaṅkaṃ bhiṇdissāmi yāva me anupādāya āsavehi cittaṃ vimuccissatī”ti paṭiññaṃ katvā pācīna dāsābhimukho nisīditvā-sūriye anatthamiteyeva mārabalaṃ vidhametvā paṭhamayāme pubbe nivāsañāṇaṃ majjhimayāme cutūpapātañāṇaṃ patvā pacchimayāmāvasāne dasabala catuvesārajjādi sabbaguṇapatimaṇḍitaṃsabbaññutañāṇaṃ paṭivijjhitvā satta sattāhaṃ bodhisamīpeyeva vītināmetvā aṭṭhame sattāhe ajapāla nigrodhamūle nisinno dhammagambhīratā paccavekkhanena appossukkataṃ āpajjamāno dasadahassī mahābrahma parivārena sahampati mahābrahmunā āyācita dhammadesano buddhacakkhunā lokaṃ olokento brahmuno ajjhesanaṃ ādāya kassa nu kho paṭhamaṃ dhammaṃ deseyyanti olokento āḷāruddakānaṃ kālakatabhāvaṃ ñatvā pañcavaggīyānaṃ bhikkhunaṃ bahūpakārataṃ anussaritvā uṭṭhāyāsanā kāsipuraṃ gacchanto antarāmagge upakena saddhiṃ mantetvā āsāḷahipuṇṇama divase isipatane migadāye pañca vaggiyānaṃ bhikkhunaṃ vasanaṭṭhānaṃ patvā te ananucchavikena āvuso vādena samudācarante aññāpetvā dhammacakkaṃ pavattento aññākoṇḍaññattherapamukhe aṭṭhārasa koṭiyo amatapānaṃ pāyesi.

9. The Bodhisatta, having gone forth, gradually reached Rājagaha. There, after wandering for alms, he sat on the slope of Mount Paṇḍava. Being invited by the Magadhan king to accept his kingdom, he declined it. Having attained omniscience, he made a promise to come to the kingdom of Tissa. Approaching Āḷāra and Uddaka, he was not satisfied with the superior attainments he gained in their presence. For six years, he exerted himself in the great striving. On the full moon day of Visākha, having consumed the milk-rice offered by Sujātā in the market town of Senāni, he let the golden bowl float down the Nerañjarā River. On the bank of the Nerañjarā, in the great forest grove, he spent the daytime in various meditative attainments. In the evening, accepting the handful of grass offered by Sotthiya, his virtues praised by Kāḷa, the Nāga king, he ascended the seat of enlightenment. Spreading the grass, he made the resolve: "I will not break this cross-legged posture until my mind is liberated from the taints without clinging." Sitting facing east, even before the sun had set, he vanquished the forces of Māra. In the first watch of the night, he attained the knowledge of past lives; in the middle watch, the knowledge of the passing away and arising of beings; and by the end of the last watch, he penetrated the knowledge of omniscience, adorned with all virtues such as the ten powers and the four confidences. He spent seven weeks in the vicinity of the Bodhi tree. In the eighth week, seated at the foot of the Ajapāla banyan tree, reflecting on the profundity of the Dhamma, he became disinclined to teach. Then, requested to teach by Sahampati, the great Brahmā, surrounded by his retinue of ten thousand great Brahmās, he surveyed the world with the Buddha-eye. Accepting the Brahmā's request and considering to whom he should teach the Dhamma first, he perceived that Āḷāra and Uddaka had passed away. Remembering the great service of the group of five monks, he rose from his seat and set out for Kāsī. On the way, he conversed with Upaka. On the full moon day of Āsāḷha, he arrived at the deer park in Isipatana, the dwelling place of the group of five monks. When they addressed him with the unsuitable term "friend," he instructed them and set in motion the Wheel of Dhamma, causing eighteen crores of beings, with the Elder Aññākoṇḍañña at their head, to drink the nectar of the deathless.

Tato paṭṭhāya cattālīsa saṃvaccharāni ṭhatvā caturāsīti dhammakkhaṇdha sahassāni desetvā gaṇanapathamatīte satte bhavakantārato santāretvā sabbabuddhakiccāni niṭṭhapetvā kusinārāyaṃ upavattane mallānaṃ sālavane yamakasālāna mantare uttasīsakaṃ paññatte mañcake visākhapuṇṇama divase dakkhiṇena passena sato sampajāno anuṭṭhānaseyyāya nipajji. Tadā kira bhagavato pūjāya yamakasālā sabbapāliphullā mūlato paṭṭhāya yāva aggā ekacchannā ahesuṃ. Na kevalañca yamakasālāyeva sabbepi rukkhasākhā sabbapāliphullāva ahesuṃ.

From that time, for forty years, he taught eighty-four thousand sections of the Dhamma, leading countless beings across the wilderness of existence. Having completed all the duties of a Buddha, on the full moon day of Visākha, he lay down between the twin sāla trees in the sāla grove of the Mallās at Kusinārā, on a prepared couch with his head to the north. He lay down on his right side, mindful and clearly comprehending, in the posture from which he would not rise again. At that time, it is said, in honor of the Blessed One, the twin sāla trees were entirely covered with blossoms of all kinds from root to tip. Not only the twin sāla trees, but all the branches of every tree were also in full bloom.

Na kevalañca tasmiṃyeva uyyāne sakalepi dasasahassa cakkavāḷe phalupaga rukkhā phalaṃ gaṇhiṃsu. Sabbarukkhānaṃ khaṇdhesu khaṇdha padumāni, [O 0.0018] valalīsu valalipadumāni. Ākāse ullokapadumāni, pathavitalaṃ bhiṇditvā daṇḍakapadumāni pupphiṃsu. Sabbo mahā samuddo pañcavaṇṇa padumasañchanno ahosi tiyojana sahassavitthato pana himavā ghanabaddhamorapiñchakalāpo viya nirantaraṃ mālādāmagavakkhito viya suṭṭhu pīḷetvā ābaddhapupphavaṭaṃsako viya supūrita pupphacaṅgeṭakaṃ viya ca atiramaṇīyo ahosi.

Not only in that very park, but throughout the entire ten-thousandfold world-system, fruit-bearing trees bore fruit. On the trunks of all the trees were trunk-lotuses, and on the creepers, creeper-lotuses. In the sky, sky-lotuses appeared, and staff-lotuses bloomed, piercing the surface of the earth. The entire great ocean was covered with five-colored lotuses. The Himālaya, three thousand leagues across, was exceedingly delightful, like a tightly bound sheaf of peacock feathers, as if continuously latticed with garlands and wreaths, like a well-pressed and bound floral diadem, and like a flower-basket filled to overflowing.

Yamakasālā bhummadevatāni sañcālitakkhaviṇdhaṭapā tathāgatasarīrassa uparipupphāni vikiranti. Dibbānipi maṇdārava pupphāni antalikkhā patanti. Tāni honti suvaṇṇa vaṇṇāni paṇṇacchattappamāṇāni mahātumbamattaṃ reṇu gaṇhanti na kevalañca maṇdārava pupphāneca, aññānipi sabbāni pāricchattaka pupphādīni suvaṇṇavaṅgoṭakāni rajatacaṅgoṭakāni ca pūretvā pūretvā tidasa purepi brahmalokepi ṭhitāhi devatāhi vividhāni antarā avippakiṇṇāneva hutvā āgantvā pattakiṃjakkha reṇu cuṇṇehi tathāgatassa sarīrameva okīranti na kevalañca devatānaṃyeva nāga supaṇṇa manussānampi upakappana caṇdana cuṇṇāni na kevalañca caṇdanacuṇṇāneva kāḷānusārī tagara lohitacaṇdanādi sabbagandhajātacuṇṇāni, haritālañjana suvaṇṇa rajata cuṇṇāni, sabbagandhavāsa vikatiyo suvaṇṇa rajatādisamugge pūretvā cakkavāḷa mukhavaṭṭiādisu ṭhitāhi devatāhi paviddhā antarā avippakiritvā tathāgatasseva sarīraṃ okiranti dibbānipi turiyāni antalikkhe vajjanti. Na kevalañca tāniyeva sabbānipi tantibaddha cammapariyonaddha susirādi bhedāni dasasahassacakkavāḷe deva-nāgava-supaṇṇa-manussānaṃ turiyāni ekacakkavāḷe sannipatitvā antalikkhe vajjanti.

The earth deities of the Yamaka Sāla grove, with agitated eyes and trembling forms, scattered flowers upon the Tathāgata's body. Divine mandārava flowers also fell from the sky. These were golden in color, the size of a leaf-umbrella, and held pollen in the measure of a large gourd. Not only mandārava flowers, but all other flowers such as pāricchattaka, were brought by the deities standing in the city of the Thirty-three and in the Brahmā world, filling golden and silver baskets. These various flowers, arriving without being scattered on the way, sprinkled the Tathāgata’s body with powdered petals, filaments, and pollen. Not only from the deities, but also the prepared sandalwood powders of nāgas, supaṇṇas, and humans; and not only sandalwood powder, but also powders of all kinds of fragrances such as black aloeswood, tagara, and red sandalwood; powders of orpiment, collyrium, gold, and silver; and all kinds of perfumes and scents were filled into containers of gold and silver. Cast by deities standing at the rim of the world-system, they arrived without being scattered on the way and sprinkled the Tathāgata’s body. Divine musical instruments also sounded in the sky. Not only those, but all musical instruments of devas, nāgas, supaṇṇas, and humans from the ten-thousandfold world-system—of all kinds, such as stringed, leather-covered, and wind instruments—having assembled in one world-system, sounded in the sky.

Varacāraṇadevatā kira nāmekā dīghāyukā devatā mahāpuriso manussapathe nibbattitvā buddho bhavissatīti sutva paṭisaṇdhidivase gaṇhitvā gamissāmāti mālā ganthituṃ ārabhiṃsu tā gaṇthamānāva mahāpurise mātukucchiyaṃ nibbatte tumbhe kassa gaṇthathāti vuttā na tāva tiṭṭhāti kucchito nikkhamana divase gahetvā gamissāmāti āhaṃsu puna nikkhentoti sutvā mahābhinikkhamanadivase gamissāmāti mahābhinikkhamanaṃ nikkhantoti sutvā abhisambodhi divase gamissāmāti. Ajja abhisambuddhoti sutvā dhammacakkappavattanadivase gamissāmāti dhammacakkaṃ pavattayīti sutvā yamakapāṭihāriyadivase gamissāmāti. Ajja yamakapāṭihāriyaṃ karīti sutvā devo rohanadivase gamissāmāti. Ajja devorohanaṃ karīti sutvā āyusaṅkhārossajjane gamissāmāti āyusaṅkhāraṃ ossajīti sutvā na tāva niṭṭhāti parinibbānadivase gamissāmāti?

It is said there was a certain long-lived deity named Varacāraṇadevatā. Hearing that the Great Being would be born on the human path and become a Buddha, they began weaving garlands, thinking, "On the day of his conception, we will take these and go." While they were weaving, the Great Being was conceived in his mother’s womb. When they were asked, "For whom are you weaving these?" they replied, "Not yet. We will take them and go on the day he comes forth from the womb." Having heard he had come forth, they thought, "We will go on the day of his great renunciation." Having heard he had made the great renunciation, they thought, "We will go on the day of his full enlightenment." Having heard, "Today he has become fully enlightened," they thought, "We will go on the day of the setting in motion of the Wheel of Dhamma." Having heard that the Wheel of Dhamma was set in motion, they thought, "We will go on the day of the Twin Miracle." Having heard, "Today he performs the Twin Miracle," they thought, "We will go on the day of the descent from the deva world." Having heard, "Today he makes the descent from the deva world," they thought, "We will go on the day of relinquishing the life-span." Having heard that he had relinquished his life-span, they thought, "It is not yet finished; we will go on the day of his Parinibbāna."
