Ajja [O 0.0019] bhagavā yamakasālānamantare dakkhiṇena passena sato sampajāno sīhaseyyaṃ upagato balava paccusamaye parinibbāyissati tumhe kassa gaṇthathāti vuttā pana kinnāmetaṃ ajjeva mātukucchiyaṃ paṭisaṇdhiṃ gaṇhi ajjeva kucchito nikkhanto, ajjeva mahābhinikkhamanaṃ nikkhami. Ajjevabuddho ahosi ajjeva dhammacakkaṃ pavattayi. Ajjeva yamakapāṭihāriyaṃ akāsi. Ajjeva devalokā otinaṇṇā. Ajjeva āyusaṅkharaṃ ossaji. Ajjeva kira parinibbuto. Nanu nāma dutiya divase yāgupāna kālamattampi ṭhātabbaṃ assa, dasa pāramiyo pūretvā buddhattaṃ pattassa nāma ananucchavikanti apari niṭṭhitāva mālāyo gahetvā āgamma anto cakkavāḷe okāsaṃ ālabhamānā cakkavāḷa mukhavaṭṭiyaṃ labhitvā ādhāvantiyo hatthena hatthaṃ gīvāya gīvaṃ gahetvā tīṇi ratanāni ārabbhadvattiṃsamahapurisalakkhaṇāni chabbaṇṇaraṃsiyo dasapāramiyo addhacchaṭṭhāni jātakasatāni cuddassa buddhañāṇāni ārabbha gāyitvā tassa tassa avasāne sahāya he sahāya heti vadanti. Idametaṃ paṭiccavuttaṃ-dibbānipi saṃgītāni antalikkhe vattantiti.

Today, the Blessed One, lying between the twin sāl trees on his right side in the lion’s posture, mindful and fully aware, will attain final Nibbāna at the break of dawn. When asked, 'For whom are you weaving?' they exclaimed, 'What is this? Today he was conceived in his mother’s womb, today he emerged from the womb, today he undertook the great renunciation, today he became the Buddha, today he set in motion the Wheel of Dhamma, today he performed the twin miracle, today he descended from the heavenly realm, today he relinquished his life-force, and today, it seems, he will attain final Nibbāna. Should he not remain for even the time it takes for a morning gruel on the second day? For one who has fulfilled the ten perfections and attained Buddhahood, is this not unbefitting?' Even before the garlands were fully woven, they came, and not finding space within the world-sphere, they obtained a place at the edge of the world-sphere and rushed forward, hand in hand, neck to neck, singing praises of the Three Jewels, the thirty-two marks of a great man, the six-colored rays, the ten perfections, the five hundred and fifty Jātaka stories, and the fourteen types of Buddha-knowledge. At the end of each verse, they called out, 'O friend! O friend!' This was said in reference to that: 'Celestial songs, too, resounded in the sky.'

Bhagavā pana evaṃ mahatiyā pūjāya vattamānayā paṭhamayāme mallānaṃ dhammaṃ desesi majjhimayāme subhaddassa dhammaṃ desetvā taṃ maggaphale patiṭṭhāpesi pacchimayāme bhikkhu ovaditvā balava paccūsa samaye mahāpathaviṃ kampento anupādisesāya nibbānadhātuyā parinibbāyi parinibbute pana bhagavati lokanāthe ānaṇdatthero mallarājunaṃ taṃ pavattiṃ āroceti te sutvāva gaṇdhamālaṃ sabbañca tālāvacaraṃ pañca ca dussayugasatāni ādāya gantvā bhagavato sarīraṃ naccehi - gītehi - vāditehi - mālehi - gaṇdhehi sakkarontā garukarontā mānentā - pūjentā celavitānāni karontā maṇḍala mālāni paṭiyādentā evaṃ tā divasaṃ vītināmesuṃ.

While such great offerings were being made, the Blessed One, in the first watch of the night, taught the Dhamma to the Mallas. In the middle watch, having taught the Dhamma to Subhadda, he established him in the path and its fruit. In the last watch, he exhorted the monks. At the break of dawn, shaking the great earth, he attained final Nibbāna, the Nibbāna-element with no remainder. After the Blessed One, the Lord of the World, had attained final Nibbāna, the Venerable Ānanda announced that event to the Malla princes. Having heard this, they took perfumes, garlands, all kinds of musical instruments, and five hundred pairs of cloths, and went to honor the Blessed One’s body with dances, songs, instrumental music, garlands, and perfumes, honoring, respecting, revering, and venerating it. They erected cloth canopies and prepared circular garlands, and thus they passed that day.

Atha devatānañca kosinārakānaṃ mallānañca etadahosi-ativikālo kho ajja bhagavato sarīra jhāpetuṃ, svedāni bhagavato sarīraṃ jhapessāmāti tathā dutiyampi divasaṃ vītināmesuṃ. Tathā tatiyaṃ catutthaṃ pañcamaṃ chaṭṭhampi divasaṃ vītināmesuṃ satta me divase devatā ca dosinārakā mallā ca bhagavato sarīraṃ dibbehi mānusakehi ca nacchehi - gītehi - vāditehi - mālehi - gaṇdhehi sakkāronto garukarontā mānentā pūjentā nagaramajjhena nīharitvā yattha makuṭabaṇdhanaṃ nāma mallānaṃ cetiyaṃ tattha nikkhipiṃsu

Then this thought occurred to the deities and the Mallas of Kusinārā: 'It is too late today to cremate the Blessed One's body; we will cremate the Blessed One's body tomorrow.' Thus they passed the second day, and in the same way the third, fourth, fifth, and sixth days. On the seventh day, the deities and the Mallas of Kusinārā, honoring, respecting, revering, and venerating the Blessed One's body with divine and human dances, songs, instrumental music, garlands, and perfumes, carried it through the middle of the city and placed it at the Mallas' shrine named Makuṭabandhana.

Tena kho pana samayena kusinārā yāva sandhisamala saṃkaṭīrājannumattena odhinā maṇdārava pupphena satthatā hoti atha kho kosinārakā [O 0.0020] mallā bhagavato sarīraṃ cakkavattissa sarīraṃ viya ahatena vatthena veṭhesuṃ ahatena vatthena veṭhetvā vihatena kappāsena veṭhesuṃ vihatena kappāsena veṭhetvā ahatena vatthena veṭhesuṃ. Eteneva nayena pañcahiyugasatehi veṭhetvā āyasāya teladoṇiyā pakkhipitvā aññissā āyasāya doṇiyā paṭikujjetvā sabbagandhānaṃ citakaṃ karitvā bhagavato sarīraṃ citakaṃ āropesuṃ. Tena kho pana samayena āyasmā mahā kassapo pāvāya kusināraṃ addhānamagga paṭipanno hoti mahatābhikkhusaṅghena saddhiṃ pañcamattehi bhikkhusatehi tena kho pana samayena there cittaṃ pasādetvā sagge nibbattā devatā tasmiṃ samāgame theraṃ adisvā kuhinnu kho amhākaṃ kulupaga theroti āvajjento antarāmaggapaṭipannaṃ disvā amhākaṃ kulupaga there avaṇdite citako mā pajjalitthāti adhiṭṭhihiṃsu.

At that time, Kusinārā was strewn knee-deep with mandārava flowers, right up to the drains and rubbish heaps. Then the Mallas of Kusinārā wrapped the Blessed One’s body with new cloth, like the body of a wheel-turning monarch. Having wrapped it with new cloth, they wrapped it with carded cotton. Having wrapped it with carded cotton, they wrapped it again with new cloth. In this way, they wrapped the body with five hundred pairs of cloths, placed it in an iron oil-trough, and covered it with another iron trough. Having made a pyre of all kinds of fragrant substances, they placed the Blessed One’s body upon the pyre. At that time, the Venerable Mahā Kassapa was traveling along the road from Pāvā to Kusinārā with a great company of about five hundred monks. Deities who had gained faith in the Elder and were reborn in heaven, not seeing the Elder at that assembly, wondered, 'Where, indeed, is our family-attending Elder?' Reflecting, they saw him on the road and made a resolution: 'May the pyre not ignite until our family-attending Elder has paid homage.'

Atha kho cattāro mallapāmokkhā sīsaṃ nahātā ahatāni vatthāni nicatthā vī saṃratanasatikaṃ caṇdanacitakaṃ ālimpessāmāti aṭṭhapi solasapi dvattiṃsāpi janā hutvā yamaka ukkayo gahetvā tālavaṇeṭahi vijantā bhastāni dhamantā na sakkontiyeva aggiṃ gāhāpetuṃ. Atha kho kosinārakā mallā citakassa apajjalana kāraṇaṃ āyasmantaṃ anuruddhaṃ pucchitvaṃ devatānaṃ abhippāyaṃ sutvā mahākassapo kira bho pañcahi bhikkhusatehi saddhiṃ dasabalassa pāde vandissāmīti āgacchati tasmiṃ kira anāgate citako na pajjalati kīdiso bhoso bhikkhu kālo odāto dīgho rasso evarūpe nāma bho bhikkhumhi ṭhito kiṃ dasabalassa parinibbānaṃ nāmāti deci gaṇdhamālādi hatthā paṭipathaṃ gacchiṃsu keci vīthiyo vicittā katvā āgamanamaggaṃ olokayamānā aṭṭhaṃsu. Atha kho āyasmā mahākassapo yena kusinārā makūṭabaṇdhanaṃ nāma mallānaṃ cetiyaṃ, yena bhagavato citako tenupasaṅkami. Upasaṅkamitvā ekaṃsaṃ cīvaraṃ katvā tikkhattuṃ citakaṃ padakkhiṇaṃ katvā āvajjentova sallakkhesi-imasmiṃ ṭhāne pādāti tate pādasamīpe ṭhatvā abhiññāpādakaṃ catutthajjhānaṃ samāpajjitvā vuṭṭhāya arasahassa patimaṇḍitā dasabalassa pādā saddhiṃ kappāsa paṭalehi pañcadussayugasatāni suvaṇṇadoṇiṃ vaṇdanacitakañca dvedhā katvā mayhaṃ uttamaṅge sirasi patiṭṭhahantūti adhiṭṭhāsi saha adhiṭṭhāna cittena tāni dussayugādini dvedhā katvā valāhakantarā puṇṇacaṇdo viya pādā nikkhamiṃsu.

Then four chief Mallas, having bathed their heads and put on new clothes, intended to light the sandalwood pyre. But even with eight, sixteen, or thirty-two men holding torches in pairs, fanning with palm-leaf fans, and blowing bellows, they could not make the fire catch. Then the Mallas of Kusinārā asked the Venerable Anuruddha the reason the pyre would not ignite. Hearing the intention of the deities, they learned: 'The Venerable Mahā Kassapa is coming with five hundred monks to pay homage to the feet of the Ten-Powered One. It seems that until he arrives, the pyre will not burn.' They asked, 'Sirs, what kind of monk is he? Dark or fair, tall or short? Is the Parinibbāna of the Ten-Powered One to be delayed for such a monk?' Then some, holding garlands and perfumes, went to meet him on the road, while others, having elaborately decorated the streets, stood watching the path of his arrival. Then the Venerable Mahā Kassapa went to the Mallas' shrine named Makuṭabandhana in Kusinārā, where the Blessed One's pyre was, and approached it. Having approached, he arranged his robe over one shoulder, circumambulated the pyre three times, and reflecting, he discerned: 'The feet are in this place.' Standing near the feet, he entered the fourth jhāna, which is a basis for supernormal powers. Upon emerging, he made a resolution: 'May the feet of the Ten-Powered One, adorned with the thousand-spoked wheel, together with the layers of cotton, the five hundred pairs of cloths, the golden trough, and the sandalwood pyre, split in two and rest upon my head, the highest part of my body.' With that very thought of resolution, those wrappings split in two, and the feet emerged like the full moon from within the clouds.

Thero vikasita rattapaduma sadise hatthe pasāretvā suvaṇṇavaṇṇe satthu pāde yāva gopphakā bāḷhaṃ gahetva attano siravare patiṭṭhāpesi mahājano taṃ acchariyaṃ disvā ekappahāre neva mahānādaṃ nadi. Gaṇdhamālādīhi pūjetvā yathāruciṃ vaṇdi. Eva pana [O 0.0021] therena ca mahājanena ca tehi ca pañcahi bhikkhusatehi vaṇdita mattetherassa hatthato muñcitvā alattaka vaṇṇāni bhagavato pādatālāni dāruādisu kiñci acāletvāva yathāṭṭhāne patiṭṭhahiṃsu. Bhagavato pādesu nikkhamantesu vā pavisantesu vā kappāsaaṃsu vā dasātantu vā telabiṇdu vā dārukhaṇḍaṃ vā ṭhānā calitaṃ nāma nāhosi. Sabbaṃ yathāṭṭhāne ṭhitameva ahosi. Uṭṭhahitvā pana atthaṅgate caṇde viya sūriye viya ca tathāgatassa pādesu antarahitesu mahājano mahākaṇditaṃ kaṇdi. Parinibbutakālato adhikataraṃ kāruññaṃ ahosi atha kho devātānubhāvena panesa citake samantato ekappahāreneva pajjali. Jhāyamānassa bhagavato sarīrassa chavi camma maṃsādīnaṃ neva chārikāmattampi antamaso paññāyittha na masi, sumana makula sadisā pana dhotamutta sadisā suvaṇṇasadisā ca dhātuyo avasissiṃsu.

The Elder, with hands outstretched like a fully bloomed red lotus, firmly grasped the golden-hued feet of the Teacher up to the ankles and placed them upon his own excellent head. The great crowd, seeing this marvel, all at once raised a mighty cry. They worshipped with perfumes, garlands, and other offerings, paying homage as they pleased. Thus, when the Elder, the great assembly, and the five hundred monks had paid homage, the Blessed One’s lac-dyed soles detached from the Elder’s hands and settled back into their place upon the pyre's wood without causing the slightest disturbance. Whether the Blessed One’s feet were emerging or retracting, not even a cotton fiber, a ten-stranded thread, a drop of oil, or a splinter of wood was displaced. Everything remained exactly as it was. But when the Tathāgata’s feet vanished, like the setting moon or sun, the great crowd wailed loudly. Their sorrow was even greater than at the time of his final passing. Then, by the power of the devas, the funeral pyre ignited from all sides at once. As the Blessed One’s body burned, not even a trace of ash from his skin, hide, flesh, and so on was to be seen, nor any soot. Only relics remained, resembling jasmine buds, washed pearls, and gold.

Dīghāyuka buddhānañhi sarīraṃ suvaṇṇakkhaṇdhasadisaṃ ekaghanameva hoti. Bhagavā pana ahaṃna ciraṃ ṭhatvā ṭhatvā parinibbāyāmi. Mayhaṃ sāsanaṃ na tāva sabbattha vitthāritaṃ, tasmā parinibbutassapi me sāsasamattampi dhātuṃ gahetvā attano attano vasanaṭṭhāne cetiyaṃ katvā paricaranto mahājano saggaparāyano hotiti dhātūnaṃ vikiraṇaṃ adhiṭṭhāsi. Kati panassa dhātuyo vippakiṇṇā, kati na vippakiṇṇāti catasso dāṭṭhā, dve akkhakā, uṇhīsanti imā sattadhātuyo na vippakiṇṇā. Sesā vippakiriṃsu. Tattha sabbakhuddakā dhātu sāsapa bījamattā ahosi mahādhātu majjhe bhinna taṇḍulamattā. Atimahatī majjhe bhinnamugga bījamattā ahosi.

The bodies of long-lived Buddhas are like solid masses of gold, being of a single consistency. But the Blessed One reflected: 'I will not remain for long, but will attain Parinibbāna. My Dispensation has not yet spread everywhere; therefore, even after my Parinibbāna, if the great multitude take a relic even the size of a mustard seed, build a cetiya in their own dwelling places, and attend to it, they will be destined for a heavenly realm.' Thus, he resolved upon the scattering of his relics. How many of his relics were scattered, and how many were not? Four canine teeth, two collarbones, and the cranial protuberance—these seven relics were not scattered. The rest were scattered. Among them, the smallest relics were the size of mustard seeds, the medium ones were the size of broken rice grains, and the largest were the size of split mung beans.

Daḍḍhe kho pana bhagavato sarīre ākāsato aggabāhumattāpi jaṅghamattāpi tālakkhaṇdhamattāpi udakadhārā patitvā citakaṃ nibbāpesi na kevalaṃ ākāsatoyeva parivāretvā ṭhitasālarukkhānampi sākhattara viṭapantarehi udakadhārā nikkhamitvā nibbāpesuṃ, bhagavato citako mahanto, samantā pathaviṃ bhiṇditvā taṃṅgalasīsamattā udakavaṭṭi eḷika vaṭaṃsaka sadisā gantvā citakameva gaṇhi mallarājāno ca suvaṇṇaghaṭe rajataghaṭe ca pūretvā ābhata nānāgaṇdhodakena suvaṇṇa rajatamayehi aṭṭhadantakehi vikiritvā caṇdanacitakaṃ nibbāpesuṃ. Tattha citake jhayamāne parivāretvā ṭhita sālarukkhānaṃ sākhantarehi viṭapantarehi pattantarehi ca jāle uggacchante pattaṃ vā sākhā vā daḍḍhā nāma natthi kipillikāpi makkaṭakāpi pāṇakāpi jālānaṃ antare neva vicaranti.

Now, when the Blessed One's body was burnt, streams of water fell from the sky—as thick as a forearm, as a thigh, and as a palm trunk—and extinguished the pyre. Not only from the sky, but from the surrounding sāl trees, too, streams of water emerged from between their branches and twigs and extinguished it. Furthermore, breaking through the earth all around, torrents of water as thick as a plough-head, spiraling like a ram's horn, rose up and enveloped the pyre. The Malla kings also, filling golden and silver vessels with various fragrant waters that had been brought, sprinkled them with golden and silver eight-toothed sprinklers and extinguished the sandalwood pyre. While the pyre was burning and flames were rising between the branches, twigs, and leaves of the surrounding sāl trees, not a single leaf or branch was burnt. Not even ants, spiders, or other small creatures moved about within the flames.

Ākāsato patita udakadhārāsupi sālarukkhehi nikkhantौdakadhārāsupi pathaviṃ bhiṇditvā nikkhanta udakadhārāsupi dhammatāva pamāṇaṃ evaṃ [O 0.0022] citakaṃ nibbāpetvā pana mallarājāno santhāgāre catujātigaṇdha paribhaṇḍaṃ kāretvā lāja pañcamāni pupphāni vikiritvā upari celavitānaṃ baṇdhāpetvā suvaṇṇatārakāhi khacetvā tattha gaṇdhadāma mālādāma ratanadāmāni olambetvā satthāgārato yāva makuṭa baṇdhana saṅkhātā sīsappasādhana maṅgalasālā tāva ubhohi passehi sāṇikilañja parikkhepaṃ kāretvā upari celavitānaṃ baṇdhāpetvā suvaṇṇatārakāhi khacetvā tatthāpi gaṇdhadāma mālādāma ratanadāmāni olambetvā maṇidaṇḍehi pañcavaṇṇadhaje ussapetvā samantā dhajapatākā pirikkhipitvā sittasammaṭṭhāsu vīthisu kadaliyo puṇṇaghaṭe ca ṭhapetvā daṇḍadīpikā jāletvā alaṅkata hatthikkhaṇdhe sahadhātūhi suvaṇṇadoṇiṃ ṭhapetvā mālāgaṇdhādīhi pūjetvā sādhukīḷaṃ kīḷantā antonagaraṃ pavesetvā santhāgāre sarabhamaya pallaṅke ṭhapetvā upari setacchattaṃ dhārayitvā satti hatthehi purisehi parikkhipāpetvā hatthīhi kumbhena kumbhaṃ paharantehi parikkhipāpetvā tato assehi gīvāya gīvaṃ paharantehi tato rathehi āṇikoṭiyā āṇikoṭiṃ pahantehi tato yodhehi bāhūhi bāhuṃ paharantehi tesaṃ pariyante koṭiyā koṭiṃ paharamānehi dhanūhi parikkhipāpesuṃ.

The streams of water that fell from the sky, that flowed from the sāl trees, and that gushed forth from the earth were, by their very nature, of the proper measure. Having thus extinguished the pyre, the Malla kings had the assembly hall prepared with four kinds of perfumes. They scattered flowers with parched grain as the fifth, had canopies hung above, and adorned them with golden stars. There they suspended garlands of perfume, flowers, and jewels. From the assembly hall to the auspicious hall for adorning the head, known as the 'Binding of the Diadem,' they set up an enclosure of curtains and mats on both sides of the path. Above, they also hung canopies, studded them with golden stars, and suspended garlands of perfume, flowers, and jewels. They raised five-colored flags on jeweled poles and surrounded the area with flags and banners. In the sprinkled and swept streets, they placed banana trees and full water pots, and lit lamps on poles. Placing the golden urn with the relics upon an adorned elephant, they honored it with garlands, perfumes, and other offerings. Celebrating with joyful ceremony, they brought it into the city. In the assembly hall, they placed it on a couch of sarabha hide, held a white parasol over it, and had it surrounded by men with spears in hand. They further surrounded it with elephants striking forehead against forehead, then with horses striking neck against neck, then with chariots striking axle-end against axle-end, then with warriors striking arm against arm, and at the very perimeter, with archers striking bow-tip against bow-tip.

Iti samantā yojanappamāṇaṃ ṭhānaṃ sannāhagacchitaṃ viya katvā ārakkhaṃ saṃvidahiṃsu. Kasmā panete evamakaṃsūti. Ito purimesu dvīsu sattāhesu te bhikkhusaṅghassa ṭhānanisajjokāsaṃ karontā khādanīya bhojanīyaṃ saṃvidahantā sādhukīḷāya okāsaṃ na labhiṃsu tato tesaṃ ahosi - imaṃ sattāhaṃ sādhukīḷaṃ kīḷissāma. Ṭhānaṃ kho panetaṃ vijjati yaṃ amhākaṃ pamattabhāvaṃ ñatvā kocīdeva āgantvā dhātuyo gaṇhayya-tasmā ārakkhaṃ ṭhapetvā kīḷissāmāti tena te evamakaṃsu.

Thus, having made the area for a yojana all around like a place clad in armor, they arranged for its protection. And why did they do this? In the previous two weeks, while preparing places for the Sangha of monks to stand and sit and arranging hard and soft food, they had no opportunity for a proper celebration. Then it occurred to them: 'This week we shall hold a proper celebration. But it is possible that someone, knowing we are negligent, might come and take the relics. Therefore, we shall celebrate only after posting a guard.' For this reason, they acted in this way.

Atha kho assosi kho rājā māgadho ajātasattu bhagavā kira kusinārāyaṃ parinibbutoti kathaṃ assosi paṭhamamevassa amaccā sutvā cintayiṃsu? Satthā nāma parinibbuto, na so sakkā puna āhariṃtu pothujjanika saddhāya pana amhākaṃ raññā sadiso natthi. Sace esa imināva niyāmena suṇissati, hadayamassa eḷissati rājā kho panambhehi anurakkhitabboti te tisso suvaṇṇadoṇiyo āharitvā catumadhurassa pūretvā rañño santikaṃ gantvā etadavocuṃ. Dve amhehi supinako diṭṭho tassa paṭighātatthaṃ tumhehi dukūlapaṭṭaṃ nivāsetvā yathā nāsāpuṭamattaṃ paññāyati evaṃ catumadhuradoṇiyaṃ nipajjituṃ vaṭṭatīti. Rājā atthavarakānaṃ vacanaṃ sutvā evaṃ hotu tātā’ti sampaṭicchitvā tathā akāsi.

Then King Ajātasattu of Magadha heard the news: 'The Blessed One, it is said, has attained Parinibbāna at Kusinārā.' How did he hear it? His ministers heard it first and reflected: 'The Teacher has attained Parinibbāna; he cannot be brought back. But when it comes to the faith of a worldling, there is none like our king. If he hears this news directly, his heart will surely break. The king must be protected by us.' So they brought three golden troughs, filled them with the four sweet substances, went to the king, and said this: 'We have seen two portents in a dream. To counteract them, you should put on a robe of fine linen and lie down in a trough of the four sweet substances so that only your nostrils are visible.' The king, hearing the words of his excellent ministers, agreed, saying, 'So be it, sirs,' and did as they said.

Atheko [O 0.0023] amacco alaṅkāraṃ omuñcitvā kese parikiriya yāya disāya satthā parinibbuto tadabhimukho hutvā añjalimpaggayha rājānaṃ āha- devamaraṇato muñcanakasatto nāma natthi. Amhākaṃ āyuvaddhakenā cetiyaṭṭhānaṃ puññakkhettaṃ abhisekapiṭṭhikā bhagavā satthā kusinārāyaṃ parinibbutoti rājā sutvā visaññī jāto catumadhuradoṇi usumaṃ muñci rājānaṃ ukkhipitvā dutiyāya doṇiyā nipajjāpesuṃ so saññaṃ labhitvā tāta kiṃ vadathāti pucchi. Satthā mahārāja parinibbutoti, puna visaññi jāto catumadhuradoṇiṃ usumaṃ muñci. Atha naṃ tatopi ukkhipitvā tatiyāya doṇiyā nipajjāpesuṃ so puna saññaṃ paṭilabhitvā tāta kiṃ vadathāti pucchi satthā mahārāja parinibbutoti. Rājā puna visaññī jāto ca madhuradoṇi usumaṃ muñci.

Then one minister, removing his ornaments and loosening his hair, faced the direction where the Teacher had attained final Nibbāna, raised his joined palms, and said to the king, "O King, there is no being who can escape death. Our life-prolonger, our shrine, our field of merit, our very seat of consecration—the Blessed One, the Teacher, has attained final Nibbāna at Kusinārā." Hearing this, the king fainted and emitted heat into the trough of four kinds of sweets. They lifted the king and laid him on the second trough. Regaining consciousness, he asked, "Dear ones, what are you saying?" They replied, "Great king, the Teacher has attained final Nibbāna." Again, he fainted and emitted heat into the trough of four kinds of sweets. They lifted him once more and laid him on the third trough. Regaining consciousness again, he asked, "Dear ones, what are you saying?" They replied, "Great king, the Teacher has attained final Nibbāna." The king fainted once more and emitted heat into the trough of sweets.

Atha naṃ tatopi ukkhipitvā nahāpetvā matthake ghaṭehi udakaṃ āsiñciṃsu rājā saññaṃ paṭilabhitvā āsanā uṭṭhāya gaṇdhaparibhāvīte maṇivaṇṇakese suvaṇṇaphalaka vaṇṇāya piṭṭhiyaṃ pakiritvā pavāḷaṅguravaṇṇāhi suvaṭṭitaṅgulīhi suvaṇṇabimbaka vaṇṇaṃ uraṃ saṃsibbanto viya gahetvā paridevamāno ummattakaveseneva antaravīthiṃ otiṇṇo so alaṅkata nāṭaka parivuto nagarā nikkhamma jīvakambavanaṃ gantvā yasmiṃ ṭhāne nisinnena bhagavatā dhammo desito taṃ oloketvā bhagavā sabbaññu nanu me imasmiṃ ṭhāne nisīditvā dhammaṃ desayittha tumhe sokasallaṃ vinodayittha. Tumhe mayhaṃ sokasallaṃ nīharittha. Ahaṃ tumhākaṃ saraṇaṃ gato. Idāni pana me paṭivacanampi na detha bhagavāti punappuna paridevitvā nanu bhagavā ahaṃ aññadā evarūpe kāle tuṭṭhe mahābhikkhusaṅghaparivārā jambudīpatale cārikaṃ carathāti suṇāma idāni pana ahaṃ tumhākaṃ ananurūpaṃ ayuttaṃ pavattiṃ suṇāmīti evamādīni ca vattā saṭṭhimattāhi gāthāhi bhagavato guṇaṃ anussaritvā cintesi mama paridevite neva na sijjhati. Dasabalassa dhātuyo āharāpessāmiti mallarājunaṃ dūtañca paṇṇañca pāhesi.

Then, lifting him up from there, they bathed him and poured water from pitchers over his head. The king, regaining consciousness, rose from his seat, and with his jewel-colored hair, scented with perfumes, spread over his back, which was the color of a golden plate, and with well-rounded fingers the color of coral sprouts, he seemed to clasp his golden-image-colored chest as he lamented. In the guise of a madman, surrounded by adorned dancers, he descended into the inner street. He left the city and went to Jīvaka’s mango grove. Gazing at the spot where the Blessed One had sat and taught the Dhamma, he lamented: "O All-Knowing One, did you not sit in this very place and teach the Dhamma to me? Did you not dispel the dart of my sorrow? Did you not draw out the arrow of my grief? I have gone to you for refuge. But now, O Blessed One, you do not even give me a reply!" Lamenting again and again, he reflected: "O Blessed One, did we not hear that at other times, contented and surrounded by a great assembly of monks, you would wander the land of Jambudīpa? But now I hear this unbefitting and improper news about you." Speaking thus and recollecting the virtues of the Blessed One in some sixty verses, he thought: "My lamentations will accomplish nothing. I shall have the relics of the Ten-Powered One brought here." So he sent a messenger and a letter to the Malla kings.

Bhagavāpi khattiyo, ahampi khattiyo, ahampi arahāmi bhagavato sarīrānaṃ thūpañca mahañca kāretunti pesetvā pana sace dassanti suṇdaraṃ, no ce dassanti āharaṇupāyena āharissāmīti caturaṅginiṃ senaṃ sannayhitvā sayampi nikkhantoyeva yathā ca ajātasattu evaṃ vesāliyaṃ licchavirājāno kapilavatthumhi sakyarājāno allakappake bulayo rāmagāmake koḷiyā veṭhadīpake brāhmaṇo pāvāyañca mallā dūtaṃ pesetvā sayampi caturaṅginiyā senāya nikkhamiṃsuyeva tattha pāceyyakā sabbehi āsannatarā kusinārāto [O 0.0024] tigāvutantare nagare vasanti. Bhagavāpi pāvaṃ pavisitvā kusināraṃ gato. Mahāparihārā panete rājāno parihārā kāreṃntāva pacchato jātā. Te sabbepi sattanagaravāsino āgattvā amhākaṃ dhātuyo vā dentu yuddhaṃ vāti kusinārā nagaraṃ parivārayiṃsu.

"The Blessed One was a noble, and I too am a noble. I am also worthy of building a stupa and holding a great festival for the relics of the Blessed One." Having sent this message, he thought, "If they give them, it will be good. If they do not, I will bring them by force." Having prepared a fourfold army, he himself set out. Just as Ajātasattu did, so too the Licchavi kings of Vesālī, the Sakyan kings of Kapilavatthu, the Bulis of Allakappa, the Koliyas of Rāmagāma, the brahmin of Veṭhadīpa, and the Mallas of Pāvā sent messengers and themselves marched out with their fourfold armies. Among them, the Mallas of Pāvā, being closer to Kusinārā than all the others, lived in a city within three gāvutas. The Blessed One had entered Pāvā and then gone to Kusinārā. These kings, however, making a great procession, arrived later. All the inhabitants of those seven cities came and surrounded the city of Kusinārā, demanding, "Either give us the relics, or give us battle!"

Tato mallarājānoetadavocuṃ-bhagavāamhākaṃgāmakkhette parinibbuto, na mayaṃ satthu sāsanaṃ pahiṇimha na gattvā ānayimha. Satthā pasa sayameva āgantvā sāsanaṃ pesetvā amhe pakkosapesi tumhepi kho pana yaṃ tumhākaṃ gāmakkhette ratanaṃ uppajjati na taṃ amhākaṃ detha sadevake loke buddharatanasamaṃ ratanaṃ nāma natthi, evarūpaṃ. Uttamaṃ ratanaṃ labhitvā mayaṃ na dassamāti evaṃ te kalahaṃ vaḍḍhetvā na kho pana tumhehiyeva mātu thanato khiraṃ pītaṃ, amhehipi pītaṃ tumheyeva purisā amhe na purisā hotu hotūti aññamaññaṃ ahaṃkāraṃ katvā sāsana paṭisāsanaṃ pesentā aññamaññaṃ mānagajjitaṃ gajjiṃsu yuddhe pana sati kosinārakānaṃyeva jayo abhavissa, kasmā dhātupāsanatthaṃ āgatā devatā tesaṃ pakkhāahesuṃ.

Then the Malla kings said this: "The Blessed One attained final Nibbāna in our village territory. We did not send a message to the Teacher, nor did we go and bring him. The Teacher came of his own accord, sent a message, and summoned us. Furthermore, whatever treasure arises in your village territory, you do not give to us. In the world with its devas, there is no treasure equal to the Buddha-jewel. Having obtained such a supreme treasure, we will not give it away." Thus they escalated the quarrel, saying, "It is not only you who have drunk milk from your mother's breast—we have drunk it too! Are you the only men, and we are not? So be it!" Mutually asserting their pride, they sent messages and counter-messages, roaring at each other with the roar of pride. But if there had been a battle, the people of Kusinārā would have won. Why? Because the deities who had come to honor the relics took their side.

Tato doṇo brāhmaṇo imaṃ vivādaṃ sutvā ete rājāno bhagavate parinibbutaṭṭhāne vividaṃ karonti na kho panetaṃ patirūpaṃ alaṃ iminā kalahena cūpasamessāmi na nti uṇṇatappadese ṭhatvā dvebhāṇavāraparimāṇaṃ doṇagajjitaṃ nāma avoca tattha paṭṭhamakabhāṇavāre tāva ekapadampi te na jāniṃsu dutiyaka bhāṇavāra pariyosāne’ācariyassa viya bhosaddo, ācariyassa viya bho saddo’ti sabbe nī ravā ahesuṃ sakalajambudīpatale kira kulaghare jāto yebhuyyena tassa na antevāsiko nāma natthi atha so te attano vacanaṃ sutvā tuṇhībhūte viditvā puna etadavoca

Then the brahmin Doṇa, hearing this dispute, thought: "These kings are causing a dispute at the place where the Blessed One attained Parinibbāna. This is not proper. Enough of this strife! I shall pacify them." Standing on an elevated spot, he uttered what is known as the "Roar of Doṇa," which was the length of two recitation sections. During the first recitation section, they did not understand even a single word. At the end of the second recitation section, they all fell silent, saying, "The voice is like our teacher’s! The voice is like our teacher’s!" It is said that throughout all of Jambudīpa, there was hardly a noble household in which someone was not his pupil. Then, seeing that they had fallen silent upon hearing his words, he spoke to them again:

“Suṇantu bhonto mama ekavākyaṃ

Amhākaṃ buddho ahu khantivādo,

Na hi sādhayaṃ uttama puggalassa

Sarīrabhaṅge siyā sampahāro

Sabbeva bhonto sahitā samaggā

Sammodamānā karomaṭṭhabhāge,

Vitthārikā hontu disāyu thūpā

Bahujjano cakkhumato pasanno”ti;

"Listen, sirs, to my single utterance: Our Buddha was an advocate of forbearance. It is not fitting for strife to arise over the division of the remains of the supreme person. Let all of us, sirs, united and in concord, rejoicing, make eight portions. May stupas be widespread in all directions, and may many people have faith in the One with Vision."

Tatrāyamattho “amhākaṃ buddho ahu khantivādo“ti buddhabhūmiṃ appatvāpi pāramiyo pūrento khantivāda tāpasakāle dhammapālakumāra kāle chaddantahatthikāle bhūridatta nāgarāja kāle campeyya [O 0.0025] nāgarāja kāle saṅkhapāla nāgarāja kāle mahākapikāle aññesupi bahūsu jātakesu paresu kopaṃ akatvā khantimeva akāsi. Khantimeva vaṇṇayi, kimaṅga pana etarahi iṭṭhāniṭṭhesu tādilakkhaṇaṃ patto sabbathāpi amhākaṃ buddho khantivādo ahosi.

Herein, this is the meaning of "Our Buddha was an advocate of forbearance": Even before attaining the state of a Buddha, while fulfilling the perfections, as the ascetic Khantivāda, as Prince Dhammapāla, as the six-tusked elephant Chaddanta, as the nāga king Bhūridatta, as the nāga king Campeyya, as the nāga king Saṅkhapāla, as the great monkey, and in many other Jātakas, he felt no anger towards others and practiced only forbearance. He praised only forbearance. How much more so now, having attained the characteristic of equanimity towards the desirable and undesirable? In every way, our Buddha was an advocate of forbearance.

Tassa evaṃ vidhassa na hi sādhaya uttamapuggalassa sarīrabhaṅge siyā sampahāro, “na hi sādhayanti“na hi sādhu ayaṃ, “sarīrabhaṅgeti sarīra bhaṅganimittaṃ dhātukoṭṭhāsahetūti attho “siyā sampahāro“ti āyudha sampahāro na hi sādhu siyāti vuttaṃ hoti. “Sabbeva bhonto sahitā“ti sabbeva bhavanto sahitā bhotha mā bhijjittha. “Samaggāti“ kāyena vācāya ca ekasannipātā ekavacanā samaggā bhotha “sammodamānā“ti cittenāpi aññamaññaṃ modamānā bhotha.’Karomaṭṭhabhāge“ti bhagavatā sarīrāni aṭṭhabhāge karoma. “Cakkhumato“ti pañcahi cakkhuhī cakkhutā buddhassa, na kevalaṃ tumheyeva bahujjano pasanno tesu ekopi laddhu ayutto nāma natthiti bahuṃ kāraṇaṃ vatvā saññāpesi.

For such a supreme person, it is not good that there should be strife over the breaking of the body. 'This is not good.' 'Upon the breaking of the body' means the sign of the body's breaking, the cause being the portions of the elements. 'There would be strife' means it is said that an assault with weapons would not be good. 'All of you, good sirs, be united' means all of you, good sirs, should be united; do not be divided. 'Be in harmony' means be united in body and speech, of one assembly, speaking with one voice. 'Rejoicing' means rejoice in mind with one another. 'Let us make eight portions' means let us make eight portions of the Blessed One's relics. 'Of the One with Vision' means the Buddha's vision with the five eyes. Not only you, but many people are devoted. Among them, there is not even one who is unworthy to receive a portion. Having stated many reasons, he convinced them.

Atha sabbepi rājāno evamāhaṃsu-tena hi brāhmaṇa tvaññeva bhagavato sarīrāni aṭṭhadhā samaṃ suvibhattaṃ vibhajāhīti. Evaṃ bhoti kho doṇo brāhmaṇo tesaṃ rājūnaṃ paṭissutvā dhātuyo samaṃ suvibhattaṃ vibhaji.

Then all the kings spoke thus: "Well then, brahmin, you yourself divide the Blessed One's relics into eight equal, well-divided portions." "Very well, sirs," replied the brahmin Doṇa, and having assented to the kings, he divided the relics equally and well.

Tatrāyamanukkamo doṇo kira tesaṃ paṭissutvā suvaṇṇadoṇiṃ vivarāpesi. Rājāno āgantvā doṇiyaṃ yevatā suvaṇṇavaṇṇā dhātuyo disvā bhagavā sabbaññu pubbe mayaṃ tumhākaṃ dvattiṃsa lakkhaṇa patimaṇḍitaṃ chabbaṇṇabuddharasmikhacitaṃ suvaṇṇavaṇṇaṃ sarīraṃ addasāma idāni pana suvaṇṇavaṇṇā dhātuyova avasiṭṭhā jātā, na yuttamidaṃ bhagavā tumhākanti parideviṃsu. Brāhmaṇo tasmiṃ samaye tesaṃ pamattabhāvaṃ ñatvā dakkhiṇadāṭhaṃ gahetvā veṭhantare ṭhapesi atha pacchā samaṃ suvibhattaṃ vibhaji. Sabbāpi dhātuyo pākatika nāḷiyā soḷasa nāḷiyo ahosuṃ. Ekeka nagaravāsino dve dve nāḷiyo labhiṃsu.

Here is the sequence of events: Doṇa, it is said, having assented to them, had a golden urn opened. The kings arrived and saw the relics in the urn, which were golden in color. They lamented, saying, "O Blessed One, All-knowing One, previously we saw your body adorned with the thirty-two marks, radiant with the six-hued Buddha rays, golden in hue. But now only golden-colored relics remain. This is not fitting for you, O Blessed One!" At that time, the brahmin, perceiving their heedlessness, took the right tooth relic and placed it in his turban. Later, he divided them equally and well. All the relics amounted to sixteen standard nāḷis. The inhabitants of each city received two nāḷis.

Brāhmaṇassa pana dhātuyo vibhajantasseva sakko devānamindo kena nu kho sadevakassa lokassa kaṅkhācchedanāya catusaccakathāya paccayabhūtā bhagavato dakkhiṇadāṭhā gahitāti olokento brāhmaṇena gahitāti disvā brāhmaṇo dāṭhāya anucchavikaṃ sakkāraṃ kātuṃ na sakkhissati gaṇhāmi nanti veṭhantarato gahetvā suvaṇṇa caṅgoṭake ṭhapetvā devalokaṃ netvā cuḷāmaṇī [O 0.0026] cetiye patiṭṭhāpesi brāhmaṇopi dhātuyo vibhajitvā dāṭhaṃ apassanto kena me ṭhā gahitāti pucchitumpi nāsakkhi. Nanu tayā dhātuyo vibhajitā, kiṃ tvaṃ paṭhamaṃyeva attano dhātūhi atthibhāvaṃ na aññāsīti, attani dosāropanaṃ sampassamāno mayhampi koṭṭhāsaṃ dethāti vattumpi nāsakkhi.

When the brahmin was dividing the relics, Sakka, the king of the devas, reflecting, "By whom, then, was the Blessed One's right tooth relic, which became the basis for the discourse on the Four Noble Truths that dispels the doubts of the world with its gods, taken?" saw that it had been taken by the brahmin. Realizing that the brahmin would not be able to make offerings befitting the tooth relic, he thought, "I will take it," and removing it from his turban, he placed it in a golden casket, took it to the deva world, and enshrined it in the Cūḷāmaṇi Cetiya. The brahmin, having divided the relics and not seeing the tooth relic, was unable even to ask, "By whom was my tooth relic taken?" Thinking, "Did you not divide the relics? Why did you not first ascertain the presence of your own relic?" and seeing the fault in himself, he was unable even to say, "Give me a portion too."

Tato ayaṃ suvaṇṇakumbhopi dhātugatikoyeva yena tathāgatassa dhātuyo minitā. Imassāhaṃ thūpaṃ karissāmīti cintetvā imaṃ me bhonto kumbhaṃ dadantūti āha tato rājāno brāhmaṇassa kumbhamadaṃsu pipphalivaniyāpi kho moriyā bhagavato parinibbutabhāvaṃ sutvā bhagavāpi khattiyo, mayampi khattiyo mayampi, arahāma labhituṃ bhagavato sarīrānaṃ bhāganti dūtaṃ pesetvā yuddha sajjā nikkhamitvā āgatā tesaṃ rājāno evamāhaṃsu - natthi bhagavato sarīrānaṃ bhāgo, vibhattāni bhagavato sarīrāni ito aṅgāraṃ garathāti. Te tato aṅgāraṃ hariṃsu.

Then this golden urn, too, was associated with the relics, for by it the Tathāgata's relics had been measured. Thinking, "I will build a stupa for this," he said, "Sirs, give me this urn!" Then the kings gave the urn to the brahmin. The Moriyas of Pipphalivana, hearing of the Blessed One's Parinibbāna, thought, "The Blessed One was a khattiya, and we also are khattiyas. We too are worthy to obtain a share of the Blessed One's relics." Sending a messenger and preparing for battle, they arrived. The kings told them, "There is no share of the Blessed One's relics left; the Blessed One's relics have been divided. Take these embers from here!" So they took the embers.

Atha kho rājā ajātasattu kusinārāya ca rājagahassa ca antare pañcavīsati yojanamaggaṃ aṭṭha usabhavitthataṃ samatalaṃ kāretvā sādisaṃ mallarājāno makuṭabaṇdhanassa ca santhāgārassa ca antare pūjaṃ kāresuṃ tādisaṃ pañcavīsatiyojanepi magge pūjā kāretvā lokassa anukkaṇṭhanatthaṃ sabbattha antarāpaṇe pasāretvā suvaṇṇadoṇiyaṃ pakkhittadhātuyo sattipañjarena parikkhipāpetvā attano vijite pañcayojanasata parimaṇḍale manusse sannipātāpesi.

Then King Ajātasattu had a road made between Kusinārā and Rājagaha, twenty-five yojanas long, eight usabhas wide, and level. The Malla kings made offerings between the Makuṭabandhana shrine and the assembly hall. Similarly, along the twenty-five yojana road, he had offerings made, spreading goods in markets everywhere to prevent the people from becoming weary. Placing the relics in a golden urn, he had it surrounded with a lattice of spears and gathered people within a five-hundred yojana area of his own domain.

Te dhātuyo gahetvā kusinārāto dhātukīḷaṃ kīḷantā nikkhamitvā yattha yattha vaṇṇavantāni pupphāni passanti, tattha tattha dhātuyo satti antare ṭhapetvā tesaṃ pupphānaṃ khīṇakāle gacchanti. Rathassa dhuraṭṭhānaṃ pacchimaṭṭhāne sampatte satta divase sādhukīḷaṃ kīḷanti evaṃ dhātuyo gahetvā āgacchantānaṃ sattavassāni sattamāsāni satta ca divasāni vitivattāni micchādiṭṭhikā samaṇassa gotamassa parinibbuta kālato paṭṭhāya balakkārena sādhukīḷikāya upaddutamhā, sabbe no kammantā naṭṭhāti ujjhāyantā manaṃ padūsetvā chaḷāsītisahassamattā apāye nibbattā, khīṇāsava āvajjitvā mahājano manaṃ padūsetvā apāye nibbattoti sakkaṃ devarājānaṃ disvā dhātu āharaṇupāyaṃ karissāmāti tassa santikaṃ gantvā tamatthaṃ ārocetvā dhātu āharaṇupāyaṃ kārohi mahārājāti āhaṃsu. Sakko āha “puthujjano nāma ajātasattunā samo saddho natthi, na so mama vacanaṃ karissati api ca kho māravibhisakasadisaṃ vibhīsakaṃ dassessāmi yakkha gāhaka khipanaka arocake karissāmi tumhe [O 0.0027]’mahārāja amanussā kupitā, dhātuyo āharāpethāti’. Vadeyyātha evaṃ so āharāpessatīti. Atha kho sakko taṃ sabbaṃ akāsi.

Taking those relics, they left Kusinārā, celebrating the relic festival. Wherever they saw beautiful flowers, there they placed the relics between the lances, and when those flowers withered, they moved on. When the head of the procession reached its final station, they celebrated joyfully for seven days. Thus, as they were coming carrying the relics, seven years, seven months, and seven days passed by. Those holding wrong views, grumbling, "Since the Parinibbāna of the ascetic Gotama, we have been harassed by this excessive festivity; all our work is ruined!", corrupted their minds, and about eighty-six thousand beings were reborn in the lower realms. The khīṇāsavas, reflecting, saw that the great multitude had corrupted their minds and been reborn in the lower realms. Then, seeing Sakka, the king of devas, they thought, "We shall devise a means to retrieve the relics." Approaching him, they explained the matter and said, "Great king, arrange a way to bring back the relics." Sakka replied, "There is no ordinary person as faithful as Ajātasattu. He will not heed my words. However, I will show a terrifying sight like Māra’s terrifying image. I will make yakkhas seize, hurl, and proclaim. You should say, ‘Great king, the non-humans are angry! Have the relics brought back!’ Thus he will have them brought back." Then Sakka did all this.

Therāpi rājānaṃ upasaṅkamitvā mahārāja amanussā kupitaṃ, dhātuyo āharāpehīti bhaṇiṃsu rājā na tāva bhante mayhaṃ cittaṃ tussati. Evaṃ santepi āharatūti āha sattame divase dhātuyo āhariṃsu. Evaṃ āhatadhātuyo gahetvā rājā rājagahe thupamakāsi itarepi rājāno attano attano balānurūpena nīharitvā sakasakaṭṭhāne thūpamakaṃsu. Doṇipi brāhmaṇo piphphalīvaniyāpi moriyā sakasakaṭṭhāne thūpamakaṃsūti.

The elders also approached the king and said, "Great king, the non-humans are angry. Please have the relics brought." The king replied, "Venerable sirs, my mind is not yet satisfied. Even so, let them be brought." On the seventh day, the relics were brought. Having taken the relics thus brought, the king built a stupa in Rājagaha. The other kings, each according to their own power, having taken away their portions, built stupas in their respective places. The brahmin Doṇa and the Moriyas of Pipphalivana also built stupas in their respective places.

Eko thūpo rājagahe - eko vesāliyā pure,

Eko kapilavatthusmiṃ - eko ca allakappake;

One stupa was in Rājagaha, one in the city of Vesāli, one in Kapilavatthu, and one in Allakappa;

Eko thūpo rāmagāme - eko ca veṭhadīpake,

Eko pāceyyake malle - eko ca kusinārake;

One stupa in Rāmagāma, one in Veṭhadīpa, one in Pāvā of the Mallas, and one in Kusinārā.

Ye te sārīrikā thūpā - jambuvadīpe patiṭṭhitā;

Aṅgārakumbhathūpehi - dasa thūpā bhavanti te;

These are the bodily relic stupas established in Jambudīpa; with the stupas of the embers and the urn, they become ten stupas in total.

Dasāpi thūpā purisuttamassa ye-

Yathānurūpaṃ nararāja pūjitā,

Sabbena lokena sadevakena te-

Namassaneyyāca bhavanti sabbadāti;

The ten stupas of the Supreme Person, fittingly honored by the king of men, are always worthy of veneration by all the world with its devas.

Dasathūpakathā

The Discourse on the Ten Stupas

10. Evaṃ patiṭṭhitesu pana thūpesu mahākassapatthero dhātūnaṃ antarāyaṃ disvā ajātasattuṃ upasaṅkamitvā mahārāja ekaṃ dhātunidhānaṃ kātuṃ vaṭṭatiti āha sādhu bhante nidhānakammaṃ tāva mama hotu, dhātuyo pana kathaṃ āharāpemiti. Na mahārāja dhātu āharaṇaṃ tuyhaṃ bhāro, amhākaṃ bhāroti. Sādhu bhante tumhe dhātuyo āharatha. Ahaṃ dhātunidhānaṃ karissāmīti. Thero tesaṃ tesaṃ rājakulānaṃ paricaraṇamattakameva ṭhapetvā sesedhātuyo āhari.

10. When the stupas were thus established, the Venerable Mahākassapa, seeing a danger to the relics, approached King Ajātasattu and said, 'Great king, it is fitting to make a single relic-deposit.' The king replied, 'Very well, venerable sir, let the work of the deposit be mine. But how shall we bring the relics?' The elder said, 'Great king, bringing the relics is not your burden; it is ours.' The king said, 'Very well, venerable sir, you bring the relics, and I will make the relic-deposit.' The elder, leaving only what was sufficient for the veneration of those various royal families, brought the remaining relics.

Rāmagāme pana dhātuyo nāgā gaṇhiṃsu tāsaṃ antarāyo natthi, anāgate laṅkādīpe mahāvihāre mahācetiyamhi nidhiyissa nīti. Tā na āharittha, sesehi sattahi nagarehi āharitvā rājagahassa pācīna dakkhina disābhāge ṭhapetvā imasmiṃ ṭhāne yo pāsāṇo atthi, so antaradhāyatu, paṃsu suvisuddhā hotu, udakañca mā uṭṭhahatūti adhiṭṭhāsi.

At Rāmagāma, however, the relics were taken by the nāgas. There was no danger to them, with the knowledge that in the future they would be enshrined in the Great Cetiya at the Mahāvihāra in Laṅkādīpa. Therefore, he did not bring them. Having brought the relics from the remaining seven cities, he placed them in the southeastern part of Rājagaha and made a resolution: 'Let whatever stone is in this place disappear, let the soil become perfectly pure, and let no water rise up.'

Rājā [O 0.0028] taṃ ṭhānaṃ khaṇāpetvā tato uddhaṭa paṃsunā iṭṭhikā kāretvā asīti mahāsāvakānaṃ thūpe kāreti idha rājā kiṃ kārehīti pucchantānampi mahāsāvakānaṃcetiyā nīti vadanti na koci dhātunidhānabhāvaṃ jānāti asītihatthagambhīre pana tasmiṃ padese jāte heṭṭhā lobhasattharaṃ sattharāpetvā tattha thūpārāme cetiya gharappamāṇaṃ tambalohamayaṃ gehaṃ kārāpetvā aṭṭhaṭṭha hari caṇdanādimaye karaṇḍe ca thūpe ca kārāpesi.

The king had that site excavated, and with the soil removed from there, he had bricks made and then had stupas constructed for the eighty great disciples. Even when people asked, 'What is the king doing here?', they were told, 'They are cetiyas for the great disciples,' and no one knew of the relic-deposit. When that place had become eighty cubits deep, he had a sheet of copper spread out below. There, he had a building made of copper, the size of a cetiya-house, constructed, and he had eight each of caskets and stupas made of yellow sandalwood and other such materials made.

Atha kho bhagavato dhātuyo haricaṇdana karaṇḍe pakkhipitvā taṃ haricaṇdanaṃ karaṇḍaṃ aññasmiṃ haricaṇdana karaṇḍe tampi aññasminti evaṃ aṭṭha haricaṇdana karaṇḍe ekato katvā eteneva upāyena aṭṭha karaṇḍe aṭṭhasu haricaṇdana thūpesu aṭṭha haricaṇdanathūpe aṭṭhasu lohitacaṇdana karaṇḍesu, aṭṭha lohitacaṇdanakaraṇḍe aṭṭhasu lohitacaṇdanathūpesu, aṭṭhalohita caṇdanathūpe aṭṭhasu dantakaraṇḍesu, aṭṭha dantakaraṇḍe aṭṭhasu dantathūpesu, aṭṭhadantathūpe aṭṭhasusabba ratanakaraṇḍesu, aṭṭha sabba ratanakaraṇḍe aṭṭhasu sabbaratanathūpesu, aṭṭha sabbaratana thūpe aṭṭhasu suvaṇṇakaraṇḍesu. Aṭṭhasuvaṇṇakaraṇḍe aṭṭhasu suvaṇṇathūpesu, aṭṭhasuvaṇṇathūpe aṭṭhasurajatakaraṇḍesu, aṭṭha rajatakaraṇḍe aṭṭhasu rajatathūpese aṭṭha rajatathūpe aṭṭhasu maṇikaraṇḍesu aṭṭhamaṇikareṇḍaaṭṭhasuvaṇṇathūpesu, aṭṭha maṇithūpe aṭṭhasu lohitaṅka karaṇḍesu, aṭṭhalohitaṅka karaṇḍe aṭṭhasu lohitaṅkathūpesu, aṭṭha lohitaṅkathūpe aṭṭhasu masāragallathūpesu, aṭṭha masāragallathūpe aṭṭhasu phalika karaṇḍesu, aṭṭha phaḷika karaṇḍe aṭṭhasu phaḷikathūpesu pakkhipi sabba uparimaṃ phaḷikacetiyaṃ thūpārāma cetiyappamāṇaṃ ahosi.

Then, having placed the relics of the Blessed One in a yellow sandalwood casket, that yellow sandalwood casket was placed in another yellow sandalwood casket, and that in another—thus, eight yellow sandalwood caskets were put together. In the same way, these eight caskets were placed in eight yellow sandalwood stupas; the eight yellow sandalwood stupas in eight red sandalwood caskets; the eight red sandalwood caskets in eight red sandalwood stupas; the eight red sandalwood stupas in eight ivory caskets; the eight ivory caskets in eight ivory stupas; the eight ivory stupas in eight all-gem caskets; the eight all-gem caskets in eight all-gem stupas; the eight all-gem stupas in eight golden caskets; the eight golden caskets in eight golden stupas; the eight golden stupas in eight silver caskets; the eight silver caskets in eight silver stupas; the eight silver stupas in eight jewel caskets; the eight jewel caskets in eight golden stupas; the eight jewel stupas in eight ruby caskets; the eight ruby caskets in eight ruby stupas; the eight ruby stupas in eight cat's eye stupas; the eight cat's eye stupas in eight crystal caskets; and the eight crystal caskets were placed in eight crystal stupas. The uppermost crystal cetiya was the size of the cetiya at the Thūpārāma.

Tassa uparisabbaratanamayaṃ gehaṃ kāresi tassa upari suvaṇṇamayaṃ, tassa upari rajatamayaṃ, tassa upari tambalohamayaṃ gehaṃ kāresi tattha sabbaratanamayaṃ vālukaṃ okiritvā jalaja thalaja pupphānaṃ sahassāni vippakiritvā addhacchaṭṭhāni jātakasatāni āsīti mahāthere suddhodana mahārājānaṃ mahāmāyādeviṃ satta sahajāte sabbānetāni suvaṇṇamayāneva kāresi pañcapañcasate suvaṇṇarajatamaye puṇṇaghaṭe ṭhapāpesi pañcasuvaṇṇadhajasate pañcasate suvaṇṇadīpake ca kārāpetvā sugaṇdhatelassa pūretvā tesu dukūlavaṭṭiyo ṭhapesi.

Above it, he had a house made of all precious gems built. Above that, one of gold; above that, one of silver; and above that, one of copper. There, he had sand made of all precious gems scattered, and thousands of aquatic and terrestrial flowers strewn about. He had all these made of gold: the five hundred and fifty Jātakas, the eighty great elders, King Suddhodana, Queen Mahāmāyā, and the seven co-natal ones. He had five hundred and fifty full pots of gold and silver placed, and had five hundred golden banners and five hundred golden lamps made. He had them filled with fragrant oil and placed fine cloth wicks in them.

Athāyasmā mahākassapo mālā mā milāyantu. Gaṇdhā mā vinassantu, dīpā mā vijjhāyantūti adhiṭṭhahitvā suvaṇṇapaṭṭaakkharāni chiṇdāpesi. Anāgate piyadāso nāma kumāro chattaṃ ussāpetvā [O 0.0029] asoko nāma dhammarājā bhavissati so imaṃ dhātuyo vitthārikā karissatīti rājā sabbapasādhanehi pūjetvā ādito paṭṭhāya dvāraṃ pidahanto nikkhami tambalohadvāraṃ pidahitvā āviñjana rajjuyaṃ kuñcikamuddikaṃ baṇdhi tattheva mahantaṃ maṇikkhaṇdhaṃ ṭhapesi anāgate daḷiddarājāno imaṃ maṇiṃ gahetvā dhātūnaṃ sakkāraṃ karontūti akkharāni chiṇdāpesī.

Then the Venerable Mahākassapa resolved, 'May the garlands not wither, may the perfumes not perish, may the lamps not be extinguished,' and had letters engraved on a golden plate: 'In the future, a prince named Piyadāsa will raise the parasol and become the Dhamma King named Asoka. He will distribute these relics widely.' The king, having paid homage with all manner of adornments, departed, closing the doors from the innermost one outwards. Having shut the copper door, he fastened the key and seal to the pulling-cord. Right there, he placed a great mass of jewels and had letters engraved: 'In the future, let impoverished kings take these jewels and pay homage to the relics.'

Sakko devarājā vissakammaṃ āmantetvā’tāta, ajātasattunā dhātunidhānaṃ kataṃ. Ettha ārakkhaṃ ṭhapehi’ti pahiṇi so āgantvā vāḷasaṅghāṭayantaṃ yojesi. Kaṭṭharūpakāni tasmiṃ dhātugabbhe phaḷikavaṇṇa khagge gahetvā vātasadisena vegena anupariyāyantaṃ yojetvā ekāya eva āṇivā baṇdhitvā samantato giñjaka vasathākārena silāparikkhepaṃ katvā upari ekāya pidahitvā paṃsuṃ pakkhipitvā bhūmiṃ samaṃ katvā tassupari pāsāṇathūpaṃ patiṭṭhāpesi.

Sakka, king of the devas, summoned Vissakamma and said, 'My dear, Ajātasattu has made a relic-deposit. Establish a guard there.' He sent him, and upon arriving, Vissakamma set up a mechanical trap. He arranged wooden figures in that relic chamber, holding swords the color of crystal, to revolve with the speed of wind, having bound them with a single pin. He made a stone enclosure all around in the shape of a brick hall, covered it on top with a single stone slab, threw earth over it, leveled the ground, and established a stone stupa on top.

Dhātunidhāna kathā

The Story of the Relic-Deposit

11. Evaṃ niṭṭhite dhātunidhāne yāvatāyukaṃ ṭhatvā thero parinibbuto, rājāpi yathākammaṃ gato, tepi manussā kālakatā, aparabhāge piyadāso kumāro chattaṃ ussāpetvā asoko nāma dhamma rājā hutvā tā dhātuyo gahetvā jambudīpe caturāsītiyā cetiya sahassesu patiṭṭhāpesi. Kathaṃ biṇdusārassa kira ekasataṃ puttā ahesuṃ te sabbe asoko attanā saddhiṃ ekamātikaṃ tissa kumāraṃ ṭhapetvā ghātesi. Ghātento cattāri vassāni anabhisitto rajjaṃ kāretvā catunnaṃ vassānaṃ accayena tathāgatassa parinibbānato dvinnaṃ vassasatānaṃ upari aṭṭhārasame vasse sakalajambudīpe ekarajjābhisekaṃ pāpuṇi.

11. Thus, when the relic-deposit was completed, the elder, having lived out his lifespan, attained Parinibbāna. The king, too, passed away according to his kamma, and those people also died. In a later time, Prince Piyadāsa raised the royal parasol and became the Dhamma King named Asoka. He took those relics and had them established in eighty-four thousand cetiyas throughout Jambudīpa. How so? It is said that King Bindusāra had one hundred and one sons. Asoka killed all of them, except for Prince Tissa, his brother by the same mother. While killing them, he ruled for four years without consecration. At the end of four years, in the eighteenth year after two hundred years had passed since the Tathāgata’s Parinibbāna, he attained the sole royal consecration over all of Jambudīpa.

Abhisekānubhāvena imā rājiddhiyo āgatā mahāpathaviyā heṭṭhā yojanappamāṇe āṇā pavatti, tathā upari ākāse, anotattadahato aṭṭhahi kājehi soḷasa pānīyaghaṭe divase divase devatā āharanti. Yato sāsane uppannasaddho hutvā aṭṭha ghaṭe bhikkhusaṅghassa adāsi dve ghaṭe saṭṭhimattānaṃ tipiṭaka bhikkhunaṃ dveghaṭe aggamahesiyā asaṇdhimittāya cattāro ghaṭe attanā paribhuñji. Devatā eva himavante nāgalatā dantakaṭṭha nāma atthi siniddhā mudukaṃ rasavantaṃ, taṃ divase divase ābhiranti yena rañño ca aggamahesiyā ca soḷasannaṃ nāṭakasahassānaṃ saṭṭhimattānaṃ bhikkhusahassānaṃ devasikaṃ dantapoṇakiccaṃ nipphajji.

By the power of his consecration, these royal psychic powers arose: his command extended for a yojana below the great earth, and likewise up into the sky. From Lake Anotatta, deities would daily bring sixteen pots of water on eight carrying poles. Having gained faith in the Sāsana, he gave eight pots to the Saṅgha of bhikkhus, two pots to the sixty or so bhikkhus who were masters of the Tipiṭaka, two pots to the chief queen Asandhimittā, and he himself used four pots. Deities also daily brought from the Himālaya a tooth-stick named Nāgalatā, which was smooth, soft, and flavorful. By this, the daily duty of tooth-cleaning was accomplished for the king, the chief queen, the sixteen thousand court dancers, and the sixty thousand or so bhikkhus.

Devasikamevassa [O 0.0030] devatā agadāmalakaṃ agadaharīṭakaṃ suvaṇṇavaṇṇañca gaṇdhasampannaṃ ambapakkaṃ āharanti tathā chaddanta dahato pañcavaṇṇaṃ nivāsana pārupaṇaṃ, pītakavaṇṇaṃ hatthapuñchanaka paṭṭaṃdibbañcajānakaṃ āharanti devasikameva panassa anulepagaṇdhaṃ pārupanatthāya asuttamayikaṃ sumanapupphapaṭaṃ mahārahañca añjanaṃ nāgabhavanato nāgarājāno āharanti. Chaddantadaheyeva uṭṭhitassa sālino nava cāha sahassāni divase divase suvā āharanti. Mūsikā nitthusa kaṇe karonti ekopi khaṇḍataṇḍulo na hoti. Rañño sabbaṭṭhānesu ayameva taṇḍulo paribhogaṃ gacchati. Madhumakkhikā madhuṃ karonti. Kammārasālāsu acchā kūṭaṃ paharanti. Dīpikā cammāni cālenti karavīka sakuṇā āgattvā madhurassaraṃ vikujentā rañño balikammaṃ karonti.

Every day, the deities brought him a medicinal myrobalan fruit, a medicinal harītaki fruit, and a golden-colored, fragrant ripe mango. Likewise, from the Chaddanta Lake, they brought a five-colored lower and upper garment, a yellow hand-wiping cloth, and a divine rug. Every day, the Nāga kings brought from the Nāga realm fragrant anointing unguents, a cloth of jasmine flowers not made of thread for wearing, and priceless collyrium. Nine thousand cartloads of rice that had grown in the Chaddanta Lake were brought daily by parrots. The mice made the grains free of chaff, and not a single broken grain remained. This very rice was used in all places for the king’s consumption. The bees produced honey. In the smithies, they struck the hammer without interruption. Leopards stretched the hides, and the karavīka birds came, singing sweetly, and performed their service for the king.

Imāhi iddhīhi samannāgato rājāeka divasaṃ suvaṇṇa saṅkhalika baṇdhanaṃ pesetvā catunnaṃ buddhānaṃ adhigata rūpadassanaṃ kappāyukaṃ mahākāḷa nāgarājānaṃ ānayitvā setacchattassa heṭṭhā mahārahe pallaṅke nisīdāpetvā aneka satavaṇṇehi jalaja thalaja pupphehi suvaṇṇapupphehi ca pūjaṃ katvā sabbālaṅkāra patimaṇḍitehi ca soḷasahi nāṭaka sahassehi samantato parikkhipitvā anantañāṇassa tāva me saddhammavara cakkavattino sammāsambuddhassa rūpaṃ imesaṃ akkhīnaṃ āpāthaṃ karohīti nibbattāsīti anubyañjana patimaṇḍitaṃ dvattiṃsa mahā purisa lakkhaṇa sassirīkatāya vikasita kamalupphala puṇḍarīka patimaṇḍitamiva salilatalaṃ tārāgaṇaraṃsijāla visaravipphutasobhāsamujjalamivagaganatalaṃ nīla-pīta-lohitādi bheda vicittavaṇṇaraṃsi vinaddha byāmappabhā parikkhepa vilāsitāya sañjhappabhānurāga iṇdadhanu vijjullatā parikkhittamiva kanakagiri sikharaṃ nānāvirāga vimalaketumālā samujjalitacārumatthakasobhaṃ nayanarasāyanamiva brahmadeva-manuja nāga-yakkha gaṇānaṃ buddharūpaṃ passanto sattadivasaṃ akkhipūjaṃ nāma akāsi

Endowed with such powers, the king, one day, sent a golden chain as a bond and had the great Nāga king Mahākāḷa, who lived for an aeon and had attained the vision of the forms of the four Buddhas, brought before him. He seated him beneath a white parasol on a splendid couch and honored him with hundreds of varieties of aquatic and terrestrial flowers, as well as golden flowers. Surrounding him with sixteen thousand dancers adorned with all ornaments, he said, 'Manifest for me the form of the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One, the noble Dhamma-Wheel-Turning One of infinite knowledge, so that it may come into the range of these eyes!' And so it manifested—adorned with the minor marks, radiant with the thirty-two marks of a great man, like a water surface embellished with blooming lotuses, water lilies, and white lotuses; shining like the sky illuminated by the spreading net of the stars' rays; its fathom-long aura adorned with the interwoven radiance of diverse colors such as blue, yellow, and red; like a golden mountain peak encircled by the glow of the evening sky, by rainbows, and by lightning flashes; its beautiful head shining brightly with a garland of various pure, bright banners; like a delight to the eyes for the hosts of Brahmās, devas, humans, Nāgas, and yakkhas. Beholding this form of the Buddha, he performed the 'eye-offering' for seven days.

Rājā kira abhisekaṃ pāpuṇitvā tīṇiyeva saṃvaccharāni bāhiraka pāsaṇḍaṃ parigaṇhi catutthe saṃvacchare buddhasāsane pasīdinaṃ brāhmaṇa jātiya pāsaṇḍānañca paṇḍaraṅga paribbājakānañca saṭṭhisahassamattānaṃ niccabhattaṃ paṭṭhapesi.

It is said that after attaining his consecration, the king for three years patronized the external sects. In the fourth year, he gained faith in the Buddha’s Dispensation and established a perpetual almsgiving for some sixty thousand heretics of brahmin birth and for white-robed wanderers.

Asoko pitarā pavattitaṃ dānaṃ attano antepure tatheva dadamāno ekadivasaṃ sīhapañjare ṭhito upasama paribāhirena ācārena [O 0.0031] bhuñjamāne asaṃyatiṇdriye avinita iriyāpathe disvā cintesi īdisaṃ dānaṃ upaparikkhitvā yuttaṭṭhāne dātuṃ vaṭṭatiti evaṃ cintetvā amacce āha. Gacchatha bhaṇe attano attano sādhusammate samaṇa brāhmaṇe antepuraṃ atiharatha, dānaṃ dassāmāti amaccā sādhu devāti rañño paṭissutvā te te paṇḍaraṅga paribbājakājīvaka nigaṇṭhādayo ānetvā ime mahārāja amhākaṃ arahantoti āhaṃsu.

Asoka, continuing the charity established by his father and giving in his own inner palace just as before, one day, while standing at a lion-window, observed those eating with conduct devoid of tranquility, with uncontrolled faculties, and with undisciplined deportment. He thought, 'It is fitting to examine such giving and to give in a proper place.' Having thus reflected, he addressed his ministers: 'Go, sirs, and bring to the inner palace those ascetics and brahmins whom you yourselves hold in high esteem. I shall give alms.' The ministers, replying, 'Very well, Your Majesty,' brought forward the white-robed wanderers, the Ājīvakas, the Nigaṇṭhas, and others, saying, 'These, great king, are our arahants.'

Atha rājā antepure uccāvacāni āsanāni paññāpetva āgacchantūti vatvā āgatāgate āha attano attano anurūpe āsane nisīdathāti ekacce bhaddapīṭhake ekacce phalakapīṭhakese nisīdiṃsu. Taṃ disvā rājā natthi etesaṃ antesāroti ñatvā tesaṃ anurūpaṃ khādanīyaṃ bhojanīyaṃ datvā uyyojesi.

Then the king, having had various high and low seats prepared in the inner palace, said, 'Let them come.' To each one as they arrived, he said, 'Sit on a seat appropriate to yourself.' Some sat on fine chairs, others on plank seats. Seeing this, the king knew, 'There is no inner substance in these ones,' and after giving them suitable hard and soft food, he dismissed them.

Evaṃ gacchante kāle eka divasaṃ sīhapañjare ṭhito addasa nigrodha sāmaṇeraṃ rājaṅgaṇena gacchantaṃ dantaṃ guttaṃ santindriyaṃ iriyāpatha sampannaṃ. Ko panāyaṃ nigrodho nāma, biṇdusāra rañño jeṭṭhaputtassa sumanarāja kumārassa putto. Tatrāyaṃ ānupubbī kathā-biṇdusāra rañño kira dubbalakāleyeva asoka kumāro attanā laddhaṃ ujjenirajjaṃ pahāya āgantvā sabbaṃ nagaraṃ attano hatthagataṃ katvā sumana rājakumāraṃ aggahesi.

As time was thus passing, one day, while standing at a lion-window, he saw the novice Nigrodha walking through the royal courtyard—controlled, guarded, with tranquil faculties, and perfect in deportment. Now, who was this Nigrodha? He was the son of Prince Sumana, the eldest son of King Bindusāra. Here is the sequential account: It is said that at the very time of King Bindusāra’s infirmity, Prince Asoka, having relinquished the kingdom of Ujjeni which he had obtained, returned, brought the entire city under his control, and had Prince Sumana seized.

Taṃ divasameva sumanassa rājakumārassa sumanā nāma devī paripuṇṇagabbhā ahosi. Sā aññātakavesena nikkhamitvā avidūre aññataraṃ caṇḍālagāmaṃ sandhāya gacchanti, jeṭṭhaka caṇḍālassa gehato avidūre ekasmiṃ nigrodha rukkhe adhivatthāya devatāya ito sumaneti vahantiyā saddaṃ sutvā tassā samīpaṃ gatā devatā attano ānubhāvena ekaṃ sālaṃ nimmiṇitvā ettha vasāhiti padāsi. Sā taṃ sālaṃ pāvisi. Gatadivaseyeva puttaṃ vijāyi.

On that very day, Prince Sumana's consort, Queen Sumanā, was with child and her time was near. She left in disguise and was heading for a certain outcaste village not far away. Near the house of the chief outcaste, she heard the voice of a deity dwelling in a nigrodha tree, calling out, 'Come here, Sumanā!' She approached the deity, who by her own power created a hall and gave it to her, saying, 'Dwell here.' She entered that hall. On the very day she arrived, she gave birth to a son.

Sā tassa nigrodha devatāya pariggahitattā nigrodhotveva nāmaṃ akāsi. Jeṭṭhaka caṇḍālo daṭṭhadivasatoppabhūti taṃ attano sāmidhītaraṃ viya maññamāno nibaddhaṃ vaṭṭaṃ paṭṭhapesi. Rājadhītā tattha satta vassāni vasi. Nigrodhakumāropi sattavassiko jāto tadā mahāvaruṇatthero nāma eko arahā dārakassa hetusampadaṃ disvā viharamāno sattavassikodāni dārako kālo naṃ pabbājetunti cintetvā rājadhītayā ārocāpetvā nigrodhakumāraṃ pabbājesi. Kumāro khuraggeyeva arahattaṃ pāpuṇi. So ekadivasaṃ pātova sarīraṃ paṭijaggitvā ācariyupajjhānaṃ [O 0.0032] vattaṃ katvā pattacīvaramādāya mātuupāsikāya gehadvāraṃ gacchāmīti nikkhami mātu nivesanaṭṭhānañcassa dakkhiṇadvārena nagaraṃ pavisitvā nagaramajjhena gantvā pācīnadvārena nikkhamitvā gantabbaṃ hoti tena ca samayena asoko dhammarājā pācīna disābhimukho sīhapañjare caṅkamati.

Because he was protected by the nigrodha-tree deity, she gave him the name Nigrodha. The chief outcaste, from the day he saw her, considering her to be his master's daughter, established a regular provision for her. The princess lived there for seven years. When Prince Nigrodha was seven years old, an arahant, the Elder Mahāvaruṇa, seeing the boy’s karmic potential, thought, 'The boy is now seven years old; it is time to ordain him.' Having informed the princess, he ordained Prince Nigrodha. The boy attained arahantship at the very touch of the razor. One day, early in the morning, after attending to his person and performing his duties to his preceptor and teacher, he took his bowl and robe and set out, thinking, 'I will go to the door of my mother, the lay devotee.' His mother's residence was situated such that one had to enter the city by the southern gate, pass through the middle of the city, and exit by the eastern gate. At that very time, Asoka, the King of Dhamma, was pacing in a lion-window, facing east.

Taṃ khaṇaṃyeva sigrodho sāmaṇero rājaṅgaṇaṃ pāpuṇi santindriyo santamānaso yugamattaṃ pekkhamāno. Tena vuttaṃ. Ekadivasaṃ sīhapañjare ṭhito addasa nigrodhasāmaṇeraṃ rājaṅgaṇena gacchantaṃ dantaṃ guttaṃ santindriyaṃ iriyāpatha sampannanti. Disvā panassa etadahosi? Ayaṃ jano sabbopi vikkhittacittohantamagapaṭibhāgo, ayampana dārako avikkhittacitto ativiyassa alokita vilokitaṃ sammiñjana pasāraṇañca sobhati, addhā etassa abbhantare lokuttaradhammo bhavissatīti rañño sahadassaneneva sāmaṇere cittaṃ pasīdi. Pemaṃ saṇṭhahi kasmā? Pubbe kira puñña karaṇakāle esa rañño jeṭṭhabhātā vāṇijako ahosi.

At that very moment, the novice Nigrodha reached the royal courtyard, with tranquil faculties and a peaceful mind, looking ahead a yoke's length. Thus it is said: 'One day, standing in a lion-window, he saw the novice Nigrodha walking through the royal courtyard—restrained, guarded, with tranquil faculties, perfect in deportment.' Upon seeing him, this thought arose in the king: 'All these other people have distracted minds, like frightened deer. But this boy is not distracted; his looking ahead and glancing about, his bending and stretching, are exceptionally graceful. Surely, there must be a supramundane state within him.' Merely upon seeing the novice, the king's heart was filled with confidence. Affection for him was established. Why? It is said that in the past, during a time of making merit, this novice had been the king's elder brother, a merchant.

Atha rājā sañjātapemo sabahumāno sāmaṇeraṃ pakkosathāti amacce pesesi aticirāyatīti puna dve tayo pesesi turitaṃ āgacchatūti sāmaraṇero attano pakatiyā eva agamāsi rājā patirūpāsanaṃ ñatvā nisīdathāti āha. Ito cito ca oloketvā natthidāni añño bhikkhuti samussita setacchattaṃ rājapaḷlaṅkaṃ upasaṅkamitvā pattagahaṇatthāya rañño ākāraṃ dassesi. Rājā taṃ pallaṅkaṃ samīpaṃ gacchantaṃ disvā evaṃ cintesi. Ajjevadāni ayaṃ sāmaṇero imassa gehassa sāmiko bhavissati. Sāmaṇero rañño hatthe pattaṃ datvā pallaṅkaṃ abharuhitvā nisīdi.

Then the king, his affection aroused and full of respect, sent his ministers to summon the novice, thinking, "He is taking too long." Again, he sent two or three more, urging, "Let him come quickly!" The novice, however, proceeded at his own pace. The king, knowing a suitable seat, said, "Please sit." Looking here and there and seeing no other bhikkhu present, the novice approached the royal couch surmounted by a white parasol and made a sign to the king to take his bowl. Seeing the novice approaching the couch, the king thought, "This very day, this novice will become the master of this house." The novice, having placed the bowl in the king's hand, mounted the couch and sat down.

Rājā attano atthāya sampāditaṃ sabbaṃ yāgu-kajjaka bhatta vikatiṃ upanāmesi. Sāmaṇero attano yāpanamattameva sampaṭicchi. Bhattakiccāvasāne rājā āha satthārā tumhākaṃ dinnovādaṃ jānāthāti jānami mahārāja ekadesenāti. Tāta, mayhampi naṃ kathehīti sādhu mahārājāti rañño anurūpaṃ dhammapade appamāda vaggaṃ anumodanatthāya abhāsi.

The king offered all the rice-gruel, hard foods, boiled rice, and various dishes that had been prepared for himself. The novice accepted only what was sufficient for his sustenance. At the conclusion of the meal, the king asked, "Do you know the instruction given to you by the Teacher?" "I know it in part, great king." "Dear one, please tell it to me as well." "Very well, great king," he said, and for the king's blessing, he recited a suitable passage from the Dhammapada, from the Chapter on Heedfulness.

Rājā pana appamādo amatapadaṃ, pamādo maccuno padanti sutva ca, aññātaṃ tāta pariyosāpehīti āha anumodanāvasāne dvattiṃsa dhurabhattāni labhitvā puna divase dvattiṃsa bhikkhu gahetvā rājantepuraṃ pavisitvā bhattakiccamakāsi rājā aññepi dvattiṃsa bhikkhu tumhehi saddhiṃyeva bhikkhaṃ gaṇhantūti eteneva upāyena divase divase vaḍḍhāpente saṭṭhi [O 0.0033] sahassānaṃ brāhmaṇa paribbājakānaṃ bhattaṃ upacchiṇditvā anto nivesane saṭṭhisahassānaṃ bhikkhunaṃ niccabhattaṃ paṭṭhapesi, nigrodhatthera gateneva pasādena, nigrodhattheropi rājānaṃ saparisaṃ tīsu saraṇesu pañcasu ca sīlesu patiṭṭhāpetvā buddhasāsane pothujjanikena pasādena acalappasādaṃ katvā patiṭṭhāpesi.

The king, upon hearing, "Heedfulness is the path to the Deathless, heedlessness the path to death," said, "I have understood, dear one; please conclude." At the end of the blessing, the novice, having received thirty-two portions of food, on the next day took thirty-two monks, entered the royal palace, and performed the meal service. The king said, "Let another thirty-two monks receive alms along with you." By this method, increasing the number day by day, he cut off the food supply for sixty thousand brahmins and wanderers and established a permanent meal offering for sixty thousand monks within his residence. Through the faith inspired by the Elder Nigrodha, the Elder Nigrodha also established the king and his retinue in the three refuges and the five precepts, making his ordinary faith into an unwavering faith in the Buddha’s dispensation.

Puna rāja asokārāmaṃ nāma mahāvihāraṃ kārapetvā saṭṭhisahassānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ bhattaṃ paṭṭhapesi. Sakalajambudīpe caturāsītiyā nagarasahassesu caturāsīti vihārasahassāni kārāpesi. Caturāsīti cetiyasahassa patimaṇḍitāni dhammeneva no adhammena, ekadivasaṃ kira rājā asokārāme mahādānaṃ datvā saṭṭhisahassa saṅkhassa bhikkhusaṅghassa majjhe nisajja saṅghaṃ catūhi pavcayehi pavāretvā imaṃ pañhaṃ pucchi bhante bhagavato desita dhammo nāma kittako hotīti. Mahārāja nava aṅgāni, khaṇdato caturāsīti dhammakkhaṇdha sahassānīti.

Again, the king had a great monastery named Asokārāma built and established a meal offering for sixty thousand monks. Throughout the whole of Jambudīpa, in eighty-four thousand cities, he had eighty-four thousand monasteries constructed, adorned with eighty-four thousand shrines, righteously and not unrighteously. It is said that one day, after giving a great donation at Asokārāma, the king sat amidst the Sangha of sixty thousand monks and, after inviting the Sangha with the four requisites, asked this question: "Venerable sirs, how much Dhamma was proclaimed by the Blessed One?" "Great king, there are nine divisions; by sections, there are eighty-four thousand sections of the Dhamma."

Rājā dhamme pasīditvā ekekaṃ dhammakkhaṇdhaṃ ekekena vihārena pūjessāmīti ekadivasameva channavuti koṭidhanaṃ vissajjetvā amacce āṇāpesi. Etha bhaṇe ekekasmiṃ nagare ekamekaṃ vihāraṃ kārentā caturāsītiyā nagarasahassesu caturāsīti vihārasahassāni kārāpethāti sayañca asokārāme asoka mahāvihāratthāya kammaṃ paṭṭhapesi.

The king, filled with faith in the Dhamma, resolved, "I will honor each section of the Dhamma with one monastery." On that very day, he spent ninety-six crores of wealth and commanded his ministers: "Come, sirs, have one monastery built in each city, and thus have eighty-four thousand monasteries constructed in eighty-four thousand cities." And he himself initiated the work for the Asoka Great Monastery in Asokārāma.

Saṅgho iṇdaguttattheraṃ nāma mahiddhiyaṃ mahānubhāvaṃ khīṇāsavaṃ navakammādhiṭṭhāyakaṃ adāsi. Thero yaṃ yaṃ na niṭṭhāti taṃ taṃ attano ānubhāvena niṭṭhāpesi. Evaṃ tīhi saṃvaccharehi vihārakammaṃ niṭṭhāpesi. Ekadivasameva sabbanagarehi paṇṇāni āgamiṃsu amaccā rañño ārocesuṃ. Niṭṭhitāni deva caturāsīti vihārasahassānīti atha rājā bhikkhusaṅghaṃ upasaṅkamitvā bhante mayā caturāsīti vihārasahassāni kāritāni dhātuyokuto labhissāmīti puca pucchi.

The Sangha appointed the Elder named Indagutta, an Arahant of great psychic power and majesty, as the supervisor of the new construction. Whatever was not finished, the Elder completed by his own power. In this way, the monastery construction was completed in three years. On a single day, messages arrived from all the cities, and the ministers informed the king: "Sire, the eighty-four thousand monasteries are completed." Then the king approached the Sangha of monks and asked, "Venerable sirs, I have had eighty-four thousand monasteries built. From where shall I obtain the relics?"

Mahārāja dhātunidhānaṃ nāma atthīti suṇoma. Na pana paññāyati asukāṭṭhāneti rājā rājagahe cetiyaṃ bhiṇdāpetvā dhātuṃ apassanto paṭipākatiyaṃ kāretvā bhikkhu-bhikkhuniyo-upāsaka-upāsikāyoti catasso parisā gahetvā vesāliṃ gato. Tatrāpi alabhitvā kapiḷavatthuṃ, tatrāpi alabhitvā rāmagāmaṃ gato, rāmagāme nāgā cetiyaṃ bhiṇdituṃ na adaṃsu. Cetiye nipatita kuddālo khaṇḍākhaṇḍaṃ hoti evaṃ tatrāpi alabhitvā allakappaṃ-pāvaṃ-kusināranti sabbattha cetiyāti bhiṇditvā dhātuṃ alabhitvā paṭipākatikāni [O 0.0034] katvā rājagahaṃ gantvā catasso parisā sannipātetvā atthi kenaci sutapubbaṃ asukaṭṭhāne nāma dhātu nidhānanti pucchi.

"Great king, we hear that there is a relic repository, but it is not known in what place it is." The king had a shrine in Rājagaha broken open, but not seeing the relics, he had it restored to its original state. Taking the fourfold assembly—monks, nuns, laymen, and laywomen—he went to Vesāli. Not obtaining them there either, he went to Kapilavatthu. Not obtaining them there either, he went to Rāmagāma. In Rāmagāma, the Nāgas did not allow the shrine to be broken. The pickaxe that fell upon the shrine shattered into pieces. Thus, not obtaining them there either, he went to Allakappa, Pāvā, and Kusinārā, breaking open shrines everywhere but not obtaining the relics. Having them restored to their original state, he went to Rājagaha, assembled the fourfold assembly, and asked, "Has anyone ever heard that there is a relic repository in such-and-such a place?"

Tattheko visaṃvassa satiko thero asukaṭṭhāne dhātunidhānanti na jānāmi. Mayhaṃ pana pitāmahatthero mayi sattavassikakāle mālācaṅgoṭakaṃ gāhāpetvā ehi sāmaṇera, asuka gacchantare pāsānathūpo atthi, tattha gacchāmāti gantvā pūjetvā imaṃ ṭhānaṃ upadhāretuṃ vaṭṭati sāmaṇeroti āha. Ahaṃ ettakameva jānāmi mahārājāti āha.

There, an elder who was one hundred and twenty years old said, "I do not know that there is a relic repository in such-and-such a place. However, when I was seven years old, my grandfather, an elder, had me hold a flower basket and said, 'Come, novice, there is a stone stupa in a certain thicket. Let us go there.' Having gone and paid homage, he said, 'Novice, it is proper to remember this place.' That is all I know, great king."

Rājā etadeva ṭhānanti vatvā gacche harāpetvā pāsāṇathūpaṃ paṃsuñca apanetvā heṭṭhā sudhābhūmiṃ addasa. Tato sudhañca iṭṭhakāyo ca harāpetvā anupubbena pariveṇā oruyha satta ratanavālikaṃ asīti hatthāni ca kaṭṭharūpāni samparivattantāni addasa. So yakkha dāsake pakkosāpetvā balikammaṃ kāretvāpi neva antaṃ na koṭiṃ pannanto devatā namassamāno ahaṃ imā dhātuyo gahetvā caturāsītiyā vihārasahassesu nidahitvā sakkāraṃ karomi mā devatā antarāyaṃ karentūti āha.

The king, saying, "This must be the place," had the thicket cleared and the stone stupa and earth removed. Beneath, he saw a surface of plaster. After having the plaster and bricks removed, he gradually descended into the chamber and saw sand of the seven jewels and eighty-cubit wooden figures revolving. Even after summoning his yakka servants and having an offering ritual performed, he could not reach an end or a limit. Then, paying homage to the deities, he said, "I will take these relics, enshrine them in eighty-four thousand monasteries, and make offerings. May the deities not create an obstacle."

Sakko devarājā cārikaṃ caranto taṃ disvā vissakammaṃ āmantetvā āha. Tāta, asoko dhammarājā dhātuyo nīharissāmīti pariveṇaṃ otiṇṇo, gantvā kaṭṭharūpāni hārehīti so pañcacūḷagāma dārakavesenāgantvā rañño purato dhanukahattho ṭhatvā hāremi mahārājāti āha. Hara tātāti. Saraṃ gahetvā sandhimhiyeva vijjhi, sabbaṃ vippakirīyittha.

Sakka, the king of the gods, while wandering on tour, saw this and addressed Vissakamma, saying, "Dear one, Asoka the Dhamma-King has descended into the chamber, intending to take out the relics. Go and remove the wooden figures." So he, having come in the guise of a village boy with five top-knots, stood before the king with a bow in his hand and said, "Great king, I shall remove them." "Remove them, dear one." Taking an arrow, he shot it right into the joint, and everything scattered.

Atha rājā āciñjane baddha kuñcika muddikaṃ gaṇhi, maṇikkhaṇdhaṃ passi, anāgate daḷiddarājāno imaṃ maṇiṃ gahetvā dhātūnaṃ sakkāraṃ karontūti pana akkharāni disvā kujjhitvā mādisaṃ nāma rājānaṃ daḷiddarājāti cattu yuttanti punappuna ghaṭetvā dvāraṃ vicaritvā antogehaṃ paviṭṭho. Aṭṭhārasa vassādhikānaṃ dvinnaṃ vassasatānaṃ upari āropitadīpā tatheva pajjalanti niluppala pupphāni taṃ khaṇaṃ āharitvā āropitāni viya, pupphasaṇtharo taṃ khaṇaṃ santhato viya, gaṇdhā taṃ muhuttaṃ piṃsitvā ṭhapitā viya.

Then the king took the sealed key that was attached to the treasury. He saw a heap of gems, and upon seeing the inscription—"In the future, poor kings shall take this gem and honor the relics"—he grew angry. "Is it fitting to call a king like me a poor king!" Striving again and again, he opened the door and entered the inner chamber. The lamps, lit over two hundred and eighteen years ago, were still burning just as before. The blue water lily flowers were as if brought and placed there at that very moment, the carpet of flowers was as if spread at that very moment, and the perfumes were as if ground and set down at that very instant.

Rājā suvaṇṇapaṭṭaṃ gahetvā anāgate piyadāsonāma kumāro chattaṃ ussāpetvā asoko nāma dhammarājā bhavissati so imā dhātuyo gahetvā vitthārikā karissatīti vācetvā diṭṭho’haṃ ayyona mahākassapattherenāti vatvā vāmahatthaṃ ābhujitvā dakkhiṇahatthena appoṭhesi.

The king, taking the golden plate, read: "In the future, a prince named Piyadāsa, raising the parasol, will become the righteous king Asoka. He will take these relics and spread them far and wide." Having read this, he said, "I have been seen by the venerable Mahākassapa Thera," and then, folding his left arm, he struck it with his right hand.

So tasmiṃ ṭhāne paricaraṇaka dhātumattakameva ṭhapetvā sesadhātuyo sabbā gahetvā dhātugharaṃ pubbe pihitanayeneva pidahitvā [O 0.0035] sabbaṃ yathā pakatiyāva kāretvā upari pāsāṇacetiyaṃ patiṭṭhāpetvā caturāsītiyā vihārasahassesudhātuyo patiṭṭhāpesi evaṃ jambudīpatale asoko dhammarājā caturāsiti cetiya sahassāni kārāpesi.

In that very place, having left only a small portion of the relics for veneration, he took all the remaining relics and, as before, sealed the relic chamber. He then restored everything to its original state and established a stone stupa above it. In this way, King Asoka, the Righteous King, established relics in eighty-four thousand monasteries and had eighty-four thousand stupas built across the land of Jambudīpa.

Sabbe thūpā sabbalokekadīpā sabbesaṃ ye saggamokkhāvahā ca, hitvā sabbaṃ kiccamaññaṃ janena vaṇdayyā te sabbathā sabbakālanti.

All stupas are the sole lamps for the entire world, bringing heaven and liberation to all beings. Having set aside all other duties, people should venerate them in every way and at all times.

Caturāsīti sahassa thūpakathā

The Account of the Eighty-Four Thousand Stupas

12. Evaṃ asoko dhammarājā caturāsīti vihārasahassamahaṃ katvā mahāthero vanditvā pucchi dāyādomhi bhante buddhasāsane’ti. Kissa dāyādo tvaṃ mahārāja, bāhirako tvaṃ sāsanassāti. Bhante channavutikoṭidhanaṃ vissajjetvā caturāsīti vihārasahassāni sacetiyāni kārāpetvā ahaṃ na dāyādo, aññe ko dāyādoti, paccaya dāyako nāma tvaṃ mahārāja, yo pana attano puttañca dhītarañca pabbājeti, ayaṃ sāsane dāyādo nāmāti.

12. Thus, King Asoka, the righteous king, having established eighty-four thousand monasteries, paid homage to the great elder and asked, "Venerable sir, am I an heir in the Buddha's Dispensation?" "An heir to what are you, great king? You are external to the Dispensation." "Venerable sir, having spent ninety-six crores of wealth and had eighty-four thousand monasteries with cetiyas constructed, if I am not an heir, then who is an heir?"
