"You, great king, are a donor of requisites. But one who has their own son and daughter go forth into homelessness—such a one is called an heir in the Dispensation."

Evaṃ vutte asoko rājā sāsane dāyādabhāvaṃ patthayamāno avidūre ṭhitaṃ mahiṇdakumāraṃ disvā sakkhissasitvaṃ tāta pabbajitunti āha. Kumāro pakatiyā pabbajitukāmo rañño vacanaṃ sutvā ativiya pāmojjajāto pabbjāmi deva, maṃ pabbājetvā sāsane dāyādo hothāti āha tena ca samayena rājadhītā saṅghamittāpi tasmiṃ ṭhāne ṭhitā hoti taṃ disvā āha-’tvampi amma pabbajituṃ sakkhissasī’ti, sādhu tātāti sampaṭicchi. Rājā putta na manaṃ labhitvā pahaṭṭhacitto bhikkhusaṅghaṃ upasaṅkamitvā bhante ime dārake pabbājetvā maṃ sāsane dāyādaṃ karothāti saṅgho rañño vacanaṃ sampaṭicchitvā kumāraṃ moggaliputta tissattherena upajjhāyena mahādevattherena ca ācariyena pabbajjāpesi. Majjhantikattherena ācariyena upasampādesi upasampadāmālakeyeva sahapaṭisambhidāhi arahattaṃ pāpuṇi.

When this was said, King Asoka, desiring the state of an heir in the Dispensation, saw Prince Mahinda standing nearby and said, "Dear son, are you able to go forth?" The prince, who by nature wished to go forth, was exceedingly joyful upon hearing the king's words and said, "I will go forth, Your Majesty. By having me ordained, may you become an heir in the Dispensation." At that time, the king's daughter Saṅghamittā was also standing in that place. Seeing her, he said, "You too, dear daughter, are you able to go forth?" She accepted, saying, "It is good, father." The king, having obtained the consent of his children, with a delighted mind approached the community of monks and said, "Venerable sirs, by ordaining these children, make me an heir in the Dispensation." The Saṅgha, accepting the king's words, had the prince ordained with the elder Moggaliputta Tissa as his preceptor and the elder Mahādeva as his teacher. He received the higher ordination with the elder Majjhantika as his teacher. In the very ordination hall, he attained arahantship together with the analytical knowledges.

Saṅghamittāyapi rājadhītāya ācariyā āyupālattheri nāma, upajjhāyā pana dhammapālattherī nāma ahosi. Atha mahiṇdatthero upasampanna kālatoppabhūti attano upajjhāyasseva santike dhammañca vinayañca pariyāpuṇanto dvepi saṅgitiyo āruḷhaṃ tipiṭaka saṅgahītaṃ sāṭṭhakathaṃ theravādaṃ tiṇṇaṃ vassānaṃ abbhantare uggahetvā attano upajjhāyassa antevāsikānaṃ sahassamattānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ pāmokkhaṃ ahosi.

For the royal daughter Saṅghamittā, her teacher was the elder nun named Āyupālā, and her preceptor was the elder nun named Dhammapālā. Then, from the time of his higher ordination, the elder Mahinda, while learning the Dhamma and Vinaya in the presence of his own preceptor, mastered within three years the Theravāda tradition—the Tipiṭaka collection with its commentaries, as established in the two Councils—and became the foremost among the thousand or so monks who were disciples of his preceptor.

Tena [O 0.0036] kho pana samayena moggaliputtatissatthero kattha nu kho anāgate sāsanaṃ suppatiṭṭhitaṃ gaveyyāti upaparikkhanto paccantimesu janapadesu suppatiṭṭhitaṃ bhavissatīti ñatvā tesaṃ tesaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ bhāraṃ katvā te te bhikkhu tattha tattha pesesi.

At that time, the elder Moggaliputtatissa, investigating, "Where, indeed, will the Dispensation be well-established in the future?", knew that it would be well-established in the border regions. Having assigned the responsibility to various monks, he sent those monks to those respective places.

Majjhantikattheraṃ kasmīra gaṇdhāra raṭṭhaṃpesesi tvaṃ etaṃ raṭṭhaṃ gantvā tattha sāsanaṃ patiṭṭhāpehīti mahādevattheraṃ tatheva vatvā mahiṃsakamaṇḍalaṃ pesesi. Rakkhitattheraṃvanavāsīṃ, yonakadhammarakkhitattheraṃ aparantakaṃ, mahādhammarakkhitattheraṃ mahāraṭṭhaṃ mahārakkhitattheraṃ yonaka lokaṃ. Majjhimattheraṃ himavantadesa bhāgaṃ, soṇattheraṃ-uttarattherañca suvaṇṇabhūmiṃ. Attano saddhivihārikaṃ mahiṇdattheraṃ iṭṭiyattherena uttiyattherena bhaddasālattherena sambalattherena ca saddhiṃ tambapaṇṇidīpaṃ gantvā ettha sāsanaṃ patiṭṭhāpethāti. Sabbepi taṃ taṃ disābhāgaṃ gacchantā attapañcamā agamiṃsu sabbepi therā gatagataṭṭhāne manusse pasādetvā sāsanaṃ patiṭṭhāpesuṃ.

He sent the elder Majjhantika to the country of Kasmīra and Gandhāra, saying, "Go to that country and establish the Dispensation there." Having said the same, he sent the elder Mahādeva to the Mahiṃsaka region. He sent the elder Rakkhita to Vanavāsī, the elder Yonaka Dhammarakkhita to Aparantaka, the elder Mahādhammarakkhita to Mahāraṭṭha, and the elder Mahārakkhita to the Yonaka country. He sent the elder Majjhima to the region of the Himālaya, and the elders Soṇa and Uttara to Suvaṇṇabhūmi. To his own disciple, the elder Mahinda, together with the elders Iṭṭiya, Uttiya, Bhaddasāla, and Sambala, he said, "Go to the island of Tambapaṇṇi and establish the Dispensation there." All of them, going to their respective regions, went as a group of five. All the elders, in every place they went, inspired faith in the people and established the Dispensation.

Mahiṇdatthero pana tambapaṇṇidīpaṃ gantvā sāsanaṃ patiṭṭhāpehīti upajjhāyena bhikkhusaṅghena ca ajjhiṭṭho kālo nu kho me tambapaṇṇidīpaṃ gantuṃ noti upadhārento muṭasīvarañño mahallakabhāvaṃ cintesi. Ayaṃ mahārājā mahallako, na sakkā imaṃ gaṇhitvā sāsanaṃ paggahetuṃ, idāni panassa putto devānampiyatisso rajjaṃ kāressati. Taṃ gaṇhitvā sakkā bhavissati sāsanaṃ paggahetuṃ, haṇda yāva so samayo āgacchati tāva ñātake olokema, puna’dāni imaṃ janapadaṃ āgaccheyyāma vā na vāti.

The elder Mahinda, having been requested by his preceptor and the community of monks to "go to the island of Tambapaṇṇi and establish the Dispensation," considered, "Is it now the right time for me to go to the island of Tambapaṇṇi, or not?" He reflected on King Muṭasīva's old age: "This great king is elderly; it is not possible to win his support and promote the Dispensation. But now his son, Devānampiyatissa, will rule the kingdom. By winning his support, it will be possible to promote the Dispensation. Well then, until that time comes, let us visit our relatives; we may or may not return to this country again."

So evaṃ cintetvā upajjhāyañca bhikkhusaṅghañca vanditvā asokārāmato nikkhamma tehi iṭṭiyādīhi catūhi therehi saṅghamittāya puttena sumanasāmaṇerena bhaṇḍukenaka ca upāsakena saddhiṃ rājagahanagara upavattake dakkhiṇa girijanapade cārikaṃ caramāno ñātake olokento cha māse atikkāmesi athānupubbena mātunivesanaṭṭhānaṃ veṭisa nagaraṃ nāma sampatto sampattañca pana theraṃ disvā theramātā devī pāde sirasā vanditvā bhikkhaṃ datvā theraṃ attanā kataṃ veṭisagiri vihāraṃ nāma āropesi.

Having reflected thus, he paid homage to his preceptor and the community of monks. Departing from the Asokārāma, accompanied by the four elders, Iṭṭiya and the others, along with Saṅghamittā's son, the novice Sumana, and the lay follower Bhaṇḍuka, he wandered on tour through the southern hill country in the vicinity of Rājagaha, visiting relatives. He passed six months in this way. Then, in due course, he arrived at the city named Veṭisa, the place of his mother’s residence. Seeing the elder upon his arrival, his mother, the queen, bowed with her head at his feet, gave him alms, and installed the elder in the monastery she had built, called the Veṭisagiri Vihāra.

Thero tasmiṃ vihāre nisanno cintesi amhākaṃ idha kattabba kiccaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ. Samayo nu kho idāni laṅkādipaṃ gantunti. Tato cintesi-anubhavatu tāva me pitarā pesitaṃ abhisekaṃ [O 0.0037] devānampiyatisso ratanattayaguṇañca suṇātu, chaṇatthañca nagarato nikkhamitvā missakapabbataṃ abhirūhatu tadā taṃ tattha dakkhissāmāti. Athāparaṃ ekamāsaṃ tattheva vāsaṃ kappesi.

The elder, seated in that monastery, reflected: "Our duty to be done here is finished. Is it now the time to go to the island of Laṅkā?" Then he thought: "First, let Devānampiyatissa experience the consecration sent by my father and hear the virtues of the Triple Gem. Let him also, for the festival, leave the city and ascend the Missaka Mountain. Then I will see him there." Thereafter, he spent another month in residence there.

Māsātikkame sakko devānamindo mahiṇdattheraṃ upasaṅkamitvā etadavoca.’ Kālakato bhante muṭasīvarājā, idāni devānampiyatissa rājā rajjaṃ kāreti. Sammāsambuddhena ca tumhe vyākatā anāgate mahiṇdo nāma bhikkhu tambapaṇṇidīpaṃ pasādessatī’ti. Tasmā tiha vo bhante kālo dīpavaraṃ gamanāya, ahampi vo sahāyo bhavissāmī.

When a month had passed, Sakka, the king of the devas, approached the elder Mahinda and said this: "Venerable sir, King Muṭasīva has passed away; now King Devānampiyatissa rules the kingdom. You have been prophesied by the Fully Enlightened One: 'In the future, a monk named Mahinda will inspire faith on the island of Tambapaṇṇi.' Therefore, venerable sir, it is time for you to go to that excellent island. I, too, will be your companion."

Thero tassa vacanaṃ sampaṭicchitvāattasattamo veṭisa pabbatavihārā vehāsaṃ uppatitva anurādhapurassa puratthimāya disāya missakapabbate patiṭṭhahi yaṃ etarahi cetiyapabbatotipi sañjānanti tassamiṃ divase tambapaṇṇidipe jeṭṭhamūla nakkhattaṃ nāma hoti rājā nakkhattaṃ ghosāpetvā chaṇaṃ karothāti amacce āṇāpetvā cattālīsa purisa sahassaparivāro nagarambhā nikkhamitvā yena missakapabbato tena pāyāsi migavaṃ kīḷitukāmo atha tasmiṃ pabbate adhivatthā devatā rañño there dassessāmīti rohita migarūpaṃ gahetvā avidūre tiṇapaṇṇāni khādamānā viya carati.

The Elder, accepting his words, rose into the air from the Veṭisa mountain monastery with six others, himself the seventh, and alighted on Missaka mountain to the east of Anurādhapura, which is now also known as Cetiya mountain. On that day in Tambapaṇṇidīpa, the Jeṭṭhamūla constellation was being observed. The king, having proclaimed a festival for the constellation and ordered his ministers, 'Prepare a celebration!', departed from the city. Surrounded by forty thousand men, he proceeded toward Missaka mountain, desiring to hunt deer. Then the deity dwelling on that mountain, thinking, 'I will show the Elder to the king,' took the form of a rohita deer and moved about nearby as if eating grass and leaves.

Rājā disvā ayuttaṃ dāni pamattaṃ vijjhitunti jīyaṃ poṭhesi. Mago ambatthalamaggaṃ gahetvā palāyituṃ ārabhi rājāpi piṭṭhito piṭṭhito anubaṇdhanto ambatthalameva āruhi. Migo therānaṃ avidūre antaradhāyi.

The king, seeing it, thought, 'It is not right to shoot a heedless creature,' and twanged his bowstring. The deer, taking the path to Ambatthala, began to flee, and the king, pursuing closely behind, ascended Ambatthala itself. The deer disappeared not far from the Elders.

Mahiṇdatthero rājānaṃ avidūre āgacchantaṃ’mamaṃyeva rājā passatu, mā itare’ti adhiṭṭhahitvā’tissa! Tissa! Ito ehī’ti āha. Rājā sutvā cintesi-imasmiṃ tambapaṇṇidīpe jāto maṃ tissoti nāmaṃ gahetvā ālapituṃ samattho nāma natthi. Ayampana chinnabhinnapaṭadharo bhaṇḍukāsāva vasano maṃ nāmena ālapati, ko nu kho ayaṃ bhavissati manusso vā amanusso vāti. Thero āha.

The Elder Mahinda, seeing the king approaching not far away, resolved, 'Let the king see only me, not the others,' and called out, 'Tissa! Tissa! Come here!' Hearing this, the king thought, 'In this island of Tambapaṇṇidīpa, there is no one able to address me by my given name, Tissa. Yet this shaven-headed man wearing torn and patched robes addresses me by name—who could this be, a human or a non-human?' The Elder said:

Samaṇā mayaṃ mahārāja - dhammarājassa sāvakā,

Taveva anukampāya - jambudīpā idhāgatāti;

'We are ascetics, great king, disciples of the King of Dhamma. Out of compassion for you, we have come here from Jambudīpa.'

Tena samayena devānampiyatissa rājā ca asokadhammarājā ca adiṭṭha sahāyakā honti. Devānampiyatissa raññe ca puññānubhāvena chātapabbatapāde ekasmiṃ veḷugumbe tisso veḷuyaṭṭhiyo nibbattiṃsu ekā latāyaṭṭhi nāma, ekā pupphayaṭṭhi nāma ekā sakuṇayaṭṭhi nāma. Tāsu latāyaṭṭhi sayaṃ rajatavaṇṇā hoti taṃ alaṅkaritvā uppannalatā kañcana vaṇṇā khāyati pupphayaṭṭhiyaṃ pana [O 0.0038] nīla-pīta-lohita-odāta-kāḷavaṇṇāni pupphāni sucibhattavaṇṭa patta kiñjakkhā hutvā khāyanti sakuṇayaṭṭhiyaṃ haṃsakukkuṭa-jīvaṃjīvakādayo sakuṇā nānāppakārāni ca catuppadāni sajīvāni viya khāyanti samuddatopissa muttā-maṇi-veḷuriyādi anekavihitaṃ ratanaṃ uppajji.

At that time, King Devānampiyatissa and King Asoka the Righteous were friends without having seen each other. By the power of King Devānampiyatissa’s merit, at the foot of Chāta Mountain, in a single bamboo clump, three bamboo stalks arose: one called the creeper stalk, one called the flower stalk, and one called the bird stalk. Among them, the creeper stalk was itself silver-colored, and the creepers that grew adorning it appeared golden-colored. On the flower stalk, flowers of blue, yellow, red, white, and black colors appeared, with pure stalks, petals, and filaments. On the bird stalk, birds such as swans, cocks, and jīvaṃjīvaka birds, as well as various kinds of quadrupeds, appeared as if alive. From the ocean also, various kinds of gems such as pearls, jewels, and lapis lazuli arose.

Tambapaṇṇiyaṃ pana aṭṭhamuttaṃ uppajjiṃsu-bhayamuttā gajamuttā rathamuttā āmalakamuttā calayamuttā aṅgulīveṭhakamuttā kakudhaphalamuttā pākatikamuttāti so tā ca yaṭṭhiyo tā ca muttāyo aññca bahuṃ ratanaṃ asokassa dhammarañño paṇṇākāratthāya pesesi. Asokopi pasīditvā pañca rājakakudhabhaṇḍāniceva aññe ca abhisekatthāya bahūpaṇṇākāre pahiṇi. Na kevalañca etaṃ āmisa paṇṇākāraṃ, imaṃ kira dhamma paṇṇākārampi pesesi.

In Tambapaṇṇī, eight kinds of pearls arose: the horse pearl, the elephant pearl, the chariot pearl, the myrobalan pearl, the bracelet pearl, the finger-ring pearl, the kakudha fruit pearl, and the natural pearl. He sent these stalks, these pearls, and many other gems as a gift to King Asoka the Righteous. Asoka, being pleased, in turn sent the five royal insignia and many other gifts for the consecration. Not only this material gift, but it is said he also sent this gift of Dhamma.

“Ahaṃ buddhañca dhammañca-saṅghañca saraṇaṃ gato,

Upāsakattaṃ vedesiṃ-sakyaputtassa sāsane,

Imesu tīsu vatthūsu-unnamesu naruttama,

Cittaṃ pasādayitvāna-saddhāya saraṇaṃ vajā’ti;”

“I have gone for refuge to the Buddha, the Dhamma, and the Sangha. I have declared myself a lay follower in the Dispensation of the Sakyan Son. In these three supreme objects, O best of men, having made your mind serene, go for refuge with faith.”

Rājā avirasutaṃ sāsanapavattiṃ anussaramāno therassa taṃ “samaṇāmayaṃ mahārāja dhammarājassa sāvakā“ti vacanaṃ sutvā ayyā nu kho āgatāti tāvadeva āvudhaṃ nikkhipitvā ekamantaṃ nisīdi sammodanīyaṃ kathaṃ kathayamāno, sammodanīyakathaṃ kurumāneyeva tasmiṃ tānipi cattālīsa purisasahassāni āgantvā taṃ parivāresuṃ. Tadā thero itarepi jane dassesi.

The king, recalling the recently heard news of the Dispensation and hearing the Elder’s words, 'We are ascetics, great king, disciples of the King of Dhamma,' thought, 'Have the noble ones arrived?' He immediately laid aside his weapon and sat to one side, engaging in courteous conversation. While he was still engaged in this courteous conversation, those forty thousand men also arrived and surrounded him. Then the Elder revealed the other monks to him.

Rājā disvā ime kadā āgatāti pucchi mayā saddhiṃyeva mahā rājāti. Idāni pana jambudīpe añññopi evarūpā samaṇā santīti. Mahārāja etarahi jambudīpo kāsāva pajjoto isivātaparivāto, tasmiṃ-

The king, seeing them, asked, “When did these arrive?” “Just now, great king, along with me.” “But now in Jambudīpa, are there other ascetics like this?” “Great king, at this time Jambudīpa is illuminated by the ochre robe, fanned by the breeze of the sages. In it—

“Te vijjā iddhipattā ca cetopariyāya kocidā,

Khīṇāsāva arahanto-bahū buddhassa sāvakāti”;

“They have attained the knowledges and psychic powers, are skilled in knowing the minds of others, Arahants whose taints are destroyed—many are the disciples of the Buddha.”

Atha rājā bhante sve rathaṃ pesessāmi. Taṃ abhirūhitvā āgaccheyyāthāti vatvā pakkākami thero acirapakkantassa rañño sumaṇasāmaṇeraṃ āmantesi ehi tvaṃ sumana dhammasavanakālaṃ ghosehīti sāmaṇero abhiññāpādakaṃ catutthajjhānaṃ samāpajjitvā vuṭṭhāya adhiṭṭhahitvā samāhitena cittena sakalatambapaṇṇidīpaṃ sācento dhammasavanakālaṃ ghosesi.

Then the king said, “Venerable sir, tomorrow I will send a chariot. Please come riding in it.” Having said this, he departed. Not long after the king had departed, the Elder addressed the novice Sumana, “Come, Sumana, announce the time for hearing the Dhamma.” The novice, having attained and emerged from the fourth jhāna, the basis of supernormal powers, and having made a determination with a concentrated mind, announced the time for hearing the Dhamma, making the entire island of Tambapaṇṇī resound.

Sāmaṇerassa saddaṃ sutvā bhummā devatā saddamanussāvesu, etenupāyena yāva brahmalokā saddo abbhuggañchi. Tena saddena [O 0.0039] mahādevatā sannipāto ahosi thero mahantaṃ devatā santipātaṃ disvā samacittasuttantaṃ kathesi. Kathā pariyosāne asaṅkheyyānaṃ devānaṃ dhammābhisamayo ahosi. Bahū nāga supaṇṇā ca saraṇesu patiṭṭhahiṃsu. Atha tassā rattiyā accayena rājā therānaṃ rathaṃ pesesi. Therā na mayaṃ rathaṃ āruhāma. Gaccha tvaṃ, pacchā mayaṃ āgacchissāmāti vatvā vehāsaṃ abbhuggantvā anurādhapurassa pacchimadisāya paṭhamaka cetiyaṭṭhāne otariṃsu.

Hearing the novice’s voice, the earth deities echoed the sound, and by this means, the sound ascended as far as the Brahma world. At that sound, a great assembly of deities took place. Seeing the vast gathering of deities, the Elder expounded the Samacittasutta. At the end of the discourse, countless deities attained understanding of the Dhamma. Many nāgas and supaṇṇas were established in the refuges. Then, when that night had passed, the king sent a chariot for the Elders. The Elders said, “We will not mount the chariot. You go ahead; we will come later.” Then, rising into the air, they descended at the site of the first cetiya to the west of Anurādhapura.

Rājāpi sārathiṃ pesetvā anto nivesane maṇḍapaṃ paṭiyādetvā cintesi’ nisīdissanti nu kho ayyā āsane na nisīdissantī“ti. Tassevaṃ cintayantasseva sārathi nagaradvāraṃ patvā addasa there paṭhamataraṃ āgantvā kāyabaṇdhanaṃ baṇdhitvā cīvaraṃ pārupante, disvā ativiya pasannamānaso hutvā āgantvā rañño ārocesi āgatā deva therāti.

The king, having sent his charioteer, had a pavilion prepared within the palace and thought, “Will the noble ones sit on the seats, or will they not sit?” While he was thinking thus, the charioteer, upon reaching the city gate, saw the Elders who had arrived earlier, fastening their waistbands and putting on their robes. Seeing this, his mind became exceedingly serene, and he returned to inform the king, saying, “The Elders have arrived, O king!”

Rājā rathaṃ āruḷhāti pucchi na ārūḷhā deva, api ca pacchato nikkhamitvā paṭhamataraṃ āgantvā pācīnadvāre ṭhitāti. Rājā rathaṃ na ārubhiṃsūtī sutvā tena hi bhaṇe bhummattharaṇa saṅkhepena āsanāni paññāpethāti vatvā paṭipathā āgamāsi amaccā pathaviyaṃ taṭṭikaṃ paññāpetvā upari kojavakādīni vicittattharaṇāni paññāpesuṃ rājāpi gantvā there vanditvā mahiṇdattherassa hatthato pattaṃ gahetvā mahatiyā pūjāya ca sakkārena ca there paṇītena khādanīyena bhojanīyena sahatthā santappetvā anulādevī pamukhā pañca itthisatāni therānaṃ abhivādanaṃ pūjāsakkārañca kāronatūti pakkosāpetvā ekamantaṃ nisīdi. Thero rañño saparijanassa dhammaratanavassaṃ vassento petavatthuṃ-vimānavatthuṃ-saccasaṃyuttañca kathesi. Taṃ sutvā tānipi pañca itthisatāni sotāpattiphalaṃ sacchikariṃsu.

The king asked, “Have they boarded the chariot?” “No, lord, but having departed from behind, they arrived first and are standing at the eastern gate.” Hearing that they had not boarded the chariot, he said, “Then, sirs, arrange seats quickly with ground coverings,” and went forth to meet them. The ministers spread mats on the ground and laid out ornate coverings such as kojavaka above them. The king also went, paid homage to the Elders, took the alms bowl from the hand of the Elder Mahinda, and with great offerings and honors, personally satisfied the Elders with excellent food and drink. Then, having summoned Queen Anulā, foremost among five hundred women, to pay respects and offer homage to the Elders, he sat to one side. The Elder, raining the jewel of the Dhamma upon the king and his retinue, expounded the Petavatthu, the Vimānavatthu, and the Saccasaṃyutta. Hearing this, those five hundred women also realized the fruit of stream-entry.

Tadā nāgarā therānaṃ guṇe sutvā there daṭṭhuṃ na labhāmāti upakkosanti atha rājā idha okāso natthīti cintetvā,’gacchatha bhaṇe hatthisālaṃ paṭijaggitvā vālukaṃ okiritvā pañcavaṇṇāni pupphāni vikiritvā vitānaṃ baṇdhitvā maṅgala hatthiṭṭhāne therānaṃ āsanāni paññāpethā’ti āha, amaccā tathā akaṃsu.

Then the townspeople, hearing of the virtues of the Elders, complained, thinking, “We cannot get to see the Elders.” Then the king, considering, “There is no opportunity here,” said, “Go, sirs, prepare the elephant hall, scatter sand, strew flowers of five colors, hang up a canopy, and set up seats for the Elders in the auspicious elephant place.” The ministers did accordingly.

Thero tattha gantvā nisīditvā devadūtasuttantaṃ kathesi kathā pariyosāne pāṇasahassaṃ sotāpattiphale patiṭṭhahi tathā hatthisālā sambādhāti dakkhiṇadvāre naṇdanuyyāne āsanaṃ paññāpesuṃ. Thero tattha nisīditvā āsivisopama suttantaṃ kathesi. Tampi sutvā pāṇasahassaṃ sotāpattiphalaṃ paṭilabhi evaṃ āgatadivasato [O 0.0040] dutiyadivase aḍḍhateyyānaṃ pāṇasahassānaṃ dhammābhisamayo ahosi.

The Elder went there, sat down, and expounded the Devadūta Sutta. At the end of the discourse, a thousand beings were established in the fruit of stream-entry. As the elephant hall was crowded, they prepared a seat at the southern gate in the Nandana Garden. The Elder sat there and expounded the Āsivisopama Sutta. Hearing this, another thousand beings attained the fruit of stream-entry. Thus, on the second day from the day of arrival, two and a half thousand beings realized the Dhamma.

Therassa naṇdanavane āgatāgatāhi kulitthīhi kulasuṇhāhi kulakumārīhi saddhi samodamānasseva sāyaṇhasamayo jāto, thero kālaṃ sallakkhetvā gacchāmi’dāni missaka pabbatanti uṭṭhahi. Amaccā mahāmeghavanuyyāne there vāsesuṃ rājāpi kho tassā rattiyā accayena therassa samīpaṃ gantvā sukhasayitabhāvaṃ pucchitvā kappati bhante bhikkhusaṅghassa ārāmoti pucchi. Thero kappati mahārājāti āha.

In the Nandana Garden, while the Elder was conversing with the noble women, daughters-in-law, and maidens who had come, evening approached. Perceiving the time, the Elder rose, thinking, “Now I will go to Missaka Mountain.” The ministers accommodated the Elder in the Mahāmeghavana Garden. The king, when that night had passed, approached the Elder, inquired about his comfortable rest, and asked, “Venerable sir, is a monastery suitable for the Bhikkhu Saṅgha?” The Elder replied, “It is suitable, great king.”

Rājā tuṭṭho suvaṇṇa bhiṃkāraṃ gahetvā therassa hatthe udakaṃ pātetvā mahāmeghavanuyyānaṃ adāsi thero punadivasepi rājageheyeva bhuñjitvā naṇdanavane anamataggiyāni kathesi. Puna divase aggikkhaṇdhopama suttantaṃ kathesi. Eteneva upāyena satta divasāni kathesi aḍḍha nacamānaṃ pāṇasahassānaṃ dhammābhisamayo ahosi. Sattame divase pana thero antopure rañño appamāda suttantaṃ kathayitvā cetiyagirimeva agamāsi.

Pleased, the king, taking a golden pitcher, poured water into the Elder’s hand and gave him the Mahāmeghavana Garden. The next day, after eating in the royal palace, the Elder expounded the Anamatagga Sutta in the Nandana Garden. On another day, he expounded the Aggikkhandhopama Sutta. In this way, he expounded for seven days, and eight and a half thousand beings realized the Dhamma. On the seventh day, the Elder, having expounded the Appamāda Sutta in the king’s inner palace, went to Cetiyagiri.

Atha kho rājā thero āyācito sayamevāgato, tasmā tassa anāpucchā gamanampi bhaveyyāti cintetvā rathaṃ abhirūhitvā, cetiyagiriṃ agamāsi mahatā rājānubhāvena gantvā therānaṃ santikaṃ upasaṅkamanto ativiya kilantarūpo hutvā upasaṅkami. Tato naṃ thero āha kasmā tvaṃ mahārāja evaṃ kilama māno āgatoti. Tumhe mama gāḷhaṃ ovādaṃ datvā idāni gantukāmā nukhoti jānanatthaṃ bhanteti. Na mayaṃ mahārāja gantukāmā, api ca vassupanāyika kālonamāyaṃ samaṇena nāma vassupanāyikaṃ ṭhānaṃ ñātuṃ vaṭṭatīti. Rājāpi kho taṃkhaṇaṃyeva karaṇḍaka cetiyaṅgaṇaṃ parikkhipitvā aṭṭhasaṭṭhiyā leṇesu kammaṃ paṭṭhapetvā nagarameva agamāsi.

Then the king, thinking, “The Elder, though invited, came of his own accord; therefore, he might depart without asking,” mounted his chariot and went to Cetiyagiri. Arriving with great royal pomp, he approached the Elders, appearing very weary. Then the Elder said to him, “Great king, why have you come here so exhausted?” “Venerable sir, I came to know whether you, having given me firm advice, now wish to depart,” he replied. “Great king, we do not wish to depart; however, the time of entering the rains is near, and it is fitting for a monk to know the place for entering the rains.” The king, at that very moment, had the courtyard of the Karaṇḍaka Cetiya enclosed, and having arranged for work to begin in sixty-eight caves, returned to the city.

Tepi thero mahājanaṃ ovadamānā cetiyagirimhi vassaṃ vasiṃsu. Athāyasmā mahāmahiṇdo vutthavasso pavāretvā kattikapuṇṇamāyaṃ uposathadivase rājānaṃ etadavoca ciradiṭṭho no mahārāja sammāsambuddho abhivādana paccupaṭṭhāna añjali kamma sāmīci kamma karaṇaṭṭhānaṃ natthi, tenamhaṃ ukkaṇṭhitāti tanu bhante tumhe avocuttha parinibbuto sammāsambuddhoti kiñcāpi mahārāja parinibbuto, athassa sarīradhātuyo tiṭṭhantīti. Aññā tambhante thūpaṃ patiṭṭhāpemi bhūmibhāgaṃ vicinathāti. Api ca dhātuyo kuto lacchāmīti sumanena saddhiṃ mantehi mahārājāti rājā sumanaṃ upasaṅkamitvā pucchi’kutodāni bhantedhātuyo lacchāmā’ti sumano [O 0.0041] āha-appossukko tvaṃ mahārāja, vīthiyo sodhāpetvā dhaja patāka puṇṇaghaṭādīhi alaṅkārāpetvā saparijjano uposathaṃ samādiyitvā sabbatālāvacare upaṭṭhapetvā maṅgalahatthiṃ sabbālaṅkārehi patimaṇḍitaṃ kāretvā upari vassa setacchattaṃ ussāpetvā sāyaṇhasamaye mahānāgavanuyyānābhimukho yā hi addhā tasmiṃ ṭhāne dhātuyo lacchasiti, rājā sādhūti sampaṭicchi. Thero cetiyagirimeva agamiṃsu.

Those elders, instructing the people, spent the rains retreat at Cetiyagiri. Then the Venerable Mahinda, having completed the rains retreat and observed the Pavāraṇā, addressed the king on the Uposatha day of the Kattika full moon: "O great king, we have not seen the Perfectly Enlightened One for a long time. There is no opportunity for us to pay respects, attend upon him, offer salutations, or perform proper duties. Therefore, we are disheartened." The king replied, "But, venerable sir, did you not say that the Perfectly Enlightened One has attained Parinibbāna?" "Though the Perfectly Enlightened One has attained Parinibbāna, O great king, his bodily relics still remain." The king said, "Very well, venerable sir, I will establish a stupa. Please select a suitable site. But from where shall we obtain the relics?" The Elder replied, "Consult with Sumana, O great king." The king approached Sumana and asked, "Venerable sir, from where can we now obtain the relics?" Sumana replied, "Do not worry, great king. Have the streets cleaned, have them adorned with banners, flags, and full pots, observe the Uposatha with your retinue, arrange all kinds of musical performances, have a royal elephant decorated with all ornaments, raise a white umbrella of state above it, and in the evening, proceed toward the Mahānāga Garden. You will certainly obtain the relics in that place." The king agreed, saying, "Very well." The elders returned to Cetiyagiri.

Tatrāyasmā mahiṇdatthero sumana sāmaṇeramāha- gaccha tvaṃ sāmaṇera jambudīpe ayyakaṃ asokadhammarājānaṃ upasaṅkamitvā mama vacanena evaṃ vadehi’sahāyo te mahārāja devānampiya tisso buddhasāsane pasanno thūpaṃ patiṭṭhāpetukāmo tumhākaṃ kira hatthe bhagavato paribhuttapatto cevadhātu ca atthi, tamme dethā’ti taṃ gahetvā sakkaṃ devarājānaṃ upasaṅkamitvā tumhākaṃ kira mahārāja hatthe dve dhātuyo atthi dakkhiṇadāṭhā ca dakkhiṇakkhakañca, tatotumhe dakkhiṇadāṭhaṃpūjetha, dakkhiṇakkhakaṃ pana mayhaṃ dethāti, evañca naṃ vadehi kasmā tvaṃ mahārāja amhe tambapaṇṇidīpaṃ pahiṇitvā pamajjitthāti.

There the Venerable Mahinda Thera addressed the novice Sumana: "Go, novice, to Jambudīpa, approach your grandfather, King Asoka the Righteous, and say to him on my behalf: 'Your friend, King Devānampiya Tissa, is devoted to the Buddha's Dispensation and wishes to establish a stupa. It is said that in your possession are the almsbowl used by the Blessed One and a relic. Please give them to me.' Having taken them, approach Sakka, the King of Devas, and say to him: 'O Great King, it is said that in your possession are two relics—the right tooth-relic and the right collarbone relic. Therefore, you should worship the right tooth-relic, but give the right collarbone relic to me.' And say this to him also: 'Why, O Great King, having sent us to the island of Tambapaṇṇi, have you been neglectful?'"

Sādhu bhanteti kho sumano therassa vacanaṃ sampaṭicchitvā tāvadeva pattacīvaramādāya vehāsamabbhuggantvā pāṭaliputtadvāre oruyha rañño santikaṃ gantvā tamatthaṃ ārocesi rājā tuṭṭho sāmaṇerassa hatthato pattaṃ gahetvā bhojetvā bhagavato pattaṃ gaṇdhehi ubbaṭṭetvā varamuttasadisānaṃ dhātūnaṃ pūretvā adāsi.

"Very well, venerable sir," said Sumana, accepting the elder's words. At that very moment, taking his bowl and robe, he rose into the sky, descended at the gate of Pāṭaliputta, went to the king, and informed him of the matter. The king, pleased, took the bowl from the novice's hand, fed him, anointed the Blessed One's bowl with perfumes, filled it with relics resembling excellent pearls, and gave it.

So tuṃ gahetvā sakkaṃ devarājānaṃ upasaṅkami sakko devārājā sāmaṇeraṃ disvā kiṃ bhante sumana āhiṇḍasīti āha. Tvaṃ mahārāja amhe tambapaṇṇidīpaṃ pahiṇitvā kasmā pamajjasīti, tappamajjāmi bhante, vadehi kiṃ karomīti. Tumhākaṃ kira hatthe dve dhātuyo atthi dakkhiṇadāṭhā ca dakkhiṇakkhakañca, tato tumhe dakkhiṇadāṭhaṃ pūjetha dakkhiṇakkhakaṃ pana mayhaṃ dethāti. Sādhu bhanteti kho sakko devānamiṇdo yojanappamānaṃ maṇithūpaṃ ugghāṭetvā dakkhiṇakkhakaṃ nīharitvā sumanassa adāsi.

Taking it, he approached Sakka, the King of Devas. Sakka, seeing the novice, said, "Why, Venerable Sumana, do you wander about?" He replied, "O Great King, having sent us to the island of Tambapaṇṇi, why are you neglectful?" "I have been neglectful, venerable sir. Speak, what shall I do?" "It is said that you have in your possession two relics—the right tooth-relic and the right collarbone relic. Therefore, you should worship the right tooth-relic, but give the right collarbone relic to me." "Very well, venerable sir," said Sakka, the Lord of Devas. He then opened a jeweled shrine a yojana in size, extracted the right collarbone relic, and gave it to Sumana.

So taṃ gahetvā cetiyagirimhiyeva patiṭṭhāsi atha kho mahiṇdapamukhā sabbe te mahānāgā asokadhammarājena dinna dhātuyo cetiyagiriyamhiyeva patiṭṭhāpetvā dakkhiṇakkhakaṃ ādāya vaḍḍhamānakacchāyāya mahānāga vanuyyānamagamaṃsu. Rājāpi kho sumanena vuttappakāraṃ pūjāsakkāraṃ katvā hatthikkhaṇdhavaragato sāyaṃ maṅgalahatthimatthake setacchattaṃ dhārayamāno mahānāga vanuyyānaṃ sampāpuṇi.

Taking it, he arrived at Cetiyagiri. Then all those great elders, headed by Mahinda, having enshrined the relics given by King Asoka the Righteous on Cetiyagiri, took the right collarbone relic and proceeded to the Mahānāga Park as the shadows lengthened. The king, too, having performed the worship and honors as instructed by Sumana, mounted his excellent elephant, and in the evening, bearing a white umbrella of state on the head of the auspicious elephant, arrived at the Mahānāga Park.

Athassa [O 0.0042] etadahosi- sace ayaṃ sammāsambuddhassa dhātu, chattaṃ apanamatu, maṅgalahatthi jaṇṇukehi bhūmiyaṃ patiṭṭhāhatu, dhātu caṅgoṭakaṃ mayhaṃ matthake patiṭṭhahatūti saha rañño cittuppādena chattaṃ apanami hatthijaṇṇukehi patiṭṭhahi dhātucaṅgoṭakaṃ rañño matthake patiṭṭhahi rājā amatenevābhisittagatto paramena pītipāmojjena samannāgato hutvā pucchi dhātuṃ bhante kiṃ karomīti. Hatthikumbhiyeva tāva mahārāja ṭhapehīti. Rājā dhātuvaṅgoṭakaṃ hatthikumbhe ṭhapesi. Pamudito nāgo kuñcanādaṃ nadi mahāmegho uṭṭhahitvā pokkharavassaṃ vasisi ukadapariyantaṃ katvā mahābhūmicālo ahosi paccantepi nāma sammāsambuddhassa dhātuyo patiṭṭhahissantīti.

Then it occurred to him: "If this is indeed a relic of the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One, may the umbrella of state lower itself, may the auspicious elephant kneel down on the ground with its knees, and may the relic casket rest upon my head." No sooner had the king thought this than the umbrella of state lowered itself, the elephant knelt down on the ground with its knees, and the relic casket rested upon the king's head. The king, feeling as though his body had been anointed with the nectar of immortality, was filled with supreme joy and delight, and asked, "Venerable sir, what should I do with the relic?" "For now, great king, place it on the elephant's forehead." The king placed the relic casket on the elephant's forehead. The delighted elephant trumpeted; a great cloud arose and rained down a shower of lotuses, making the ground watery up to its edge; a great earthquake occurred, as if to proclaim that relics of the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One would be enshrined even in the borderlands.

Atha so hatthināgo aneka tālāvacaraparivuto ativiya uḷārena pūjāsakkārena sakkariyamāno pacchimadisābhimukho hutvā apasakkanto yāva nagarassa puratthima dvāraṃ tāva gantvā puratthimena dvārena nagaraṃ pavisitvā sakalanagare uḷārāya pūjāya kayirāmānāya dakkhiṇadvārena nikkhamitvā thūpārāmassa pacchimadisābhāge pabhejavatthu nāma kira atthi tattha gantvā puna thūpārāmābhimukho eva paṭinivatti. So ca purimakānaṃ tiṇṇaṃ sammāsambuddhānaṃ dhammakarakaṃ kāyabaṇdhanaṃ udakasāṭīkaṃ patiṭṭhāpetvā katacetiyaṭṭhānaṃ hotī. Tadetaṃ vinaṭṭhesupi cetiyesu devatānubhāvena kaṇṭaka samākiṇṇasākhāhi nānāgacchehi parivutaṃ tiṭṭhati. Mā naṃ koci ucciṭṭhā suvimala kacavarehi sandusesīti.

Then that great elephant, surrounded by many musicians and being highly honored with great offerings and respect, turned toward the western direction and moved away. Going as far as the eastern gate of the city, he entered the city through the eastern gate, and while grand offerings were being made throughout the entire city, he exited through the southern gate. In the western part of the Thūpārāma, there is said to be a place called Pabhejavatthu. Having gone there, he turned back toward the Thūpārāma. That was the place where a cetiya had been made by enshrining the water-strainer, body-band, and bathing-robe of the three previous Perfectly Self-Enlightened Buddhas. Even though those cetiyas had vanished, by the power of the deities, the place remained surrounded by thorny branches and various thickets, lest anyone defile it with leftover food or other unclean refuse.

Atha tassa hatthino purato gantvā rājapurisā sabba gacche chiṇditvā bhūmiṃ sodhetvā taṃ hatthatalasadisaṃ akaṃsu. Hatthināgo gantvā taṃ ṭhānaṃ purato katvā tassa pacchimadisābhāge bodhirukkhaṭṭhane aṭṭhāsi. Athassa matthakato dhātuṃ oropetuṃ ārabhiṃsu nāgo oropetuṃ na deti. Theraṃ pucchi, kasmā bhante nāgo dhātuṃ oropetuṃ na detīti

Then, the king's men went ahead of the elephant, cut down all the bushes, cleared the ground, and made it as smooth as the palm of a hand. The great elephant went forward, placed that spot before him, and stood at the location of the Bodhi tree on its western side. Then, they began to lower the relic from its head, but the elephant would not allow it to be lowered. They asked the elder, "Venerable sir, why does the elephant not allow the relic to be lowered?"

Āruḷhaṃ mahārāja oropetuṃ na vaṭṭatiti. Tasmiñca kāle abhayavāpiyā udakaṃ jinnaṃ hoti samantā bhūmi phalitā suuddharā mattikapiṇḍā. Tato mahājano sīghasīghaṃ mattikaṃ āharitvā hatthakumbhappamāṇaṃ vatthumakāsi. Tāvadeva thūpakaraṇatthaṃ iṭṭhikā kātuṃ ārabhiṃsu. Yāva iṭṭhikā pariniṭṭhanti tāva hatthināgo katipāhaṃ divā bodhirukkhaṭṭhāne hatthisālāyaṃ tiṭṭhati. Rattiyaṃ thūpapatiṭṭhāna bhūmiyaṃ pariyāyati.

"Great king, it is not proper to bring down one who has ascended." At that time, the water in the Abhaya tank had receded, the surrounding ground was cracked all over, and lumps of earth were easily extracted. Then, the people quickly brought earth and made a foundation the size of an elephant's frontal globe. Immediately, they began making bricks for the construction of the stupa. While the bricks were being finished, the great elephant stood for some days in the elephant stable at the location of the Bodhi tree during the day. At night, it would walk around the ground where the stupa was to be established.

Atha vatthuṃ vināpetvā rājā theraṃ pucchi, kīdiso bhante thūpo kātabboti. Vīhirāsi sadiso mahārājāti, sādhu bhanteti rājā jaṅghappamāṇaṃ [O 0.0043] thūpaṃ cināpetvā dhātuoropanatthāya mahāsakkāraṃ kāresi.

Then, after having the site prepared, the king asked the elder, "Venerable sir, what kind of stupa should be built?" "Like a heap of paddy, great king." "Very well, venerable sir," said the king, and he had a stupa built to the height of a man's lower leg. For the enshrinement of the relic, he arranged great honors.

Tato sakalanāgarā ca jānapadā ca dhātumha dassanatthaṃ santipatiṃsu sannipatite ca tasmiṃ mahājane dasabalassa dhātu hatthikumbhato sattatālappamānaṃ vehāsamabbhuggantvā yamakapāṭihāriyaṃ dassesi. Tehi tehi dhātuppadesehi chabbaṇṇaraṃsiyo udakadharā ca aggikkhaṇdhā ca pavattanti. Sāvatthiyaṃ gaṇḍambamūle bhagavatā dassita pāṭihāriya sadisaṃ eva pāṭihāriyaṃ ahosi. Tañca kho neva therānubhāvenana devatānubhāvena, api ca kho buddhānaṃyeva ānubhāvena bhagavā kira dharamānova adhiṭṭhāsi - tambapaṇṇidīpe anurādhapurassa dakkhiṇadisābhāge purimakānaṃ tiṇṇaṃ buddhānaṃ cetiyaṭṭhāne mama dakkhiṇakkhakadhātu patiṭṭhāna divase yamakapāṭihāriyaṃ hotūti.

Thereupon, all the townspeople and country-folk gathered to see the relic. When the great crowd had assembled, the relic of the Ten-Powered One rose seven tālas high into the sky from the elephant's forehead and displayed the Twin Miracle. From the various positions of the relic, six-colored rays, streams of water, and masses of fire issued forth. The miracle was just like the one shown by the Blessed One at the foot of the Gandamba tree in Sāvatthī. And this was not due to the elder's power, nor the deities' power, but solely due to the power of the Buddhas. It is said that the Blessed One, while still living, had resolved: "On the day my right collarbone relic is enshrined in the place of the cetiyas of the three previous Buddhas in the southern part of Anurādhapura on Tambapaṇṇi Island, may the Twin Miracle occur."

Evaṃ acintiyā buddhā-buddhadhammā acintiyā,

Acintiyesu pasannānaṃ-vipāko hoti acintiyoti;

Thus, Buddhas are inconceivable, and the qualities of a Buddha are inconceivable. For those who have faith in the inconceivable, the result is inconceivable.

Dhātusarīrato nikkhanta udakaphusitehi sakalehi tambapaṇṇidīpatale na koci aphuṭṭhokāso nāma ahosi. Evamassa taṃ dhātu sarīraṃ udakaphusitehi tambapaṇṇitthalassa paridāhaṃ vūpasametvā mahājanassa pāṭihāriyaṃ dassetvā otaritvā rañño matthake patiṭṭhāsi rājā saphalaṃ manussattapaṭilābhaṃ maññamāno mahantaṃ sakkāraṃ katvā dhātuṃ patiṭṭhāpesi. Saha dhātupatiṭṭhānena mahābhūmicālo ahosi niṭṭhite pana thūpe rājā ca rājā bhātikā ca deviyo ca de-nāga-yakkhānaṃ vimbhayakaraṃ paccekaṃ thūpamakaṃsu.

By the drops of water that issued from the relic-body, there was no spot on the entire surface of the island of Tambapaṇṇi that remained untouched. In this way, that relic-body, having calmed the burning heat of the land of Tambapaṇṇi with those drops of water and displayed a miracle to the great multitude, descended and settled upon the king’s head. The king, considering his attainment of a human birth to be fruitful, performed great veneration and enshrined the relic. With the enshrinement of the relic, a great earthquake occurred. When the stupa was completed, the king, the king's brother, and the queens each made a stupa, causing wonder to devas, nāgas, and yakkhas.

Evaṃ jino dhātusarīrakena

Gatopi santiṃ janatāhitañca,

Sukhañca dhammā bahudhā kareyya

Ṭhito hi nāthonukaraṃ kareyya;

Thus the Victor, though having gone to peace, would, by means of his relic-body, bring about in many ways the welfare and happiness of the people through the Dhamma. For the Master, though so established, would act in a corresponding manner.

Sādhujana manopasādanatthāya kate thūpavaṃse thūpārāma kathā,

Here ends the account of the Thūpārāma in the Thūpavaṃsa, composed for the gladdening of the minds of virtuous people.

13. Niṭṭhitāya pana dhātupūjāya patiṭṭhite dhātuvare mahiṇdatthero mahāmeghavanuyyānameva gantvāvāsaṃ kappesi. Tasmiṃ kho pana samaye anulādevī pabbajitukāmā hutvā rañño ārocesi. Rājā tassā vacanaṃ sutvā theraṃ etadavoca? Bhante anulādevī pabbajitukāmā, pabbājetha nanta na mahārāja amhākaṃ mātugāmaṃ pabbājetuṃ kappati. Pāṭaliputte pana mayhaṃ bhagini saṅghamittā therī nāma atthi, taṃ pakkosāpehi mahārāja, imasmiñca dīpe purimakānañca [O 0.0044] tiṇṇaṃ sammāsambuddhānaṃ bodhi patiṭṭhaṃsi amhākampi bhagavato sarasa raṃsijāla vissajjanakena bodhinā patiṭṭhātabbaṃ, tasmā sāsanaṃ pahiṇeyyasi yathā saṅghamittā bodhiṃ gahetvā āgaccheyyāti.

13. When the veneration of the relic was completed and the excellent relic was enshrined, the Elder Mahinda went to the Mahāmeghavana park and took up residence there. Now at that time, Queen Anulā, desiring to go forth, informed the king. The king, having heard her words, said this to the Elder: “Venerable sir, Queen Anulā wishes to go forth; please ordain her.” “No, great king, it is not proper for us to ordain women. However, in Pāṭaliputta there is my sister, the Therī named Saṅghamittā; have her summoned, great king. Moreover, on this island the Bodhi trees of the three previous Perfectly Self-Enlightened Ones were established; and for us too, the Bodhi tree of our Blessed One, which emits a network of delightful rays, should be established. Therefore, you should send a message so that Saṅghamittā may come bringing the Bodhi tree.”

Rājā therassa vacanaṃ sampaṭicchitvā amaccehi saddhiṃ mantento ariṭṭhaṃ nāma attano bhāgineyyaṃ āha sakkhissasi tvaṃ tāta pāṭalīputtaṃ gantvā mahābodhinā saddhiṃ ayyaṃ saṅghamittattheriṃ ānetunti sakkhissāmi deva sace me pabbajjaṃ anujānissasīti.

The king, having accepted the Elder’s words and consulting with his ministers, said to his nephew named Ariṭṭha: “My dear, will you be able to go to Pāṭaliputta and bring the venerable Therī Saṅghamittā together with the Great Bodhi Tree?” “I will be able to, Your Majesty, if you permit me to go forth.”

Gaccha tāta theriṃ ānetvā pabbajjāhīti. So rañño ca therissa ca sāsanaṃ gahetvā therassa adhiṭṭhānavasena ekadivase na jambukolapaṭṭanaṃ gantvā nāvaṃ abhirūhitva samuddaṃ atikkamitvā pāṭalīputtaṃ gantvā rañño sāsanaṃ ācikkhi-putto te deva mahiṇdathero evamāha. Sahāyassa kira te devānampiyatissassa bhātujāyā anulādevī nāma pabbajitukāmā, taṃ pabbājetuṃ ayyaṃ saṅghamittattheriṃ pahiṇeyyātha, ayyāya eva ca saddhiṃ mahābodhinti.

“Go, my dear; bring the Therī and then go forth.” He, having taken the message of the king and the Elder, by the Elder’s power of determination, went to Jambukolapaṭṭana in a single day, boarded a ship, crossed the ocean, and reached Pāṭaliputta. He conveyed the message to the king: “Your son, the Elder Mahinda, says this, O king: ‘It seems your friend Devānampiyatissa’s sister-in-law, named Queen Anulā, wishes to go forth. You should send the venerable Therī Saṅghamittā to ordain her, and with the venerable one, the Great Bodhi Tree.’”

Therassa sāsanaṃ ārocetvā saṅghamittattheriṃ upasaṅkamitvā evamāha. Ayye tumhākaṃ bhātā mahiṇdatthero maṃ tumhākaṃ santikaṃ pesesi devānampiyatissa rañño bhātujāyā anulādevī nāma pañcahi kaññāsatehi pañcahi ca antepurisā satehi saddhiṃ pabbajitukāmāti, taṃ kira āgantvā pabbājethāti.

Having announced the Thera’s message, he approached the Theri Saṅghamittā and said this: “Venerable lady, your brother, the Thera Mahinda, has sent me to you, saying: ‘King Devānampiyatissa’s sister-in-law, named Princess Anulā, wishes to go forth with five hundred maidens and five hundred palace women. Please come and ordain them.’”

Sā tāva deva turita turitā gantvā raññā tैmatthaṃ ārocetvā gacchāmahaṃ mahārāja tambapaṇṇidīpanti āha.

She, having quickly gone to the king and informed him of this matter, said, “I am going, great king, to the island of Tambapaṇṇi.”

Tena hi amma mahābodhiṃ gahetvā gacchāhīti vatvā pāṭalīputtato yāva mahābodhi tāva maggaṃ paṭijaggāpetvā sattayojanāyāmaya tiyojana vitthatāya mahatiyā senāya pāṭaliputtato nikkhamitvā ariyasaṅghaṃ ādāya mahābodhisamīpaṃ agamāsi.

“Then, mother, take the Great Bodhi Tree and go,” he said, and had the road prepared from Pāṭaliputta up to the Great Bodhi Tree. Then, taking the noble Saṅgha, he set out from Pāṭaliputta with a vast army, seven yojanas in length and three yojanas in width, and went to the vicinity of the Great Bodhi Tree.

Senāya samussita dhajapatākaṃ nānāratana vicittaṃ anekālaṅkāra patimaṇḍitaṃ nānāvidha kusuma samākiṇṇaṃ aneka turiya saṃghraṭṭhaṃ mahābodhiṃ parikkhipi tato rājā puppha-gaṇdha-mālādīhi pūjetvā tikkhattuṃ padakkhiṇaṃ katvā aṭṭhasu ṭhānesu vanditvā uṭṭhāya añjalimpaggayha ṭhatvā saccavacanakiriyāya bodhiṃ gaṇhitukāmo ratanapīṭhaṃ āruyha tulikaṃ gahetvā manosilāya lekhaṃ katvā yadi mahābodhinā laṅkādīpe patiṭṭhātabbaṃ, yadi cāhaṃ buddhasāsane nibbematiko bhaveyyaṃ, mahābodhi sayameva imasmiṃ suvaṇṇakaṭāhe patiṭṭhahatūti saccakiriyamakāsi saha saccakiriyāya [O 0.0045] bodhisākhā manosilāya paricchinnaṭṭhānehi chiṇditvā gaṇdha kalala pūrassa suvaṇṇakaṭāhassa upari aṭṭhāsi.

The army, with raised banners and flags, adorned with various jewels and decorations, strewn with many kinds of flowers, and resounding with numerous musical instruments, encircled the Great Bodhi Tree. Then the king, having worshipped it with flowers, perfumes, and garlands, circumambulated it three times, paid homage at eight places, stood up with hands reverently joined, and, desiring to take the Bodhi Tree by a solemn declaration of truth, ascended the jeweled seat, took a fine brush, and made a mark with red arsenic. He made a solemn declaration of truth: “If the Great Bodhi Tree is to be established in the island of Laṅkā, and if I am without doubt concerning the Buddha’s Dispensation, may the Great Bodhi Tree itself be established in this golden pot!” Simultaneously with this solemn declaration, the branch of the Bodhi Tree severed itself at the places marked with red arsenic and stood upon the golden pot filled with fragrant paste.

Tato rājā mahābodhiṃ bodhimaṇḍato mahantena sakkārena pāṭalīputtaṃ ānetvā sabbaparihārāni datvā mahābodhiṃ gaṅgāya nāvaṃ āropetvā sayampi nagarato nikkhamitvā viñjhāṭaviṃ samatikkamma anupubbena sattadivasehi tāmalittiṃ anuppatto antaramagge deva-nāga-manussā uḷāraṃ mahābodhi pūjamakaṃsu.

Then the king, with great veneration, brought the Great Bodhi Tree from the Bodhimaṇḍala to Pāṭaliputta, gave all honors, placed the Great Bodhi Tree on a ship on the Ganges, and himself, departing from the city and crossing the Viñjhā forest, gradually arrived at Tāmalitti in seven days. Along the way, gods, nāgas, and humans made magnificent offerings to the Great Bodhi Tree.

Rājāpi samuddatīre sattadivasāni mahābodhiṃ ṭhapetvā mahantaṃ sakkāraṃ katvā bodhimpi saṃṅghamittattherimpi saparivāraṃ nāvaṃ āropetvā gacchati vatare dasabalassa sarasa raṃsijālā muñcamāno mahābodhi rukkhoti kaṇditvā añjalimpaggahetvā assūni pavattayamāno aṭṭhāsi sāpi kho mahābodhi samarūḷhanāvā passato passato mahārājassa mahāsamuddatalaṃ pakkhaṇdi mahāsamuddepi samantā yojanaṃ vīci vūpasantā pañcavaṇṇāni padumāni pupphitāni antalikkhe dibbaturiyāni vajjiṃsu ākāse jala thala sannissitāhi devatāhi pavattitā ativiya uḷāra pūjā ahosi. Evaṃ mahatiyā pūjāya sā nāvā jambukolapaṭṭanaṃ pāvisi.

The king, having kept the Great Bodhi Tree on the seashore for seven days and paid great veneration, placed the Bodhi Tree, along with the Theri Saṅghamittā and her retinue, on the ship. As it departed, the king, with hands clasped in reverence, stood weeping and exclaiming, “Oh, the Great Bodhi Tree, emitting a network of delightful rays like the Ten-Powered One!” Indeed, that Great Bodhi Tree, having boarded the ship, rushed into the great ocean before the very eyes of the great king. For a yojana around in the great ocean, the waves subsided, five-colored lotuses bloomed, and divine instruments resounded in the sky. An exceedingly magnificent worship, performed by deities dwelling in the sky, on water, and on land, took place. Thus, amid such great veneration, that ship entered the port of Jambukolapaṭṭana.

Devānampiyatissa mahārājāpi uttaradvārato paṭṭhāya yāva jambukolapaṭṭanā maggaṃ sodhāpetvā alaṅkārāpetvā nagarato nikkhamana divase uttaradvāra samīpe saddasālāvatthusmiṃ ṭhito tāya vibhūtiyā mahāsamudde āgacchantaṃyeva mahābodhiṃ therassānubhāvena disvā tuṭṭhamānaso nikkhamitvā sabbaṃ maggaṃ pañcavaṇṇehi pupphehi okiranto antarantarā pupphaagghiyāni ṭhapento ekāheneva jambukolapaṭṭanaṃ gantvā sabbatālāvacara parivuto puppha dhūpagaṇdhādīhi pūjayamāno galappamaṃnaṃ udakaṃ oruyha āgato vata redasabalassa sarasa raṃsijālaṃ vissajjanako bodhirukkhoti pasannacitto mahābodhiṃ ukkhipitvā uttamaṅge sirasmiṃ patiṭṭhapetvā mahābodhiṃ parivāretvā āgatehi soḷasahi jātisampannakulehi saddhiṃ samuddato paccuttaritvā samuddatīre bodhiṃ ṭhapetvā tīṇi divasāni sakala tambapaṇṇi rajjena pūjesi.

King Devānampiyatissa, having had the road cleared and adorned from the northern gate up to the port of Jambukola, stood near the northern gate at the place called Saddasālā on the day of his departure from the city. There, through the Thera’s power, he saw the Great Bodhi Tree arriving on the great ocean with such splendor. With a joyful mind, he went forth, scattering the entire road with flowers of five colors, setting up flower-offerings at intervals, and reached the port of Jambukola in a single day. Surrounded by all the Tālavacara, he worshipped with flowers, incense, and fragrances. Descending into the water up to his neck, with a serene mind he said, “Indeed, this is the Bodhi Tree, emitting a network of delightful rays like the Ten-Powered One!” Lifting up the Great Bodhi Tree, he placed it upon his head, the highest part of his body. Together with the sixteen families of noble birth who had come to escort the Great Bodhi Tree, he brought it ashore from the ocean. After placing the Bodhi Tree on the shore, he honored it for three days with the entire kingdom of Tambapaṇṇi.

Atha catutthe divase mahābodhiṃ ādāya uḷāraṃ pūjaṃ kurumāno anupubbena anurādhapuraṃ sampatte anurādhapurepi mahāsakkāraṃ katvā cātuddasī divase vaḍḍhamānakacchāyāya mahābodhiṃ uttaradvārena pavesetvā nagaramajjhana atiharitvā dakkhiṇadvārena nikkhamitvā dakkhiṇadvārato pañcadhanu satike ṭhāne yattha amhākaṃ [O 0.0046] sammāsambuddho nirodha samāpattiṃ samāpajjitvā nisīdi. Purimakā ca tayo sammāsambuddhā samāpattiṃ appetvā nisīdiṃsu. Yattha ca kakusaṇdhassa bhagavato sirīsabodhi, konāgamanassa bhagavato udumbarabodhi, kassapassa bhagavato nigrodhabodhi, patiṭṭhāpesi -tasmiṃ mahāmeghavanuyyānassa tilakabhūte katabhūmi parikamme rājavatthudvārakoṭṭhakaṭṭhāne mahābodhiṃ patiṭṭhāpesi.

Then, on the fourth day, taking the Great Bodhi Tree and performing a magnificent offering, he gradually arrived at Anuradhapura. In Anuradhapura too, having made great veneration, on the fourteenth day, as the shadow was waxing, he brought the Great Bodhi Tree through the northern gate. Carrying it through the middle of the city, he exited through the southern gate, and from the southern gate, at a distance of five hundred bow-lengths, in the place where our Perfectly Self-Enlightened One sat, having attained the attainment of cessation, and where the three previous Perfectly Self-Enlightened Ones also sat, having entered that attainment—there, at the site where the sirisa Bodhi tree of the Blessed One Kakusandha, the udumbara Bodhi tree of the Blessed One Konāgamana, and the nigrodha Bodhi tree of the Blessed One Kassapa were established—in that very spot, the most distinguished place of the Mahāmeghavana pleasure grove, on ground that had been prepared, near the gate of the royal residence, he established the Great Bodhi Tree.

Evaṃ laṅkāhitatthāya-sāsanassaca vuddhiyā,

Mahāmeghavane ramme-mahābodhi patiṭṭhitoti;

Thus, for the benefit of Laṅkā and for the growth of the Dispensation, in the delightful Mahāmeghavana, the Great Bodhi Tree was established.

Bodhi āgamanakathā

The Account of the Arrival of the Bodhi Tree

14. Anulādevī pañcahi kaññāsatehi pañcahi antepurisā satehīti mātugāmasahassena saddhiṃ saṅghamittattheriyā santike pabbajitvā nacirasseva saparivārā arahatte patiṭṭhāsi. Ariṭṭhopi kho rañño bhāgineyyo pañcahi purisa satehi saddhiṃ therassa santike pabbajitvā saparivāro nacirasseva arahatte patiṭṭhāsi. Athekadivasaṃ rājā bodhiṃ vanditvā therena saddhiṃ thūpārāmaṃ gacchati. Tassa lohapāsādaṭṭhānaṃ sampattassa purisā pupphāni āhariṃsu rājā therassa pupphāni adāsi. Thero pupphehi lohapāsādaṭṭhānaṃ pūjesi pupphesu bhūmiyā patita mattesu mahābhūmicālo ahosi?

14. Anulādevī, together with five hundred maidens and five hundred palace women—thus a thousand women—took ordination under the Theri Saṅghamittā and, before long, she and her retinue became established in arahantship. Ariṭṭha, the king’s nephew, also ordained under the Thera with five hundred men and, before long, he and his retinue became established in arahantship. One day, the king paid homage to the Bodhi Tree and went with the Thera to the Thūpārāma. When they arrived at the site of the Lohapāsāda, attendants brought flowers. The king gave the flowers to the Thera. The Thera venerated the site of the Lohapāsāda with the flowers. As soon as the flowers fell to the ground, a great earthquake occurred.

Rājā kasmā bhante bhūmi calitāni pucchi. Imasmiṃ mahārāja okāse anāgate saṅghassa uposathāgāraṃ bhavissati. Tassetaṃ pubbanimittanti āha. Puna tassa mahācetiyaṭṭhānaṃ sampattassa campaka pupphāni abhihariṃsu. Tānipi rājā therassa adāsi. Thero mahācetiyaṭṭhānaṃ pupphehi pūjetvā vandi tāvadeva mahāpathavī saṃkampi. Rājā bhante kasmā pathavī kampitthāti pucchi. Mahārāja imasmiṃ ṭhāne anāgate buddhassa bhagavato asadiso mahāthūpo bhavissati. Tassetaṃ pubbanimittanti āha. Ahameva karomi bhanteti. Alaṃ mahārāja tumhākaṃ aññaṃ bahuṃ kammaṃ atthi. Tumhākaṃ pana nantā duṭṭhagāmaṇī abhayo nāma kāressatīti.

The king asked: “Venerable sir, why has the ground shaken?” “Great king, in this very place, in the future, there will be an Uposatha hall for the Saṅgha. This is a preliminary sign of that,” he said. Then, having arrived at the site of the great cetiya, they brought campaka flowers. The king gave those flowers to the Thera. The Thera, having honored the site of the great cetiya with the flowers, paid homage. Immediately, the great earth trembled. The king asked: “Venerable sir, why did the earth tremble?” “Great king, in this very place, in the future, there will be an incomparable great stupa of the Blessed Buddha. This is a preliminary sign of that,” he said. “I myself will build it, venerable sir!” “Enough, great king, you have much other work to do. Your grandson, by the name of Duṭṭhagāmaṇī Abhaya, will have it built,” he said.

Atha rājā sace bhante mayhaṃ nantā karissati. Kataṃyeva mayāti dvādasahatthaṃ pāsāṇatthamhaṃ āharāpetvā devānampiyatissa rañño nattā duṭṭhagāmaṇi abhayo nāma imasmiṃ padese thūpaṃ karotīti akkharāni likhāpetvā patiṭṭhāpesīti atha devānampiyatissarājā cetiyapappate nihitā sammāsambuddha bhutta patta pūretvā āhaṭā dhātuyohatthikkhaṇdhena āharāpetvā sakalatambapaṇṇidīpe [O 0.0047] yojane yojane thūpaṃ kāretvā dhātuyo patiṭṭhāpesi bhagavato pattaṃ pana rāja geheyava ṭhapetvā pūjamakāsīti.

Then the king said: 'Venerable sir, if my grandson builds it, then it is as if built by me.' He had a stone pillar twelve cubits high brought, and had inscribed upon it the words: 'The grandson of King Devānampiyatissa, Duṭṭhagāmaṇī Abhaya by name, will build a stūpa in this place,' and had it set up. Then King Devānampiyatissa had the relics—which had been deposited at Cetiyapabbata and were brought after filling the bowl used by the Perfectly Enlightened One—conveyed on the back of an elephant. He had stūpas built at every yojana throughout the entire island of Tambapaṇṇi and enshrined the relics therein. The Blessed One's bowl, however, he kept in the royal palace and offered worship to it.

Nidhāpetvāna sambuddha-dhātuyo pattamattakā,

Kārāpesi mahārāja-thūpe yojana yojaneti;

Having enshrined a bowlful of relics of the Perfectly Enlightened One, the great king had stūpas built at every yojana.

Yojanathūpa kathā

The Story of the Yojana-Stūpas

Atha rājā aññāni ca bahūni puññakammāni katvā cattālīsa vassāni rajjaṃ kāresi. Tassa accayena taṃ kaniṭṭho uttiya rājā dasavassāni rajjaṃ kāresi. Tassa accayena taṃ kaniṭṭho mahāsīvo dasavassāneva rajjaṃ kāresi tassa accayena tassāpi kaniṭṭho sūratisso dasavassāneva rajjaṃ kāresi tato assanāvika puttā dve damiḷā sūratissaṃ gahetvā dvevīsa vassāni dhammena rajjaṃ kāresuṃ. Te gahetvā muṭasīvassa rañño putto aselo nāma dasavassāni rajjaṃ kāresi atha coḷaraṭṭhato agantvā eḷāro nāma damilo asela bhūpatiṃ gahetvā catucattālīsa vassāni rajjaṃ kāresi eḷāraṃ gahetvā duṭṭhagāmaṇi abhayo rājā ahosi.

Then the king, having performed many other meritorious deeds, reigned for forty years. After his passing, his younger brother, King Utti, reigned for ten years. After his passing, his younger brother, Mahāsīva, reigned for ten years. After his passing, his younger brother, Sūratissa, also reigned for ten years. Then two Tamils, sons of a horse-trader, having deposed Sūratissa, reigned righteously for twenty-two years. Asela, the son of King Muṭasīva, having captured them, reigned for ten years. Then, coming from the Coḷa country, a Tamil named Eḷāra, having slain King Asela, reigned for forty-four years. Having slain Eḷāra, Duṭṭhagāmaṇī Abhaya became king.

Tadatthadīpanatthaṃ ayamanupubbakathā

For the purpose of elucidating that matter, this is the sequential narrative.

15. Devānampiyatissa rañño kira dutiyabhātiko uparājā mahānāgo nāma ahosi. Atha rañño devī attano puttassa rajjaṃ icchanti taracchanāvā vāpiṃ karontassa uparājassavisena ambaṃ yojetvā ambamatthake ṭhapetvā pesesi deviyi putte, uparājena saddhiṃ gato bhājane vivaṭe sayameva amabaṃ gahetvā khāditvā kālamakāsi uparājā taṃ kāraṇaṃ ñatvā deviyā bhīto tatoyeva attano deviñca balavāhanañca gahetvā rohaṇaṃ agamāsi. Tassa aggamahesi antarāmagge yaṭṭālavihāre nāma puttaṃ vijāyi tassa tissoti bhātunāma’makaṃsi.

15. It is said that King Devānampiyatissa had a younger brother, the viceroy, named Mahānāga. Then the king's queen, desiring the kingdom for her own son, sent a mango mixed with poison, placed on top of other mangoes, to the viceroy who was constructing a tank. The queen's son, who had gone with the viceroy, upon the container being opened, himself took that mango, ate it, and died. The viceroy, understanding the situation and fearing the queen, immediately took his own queen, his forces, and transport, and went to Rohaṇa. On the way, his chief queen gave birth to a son at a place called Yaṭṭhālavihāra, and he was given the name Tissa.

So tato gantvā mahāgāme vasanto rohaṇe rajjaṃ kāresi tassa accayena tassa putto yaṭṭālatisso mahāgāmeyeva rajjaṃ kāresi. Tassa accayena tassāpi putto goṭhābhayo nāma tattheva rajjaṃ kāresi. Goṭhābhayassa putto kākavaṇṇatisso nāma tattheva rajjaṃ kāresi. Kākavaṇṇatissa rañño kira kalyāṇitissa rañño dhītā vihāramahādevī nāma aggamahesi ahosi sā rañño piyā ahosi manāpā. Rājā tāya saddhiṃ samaggavāsaṃ vasanto puññāni karonto vihāsi. Athekadivasaṃ devī rājageheyeva bhikkhusaṅghassa mahādānaṃ datvā sāyaṇhasamaye [O 0.0048] gaṇdhamālādīni gāhāpetvā dhammaṃ sotuṃ vihāraṃgatā, tattha nipannaṃ bāḷhagilānaṃ āsannamaraṇaṃ sīlavantaṃ sāmaṇeraṃ disvā gaṇdhamālādīhi pūjetvā attano sampattiṃ vaṇṇetvā mama puttabhāvaṃ patthetha bhanteti yāci.

He then went from there and, dwelling in Mahāgāma, ruled over Rohaṇa. After his passing, his son Yaṭṭhālatissa ruled in Mahāgāma. After his passing, his son Goṭhābhaya also ruled there. Goṭhābhaya’s son, Kākavaṇṇatissa, also ruled there. It is said that King Kākavaṇṇatissa's chief queen was named Vihāramahādevī, the daughter of King Kalyāṇitissa. She was dear and pleasing to the king. The king dwelt in harmony with her, performing meritorious deeds. One day, the queen, having given a great alms-offering to the community of monks in the royal palace itself, went to the monastery in the evening, taking perfumes and garlands, to listen to the Dhamma. There, she saw a virtuous novice lying down, gravely ill and near death. She honored him with perfumes and garlands, described her own good fortune, and pleaded: 'Venerable sir, please aspire to be my son.'

So na icchi, yāpi punappuna yāciyeva. Sāmaṇeropi evaṃ sante sāsanānuggahaṃ kātuṃ sakkātisampaṭicchitvā gatinimittavasena upaṭṭhitampi devalokaṃ chaḍḍetvā nikantivasena suvaṇṇa sivikāya gacchantiyā deviyā kucchimhi paṭisaṇdhiṃ gaṇhi.

He did not wish for it, yet she pleaded again and again. The novice, thinking, 'In this way, it is possible to support the Dispensation,' accepted. Though the heavenly realm had appeared to him as a sign of his future destiny, he abandoned it and, through the power of his aspiration, took conception in the womb of the queen as she was traveling in a golden palanquin.

Sā dasamāsaccayena puttaṃ vijāyi. Tassa gāmaṇi abhayoti nāmaṃ kariṃsu. Aparabhāge aparampi tassa tissoti nāmaṃ kariṃsu. Gāmaṇi kumāro kamena vaḍḍhento soḷasa vassiko hutvā hatthassa tharā sippesu kovido tejobala parakkama sampanno ahosi. Atha kho kākavaṇṇatissa rājā naṇdhimitto-suranimmalo-mahāseno-goṭhayimbaro-theraputtābha- yo-bharaṇo-veḷusumano-khañcadevo-phussadevo-labhiyyavasabhoti ime dasāmahāyodhe puttassa santike ṭhapetvā vāsesi.

After the passing of ten months, she gave birth to a son. They gave him the name Gāmaṇi Abhaya. Later, she bore another son, and they gave him the name Tissa. Prince Gāmaṇi, growing up in due course, at the age of sixteen became skilled in elephant-lore and other arts, and was endowed with majesty, strength, and valor. Then King Kākavaṇṇatissa appointed these ten great warriors—Nandhimitta, Suranimmala, Mahāsena, Goṭhayimbara, Theraputtābhaya, Bharaṇa, Veḷusumana, Khañjadeva, Phussadeva, and Labhiyavasabha—and had them reside in the presence of his son.

Tesaṃ uppattikathā mahāvaṃsato gahetabbā rājā dasamahāyodhānaṃ puttassa sakkārasamaṃsakkāraṃ kāresi tissakumāraṃ janapada rakkhanatthāya dīghavāpiyaṃ ṭhapesi athekadivasaṃ gāmiṇī kumāro attano balavāhana sampattiṃ disvā damiḷehi saddhiṃ yujjhassāmīti rañño kathāpesi. Rājā puttaṃ anurakkhanto alaṃ oragaṅganti nivāresi. So yāva tatiyaṃ kathāpesi rājā kujjhitvā hemasaṅkhalikaṃ karotha baṇdhitvā rakkhissāmīti. Abhayo pitu rañño kujjhitvā palāyitvā malayaṃ agamāsi tato paṭṭhāya pitari duṭṭhattā duṭṭhagāmaṇīti paññāto rājā puttānaṃ kalahaṭṭhānaṃ āgamanatthāya yodhehi sapathaṃ kāresi.

The stories of their origins should be understood from the Mahāvaṃsa. The king paid the ten great warriors honor equal to that which he paid his son. He stationed Prince Tissa at Dīghavāpi for the protection of the country. Then one day, Prince Gāmaṇi, seeing the extent of his own forces and transport, sent a message to the king, saying: 'I will fight with the Tamils.' The king, wishing to protect his son, forbade him, saying: 'The region on this side of the river is enough!' When the prince sent the message a third time, the king grew angry and said: 'Make a golden chain! I will bind him and keep him.' Abhaya, angered at his father the king, fled to the Malaya region. From that time, on account of his anger toward his father, he became known as Duṭṭhagāmaṇī. The king made the warriors swear an oath that they would not take sides in any quarrel between his sons.

Atha kākavaṇṇatissa rājā catusaṭṭhivihāre kāretvā catusaṭṭhi saṃvaccharāneva ṭhatvā kālamakāsi tissakumāro pitukālakatabhāvaṃ sutvā dīghavāpito āgantvā pitu sarīrakiccaṃ kāretvā mātaraṃ - kaṇḍula hatthiñca gahetvā bhātu bhayā dīghavāpiṃ agamāsi. Amaccā sannipatitvā taṃ pavattiṃ vatvā duṭṭhagāmaṇitissa santikaṃ pesesuṃ. So taṃ sāsanaṃ sutvā bhuttasālaṃ āgamma bhātu santikaṃ dūte pesetvā tato mahāgāmaṃ āgantvā abhisekaṃ patvā mātaraṃ kaṇḍulahatthiñca pesetūti yāva tatiyaṃ bhātu santikaṃ lekhā pesetvā apesana bhāvaṃ ñatvā yuddhāya nikkhami. Kumāropi yuddhasajjohutvānikkhami. Cuḷaṅgaṇiya piṭṭhiyaṃ dvinnaṃ bhātūnaṃ mahāyuddhaṃ ahosi.

Then King Kākavaṇṇatissa, having had sixty-four monasteries built, passed away after living for sixty-four years. Prince Tissa, hearing that his father had passed away, came from Dīghavāpi, had the funeral rites for his father performed, and then, fearing his brother, took his mother and the elephant Kaṇḍula and returned to Dīghavāpi. The ministers assembled, reported these events, and sent a message to Duṭṭhagāmaṇī. Hearing that message, he came to Bhuttasāla and sent messengers to his brother. Then, having come to Mahāgāma and received the royal consecration, he sent letters to his brother up to a third time, saying, 'Send our mother and the elephant Kaṇḍula.' Understanding that he would not send them, he marched out for battle. The prince, also prepared for war, marched out. A great battle between the two brothers took place on the plain of Cuḷaṅgaṇiya.

Te [O 0.0049] kira yodhā sapathassa katattā tesaṃ yuddhe sahāyā na bhaviṃsu. Tadā rañño anekasahassa manussā mariṃsu. Rājā parajjitvā tissāmaccaṃ dighatuṇikaṃ vaḷavañca gahetvā palāyi. Kumāro pacchato pacchato anubaṇdhi. Antare bhikkhu pabbataṃ māpesuṃ. Taṃ disvā kumāro bhikkhusaṅghassa kammanti ñatvā nivatti rājā palāyitvā kappaṇdakara nadiyā jalamālatitthaṃ nāma gantvaṃ chātomhiti āha. Amacco suvaṇṇasarake pakkhittabhattā niharitvā adāsi.

Indeed, on account of the oath they had sworn, those warriors were not allies to either side in the battle. At that time, many thousands of the king's men perished. The king, being defeated, fled, taking with him a minister named Tissa and the mare Dīghatūṇikā. The prince pursued him closely. In the space between them, the monks created an illusory mountain. Seeing this, the prince, recognizing it as an act of the community of monks, turned back. The king, having fled, went to the ford named Jalamālatittha on the Kappaṇdakara River and said, 'I am hungry.' The minister took out rice that had been placed in a golden bowl and gave it to him.

Rājā kālaṃ sallakkhetvā saṅghassa datvā bhuñjāmiti saṅghassa-amaccassa-vaḷavāya-attano cāti catubhāgaṃ katvā kālā ghosāpesi tadā piyaṅgudipato kuṭumbiyatissatthero nāma āgantvā purato aṭṭhāsi. Rājā theraṃ disvā pasannamānaso saṅghassa ṭhapitabhāgaṃ attano bhāgañca therassa patte pakkhipiṃ amaccopi attano bhāgaṃ pakkhipi vaḷavāpi dātukāmā ahosi. Tassābhippāyaṃ ñatvā amacco tassāpi bhāgaṃ patte pakkhipi.

The king, observing the time, had it announced, 'Having given to the Sangha, let us eat,' dividing the food into four portions: for the Sangha, the minister, the mare, and himself. At that time, the elder Kuṭumbiyatissa from Piyaṅgudīpa arrived and stood before him. The king, seeing the elder, with a pleased mind, placed the portion set aside for the Sangha and his own portion into the elder's bowl. The minister also placed his portion into it, and the mare also wished to give. Knowing her intention, the minister placed her portion into the bowl as well.

Iti so rājā therassa paripuṇṇa bhattapattaṃ adāsi. Thero pattaṃ gahetvā gantvā gotamattherassa nāma adāsi. So pañcasata bhikkhu bhojetvā puna tato laddhehi bhāgehi pattapūretvā ākāse khipi patto gantvā rañño purato aṭṭhāsi tisso pattaṃ gahetvā rājānaṃ bhojetvā tato sayaṃ bhuñjitvā vaḷavaṃ bhojesi. Tato rājā sannāhaṃ cumbaṭakaṃ katvā pattaṃ vissajjesi. Tato gantvā therassa hatthe patiṭṭhāsi, rājā puna mahāgāmaṃ āgantvā senaṃ saṅkaḍḍivo saṭṭhisahassabalā gahetvā puna bhātarā saddhiṃ yujjhi tadā kumārassa aneka sahassaṃ manussā patiṃsu.

Thus the king gave the elder a bowl filled with food. The elder, taking the bowl, went and gave it to the elder named Gotama. He fed five hundred monks, and then with the portions received from them, he filled the bowl again and threw it into the sky. The bowl went and stood before the king. Prince Tissa, taking the bowl, fed the king, then ate himself, and fed the mare. Then the king, having made a head-pad from his armor, sent the bowl away. It then went and rested in the elder’s hand. The king returned to Mahāgāma, gathered his army, and with a force of sixty thousand, went to fight with his brother again. At that time, many thousands of the prince's men fell.

Kumāro palāyitvā vihāraṃ pavisitvā mahātherassa gehaṃ pāvisi.

The prince fled and, entering the monastery, went into the great elder's dwelling.

Rājā pacchato pacchato anubaṇdhanto vihāraṃ paviṭṭhabhāvaṃ ñatvā nivatti pacchā therā te ubho bhātaro aññamaññaṃ khamāpesuṃ. Tadā rājā sassakammāni kāretuṃ tissakumāraṃ dīghavāpimeva pahiṇitvā sayampi bheraṃ carāpetvā sassakammāni kāresi. Atha mahājanassa saṅgahaṃkatvā kunte dhātuṃ nidhāpetvā balavāhana parivuto tissārāmaṃ gantvā saṅghaṃ vanditvā bhante sāsanaṃ jotetuṃ pāragaṅgaṃ gamissāmi sakkāretuṃ amhehi sahagāmino bhikkhu dethāti āha.

The king, following closely behind, realized that the prince had entered the monastery and turned back. Later, the elders had the two brothers ask forgiveness of each other. Then the king sent Prince Tissa to Dīghavāpi to have agricultural work done, while he himself had a drum beaten to proclaim the farming activities. Then, having gathered a great multitude, he had a relic enshrined in his spear and, surrounded by his army and mounts, went to Tissārāma. There, he paid homage to the Sangha and said, 'Venerable sirs, I will go to the far side of the river to illuminate the Dispensation. Please give us monks to accompany us, that we may honor them.'

Saṅgho pañcasatabhikkhu adāsi. Rājā bhikkhusaṅghaṃ gahetvā kaṇḍulahatthimāruyha yodhehi parivuto mahatā balakāyena yuddhāya [O 0.0050] nikkhamitvā mahiyaṅgaṇaṃ āganantvā tattha damiḷehi saddhiṃ yujjhanto mahiyaṅgaṇe kañcuka thūpaṃ kāresi. Tassa thūpassa vibhāvanatthaṃ ayamānupubbakathā.

The Sangha gave five hundred monks. The king, taking the community of monks, mounted the elephant Kaṇḍula and, surrounded by warriors with a great army, went out to battle. Arriving at Mahiyangana, he fought with the Damiḷas and there erected the Kañcuka Thūpa. For the elucidation of that stupa, this is the connected account.

16. Bhagavā kira bodhite navame māse imaṃ dīpamāgantvā gaṅgātīre tiyojanāyate yojanavitthate mahānāgavanuyyāne yakkhasamāgamaṃ āgantvā tesaṃ yakkhānaṃ uparibhāge mahiyaṅgaṇa thūpassa ṭhāne vehāsayaṃ ṭhito vuṭṭhi vātandhakārādīhi yakkhe santāsetvā tehi abhayaṃ yācito tumhākaṃ abhayaṃ dassāmi tumhe samaggā mayhaṃ nisīdanaṭṭhānaṃ dethāti āha.

16. It is said that the Blessed One, in the ninth month after his enlightenment, came to this island. He came to a gathering of yakkhas in the great Nāga-forest park, three yojanas long and one yojana wide, on the bank of the river. Standing in the air above those yakkhas, at the place of the Mahiyangana Thūpa, he terrified them with rain, wind, darkness, and so forth. When begged by them for safety, he said, 'I will grant you safety. All of you together, give me a place to sit.'

Yakkhā, mārisa te imaṃ sakaladīpaṃ demi. Abhayaṃ no dehīti āhaṃsu. Tato bhagavā tesaṃ bhayaṃ apanuditvā tehi dinnabhabhūmiyaṃ cammakhaṇḍaṃ pattharitvā tattha nisinno tejokasiṇaṃ samāpajjitvā cammakhaṇḍaṃ samantato jāletvā vaḍḍhesi. Te cammakhaṇḍena abhibhūnā samantato sāgara pariyante rāsibhūtā ahesuṃ bhagavā iddhibalena giridīpaṃ nāma idhānetvā tattha yakkhe pavesetvā dīpaṃ yathāṭṭhāne ṭhapetvā cammakhaṇḍaṃ saṅkhipi tadā devatā samāgamo ahosi. Tasmiṃ samāgame bhagavā dhammaṃ desesi-tadā.

'Venerable Sir, we give you this entire island. Grant us safety,' they said. Then the Blessed One, having dispelled their fear, spread a piece of hide on the ground given by them and sat down there. Having entered the fire-kasiṇa, he caused the piece of hide to blaze and expand all around. Overpowered by the piece of hide, they were massed together on every side at the ocean's edge. By his psychic power, the Blessed One brought the island named Giridīpa here, made the yakkhas enter it, and then established that island in its former place and contracted the piece of hide. Then there was a gathering of deities. At that gathering, the Blessed One taught the Dhamma.

“Nekesaṃ pāṇakoṭīnaṃ-dhammābhisamayo ahū,

Saraṇesu ca sīlesu-ṭhitā āsuṃ asaṅkhiyā;

'For many koṭis of beings, there was a penetration of the Dhamma; and innumerable beings were established in the refuges and precepts.'

Sotāpattiphalaṃ patvā-sele sumanakūṭake,

Mahāsumana deviṇdo-pūjiyaṃ yāci pūchiyaṃ;

Having attained the fruit of stream-entry, on the mountain Sumanakūṭa, the great deva Mahāsumana, lord of gods, requested an object of worship.

Siraṃ parāmasitvāna-nilāmalasiroruhe,

Pāṇimatte adā kese tassa pāṇihito jino;

The Victor, touching his head of dark-blue, spotless hair, gave a handful of hairs, placing them in Mahāsumana's hand.

So taṃ suvaṇṇicaṅgoṭaṃ-carenādāya satthuno, nisinnaṭṭhāna racite-nānāratanasañcaye.

He, having received them in a splendid golden casket, at the place where the Teacher had sat, which was constructed with a collection of various jewels,

Uccato sattaratane-ṭhapetvāna siroruhe,

Taṃ iṇdanīla thūpena-pidahesi namassi ca”;

placed the hairs upon a pile seven ratanas high, covered it with a stupa of sapphire, and paid homage.

Parinibbute pana bhagavati dhammasenāpati sāri puttattherassa antevāsiko sarabhū nāmeko thero citakato gīvaṭṭhidhātu gahetvā bhikkhu saṅghaparivuto āgantvā tasmiṃyeva cetiye patiṭṭhāpetvā meghavaṇṇapāsāṇehi chādetvā dvādasa hatthubbedhaṃ thūpaṃ kāretvā pakkāmi. Atha devānampiyatissa rañño bhātā cūḷābhayo nāma taṃ abbhutaṃ cetiyaṃ disvā tiṃsahatthubbedhaṃ cetiyaṃ kāresi. Idāni duṭṭhagāmaṇīpi abhayarājā mahiyaṅgaṇaṃ āgantvā tattha damiḷe maddanto asitihatthubbedhaṃ kañcukacetiyaṃ kāretvā pūjamakāsi.

When the Blessed One had attained Parinibbāna, the Elder named Sarabhū, a disciple of the Elder Sāriputta, the General of the Dhamma, took the collarbone relic from the funeral pyre, came surrounded by the community of monks, enshrined it in that very cetiya, covered it with cloud-colored stones, had a stupa built twelve cubits high, and departed. Then King Devānampiyatissa’s brother, named Cūḷābhaya, seeing that marvelous cetiya, constructed a cetiya thirty cubits high. Then King Duṭṭhagāmaṇī Abhaya also came to Mahiyangana and, crushing the Damiḷas there, built a covering cetiya eighty cubits high and made offerings.

Evamaccāyikaṃ [O 0.0051] kammaṃ-karontāpi guṇākarā, karonti puññaṃ sappaññā-saṃsārabhasa bhīrukāti.

Thus, even while performing urgent tasks, the wise, who are a mine of good qualities and are fearful of the terror of saṃsāra, make merit.

Mahiyaṅgana thūpakathā

The Story of the Mahiyangana Thūpa.

17. Tato rājā damiḷehi saddhiṃ yujjhitvā chattadamiḷaṃ gaṇhitvā tatra bahu damiḷe ghātetvā ambatitthaṃ āgantvā ambadamiḷaṃ catūhi māsehi gaṇhi. Tato oruyha mahabbale sattadamiḷe ekāheneva gaṇhi tato antarasobbhe mahākoṭṭha damiḷaṃ-doṇagāme gavara damiḷaṃ-hālakole mahissariya damiḷaṃ-nāḷisobbhe nāḷika damiḷaṃ-dighābhasagallamhi dighābhaya damiḷaṃ gaṇhi. Tato kacchatitthe kiñcisīsa damiḷaṃ catūhi māsehi gaṇhi tato veṭha nagare tāḷa damiḷaṃ, bhāṇakadamiḷañca-vahiṭṭhe vahiṭṭha damiḷaṃ=gāmaṇimhi gāmaṇi damiḷaṃ-kumbugāmamhi kumbu damiḷaṃ-naṇdika gāmamhi naṇdika damiḷaṃ-khāṇugāmamhi khāṇu damiḷaṃ-tambunnagāmake mātula bhāgineyye dve damiḷe gaṇhi tadā-

17. Then the king, having fought with the Damiḷas, captured the Damiḷa Chatta, slew many Damiḷas there, and came to Ambatittha, where he captured the Damiḷa Amba in four months. Descending from there, he seized the mighty Damiḷa Satta in a single day. Then, in Antarasobbha, he captured the Damiḷa Mahākoṭṭha; in Doṇagāma, the Damiḷa Gavara; in Hālakola, the Damiḷa Mahissariya; in Nāḷisobbha, the Damiḷa Nāḷika; and in Dīghābhayasagalla, the Damiḷa Dīghābhaya. Next, at Kacchatittha, he captured the Damiḷa Kiñcisīsa in four months. Then, in the city of Veṭha, he took the Damiḷa Tāḷa and the Damiḷa Bhāṇaka; in Vahiṭṭha, the Damiḷa Vahiṭṭha; in Gāmaṇi, the Damiḷa Gāmaṇi; in Kumbugāma, the Damiḷa Kumbu; in Nandikagāma, the Damiḷa Nandika; in Khāṇugāma, the Damiḷa Khāṇu; and in Tambunnagāmaka, he seized two Damiḷas, an uncle and his nephew. At that time—

“Ajānitvā sakaṃ senaṃ-ghātenti sajanā iti,

Sutvāna saccakiriyaṃ-akari tattha bhupati;

'Not recognizing their own army, my own people are slaying each other.' Hearing this, the king there made an act of truth:

Rajjasukhāya vāyāmo-nāyaṃ mama kadāpi ca,

Sambuddhasāsanasseva-ṭhapanāya ayaṃ mama;

'This endeavor of mine is never for the delight of sovereignty. Rather, it is for the establishment of the Sambuddha's Dispensation.'

Tena saccena me senā-kāyopagata bhaṇḍakaṃ,

Jālavaṇṇaṃva hotūti-taṃ tatheva tadā ahu;”

'By this truth, may the armor on the bodies of my soldiers become the color of fire.' And so it was at that time.

Evaṃ rājā gaṅgātīre damiḷe ghātesi. Ghātita so sabbe āgantvā vijita nagare pavisiṃsu. Tadā rājā vijita nagaraṃ gaṇhituṃ vīmaṃsanatthāya āgacchantaṃ naṇdhimittaṃ disvā kaṇḍulaṃ muñcesi. Kaṇḍulopi taṃ gaṇhituṃ āgañchi tadā naṇdhimitto hatthetahi ubho dante bāḷhaṃ gahetvā pīḷetvā ukkuṭikaṃ nisīdāpesi. Rājā ubho vimaṃsetvā vijitanagaraṃ āgato. Tato dakkhiṇadvāre yodhānaṃ mahāsaṅgāmo ahosi puratthimadvāre veḷusumano assaṃ āruyha bahū damiḷe ghātesi damiḷā anto pivisitvā dvāraṃ thakesuṃ. Tato rājā yodhe vissajjesi, kaṇḍulahatthi naṇdhimitto suranimmalo ca dakkhiṇadvāre kammaṃ kariṃsu mahāsoṇo goṭhayimbaro theraputtābhayo cāti ime tayo itaresu tīsu dvāresu kammaṃ kariṃsu.

Thus the king slew the Damiḷas on the banks of the river. Having slain them all, they came and entered the city of Vijita. Then the king, wishing to consider how to take the city of Vijita, saw Nandhimitta approaching and released the elephant Kaṇḍula. Kaṇḍula also came to seize him, but then Nandhimitta, gripping both tusks tightly with his hands, squeezed them and made the elephant sit on its haunches. The king, having tested both, came to the city of Vijita. Then, at the southern gate, a great battle of the warriors took place. At the eastern gate, Veḷusumana, mounting his horse, slew many Damiḷas. The Damiḷas entered inside and secured the gate. Then the king dispatched the warriors. The elephant Kaṇḍula, Nandhimitta, and Suranimmala performed their duty at the southern gate. Mahāsoṇa, Goṭhayimbara, and Theraputtābhaya—these three performed their duty at the other three gates.

Tañca nagaraṃ parikhāttaya parikkhittaṃ, daḷha pākāra gopuraṃ, ayo dvārayuttaṃ ahosi kaṇḍulo jāṇuhi ṭhatvā silā sudhā iṭṭhakā bhiṇditvā ayodvāraṃ pāpuṇi tadā damiḷā gopure ṭhatvā nānāvudhāni [O 0.0052] khipiṃsu pakka ayoguḷe cevapakkaṭṭhita silesañca hatthipiṭṭhiyaṃ pakkhipiṃsu? Tadā kaṇḍulo vedanaṭṭo udakaṭṭhānaṃ gantvā udake ogāhi. Tadā goṭhayimbaro na idaṃ surāpānaṃ bhavati. Ayodvāra vighāṭanaṃ nāma, gaccha dvāraṃ vighāṭehīti āha. Taṃ sutvā jātābhimāto kuñcanādaṃ katvā udana uggamma thale aṭṭhāsi atha hatthivejjo silesaṃ dhovitvā osadhaṃ akāsi.
