Tato rājā hatthiṃ āruyha pāṇinā kumbhe parāmasitvā sakala laṅkātale rajjaṃ tava dammīti tosetvā varabhojanaṃ bhojetvā vaṇaṃ sāṭakena veṭhetvā suvammītaṃ katvā vammapiṭṭhiyaṃ mahisacammaṃ sattaguṇaṃ katvā baṇdhitvā tassupari telacammaṃ baṇdhitvā taṃ vissajjesi so asani viyagajjanto gantvā dāṭhāhi padaraṃ vijjhitvā pādena ummāraṃ hani. Dvāraṃ bāhāhi saddhiṃ ayodvāraṃ mahāsaddena bhūmiyaṃ pati. Gopure dabbasambhāraṃ pana hatthipiṭṭhiyaṃ patantaṃ disvā naṇdhimitto bāhāhi paharitvā pavaṭṭesi tadā kaṇḍulo dāṭhāpīḷanaveraṃ chaḍḍhesi.

And that city was surrounded by three moats, a strong rampart and gate towers, and was fitted with iron doors. Kaṇḍula, getting on his knees, broke through stone, plaster, and brick and reached the iron gate. Then the Damiḷas, standing on the gate towers, hurled various weapons; they threw heated iron balls and boiling pitch onto the elephant's back. Then Kaṇḍula, afflicted by pain, went to a place with water and submerged himself. Then Goṭhayimbara said, "This is no time for drinking liquor! This is the breaking of the iron gate. Go, break the gate!" Hearing this, his pride aroused, he made a trumpeting sound, emerged from the water, and stood on the ground. Then the elephant-doctor washed away the pitch and applied medicine. Then the king, mounting the elephant, stroked its forehead with his hand and, encouraging it, said, "I will give you the kingship of the whole of Laṅkā." He fed it fine food, wrapped its wound with a cloth, made it well-armored, bound a seven-layered buffalo hide upon its armored back, bound an oiled hide over that, and sent it forth. Roaring like thunder, it went and pierced the door-post with its tusks and struck the threshold with its foot. The iron door, together with its frame, fell to the ground with a great noise. Seeing the debris from the gate tower falling on the elephant's back, Nandhimitta struck it with his arms and cast it aside. Then Kaṇḍula forsook his grudge over the squeezing of his tusks.

Tato kaṇḍulo attano piṭṭhiṃ ārūhanatthāya naṇdhimittaṃ olokesi. So tayā katamaggena na pavisissāmīti aṭṭhārasa hatthubbedhaṃ pākāraṃ bāhunā paharitvā aṭṭhusahappamāṇaṃ pākārappadesaṃ pātetvā suranimmalaṃ olokesi sopi tena katamaggaṃ anicchanto pākāraṃ laḍḍītvā nagarabbhantare pati goṭhayimbaropi - soṇopi - theraputtābhayopi ekeka dvāraṃ bhiṇditvā pavisiṃsu-tato.

Then Kaṇḍula looked at Nandhimitta to have him mount his back. He, thinking, "I will not enter by the way made by you," struck the eighteen-cubit-high rampart with his arm, brought down a section of the rampart measuring eight usabhas, and looked at Suranimmala. He too, not wanting the way made by the other, leaped over the rampart and fell inside the city. Goṭhayimbara, Soṇa, and Theraputtābhaya also entered, each breaking a gate.

“Hatthi gahetvā rathacakkaṃ-mitto sakaṭa pañjaraṃ,

Nāḷikerataruṃ goṭho-nimmalo khaggamuttamaṃ;

Nandhimitta took a chariot wheel and the frame of a cart; Goṭhayimbara, a coconut tree; Suranimmala, an excellent sword;

Tālarukkhaṃ mahāsoṇo-theraputto mahāgadaṃ,

Visuṃ visuṃ vīthigatā-damiḷe nattha cuṇṇayuṃ”;

Mahāsoṇa, a palm tree; Theraputta, a great mace. Going along the streets, each separately, they utterly crushed the Damiḷas."

Evaṃ vijitanagaraṃ catūhi māsehi bhiṇditvā damiḷe māretvā tato garilokaṃ nāma gantvā giriya damiḷaṃ aggahesi. Tato mahela nagaraṃ gantvā catūhi māsehi mahela rājānaṃ gaṇhiṃ tato rājā anurudhapuraṃ gacchanto paritokāsapabbate nāma khaṇdhāvāraṃ nivāsetvā tattha taḷākaṃ kāretvā jeṭṭhamūlamāsamhi udakakīḷaṃ kīḷi. Eḷāropi duṭṭhagāmaṇissa āgatabhāvaṃ sutvā amaccehi saddhiṃ mantetvā sve yuddhaṃ karissāmāti nicchayaṃ akāsi punadivase sannaddho mahāpabbata hatthiṃ āruyha mahā balakāya parivuto nikkhami. Gāmaṇīpi mātarā saddhiṃ mantetvā dvattiṃsa bala koṭṭhake kāretvā chattadhare rājarūpake tattha tattha ṭhapesi. Abbhantara koṭṭhake sayaṃ aṭṭhāsi.

Thus, having breached the city of Vijita in four months and having slain the Damiḷas, he then went to a place called Gariloka and seized the Damiḷa Giri. Then, going to the city of Mahela, he captured King Mahela in four months. Then the king, while proceeding to Anurādhapura, set up camp on a mountain called Kāsapabbata, had a tank made there, and celebrated a water festival in the month of Jeṭṭha. Eḷāra, hearing of Duṭṭhagāmaṇī's arrival, consulted with his ministers and decided, "Tomorrow we shall do battle." The next day, fully armed, he mounted the great elephant Pabbata and went forth, surrounded by a great army. Gāmaṇī, after consulting with his mother, had thirty-two fortified posts made, placing royal figures with parasol-bearers here and there. He himself stood in the innermost post.

Tato [O 0.0053] saṅgāme vattamāne eḷāra rañño dīghajattu nāma mahā yodho khaggaphalakaṃ gahetvā bhūmito aṭṭhārasa hatthaṃ nabhamuggantvā rājarūpaṃ chiṇditvā paṭhamaṃ bala koṭṭhakaṃ bhiṇdi evaṃ sesepi balakoṭṭhake bhiṇditvā mahāgamaṇinā ṭhataṃ balakoṭṭhakaṃ āgami. Tadā suranimmalo rañño parigacchantaṃ disvā attano nāmaṃ sāvetvā taṃ akkosi taṃ sutvā dīghajantu paṭhamaṃ imaṃ māremīti kujjhitvā ākasamabbhuggantvā attanopari otarantaṃ disvā saranimmalo attano phalakaṃ upanāmesi. Itaropi phalakena saddhiṃ taṃ bhiṇdissa mīti cintetvā phalakaṃ pahari. Itaro phalakaṃ muñci, dīghajantu phalakaṃ chiṇdanto bhūmiyaṃ pati. Suranimmalo taṃ sattiyā pahari, phussadevo taṃ khaṇe saṅkhaṃ dhami, asanisaddo viya ahosi ummādappattā viya manussā ahesuṃ. Tato damiḷa senā bhijjittha, eḷāro palāyittha tadāpi bahu damiḷe ghātesuṃ.

Then, as the battle was underway, a great warrior of King Eḷāra named Dīghajattu, taking sword and shield, leaped eighteen cubits into the air from the ground, cut down the royal figure, and broke through the first fortified post. In the same way, he broke through the remaining fortified posts and came to the post where Mahāgāmaṇī stood. Then Suranimmala, seeing him passing the king, announced his own name and reviled him. Hearing this, Dīghajattu became angry and, thinking, "First I will kill this one," leaped into the air. Seeing him descending upon him, Suranimmala raised his shield. The other, thinking, "I will split him together with his shield," struck the shield. The other released the shield; Dīghajattu, while cutting the shield, fell to the ground. Suranimmala struck him with his spear. At that moment, Phussadeva blew his conch; it was like a clap of thunder. The men became as if possessed. Then the Damiḷa army was broken, and Eḷāra fled. Even then, they slew many Damiḷas.

“Tattha vāpijalaṃ āsi-hatānaṃ lohitāvilaṃ,

Tasmā kulatthavāpīti-nāmato vissutā ahū;

There the water of the tank was turbid with the blood of the slain; therefore it became famous by the name Kulatthavāpī.

Carāpetvā tahiṃ bheriṃ-duṭṭhagāmiṇi bhūpati,

Na hanissatu eḷāraṃ-maṃ muñciya paro iti;

Having had a drum beaten there, King Duṭṭhagāmaṇī proclaimed: "Let no other but myself slay Eḷāra."

Sannaddho sayamāruyha-sannaddhaṃ kaṇḍulaṃ kariṃ,

Eḷāraṃ anubaṇdhanto-dakkhiṇadvāra māgami;

Armored, he himself mounted the armored elephant Kaṇḍula. Pursuing Eḷāra, he came to the southern gate.

Pure dakkhiṇabhāgamhi-ubho yujjhiṃsu bhūmipā,

Tomaraṃ khipi eḷāro-gāmaṇi taṃ avañcayi;

In the southern part of the city, the two kings fought. Eḷāra hurled a dart; Gāmaṇī evaded it.

Vijjhāpesi ca dantehi-taṃ hatthiṃ sakahatthinā,

Tomaraṃ khipi eḷāro-sahatthi tattha so pati;

And with his own elephant, he had that elephant pierced with its tusks. Eḷāra hurled his dart; he fell there together with his elephant.

Tato vijita saṅgāmo-sayoggabalavāhano,

Laṅkā ekātapattaṃ so-katvāna pāvisi puraṃ;”

Then, victorious in battle, with his retinue, forces, and vehicles, he, having brought Laṅkā under a single parasol, entered the city.

Atha rājā nagare bheriṃ carāpetvā samantā yojanappamāṇe manusse sannipātetvā eḷāra rañño sarīraṃ mahantaṃ sakkāraṃ kāretvā kūṭāgārena netvā jhāpetvā tattha teciyaṃ kāretvā parihāramadāsi. Ajjapi rājāno taṃ padesampatvā bheriṃ na vādāpenti. Evaṃ duṭṭhagāmaṇi abhaya mahārājā dvattiṃsa damiḷa rājāno māretvā laṅkādīpaṃ ekacchattamakāsi.

Then the king, having had a drum beaten in the city, assembled the people from a yojana all around and, paying great honor to the body of King Eḷāra, had it carried in a palanquin, cremated, a cetiya built there, and granted endowments for it. Even to this day, kings, upon reaching that place, do not have their drums beaten. Thus Duṭṭhagāmaṇī Abhaya, the great king, having slain thirty-two Damiḷa kings, brought the island of Laṅkā under a single parasol.

Yadā duṭṭhagāmaṇi vijitaṃ nagaraṃ gaṇhi tadā dīghajantu yodho eḷāraṃ upasaṅkamitvā attano bhāgineyyassa bhallukassa yodha bhāvaṃ ācikkhitvā idhāgamanatthāya tassa santikaṃ pesesi. Bhallukopi eḷārassa daḍḍhadivasato sattame divase saṭṭhiyā purisa [O 0.0054] sahassehi saddhiṃ otiṇṇo rañño matabhāvaṃ sutvāpi lajjāya yujjhissāmīti mahātitthato nikkhamitvā kolambahālake nāma gāme khaṇdhāvāraṃ nivesesi. Rājāpi tassā’gamanaṃ sutvā sannaddho kaṇḍulaṃ āruyha yodhaparivuto mahatā balakāyena abhinikkhami. Phussadevopi pañcāvudha sannaddho rañño pacchimāsane nisīdi. Bhallukopi pañcāvudha sannaddho hatthiṃ āruyha rājābhimukho agañchi. Tadā kaṇḍulo tassa vegamaṇdi bhāvatthaṃ sanikaṃ sanikaṃ paccosakaki senāpi hatthinā saddhiṃ tatheva paccosakaki.

When King Duṭṭhagāmaṇī captured the fortress of Vijita, the warrior Dīghajantu approached Eḷāra and, having informed him of his nephew Bhalluka’s prowess as a warrior, sent for him to come there. On the seventh day from Eḷāra's cremation, Bhalluka, accompanied by sixty thousand men, also landed. Though he heard of the king’s death, out of shame he resolved to fight. Departing from Mahātittha, he encamped in a village named Kolambahālaka. The king, hearing of his approach, armed himself, mounted his elephant Kaṇḍula, and set out with a great force surrounded by warriors. Phussadeva, also armed with the five weapons, sat on the king’s rear seat. Bhalluka, likewise armed with the five weapons, mounted an elephant and advanced toward the king. Then Kaṇḍula, to check his opponent's speed, gradually retreated, and the army also retreated along with the elephant.

Rājā phussadevaṃ āha ayaṃ hatthi pubbe aṭṭhavīsatiyā yuddhesu apaccosakakitvā idāni kasmā pana paccosakkatīti. So āhādeva. Amhākameva jayo ayaṃ gajo jayabhūmiṃ avekkhanto paccosakkati jayabhūmiṃ patvā ṭhassatīti? Nāgopi paccosakakitva pura devassa passe mahāvihāra sīmante aṭṭhāsi.

The king said to Phussadeva, "This elephant has never retreated in twenty-eight previous battles. Why then does it retreat now?" He replied, "Your Majesty, victory is surely ours. This elephant retreats while seeking a ground of victory; upon reaching the ground of victory, it will stand firm." The great elephant, having retreated, stood near the royal city, at the boundary of the Mahāvihāra.

Tato bhalluko rājābhimukhā āgantvā rājānaṃ uppaṇḍesi rājāpi khaggatalena mukhaṃ pidhāya taṃ akkosi rañño mukhe vijjhissāmīti saraṃ khipi. So khaggatala māgaccabhūmiyaṃ pati, bhalluko mukhe viddhesmiti saññāya ukkuṭṭhi, akāsi tadā rañño pacchimāsane nisinno phussadevo rañño kuṇḍalaṃ ghaṭento tassa mukhe kaṇḍaṃ pātesi rañño pāde katvā patamānassa jāṇumhi aparena kaṇḍena vijjhitvā rañño sīsaṃ katvā pātesi. Rājā laddhajayo nagaraṃ āgantvā saraṃ āharāpetvā puṅkhena ujukaṃ ṭhapāpetvā taṃ pamāṇaṃ kahāpaṇarāsiṃ katvā phussadevassa adāsi.

Then Bhalluka came towards the king and taunted him. The king covered his face with the flat of his sword and rebuked him. Bhalluka shot an arrow, thinking, “I will strike the king in the mouth!” The arrow struck the flat of the sword and fell to the ground. Bhalluka, thinking he had struck the king in the mouth, uttered a cry of triumph. At that moment, Phussadeva, sitting on the king’s rear seat, while touching the king's earring, shot an arrow into Bhalluka's mouth. As Bhalluka was falling, Phussadeva pierced his knee with another arrow and made his head fall. The king, having gained victory, returned to the city. He had the arrow brought, set it upright on its feathered end, and made a pile of coins equal to its height, which he gave to Phussadeva.

Evaṃ laṅkārajjaṃ ekacchattaṃ katvā rājā yodhānaṃ yathānurūpaṃ ṭhānantaraṃ adāsi. Theraputtābhayo pana dīyamānaṃ ṭṭhānantaraṃ na gaṇhi kasmā na gaṇhasiti pucchino yuddhaṃ atthi mahārājāti āhaṃ. Idāni ekarajje kate kiṃ nāma yuddhanti pucchite kilesa corehi yujjhissa mīti āha. Rājā punappunaṃ nivāresi sopi punappuna yācitvā rājānuññāya pabbajitvā vipassanāya kammaṃ karonto arahattaṃ patvā pañca khīṇāsavasata parivāro ahosi.

Thus, having brought the kingdom of Laṅkā under a single parasol, the king gave positions to the warriors according to their merit. But Theraputtābhaya did not accept the position being offered. When asked, "Why do you not accept it?" he said, "Great king, there is a war to be fought." When asked, "Now that a single kingdom has been established, what war is there?" he said, "I will fight with the thieves which are the defilements." The king repeatedly tried to dissuade him, but he repeatedly requested and, with the king's permission, went forth. Practicing insight meditation, he attained Arahantship and dwelt surrounded by five hundred Arahants.

Tato rājā attano pāsādatale sirīsayanagato mahatiṃ sampattiṃ oloketvā akkhobhiṇi senāghātaṃ anussari. Anussarantassa rañño mahantaṃ demanassaṃ uppajji sagga maggantarāyo me bhaveyyāti.

Then the king, lying on his royal bed on the palace terrace, beheld his great prosperity and recalled the slaughter of an immense army. As he recalled this, great dejection arose in the king, thinking, “This may be an obstacle for me on the path to heaven.”

Tadā piyaṅgudīpe arahanto rañño paricitakkaṃ ñatvā taṃ assāsetuṃ aṭṭha arahante pesse. Te āgantvā āgatabhāvā nivedetvā [O 0.0055] pāsādatalaṃ abhiruhiṃsu rājā there vanditvā āsane nisīdāpetvā āgata kāraṇaṃ pucchi. Therāpi āgata kāraṇaṃ vatvā rañño tena kammunā sagga-mokkhantarāyabhāvaṃ bodhetva pakkamiṃsu rājā tesaṃ vacanaṃ sutvā assāsaṃ paṭilabhitvā vanditvā (te vissajjetvā) sirisayanagato puna cintesi.

At that time, the Arahants on the island of Piyaṅgudīpa, knowing the king’s thoughts, sent eight Arahants to console him. They arrived, announced their arrival, and ascended the palace terrace. The king paid homage to the elders, seated them, and asked the reason for their coming. The elders explained their purpose, making the king understand that that action was not an obstacle to his path to heaven and liberation, and then they departed. Having heard their words, the king regained his composure, paid homage to them, and dismissed them. Retiring to his royal bed, he reflected once more.

Mātipitaro kho pana mā co kadācipi vinā saṅghena āhāraṃ bhuñjathāti amhehi sapathaṃ kāresuṃ bhikkhusaṅghassa adatvā bhuttaṃ atthi nu kho natthiti cintayanto satisammosena saṅghassa adatvā pātarāsakāle paribhuttaṃ ekaṃyeva maricavaṭṭiṃ addasa. Disvā ca ayuttaṃ mayā kataṃ daṇḍakammaṃ me kātabbantī cintesi atha rājā chattamaṅgala sattāhe vītivatte mahatā rājānubhāvena mahantena kīḷāvidhānena udakakīḷaṃ kīḷituṃ abhisittānaṃ rājunaṃ cārittānu pālanatthañca tissavāpiṃ agamāsi. Rañño sabbaṃ paricchadaṃ upāhanachattāni ca maricavaṭṭi vihāraṭṭhānamhi ṭhapayiṃsu.

“My parents made us swear an oath, saying, ‘Do not ever eat food without first offering to the Saṅgha.’ Considering whether I had ever eaten without giving to the Saṅgha, I remembered that, due to a lapse in mindfulness, I had eaten just one chili pod during the morning meal without offering it to the Saṅgha. Seeing this in my mind, I thought, ‘I have done what is improper. A penance must be performed by me.’ Then, after the seven-day festival of the royal parasol had passed, the king, with great royal splendor and grand festivities, went to the Tissavāpi to enjoy water sports and to maintain the customs of anointed kings. All the king's regalia, including his sandals and parasol, were placed at the site of the Maricavaṭṭi Vihāra.

Tatrāpi thūpaṭṭhāne rājapurisā rañño sadhātukaṃ kuntaṃ ujukaṃ ṭhapesuṃ. Rājā divasabhāgaṃ orodha parivuto kīḷitvā sāyaṇhe jāte nagaraṃ gamissāma kuntaṃ vaḍḍhethāti āha. Rājapurisā kuntaṃ gaṇhantā cāletuṃ nāsakkhiṃsu rājasena, taṃ acchariyaṃ disvā samāgantvā gaṇdhamālādīhi pūjesi rājāpi mahantaṃ acchariyaṃ disvā haṭṭhamānaso samantā ārakkhaṃ saṃvidahitvā nagaraṃ pāvisi.

There, at the place for the stūpa, the king's men set upright the king's spear which contained relics. The king, having sported for the day surrounded by his harem, said when evening came, “We will go to the city; take up the spear.” The king's men, attempting to take the spear, were unable to move it. The royal army, seeing this marvel, gathered and honored it with perfumes, garlands, and so on. The king, also seeing this great marvel, became delighted and, having arranged for protection all around, entered the city.

Tato rājā kuntaṃ parikkhipāpetvā cetiyaṃ taṃ parikkhipāpetvā vihārañca kāresi. Vihāro tīhi saṃvaccharehi niṭṭhāsiṃ rājā vihāramahatthāya saṅghaṃ sannipātesi bhikkhūnaṃ satasahassāni bhikkhunīnaṃ navuti sahassāni sannipatiṃsu tasmiṃ samāgame rājā saṅghaṃ vanditvā evamāha bhante vissaritvā vinā saṅghena maricavaṭṭikaṃ paribhuñjiṃ tadatthaṃ daṇḍakammaṃ me hotūti-sacetiyaṃ maricavaṭṭiyaṃ vihāraṃ kāresiṃ patigaṇhātu bhante saṅgho sacetiyaṃ vihāranti dakkhiṇodakaṃ pātetvā bhikkhusaṅghassa vihāraṃ adāsi.

Then the king had a cetiya built enclosing the spear, and also had a monastery built enclosing that. The monastery was completed in three years. The king assembled the Saṅgha for the dedication of the monastery. One hundred thousand monks and ninety thousand nuns assembled. At that gathering, the king, having paid homage to the Saṅgha, said, “Venerable sirs, through forgetfulness I partook of a chili pod without the Saṅgha. For that, let this be my penance: I have had this Maricavaṭṭi Vihāra with its cetiya built. May the Saṅgha, venerable sirs, accept this monastery with its cetiya.” Pouring the water of donation, he gave the monastery to the Saṅgha of monks.

Vihārassa samantato bhikkhusaṅghassa nisīdanatthāya mahantaṃ maṇḍapaṃ kāresi maṇḍapa pādā abhayavāpiyā jale patiṭṭhitā ahesuṃ. Sesokāse kathācanatthi. Tattha bhikkhusaṅghaṃ nisīdāpetvā sattāhaṃ mahādānaṃ datvā sabbaparikkhāraṃ adāsi. Tattha saṅghattherena laddha parikkhāro satasahassagghanako ahosi.

Around the monastery, he had a large pavilion built for the Saṅgha of monks to be seated. The pillars of the pavilion were established in the waters of the Abhayavāpi. There is no further account of that place. There, having seated the Saṅgha of monks, for seven days he gave a great donation, providing all the requisites. The requisites received by the elder of the Saṅgha there were worth a hundred thousand.

Evaṃ- [O 0.0056]

Thus—

“Yuddhe dāne ca sūrena-sūrinā ratanattame,

Pasannāmalacittena-sāsanujjotanatthinā;

“By the hero in battle and in giving, by the one wise concerning the Triple Gem, with a clear and stainless mind, desiring to illuminate the Teaching;

Raññā kataññunā tena-thūpakārāpanādito,

Vihāramahanantāni - pūjetuṃ ratanattayaṃ;

by that grateful king, beginning with the construction of stūpas and great monasteries in order to honor the Triple Gem;

Pariccattadhanānettha-anagghāni vimuñciya,

Sesāni honti ekāya-ūnā visatikoṭiyo;”

the wealth expended on these, excepting those things that were priceless, amounted to nineteen crores.”

Evaṃ sapañño bhidūre asāre,

Dehe dhane saṅgamatikkamitvā

Katvāna puññaṃ sukhasādanatthaṃ,

Sāraṃ gahetuṃ satataṃ yateyyāti;

Thus a wise person, having overcome attachment to the fragile and essenceless body and wealth, having made merit for the attainment of happiness, should always strive to grasp the essence.

Marīcavaṭṭi vihārakathā

The Story of the Maricavaṭṭi Vihāra

18. Tato rājā cintesi mahāmahiṇdatthero kira mama ayyakassa devānampiyatissa rañño evamāha. Nattā te mahārāja duṭṭhagāmaṇi abhayo visaṃ hatthasatikaṃ sovaṇṇamāliṃ thūpaṃ kāressati. Saṅghassa ca uposathāgārabhūtaṃ navabhūmakaṃ lohapāsādaṃ kāressatīti cintetvā ca pana olokento rājagehe karaṇḍake ṭhapitaṃ suvaṇṇapaṭṭalekhaṃ disvā taṃ vācesi. Anāgate cattālīsaṃ vassasataṃ atikkamma kākavaṇṇatissassa putto duṭṭhagāmaṇi abhayo idañcidañca kāressatīti sutvā haṭṭho udaggo appoṭhesi-ayyena kira vatamhi diṭṭho mahā mahiṇdenāti.

18. Then the king thought: "The Great Elder Mahinda, it is said, spoke thus to my grandfather, King Devānampiyatissa: 'Your grandson, O great king, Duṭṭhagāmaṇi Abhaya, will build a stupa called Sovannamali, one hundred and twenty cubits high, and will build a nine-storied Lohapāsāda for the Sangha as an Uposatha hall.'" Having reflected thus, and while looking around, he saw a golden plaque placed in a casket in the royal palace and had it read. Hearing that "In the future, after four thousand years have passed, Duṭṭhagāmaṇi Abhaya, the son of Kākavaṇṇatissa, will build this and that," he became joyful and elated, clapping his hands: "Indeed, I have been seen by the noble Elder Mahinda!"

Tato pātova mahāmeghavanaṃ gantvā bhikkhusaṅghaṃ sannipātetvā etadavoca? Bhante bhikkhusaṅghassa uposathāgāraṃ katvā devavimāna sadisaṃ pāsādaṃ kāressāmi devalokaṃ pesetvā paṭe vimānākāraṃ likhāpetvā me dethāti? Saṅgho aṭṭha khīṇāsave pesesi. Te tāvatiṃsabhavanaṃ gantvā dvādasayojanubbedhā aṭṭhacattālīsa yojana parikkhepaṃ kūṭāgāraṃ sahassa patimaṇḍitaṃ navabhūmakaṃ sahassagabbhaṃ khīraṇa devadhītāya puññānubhāvanibbattaṃ ākāsaṭṭhaṃ ratanapāsādaṃ oloketvā hiṅgulakena paṭe tadākāraṃ likhitvā ānetvā bhikkhusaṅghassa adaṃsu saṅgho rañño pāhesi.

Then, early in the morning, he went to the Mahāmeghavana, assembled the community of monks, and said: "Venerables, I will have an Uposatha hall built for the Sangha, a palace resembling a celestial mansion. Send someone to the heavenly realm to have the mansion's design drawn on cloth and bring it to me." The Sangha sent eight Arahants. They went to the Tāvatiṃsa realm and beheld the palace, twelve yojanas high and forty-eight yojanas in circumference, adorned with a thousand pinnacles, having nine stories and a thousand chambers—a celestial mansion born from the merit of Khīraṇā, the divine daughter, standing in the sky. Observing that jeweled palace, they drew its likeness on a cloth with vermilion and brought it back to the Sangha, who presented it to the king.

Taṃ disvā rājā tuṭṭhamānaso tadā taṃ lekhatulyaṃ lohapāsādaṃ kāresi. Kammantārambha kāle pana catusu dvāresu aṭṭhasatasahassāni hiraññāni ṭhapāpesi tadā catusu dvāresu sahassa sahassaṃ vatthapuṭāni ceva guḷa-tela-sakkhara-madhupurā anekasahassacāṭiyo ca [O 0.0057] ṭhapāpesi. Pāsāde amūlakena kammaṃ na kātabbanti bheriṃ cārapetvā amūlakena katakammaṃ agghāpetvā kārakānaṃ mūlaṃ dāpesi. Pāsādo ekekena passena hatthasata hatthasatappamāṇo ahosi tathā ubbedhana, navabhūmāyo cassa ahesuṃ ekekissā bhūmiyā sataṃ sataṃ kūṭāgārāni, tāni sabbānipi ratanakhacitāni ceva suvaṇṇa kiṅkiṇikāpanti parikkhittāni ca ahesuṃ tesaṃ kūṭāgārāni nānāratana bhūsikā pavāḷa vedikā ceva, tāsaṃ padumāni ca nānāratana vicittāneva ahesuṃ. Tathā sahassagabbhā ca nānāratana khacitā sīyapañjara vibhūsitā ca. Vessavanassa nārivābhanayānaṃ sutvā tadākāraṃ majjhe ratana maṇḍapaṃ kāresi.

Seeing it, the king, delighted in mind, then had a Brazen Palace built identical to the drawing. At the start of the construction, he placed eight hundred thousand silver coins at the four gates. At the four gates, he also arranged a million bundles of cloth, along with many thousands of jars filled with molasses, oil, sugar, and honey. Proclaiming with drums that no work should be done without payment, he assessed the value of work done without payment and had the workers paid for it. The palace measured a hundred cubits on each side, and its height was also a hundred cubits. It had nine stories, each with a hundred gabled chambers, all inlaid with jewels and encircled by rows of golden tinkling bells. Those gabled chambers had coral railings adorned with various gems, and their lotuses were embellished with diverse jewels. Likewise, the thousand chambers were inlaid with various gems and adorned with latticed windows. Hearing of Vessavaṇa’s Nārivāhana vehicle, he also built a jeweled pavilion in the center, resembling it.

So anekehi ratanatthambhehi sīhavyagghādi rūpehi devatā rūpehi ca patimaṇḍito samantato olambaka muttā jālena ca parikkhitto ahosi pavāḷavedikā cassa pubbe vuttappakārāva sattaratana vicittamaṇḍapa majjhe pana eḷikamaya bhūmiyā dantamaya pallaṅko ahosi apassenampi dantamayameva, so suvaṇṇasūriyamaṇḍalehi rajata caṇda maṇḍalehi muttāmaya tārakāhi ca vicitto tattha tattha yathārahaṃ nānāratanamaya padumāni ceva pasāda janakāni ca jātakāni antarantarā suvaṇṇalatāyo ca kāresi. Tattha mahagghaṃ paccattharaṇaṃ attharitvā manuññaṃ danta vijaniṃ ṭhapesi. Pavāḷamaya pādukā kāresi. Tathā pallaṅkassopari eḷikabhūmiyā patiṭṭhitaṃ rajatamayadaṇḍaṃ setaccattaṃ kāresi. Tattha sattaratanamayāni aṭṭhamaṅgalāni antarantarā ca maṇimuttāmayā catuppāda pantiyo ca kāresi. Chattante cassa ratanamaya ghaṇṭāpantiyo olambiṃsu.

It was adorned with numerous jeweled pillars, figures of lions, tigers, and deities, and surrounded on all sides by hanging pearl nets; its coral railings were as described before. In the center of the pavilion, variegated with the seven jewels, on a floor of fleece, stood an ivory couch. Even its backrest was made of ivory. It was decorated with golden sun disks, silver moon disks, and pearl stars. He had lotus flowers of various precious gems, delightful Jātaka scenes, and golden vines arranged at appropriate intervals. A priceless spread was laid out there, and a lovely ivory fan was placed. Coral sandals were made. And above the couch, established on the fleece floor, a white parasol with a silver staff was erected. There, the eight auspicious symbols made of the seven jewels were placed, along with rows of gem-and-pearl footstools at intervals. From the edge of the parasol, rows of jeweled bells hung down.

Pāsādo chattaṃ pallaṅko maṇḍapo cāti cattāro anagghā ahesuṃ mahagghāni mañcapīṭhāni paññatvo tattha mahagghāni kambalāni bhummattharaṇāni attharāpesi āvamana kumhi uḷuṅko ca sovaṇṇamayāyeva ahesuṃ. Sesa paribhoga bhaṇḍesu vattabbameva natthi dvārakoṭṭhakopi manohara pākārena parikkhitto. Tambalohiṭṭhikābhi pana chāditattā pāsādassa lohapāsādoti vohāro ahosi.

The palace, the parasol, the couch, and the pavilion—these four were priceless. Expensive beds and chairs were arranged there, and costly blankets and floor coverings were spread. The water pot for rinsing and the ladle were made of pure gold. There was nothing further to be said about the remaining articles of use. The gatehouse too was enclosed by a delightful wall. Because the palace was covered with copper tiles, it came to be known as the Brazen Palace.

Evaṃ tāvatiṃsabhavane devasabhā viya pāsādaṃ niṭṭhāpetvā saṅghaṃ sannipātesi. Maricavaṭṭi vihāramage viya saṅgho sannipati. Paṭhamabhūmiyaṃ puthujjanāyeva aṭṭhaṃsu dutiyabhūmiyā tepiṭakā, tatiyādisu tīsu bhūmisu kamena sotāpanna - sakadāgāmi - anāgāmino, upari catusu bhūmisu khīṇāsavāyeva aṭṭhaṃsu. Evaṃ saṅghaṃ sannipātetvā saṅghassa pāsādaṃ datvā maricavaṭṭi vihāramahe viya sattāhaṃ mahādānamadāsīti.

Thus, having completed the palace like the assembly hall of the gods in the Tāvatiṃsa heaven, he gathered the Sangha. The Sangha assembled as if for the festival of the Maricavaṭṭi monastery. On the first floor, only worldlings stood; on the second floor, those proficient in the Tipiṭaka; on the next three floors, in order, Stream-enterers, Once-returners, and Non-returners; and on the upper four floors, only Arahants stood. Having gathered the Sangha in this way and given the palace to the Sangha, he offered a great almsgiving for seven days, just as at the festival of the Maricavaṭṭi monastery.

“Pāsādahetu [O 0.0058] cattāni-mahācāgena rājinā,

Anagghāni ṭhapetvāna-ahesuṃ tiṃsakoṭiyo;”

Excluding the four priceless items, the cost for the palace, by the king of great generosity, amounted to thirty crores.

Pahāya gamanīyantaṃ-datvāna dhanasañcayaṃ,

Anugāmidhanaṃ dānaṃ-evaṃ kubbanti paṇḍitā;

Forsaking that which must be left behind, having given away accumulated wealth, the wise give a gift that is a treasure which follows them; thus do the wise act.

19. Athekadivasaṃ rājā satasahassaṃ vissajjetvā mahābodhi pūjaṃ kāretvā nagaraṃ pavisanto thūpaṭṭhāne patiṭṭhitaṃ silāthūpaṃ disvā mahiṇdattherena vuttavacanaṃ anussaritvā mahāthūpaṃ kāressāmīti katasanniṭṭhāno nagaraṃ pavisitvā mahaṃtalaṃ āruyha subhojanaṃ bhuñjitvā sirisayanagato evaṃ cintesi mayā damiḷe maddamānena ayaṃ loko ativiya pīḷito, kena nu kho upāyena lokassa piḷanaṃ akatvā dhammena samena mahā cetiyassa anucchavikaṃ iṭṭhakā uppādessāmīti taṃ cintitaṃ chattaṃ adhivatthā devatā jānitvā rājā evaṃ cintesīti ugghosesi.

19. One day, after spending a hundred thousand and arranging a great offering to the Mahābodhi, the king, while entering the city, saw a stone monument standing at the site of the stupa. Remembering the words spoken by the Elder Mahinda, he resolved, "I will build a great stupa," and entered the city. Ascending to the upper floor, he enjoyed a fine meal and, lying on the royal couch, thought thus: "While I was crushing the Tamils, this populace was greatly afflicted. By what means can I produce bricks suitable for the great cetiya righteously and justly, without afflicting the people?" The deity inhabiting his parasol, knowing that the king was thinking thus, proclaimed it aloud.

Paramparāya devalokepi kolāhalamahosi taṃ ñatvā sakko devarājaṃ vissakammaṃ āmantetvā’tāta! Vissa kamma! Duṭṭhagāmaṇi abhaya mahārājā mahā cetiyassa iṭṭhakatthāya cintesi. Tvaṃ gantvā uttarapasse nagarato yojanappamāṇe ṭhāne gambhira nadiyā tīre iṭṭhakā māpetvā ehi’ti pesesi.

Through successive reports, a great commotion arose even in the heavenly realms. Knowing this, Sakka, the king of the gods, summoned Vissakamma and said, "My dear Vissakamma! The great king Duṭṭhagāmaṇi Abhaya is considering how to obtain bricks for the great cetiya. Go and have bricks made on the bank of a deep river, about a yojana from the city to the north, and then return." Thus he sent him.

Taṃ ñatvā vissakamma devaputto āgantvā tattheva mahācetiyānucchavikaṃ iṭṭhakā māpetvā devapurameva gato. Puna divase eko sunakhaluddo sunakhe gahetvā araññaṃ gantvā tattha tattha vicaranto taṃ ṭhānaṃ patvā iṭṭhakā adisvā ca nikkhami. Tasmiṃ khaṇe ekā bhummā devatā tassa iṭṭhakā dassetuṃ mahantaṃ godhāvaṇṇaṃ gahetvā luddassa sunakhānañca attānaṃ dassetvā tehi anubaddho iṭṭhakābhimukhaṃ attvā antaradhāyi.

Understanding this, the divine son Vissakamma came and, right there, had bricks made suitable for the great cetiya before returning to the city of the gods. The next day, a hunter with his dogs went into the forest and, wandering here and there, reached that spot. Not seeing the bricks, he departed. At that moment, an earth-deity, wishing to show him the bricks, took on the appearance of a large iguana and revealed itself to the hunter and his dogs. Pursued by them, it fled toward the bricks and then vanished.

Sunakhaluddo iṭṭhakā disvā amhākaṃ rājā thūpaṃ kāretukāmo, mahanto vata no paṇṇākāro laddhoti haṭṭhamānaso puna divase pātoca āgantvā attanā diṭṭhaṃ iṭṭhaka paṇṇākāraṃ rañño nivedesi. Rājā taṃ sāsanaṃ sutvā attamano hutvā tassa mahantaṃ sakkāraṃ kāretvā taṃyeva iṭṭhaka gopanaṃ kāresi. Tato rājā ahameva iṭṭhakolokanatthāya gacchāmi - kuntaṃ vaḍḍhethāti āha.

The dog-hunter, seeing the bricks, thought joyfully, 'Our king wishes to have a stupa built—what a great gift has been obtained by us!' The next day, he came early in the morning and reported to the king the gift of bricks he had seen. Hearing this message, the king was delighted, had him greatly honored, and appointed him guardian of those very bricks. Then the king said, 'I myself will go to inspect the bricks—prepare the spear.'

Tasmiṃyeva khaṇe puna aññaṃ sāsanaṃ āhariṃsu. Nagarato tiyojanamatthake ṭhāne pubbuttarakaṇṇe ācāra viṭṭhigāme tiyāmarattiṃ abhippavaṭṭe deve soḷasa karīsappamāṇe padese suvaṇṇabījāni uṭṭhahiṃsu. Tāni pamāṇato ukkaṭṭhāni vidatthippamāṇāni. Omakāni aṭṭhaṅgulappamāṇāni ahesuṃ, atha vibhātāya rattiyā [O 0.0059] gāmavāsino suvaṇṇabijāni disvā rāchā’rahaṃ vata bhaṇḍaṃ uppannanti samantato ārakkhā saṃvidahitvā suvaṇṇabījāni pātiyaṃ pūretvā āgantvā rañño dassesuṃ rājā tesampi yathārahaṃ sakkāraṃ kāretvā teyeva suvaṇṇa gopake akāsi.

At that very moment, they brought another message. In a place three yojanas from the city, in the northeast direction, in the village of Ācāra Viṭṭhi, after it had rained continuously for three watches of the night, golden seeds sprouted in an area measuring sixteen karīsas. The largest of them were a span in size, while the smallest were the size of eight fingers. Then, at dawn, the villagers saw the golden seeds and thought, 'Truly, a treasure worthy of a king has arisen!' After setting up guards all around, they filled a bowl with the golden seeds, came, and showed them to the king. The king had them also honored as was fitting and appointed those very villagers as guardians of the gold.

Atha tasmiṃyeva khaṇe aññaṃ sāsanaṃ āhariṃsu. Nagarato pācīnapasse sattayojana matthako ṭhāne pāragaṅgāya tambaviṭṭhi nāma janapade tambalohaṃ uppajji. Gāmikā pātiṃ pūretvā tambalohaṃ gahetvā āgantvā rañño dassesuṃ rājā yathānurūpaṃ sakkāraṃ tesampi kāretvā teyeva gopake akāsi.

Then at that very moment, another message was brought. In a place seven yojanas to the east of the city, in the district called Tambaviṭṭhi, beyond the Gaṅgā, copper arose. The villagers filled a bowl with the copper, brought it, and showed it to the king. The king, having had them also honored appropriately, made those very villagers guardians.

Tadanantaraṃ aññaṃ sāsanaṃ āhariṃsu. Purato catuyojanamatthako ṭhāne pubbadakkhiṇa kaṇṇe sumanavāpigāme uppalakuruviṇda missakā bahū maṇayo uppajjiṃsu gāmikā pātiṃ pūretvā āgantvā mānayo rañño dassesuṃ. Rājā tesampi sakkāraṃ kāretvā teyeva gopake akāsi.

Thereupon, another message was brought. In a place four yojanas from the city, in the southeastern corner, in the village of Sumanavāpi, many gems, mixed with uppala and kuruvinda, arose. The villagers filled a bowl and, coming, showed the gems to the king. The king, having had them also honored, made those very people guardians.

Tadanantaraṃ aññampi sāsanaṃ āhariṃsu. Nagarato dakkhiṇa passe aṭṭhayojana matthake ṭhāne ambaṭṭhakola janapade ekasmiṃ leṇe rajataṃ uppajji tasmiṃ samaye nagaravāsiko eko vāṇijo bahūhi sakaṭehi haḷiddi siṅgiverādīnamatthāya malayaṃ gato, leṇassa avidūre sakaṭāni muñcitvā patodadāruṃ pariyesanto taṃ pabbataṃ abhirūḷho ekaṃ paṇasayaṭṭhiṃ addasa.

Thereupon, another message was brought. In a place eight yojanas to the south of the city, in the Ambaṭṭhakola district, silver arose in a cave. At that time, a merchant from the city, having gone to Malaya with many carts for turmeric, ginger, and so on, unharnessed his carts not far from the cave. While searching for a goad-stick, he climbed that mountain and saw a jackfruit branch.

Tassa mahantaṃ cāṭippamāṇaṃ ekameva paṇasa phalaṃ naruṇayaṭṭhiṃ nāmetvā heṭṭhā pāsāṇapiṭṭhiyaṃ aṭṭhāsi. So taṃ phalabhārena namitaṃ disvā upagantvā hatthena parāmasitvā pakkabhāvaṃ ñatvā vaṇṭaṃ chiṇdi paṇasayaṭṭhi uggantvā yaṭāṭṭhānaṃ aṭṭhāsi vāṇijo aggaṃ datvā bhuñjissāmīti cintetvā kālaṃ ghosesi. Tadā cattāro khīṇāsava āgantvā tassa purato pāturahesuṃ. Vāṇijo te disvā attamano pāde vanditvā nisīdāpetvā tassa phalassa vaṇṭāsamantā vāsiyā tacchetvā apassayaṃ luñcitvā apanāmesi. Samantato yusaṃ otaritvā apassayānitaṃ āvāṭaṃ pūresi vāṇijo manosilodakavaṇṇa paṇasayusaṃ patte pūretvā adāsi. Te khīṇāsavā tassa passantasseva ākāsamabbhuggantvā pakkamiṃsu.

On it was a single jackfruit as large as a pot, which bent the young branch down so that it rested on the stone slab below. Seeing the branch bent by the weight of the fruit, he approached, touched it, and knowing it was ripe, cut the stem. The jackfruit branch sprang back to its former position. The merchant, intending to eat it after offering the first portion, announced the time. Then four arahants arrived and appeared before him. The merchant, delighted to see them, paid homage at their feet, seated them, and having cut around the stem of the fruit with an adze, plucked out and removed the core. Juice flowed down from all sides and filled the hollow created by the removal of the core. The merchant filled bowls with jackfruit juice the color of red arsenic solution and offered it to them. The arahants, while he was still watching, rose into the sky and departed.

So puna kālaṃghosesi aññe cattaro khīṇāsavā āgamiṃsu. Tesampi hatthato patte gahetvā suvaṇṇavaṇṇehi pana samiñjehi pūretvā adāsi tesu tayo therā ākāsena pakkamiṃsu itaro iṇdaguttatthero nāma khīṇāsavo tassa taṃ rajataṃ [O 0.0060] dassetukāmo upari pabbatā otaritvā tassa leṇassa avidūre nisīditvā paṇasa miñjaṃ paribhuñjati upāsako therassa gatakāle avasesa miñjaṃ attanāpi khāditvā sesakaṃ bhaṇḍikaṃ katvā ādāya gacchanto theraṃ disvā udakañca pattadhovanasākhañca adāsi.

He announced the time again, and another four arahants arrived. Taking the bowls from their hands, he filled them with golden-hued jackfruit pulp and offered it. Three of the elders departed through the sky, but the other, the arahant named Elder Indagutta, wishing to show him that silver, descended from the mountain and sat not far from that cave, eating the jackfruit pulp. The lay devotee ate the remaining pulp himself, made a packet of the rest, and taking it with him, saw the elder as he was leaving. He offered him water and a twig for washing the bowl.

Theropi leṇadvārena sakaṭa samīpagāmi maggaṃ māpetvā iminā maggena gaccha upāsakāti āha. So theraṃ vanditvā tena maggena gacchanto leṇadvāraṃ patvā samantā leṇaṃ olokento taṃ rajatarāsiṃ disvā rajatapiṇḍaṃ gahetvā cāsiyā chiṇditvā rajatabhāvaṃ ñatvā mahantaṃ sajjhapiṇḍaṃ gahetvā sakaṭa santikaṃ gantvā tiṇodaka sampanne ṭhāne sakaṭāni nivesetvā lahuṃ anurādhapuraṃ gantvā raññño dassetvā tamatthaṃ nivedesi rājā tassāpi yathārahaṃ sakkāraṃ kāresi.

The elder, having created a path from the cave entrance toward the carts, said, 'Go by this path, lay devotee.' Paying homage to the elder, he followed that path and reached the cave entrance. Looking around the cave, he saw the pile of silver. Taking a lump of silver, he cut it with a small axe, and knowing it to be silver, took a large, fine lump. He went to where the carts were, stationed them in a spot rich in grass and water, and quickly went to Anurādhapura. Showing it to the king, he reported the matter. The king had him also honored as was fitting.

Tadantaraṃ aññampi sāsanaṃ āhariṃsu nagarato pacchima disābhāge pañca yojana matthake ṭhāne uruvela pabbata mahāmalakamattā pacāḷa missakā saṭṭhi sakaṭappamāṇamuttā samuddato thalamuggamiṃsu kevaṭṭā disvā rājārahaṃ vata bhaṇḍaṃ uppannanti rāsiṃ katvā ārakkhaṃ datvā pātiṃ pūretvā āgantvā rañño dassetvā tamatthaṃ nivedesuṃ. Rājā tesampi yathārahaṃ sakkāraṃ kāresi.

Thereupon, another message was brought. In a place five yojanas to the west of the city, sixty cartloads of pearls, mixed with coral as large as the great amalaka fruit of Uruvela Mountain, had emerged from the sea onto the shore. Fishermen, seeing them, thought, 'Truly, treasure fit for a king has arisen!' Having gathered them into a heap and set guards, they filled a basket, came, showed it to the king, and reported the matter. The king had them also honored as was fitting.

Puna aññaṃ sāsanaṃ āhariṃsu nagarato pacchimuttara kaṇṇe sattayojana matthake ṭhāne peḷivāpi gāmassa vāpiyā otiṇṇa kaṇdare pulina puṭṭhe nisadapotappamāṇa dīghato vidatthicaturaṅgulā ummāpupphavaṇṇā cattāro mahāmaṇi uppajjiṃsu. Atheko matto nāma sunakhaluddo sunakhe gahetvā tattha vicaranto taṃ ṭhānaṃ patvā disvā rājārahaṃ vata bhaṇḍanti vālikāhi paṭicchādetvā āgantvā rañño nivedesi. Rājā tassāpi yathārahaṃ sakkāraṃ kāresi. Evaṃ rājā thūpatthāya uppannāni iṭṭhakādīni tadaheva assosi iṭṭhaka rajatānaṃ uppannaṭṭhānaṃ teneva nāmaṃ labhi.

Again, another message was brought. In a place seven yojanas to the northwest of the city, near the reservoir of Peḷivāpi village, in a cave descending to the reservoir, on a sandy bank, four great gems arose. They were the color of ummā flowers, the size of a young nisada plant, and a span and four fingers in length. Then a dog-hunter named Matta, while wandering there with his dogs, reached that place and saw them. He thought, 'Truly, these are goods worthy of a king!' Covering them with sand, he came and informed the king. The king had him also honored as was fitting. Thus, on that very day, the king heard of the bricks and other things that had arisen for the stupa. The places where the bricks and silver arose received their names therefrom.

Thūpasādhana lābhakathā

The Account of Acquiring the Requisites for the Stupa

20. Atha rājā thūpatthāya uppannāni suvaṇṇādīni āharāpetvā bhaṇḍāgāresu rāsiṃ kāresi tato sabbasambhāre samatte visākha puṇṇamuposathadivase patta visākha nakkhatte mahāthūpakaraṇatthāya bhūmiparikammaṃ ārabhi. Rājā thupaṭṭhāne patiṭṭhāpitaṃ silāthūpaṃ harāpetvā thirabhāvatthāya samantato hatthipākāra pariyantaṃ gambhīrato sataratanappamāṇaṃ bhūmiṃ khanāpetvā paṃsuṃ apanetvā [O 0.0061] yodhehi guḷapāsāṇe attharāpetvā kammārakūṭehi āhanāpetvā cuṇṇavicuṇṇe kāresi.

20. Then the king, having had the gold and other materials that had arisen for the stupa brought, had them piled up in the treasuries. When all the requisites were assembled, on the full moon Uposatha day of Visakha, when the Visakha constellation was ascendant, he began the ground preparation for the construction of the great stupa. The king had the stone pillar that had been established at the stupa site removed. For the sake of stability, he had the ground excavated to a depth of one hundred ratanas all around within the boundary of the elephant-wall, and had the soil removed. He then had soldiers spread round stones, had them pounded with blacksmiths' hammers, and had them made into fine powder.

Tato cammavinaddhehi pādehi mahā hatthīhi maddāpetvā pāsāṇakoṭṭimassupari navanīta mattikaṃ attharāpesi. Ākāsa gaṅgāyahi tipatitaṭṭhāne udakabindūni uggantvā samantā tiṃsa yojanappamāṇapadese patanti yattha sayañjātasālī uppajjatti taṃ ṭhānaṃ niccameva tintattā nintasīsakoḷaṃ nāma chātaṃ. Tattha mattikā sukhumuttā navanīta mattikāti vuccati.

Then, with feet bound in leather, great elephants were made to tread upon it, and upon the stone pavement, a layer of butter-like clay was spread. From the celestial Ganges, drops of water rise at the place of the triple fall and then descend upon an area of thirty yojanas all around, where self-grown rice arises. That place, being always moist, is famed as Nintasīsakoḷa, the 'Drenched-Head Hollow.' There, the clay is exceedingly fine and is called butter-like clay.

Taṃ tato khīṇasavā sāmaṇerā āharanti tāya sabbattha mattikākiccanti ñātabbaṃ. Mattikopari iṭṭhakā attharāpesi. Iṭṭhakopari kharasukammaṃ, tassoparikuruviṇdapāsāṇaṃ, tassopariayojālaṃ, tassopari khīṇāsava sāmaṇerehi himavantato āhaṭaṃ sugaṇdhamārumbaṃ, tassopari khīrapāsāṇaṃ, tassopari eḷikapāsāṇaṃ, tassopari sīlaṃ attharāpesi. Sabbamattikākicce navanītamattikā eva ahosi.

The canker-free novices bring it from there. It should be understood that this clay was used for all clay work. Over the clay, he had bricks spread. Over the bricks, a rough plastering; over that, Kuruviṇḍa stone; over that, an iron mesh; over that, fragrant mārumba brought from the Himalayas by the canker-free novices; over that, milk-stone; over that, Eḷika stone; over that, he had a stone slab spread. In all the clay work, only butter-like clay was used.

Silāsatthāropari rasodaka santintena kapittha niyyāsena aṭṭhaṅgulabahalatambalohapaṭṭaṃ, tassopari tilatelayantintāya manosilāya sattaṅgula bahalaṃ rajatapaṭṭaṃ attharāpesi evaṃ rājā sabbākārena bhūmiparikammaṃ kārāpetvā āsāḷahi sukkapakkhassa cātuddasa divase bhikkhusaṅghā sannipātetvā evamāha. Sve puṇṇamuposathadivase uttarāsāḷha nakkhattena mahācetiye maṅgaliṭṭhakaṃ patiṭṭhāpessāmi sve thūpūṭṭhāne sabbo saṅgho santipatatūti nagare bheriṃ carāpesi mahājano uposathiko hutvā gaṇdhamālādīni gahetvā thūpaṭṭhāne sannipatatūti.

Over the stone slab, he had a copper plate eight fingers thick spread, which was smeared with kapittha resin moistened with fragrant water. Over that, he had a silver plate seven fingers thick spread, which was smeared with red arsenic moistened with sesame oil. Having thus had the ground prepared in every way, the king assembled the Sangha of bhikkhus on the fourteenth day of the waxing moon in the month of Āsāḷha and addressed them thus: 'Tomorrow, on the full moon Uposatha day, under the constellation of Uttarāsāḷha, I will establish the auspicious brick at the Great Cetiya. Let the entire Sangha gather at the stupa site tomorrow.' He then had the drum beaten throughout the city, announcing: 'Let the great populace observe the Uposatha, bring perfumes and garlands, and assemble at the stupa site.'

Tato visākhasiri devanāmake dve amacce āṇāpesi. Tumhe gantvā mahā cetiyaṭṭhānaṃ alaṅkarothāti. Te gantvā samantato rajatapaṭṭavaṇṇavālukaṃ okīrāpetvā lājapañcamakāni pupphāni vikiritvā kadalitoraṇaṃ ussāpetvā puṇṇaghaṭe ṭhapāpetvā maṇivaṇṇe veḷumhi pañcapaṇṇadhajaṃ baṇdhāpetvā gaṇdhasampannāni nānāvidha kusumānisaṇtharāpetvā nānāppakārehi taṃ ṭhānaṃ alaṅkariṃsu.

Then he commanded two ministers named Visākha and Sirideva: 'Go and adorn the great cetiya site.' They went and had sand the color of a silver sheet spread all around, scattered flowers with parched grain as the fifth, had banana-stalk archways erected, had full water pots placed, had a five-colored flag tied on a jewel-colored bamboo pole, had various kinds of fragrant flowers spread, and adorned that place in many ways.

Atha rājā sakalanagarañca vihāragāmi maggañca alaṅkārāpesi pabhātāya rattiyā nagare catusu dvāresu massu kammatthāya nahāpite, nahāpanatthāyanahāpanake, alaṅkāratthāya kappakeva nānāvirāga vattha gaṇdhamālādīni ca sūpavyañjana sampannāni madhurabhattāni ca ṭhapāpetvā sabbe nagarā ca jānapadā ca yathāruciṃ massūkammaṃ kāretvā nahātvā bhuñjitvā vatthābharaṇādīhi alaṅkaritvā mahācetiyaṭṭhānaṃ āgacchantūti āyuttakehi ārocāpesi.

Then the king had the entire city and the road leading to the monastery adorned. At dawn, he had barbers stationed at the four city gates for the work of shaving, bath attendants for bathing, and hairdressers for adornment. He had garments of various hues, perfumes, garlands, and other items, as well as sweet food endowed with soups and curries, placed there. He had his officers announce: 'Let all city and country dwellers, according to their liking, have their shaving done, bathe, eat, adorn themselves with clothes and ornaments, and then come to the great cetiya site.'

Sayampi [O 0.0062] sabbābharaṇa vibhūsito cattālīsa purisa sahassehi saddhiṃ uposako hutvā anekehi sumaṇḍita pasādhitehi amaccehi gahitārakkho alaṅkatāhi devakañña pamāhi nāṭakitthihi parivuto amaragaṇa parivuto devarājā viya attano sirisampattihā mahājanaṃ tosayanto anekehi turiya saṅghuṭṭhehi vattamānehi aparaṇhe mahāthūpaṭṭhānaṃ upagañji. Mahā cetiyaṭṭhāna maṅgalatthāya puṭabaddhāni vatthāni aṭṭhuttara sahassaṃ ṭhapāpesi. Catusu passesu vattharāsiṃ kāresi tela-madhu-sakkara-phāṇitādīni ca ṭhapāpesi.

He himself, adorned with all ornaments, having undertaken the Uposatha observance together with forty thousand men, guarded by many well-adorned and well-prepared ministers, and surrounded by dancing women resembling celestial maidens, was like the king of the gods surrounded by a divine host. Delighting the great multitude with his own glory and prosperity, amidst the sounds of many musical instruments being played, he approached the great stupa site in the afternoon. For the auspicious occasion at the great cetiya site, he had one thousand and eight bundles of cloth placed. He had piles of cloth made in the four directions, and also had oil, honey, sugar, molasses, and other items placed there.

Atha nānādesato bahū bhikkhū āgamiṃsu rājagahā samantā iṇdiguttatthero nāma asīti bhikkhusahassāni gahetvā ākāsenāgañchi tathā bārāṇasiyaṃ isipatane mahā vihārato dhammasenatthero nāma dvādasa bhikkhusahassāni. Sāvatthiyaṃ jetavana vihārato piyadassi nāma thero saṭṭhi bhikkhu sahassāni vesāliyaṃ mahā vanato buddharakkhitatthero aṭṭhārasa bhikkhu sahassāni, kosambiyaṃ ghositārāmato mahādhammarakkhitatthero tiṃsa bhikkhusahassāni. Ujjeniyaṃ dakkhiṇagiri mahā vihārato dhammarakkhitatthero cattālīsa bhikkhusahassāni-pāṭaliputte asokārāmato mittiṇṇatthero bhikkhūnaṃ satasahassāni saṭṭhiñca sahassāni gaṇdhāra raṭṭhato attinna thero nāma bhikkhūnaṃ dve satasahassāni asītiñca sahassāni. Mahāpallava bhogato mahādevatthero bhikkhūnaṃ cattāri satasahassāni saṭṭhiñca sahassāni yonakaraṭṭhe alasaṇdā nagarato yonaka dhammarakkhitatthero tiṃsa bhikkhu sahassāni. Viñjhāṭavivattaniya senāsanato uttaratthero asīti bhikkhusahassāni. Mahābodhimaṇḍa vihārato cittaguttatthero tiṃsa bhikkhusahassāni vanavāsibhogato caṇdaguttatthero asīti bhikkhusahassāni. Kelāsa mahāvihārato suribhaguttatthero channavuti sahassāni gahetvā ākāsenāgañchi.

Then, from various regions, many bhikkhus came. From around Rājagaha, the elder Indagutta, accompanied by eighty thousand bhikkhus, came through the sky. Likewise, from the great monastery at Isipatana in Bārāṇasī came the elder Dhammasena with twelve thousand bhikkhus. From the Jetavana monastery in Sāvatthī came the elder Piyadassi with sixty thousand bhikkhus. From the great forest in Vesālī came the elder Buddharakkhita with eighteen thousand bhikkhus. From the Ghosita monastery in Kosambī came the elder Mahādhammarakkhita with thirty thousand bhikkhus. From the great Dakkhiṇagiri monastery in Ujjenī came the elder Dhammarakkhita with forty thousand bhikkhus. From the Asokārāma in Pāṭaliputta came the elder Mittiṇṇa with one hundred and sixty thousand bhikkhus. From the Gandhāra country came the elder Uttinna with two hundred and eighty thousand bhikkhus. From the region of Mahāpallava came the elder Mahādeva with four hundred and sixty thousand bhikkhus. From the city of Alasandā in the Yonaka country came the elder Yonaka Dhammarakkhita with thirty thousand bhikkhus. From the Viñjhāṭavi-vattaniya hermitage came the elder Uttara with eighty thousand bhikkhus. From the great Bodhimaṇḍa monastery came the elder Cittagutta with thirty thousand bhikkhus. From the region of Vanavāsi came the elder Candagutta with eighty thousand bhikkhus. From the great Kelāsa monastery, the elder Sūriyagutta, accompanied by ninety-six thousand bhikkhus, came through the sky.

“Bhikkhūnaṃ dīpavāsīnaṃ-āgatānañca sabbaso,

Gaṇanāya paricchedo-porāṇehi na bhāsito;

Of the bhikkhus dwelling on the island and of all who had come, no limit by calculation was spoken of by the ancients;

Samāgatānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ-sabbesaṃ taṃ samāgame,

Vuttā khīṇāsavāyeva-te channavuti koṭiyo;”

Of all the bhikkhus gathered in that assembly, it was said that the ninety-six crores present were exclusively those free from cankers.

Atha saṅgho parikkhitta pavāḷavedikā viya majjhe rañño okāse ṭhapetvā aññamaññaṃ aghaṭṭetvā aṭṭhāsi. Pācinapasse buddharakkhitanāmako khīṇāsāvatthero attanāsadisanāmake pañcasatakhīṇāsave gahetvā aṭṭhāsi. Tathā dakkhiṇapasse pacchimapasse uttarapasse [O 0.0063] ca dhammarakkhita - saṅgharakkhita - ānaṇda nāmakā khīṇāsavattherā attanā sadisanāmake pañcapañcasata khīṇāsave gahetvā aṭṭhaṃsu piyadassi nāma khīṇāsavatthero mahābhikkhusaṅghaṃ gahetvā pubbuttara kaṇṇe aṭṭhāsi.

Then the Sangha stood, like an encircling coral railing, leaving a space in the middle for the king and without touching one another. On the eastern side stood the canker-free elder named Buddharakkhita, together with five hundred canker-free ones like himself. Similarly, on the southern, western, and northern sides stood the canker-free elders named Dhammarakkhita, Saṅgharakkhita, and Ānanda, each with five hundred canker-free ones like themselves. The canker-free elder named Piyadassi stood at the northeast corner with a great assembly of bhikkhus.

Rājā kira saṅghamajjhaṃ pavisantoyeva sace mayā kayiramānaṃ cetiyakammaṃ anantarāyena niṭṭhaṃ gacchati pācina-dakkhiṇa-pacchima-uttarapassesu buddharakkhita-cammarakkhita-saṅgharakkhita-ānaṇda nāmakā therā attanāsadisanāmake pañcapañcasatabhikkhu gahetvā tiṭṭhantu piyadassi nāma thero pubbuttarakaṇṇe bhikkhusaṅghaṃ gahetvā tiṭṭhatūti cintesi. Therāpi rañño adhippāyaṃ ñatvā tathā ṭhitāti vadantī siddhatthero pana maṅgalo-sumano-padumo-sīvali-caṇdagutto-sūriyagutto-iṇdagu- tto-sāgaro-cittaseno -jayaseno-acaloti imehi ekādasahi therehi parivuto puṇṇaghaṭe pūrato katvā puratthābhimukho aṭṭhāsi.

The king, it is said, upon the very moment of entering the midst of the Sangha, thought: 'If the work on the cetiya being done by me is completed without any hindrance, let the elders named Buddharakkhita, Dhammarakkhita, Saṅgharakkhita, and Ānanda, each with five hundred bhikkhus resembling himself, stand on the eastern, southern, western, and northern sides respectively. Let the elder named Piyadassi stand with the assembly of bhikkhus in the northeast corner.' The elders, understanding the king's intention, stood accordingly. Then the elder Siddhattha, surrounded by these eleven elders—Maṅgala, Sumana, Paduma, Sīvali, Candagutta, Sūriyagutta, Indagutta, Sāgara, Cittasena, Jayasena, and Acala—having placed a full pot in front, stood facing the east.

Atha rājā tathā ṭhitaṃ bhikkhusaṅghaṃ disvā pasannacitto gaṇdhamālādīhi pūjetvā padakkhiṇaṃ katvā catūsu ṭhānesu vanditvā puṇṇasaṭaṭṭhānaṃ pavisitvā suyaṇṇakhīle paṭimukkaṃrajatamayaṃ paribbhamana daṇḍaṃ vijjamānamātāpitūnaṃ ubhato sujātena sumaṇḍita pasādhitena abhimaṅgala sammatena amaccaputtena gāhāpetvā mahantaṃ cetiyāvaṭṭaṃ kāretuṃ ārabhi. Tathā kārentaṃ pana siddhatthatthero nivāresi. Evaṃ kirassa ahosi - yadi mahārājā mahantaṃ cetiyaṃ karoti, aniṭṭhiteyeva marissati anāgate dupparihariyañca bhavissatīti. Tasmiṃ khaṇe bhikkhusaṅgho mahā rāja thero pāṇḍito, therassa vacanaṃ kātuṃ vaṭṭatīti āha.

Then the king, seeing the Saṅgha of bhikkhus thus standing, with a pleased mind honored them with perfumes and garlands, circumambulated them, paid homage at the four places, and entered the pavilion of the full hundred. There, he had a silver measuring rod, fastened to a golden peg, held by a minister's son who was well-born on both sides of living parents, beautifully adorned and decorated, and considered auspicious, and began to have a great cetiya enclosure made. However, as he was having it made, the elder Siddhattha stopped him. It occurred to him thus: 'If the great king makes a large cetiya, he will die before its completion, and in the future it will be difficult to maintain.' At that moment, the Saṅgha of bhikkhus said to the great king: 'The elder is wise; it is proper to act on the elder's word.'

Rājā bhikkhusaṅghassa adhippāyaṃ ñatvā thero karotīti maññamāno kīdisaṃ bhante pamāṇaṃ karomīti āha. Thero mama gatagataṭṭhānato cetiyāvaṭṭaṃ karohīti vatvā upadisanto āvijjhitvā agamāsi. Rājā therassa vuttanayena cetiyāvaṭṭaṃ kāretvā theraṃ upasaṅkamitvā nāmaṃ pucchitvā gaṇdhamālādīhi pūjetvā vambatvā parivāretvā ṭhite sesa ekādasa there ca upasaṅkamitvā pūjetvā vanditvā tesaṃ nāmāni ca pucchitvā paribbhamana daṇḍagāhakassa amacca puttassa tāma pucchi.

The king, knowing the intention of the Saṅgha of bhikkhus and thinking that the elder was in charge, asked, "Venerable sir, what measurement should I make?" The elder, instructing him, said, "Make the cetiya enclosure from the places where I have walked," and having traced it out, he departed. The king, having had the cetiya enclosure built according to the elder's instructions, approached the elder, asked his name, honored him with perfumes, garlands, and so on, and praised him. Then, he approached the remaining eleven elders who stood surrounding him, honored them, paid homage, and asked their names as well. He also asked the name of the minister's son who was holding the measuring rod.

Ahaṃ deva suppatiṭṭhita brahmā nāmāti vutte tava pitā kiṃ nāmoti pucchitvāna naṇdiseno nāmāti vutte mātunāmaṃ pucchi. Sumanādevī nāmāti vutte sabbesaṃ nāmāni abhimaṅgalasammatāni, mayā kayaramānaṃ cetiyakammaṃ avassaṃ niṭṭhānaṃ gacchatiti haṭṭho ahosi, tato rājā majjhe aṭṭha suvaṇṇaghaṭe [O 0.0064] rajataghaṭe ca ṭhapāpetvā te parivāretvā aṭṭhuttarasayassa puṇṇaghaṭe ṭhapāpesi.

When he said, "Your Majesty, I am named Suppatiṭṭhita Brahmā," and when asked, "What is your father's name?" he replied, "Nandisena by name." Then he asked the mother's name. When he said, "Sumanādevī by name," the king was delighted, thinking, "All these names are considered highly auspicious; the cetiya work being undertaken by me will surely be completed!" Then the king had eight golden pots and silver pots placed in the middle, and surrounding them, he had one hundred and eight full pots set up.

Atha aṭṭha suvaṇṇiṭṭhakā ṭhapāpesi. Tāsu ekekaṃ parivāretvā aṭṭhuttarasata aṭṭhuttarasata rajatiṭṭhakāyo. Aṭṭhuttarasata aṭṭhuttarasata vatthāni ca ṭhapāpesi. Atha suppatiṭṭhita brahmanāmena amaccaputtena ekaṃ suvaṇṇiṭṭhakaṃ gāhāpetvā tena sadisa nāmehi ca jīvamānaka mātāpitūhi sattahi amaccaputtehi sesa sattiṭṭhakāyo gāhāpesi.

Then he had eight golden bricks placed. Around each one, he had one hundred and eight silver bricks and one hundred and eight cloths placed. Then, he had the minister's son named Suppatiṭṭhita Brahmā take one golden brick, and he had the remaining seven bricks taken by seven other minister's sons with similar names and whose parents were still living.

Tasmiṃ khaṇe mittatthero nāma puratthima disābhāge paribbhamita lekhāya bhūmiyaṃ gaṇdhapiṇḍaṃ ṭhapesi. Jayasenatthero nāma udakaṃ āsiñcitvā santintetvā samaṃ akāsi. Suppatiṭṭhitabrahmā bhadda nakkhattena evaṃ nānāvidha maṅgalānisaṅkhaṭaṭṭhāne paṭhamaṃ maṅgalikaṭṭhakaṃ patiṭṭhāpesi. Sumanattheronāma jātisumanapupphehi taṃ pūjesi. Tasmiṃ khaṇe udakapariyantaṃ katvā mahāpathavi kampo ahosi. Eteneva nayena sesa sattiṭṭhakāyopi patiṭṭhāpesuṃ.

At that moment, the elder named Mitta placed a lump of perfume on the traced line in the eastern direction. The elder named Jayasena sprinkled water, moistened it, and made it level. Suppatiṭṭhita Brahmā, at an auspicious constellation, established the first auspicious brick in a place prepared with various auspicious rites. The elder named Sumana honored it with jasmine flowers. At that moment, the great earth trembled, reaching to the water's edge. In this same way, the remaining seven bricks were also established.

Tato rājā rajatiṭṭhakāyopi patiṭṭhāpetvā gaṇdhamālādīhi pūjetvā maṅgalavidhānaṃ niṭṭhāpetvā suvaṇṇapelāyapupphāni gāhāpetvā pācīnapasse bhikkhusaṅghassa purato ṭhitaṃ mahā buddharakkhitattheraṃ upasaṅkamitvā gaṇdhamālādīhi pūjetvā vanditvā therassa parivāretvā ṭhita bhikkhūnañca nāmāni pucchitvā tato dakkhiṇapasse ṭhitaṃ mahādhammarakkhitattheraṃ pacchimapasse ṭhitaṃ mahāsaṅgharakkhitattheraṃ uttarapasse ṭhitaṃ ānaṇdattherañca upasaṅkamitvā gaṇdhamālādīhi pūjetvā pañcapatiṭṭhitena vanditvā tatheva nāmāni pucchitvā pubbuttara kaṇṇaṃ gantvā tattha ṭhitaṃ piyadassi mahātheraṃ vanditvā pūjetvā nāmāni pucchitvā santike aṭṭhāsi.

Then the king, having had the silver bricks also established, honored them with perfumes and garlands, completed the auspicious rites, and had flowers taken from a golden casket. He approached the great elder Buddharakkhita, who stood before the Saṅgha of bhikkhus on the eastern side, honored him with perfumes and garlands, paid homage, and inquired the names of the bhikkhus surrounding the elder. Then he approached the great elder Dhammarakkhita, who stood on the southern side, the great elder Saṅgharakkhita, who stood on the western side, and the elder Ānanda, who stood on the northern side. He honored each with perfumes and garlands, paid homage with the five-point prostration, and likewise inquired their names. Afterward, he went to the northeast corner, paid homage to the great elder Piyadassi who was standing there, honored him, inquired his name, and stood nearby.

Thero maṅgalaṃ vaḍḍhento rañño dhammaṃ desesi. Maṅgalapariyosāne sampatta gihiparisāsu cattālīsa sahassāni arahanto patiṭṭhahiṃsu. Cattālīsa sahassāni sotāpattiphalo sahassaṃ sakadāgāmiphale, sahassaṃ anāgāmiphale, bhikkhūnaṃ pana aṭṭhārasasahassāni arahattaṃ pāpuṇiṃsu. Bhikkhunīnaṃ catuddasa sahassānīti.

The elder, increasing the auspiciousness, taught the Dhamma to the king. At the conclusion of the auspicious discourse, among the assembled lay followers, forty thousand were established in arahantship; forty thousand attained the fruit of stream-entry, a thousand the fruit of once-returning, and a thousand the fruit of non-returning. As for the bhikkhus, eighteen thousand attained arahantship, and for the bhikkhunīs, fourteen thousand.

Thūpāramha kathā

The Story of the Thūpārāma

21. Tato rājā bhikkhusaṅghaṃ vanditvā yāca mahācetiyaṃ niṭṭhātināva me bhikkhusaṅgho bhikkhaṃ gaṇhātūti āha. Bhikkhū nādhivāsesu, anupubbena yācanto upallabhikkhūnaṃ sattāhaṃ adhivāsanaṃ labhitvā thūpaṭṭhānassa samantato aṭṭhārasasu ṭhānesu maṇḍape kārayitvā [O 0.0065] bhikkhusaṅghaṃ nisīdāpetvā sattāhaṃ mahādānaṃ datvā sabbesaṃyeva tela-madhu-pāṇitādi bhesajjaṃ datvā bhikkhusaṅghaṃ vissajjesi tato nagare bheriṃcārāpetvā sabbe iṭṭhaka vaḍḍhaki santipātesi. Te pañcasatamattā ahesuṃ.

21. Then the king, having paid homage to the Saṅgha of bhikkhus, requested, "May the Saṅgha of bhikkhus accept alms from me until the great cetiya is completed." The bhikkhus did not consent. Requesting in succession, he obtained consent from the junior bhikkhus for seven days. The king had pavilions built in eighteen places around the stūpa site, seated the Saṅgha of bhikkhus, and for seven days gave a great offering. He provided all with medicines such as oil, honey, and syrup, then dismissed the Saṅgha of bhikkhus. After that, he had a drum beaten throughout the city and summoned all the brick-workers and builders. They numbered about five hundred.

Tesu eko ahaṃ rañño cittaṃ ārādhetvā mahācetiyaṃ kātuṃ sakkomīti rājānaṃ passi rājā kathaṃ karosīti pucchi. Ahaṃ deva pesikānaṃ sataṃ gahetvā ekāhaṃ ekaṃ paṃsusakaṭaṃ khepetvā kammaṃ karomīti āha. Rājā evaṃ sati paṃsurāsikaṃ bhavissati. Tiṇarukkhādīni uppajjissanti addhānaṃ nappavattatīti taṃ paṭibāhi.

Among them, one, thinking, "I am able to please the king's mind and build the great cetiya," approached the king, who asked, "How will you do it?" He replied, "Your Majesty, taking a hundred workers, I will remove one cartload of earth each day and do the work." The king rejected him, saying, "In that case, there will be a heap of earth, grass and trees will grow, and the work will not proceed for long."

Añño ahaṃ purisasataṃ gahetvā ekāhaṃ ekaṃ paṃsugumbaṃ khepetvā kammaṃ karomīti āha.

Another said, "Taking a hundred men, I will clear one heap of earth a day and do the work."

Añño paṃsūnaṃ pañcammaṇāni khepetvā kammaṃ karomīti āha.

Another said, "I will clear five ammaṇas of earth and do the work."

Añño dve ammaṇāni khepetvā kammaṃ karomīti āha. Tepi rājā paṭibāhiyeva.

Another said, "I will clear two ammaṇas of earth and do the work." The king rejected him as well.

Atha añño paṇḍito iṭṭhaka vaḍḍhakī ahaṃ deva udukkhale koṭṭetvā suppehi vaṭṭetvā nisadepiṃsitvā paṃsūnaṃ ekammaṇaṃ ekāheneva khepetvā pesikānaṃ sataṃ gahetvā kammaṃ karomīti āha.

Then another, a wise brick-worker, said, "Your Majesty, I will pound the earth in a mortar, sift it with winnowing baskets, and compact it. I will clear one ammaṇa of this earth in a single day, take a hundred workers, and do the work."

Rājā evaṃ sati mahācetiye tiṇādīni na bhavissanti ciraṭṭhitikañca bhavissatiti sampaṭicchitvā puna pucchi-kiṃ saṇṭhānaṃ pana karissasīti.

The king, thinking, "In this way, no grass or the like will grow on the Great Cetiya, and it will be long-lasting," accepted, and asked further, "But what shape will you make it?"

Tasmiṃ khaṇe vissakamma devaputto vaḍḍhakissa sarīre adhimucci. Vaḍḍhakī suvaṇṇapātiṃ pūretvā udakamāharāpetvā pāṇinā udakaṃ gahetvā udakapiṭṭhiyaṃ āhani. Eḷikaghaṭa sadisaṃ mahantaṃ udakabubbulaṃ uṭhṭhāsi. Deva īdisaṃ karomīti āha.

At that moment, the devaputta Vissakamma possessed the craftsman's body. The craftsman had water brought, filled a golden bowl, took water in his hand, and struck the surface of the water. A large bubble arose, like an eḷikaghaṭa water-pot. He said, "Your Majesty, I will make it like this."

Rājā sādhūti sampaṭicchitvā tassa sahassagghanakaṃ sāṭaka yugalaṃ, sahassagghanakaṃyeva puṇṇakaṃ nāma suvaṇṇālaṅkāraṃ sahassagghanakā pādukā, dvādasa kahāpana sahassāni ca datvā anurūpaṭṭhāne gehañca khettañca dāpesi.

The king, accepting with "Sādhu!", gave him a pair of cloths worth a thousand, a golden ornament named Puṇṇaka also worth a thousand, sandals worth a thousand, and twelve thousand kahāpanas. He also had a suitable house and field given to him.

Tato rājārattibhāge cintesi kathaṃ nāma manusse apīletvā iṭṭhakā āharāpeyyanti devatā rañño cittaṃ ñatvā cetiyassa catusu dvāresu ekekadivasappahoṇakaṃ katvā tassāyeva rattiyā iṭṭhakārāsiṃ akaṃsu vihātāya rattiyā manussā disvā rañño ārocesuṃ. Rājā tussitvā vaḍḍhakiṃ kamme paṭṭhapesi. Devatā eteneva nayena yāva mahācetiyassa niṭṭhānaṃ tāva ekekassa [O 0.0066] divasassa pahoṇakaṃ katvā iṭṭhakā āhariṃsu. Sakaladivasabhāge kammaṃ kataṭṭhāne mattikā iṭṭhaka cuṇṇaṃ vāpi na paññāyati. Rattiyaṃ devatā antaradhāpenti.

Then, during the night, the king pondered: "How can bricks be brought without troubling the people?" The deities, knowing the king's thoughts, made a heap of bricks that very night at each of the four gates of the cetiya, enough for one day's work. At dawn, the people saw it and reported it to the king. The king, delighted, set the master builder to the work. In this manner, the deities brought enough bricks for each day until the completion of the great cetiya. Throughout the day, no trace of clay, bricks, or brick powder was to be seen at the work site. At night, the deities would make it all disappear.

Atha rājā mahācetiyekammakārāyacatuparisāyahattha kammamūlatthaṃcatusu dvāresu ekekasmiṃ dvāre soḷasa kahāpana sahassāni vatthālaṅkāra-gandha-māla-tela-madhu-phāṇita-pañcakaṭuka bhesajjāni nānāvidha sūpavyañjana saṃyuttaṃ bhattaṃ yāgukhajjakādīni aṭṭhavidha kappiyaya pānakāni pañcavidha mukhavāsa sahita tāmbūlāni ca ṭhapāpetvā mahācetiye kammaṃ karontāgahaṭṭhā vā pabbajitā vā yathājjhāsayaṃ gaṇhantu mulaṃ agahetvā kammaṃ karontānaṃ kātuṃ na dethāti āṇāpesi.

Then the king, for the workers of the great cetiya and the fourfold assembly engaged in the work, and for the payment of their labor, had placed at each of the four gates sixteen thousand kahāpanas, along with cloth, ornaments, perfumes, garlands, oil, honey, molasses, medicines of the five pungent spices, cooked rice with various curries and relishes, gruel and solid food, eight kinds of allowable drinks, and betel leaves with five kinds of mouth-fresheners. He ordered: "Let those working on the great cetiya, whether householders or renunciants, take according to their inclination. Do not allow anyone to work without taking payment."

Atheko thero cetiyakamme sahāya bhāvaṃ icchanto kammakaraṇaṭṭhāne mattikā sadisaṃ katvā attānaṃ abhisaṅkhaṭaṃ mattikā piṇḍaṃ ekena hatthena gahetvā aññena mālā gahetvā mahācetiyaṅgaṇaṃ āruyha rājakammike vañcetvā vaḍḍhakissa adāsi. So gaṇhantova pakatimatti kā nā bhavatīti ñatvā therassa mukhaṃ olokesi tassākāraṃ ñatvā tattha kolāhala mahosi anukkamena rājā sutvā āgantvā vaḍḍhakiṃ pucchi-tuyhaṃ kira bhaṇe eko bhikkhu amūlaka mattikāpiṇḍaṃ adāsīti.

Then a certain elder, wishing to assist in the work of the cetiya, fashioned by himself a lump of clay, making it similar to the clay at the work site. Holding this lump of clay in one hand and a garland in the other, he ascended to the courtyard of the great cetiya, deceived the royal workers, and gave it to the master builder. The builder, upon taking it, realized it was not ordinary clay and looked at the elder's face. Understanding the situation, a great commotion arose there. In due course, the king heard of it, came, and asked the master builder: "Is it true, my good man, that a bhikkhu gave you a lump of clay without payment?"

So evamāha-yebhuyyena ayyā ekena hatthena pupphaṃ ekena mattikāpiṇḍe gahetvā āharitvā denti tenāhaṃ ajānitvā kamme upanesiṃ “ayaṃ pana āgantuko, ayaṃ nevāsikoti ettakaṃ jānāmīti.“Tena hi taṃ theraṃ imassa dassehīti ekaṃ mahallaka balatthaṃ vaḍḍhakissa santike ṭhapesi vaḍḍhakī puna āgatakāle taṃ theraṃ balatthassa dassesi so taṃ sañjānitvā rañño ārocesi.

He replied: "For the most part, venerable sirs bring a flower in one hand and lumps of clay in the other and give them. Therefore, not knowing, I used it in the work. I only know this much: 'This one is a visitor, this one is a resident.'" "In that case, point out that elder to this man," said the king, and he placed an old, strong man near the master builder. When the elder came again, the builder pointed him out to the strong man. The strong man, recognizing him, reported it to the king.

Rājā tassa saññaṃ adāsi. To tvaṃ tayo jātisumana makulakumbhe mahābodhi aṅgaṇe rāsiṃ katvā gaṇdhañca ṭhapetvā mahābodhi aṅgaṇaṃ gata kāle āgantukassa therassa pūjanatthāya rañño dāpitaṃ gaṇdhamālānti vatvā dehīti. Balattho rañño vuttanayeneva tassa bodhi aṅgaṇaṃ gata kāle taṃ gaṇdhamālaṃ adāsi.

The king gave him an instruction: "You, take three pots of jasmine and bakula flowers, make a heap of them in the Great Bodhi courtyard, and also place perfume there. When that visiting elder goes to the Great Bodhi courtyard, say, 'This garland and perfume is a gift from the king for your veneration,' and give it to him." Following the king's instruction, when the elder went to the Bodhi courtyard, the strong man gave him that garland and perfume.

Sopi somanassappatto hutvā selasaṇtharaṃ dhovitvā gaṇdhena paribhaṇḍaṃ katvā silāsantharaṃ katvā pupphaṃ pūjetvā catusu ṭhānesu vanditvā pācīnadvāre añjaliṃ paggayha pītiṃ uppādetvā puppha pūjaṃ olokento aṭṭhāsi.

He, filled with joy, washed the stone slab, anointed it all around with perfume, prepared the stone seat, offered the flowers, paid homage at the four places, and stood at the eastern gate with hands raised in reverence, generating rapture as he gazed upon the flower offering.

Balattho tasmiṃ kāle taṃ theraṃ upasaṅkamitvā vanditvā evamāha. Bhante tumhākaṃ cetiyakamme sahāyabhāvatthāya dinnassa amūlaka [O 0.0067] mattikāpiṇḍassa mūlaṃ dinnabhāvaṃ rājā jānāpeti attano vandanena vandāpetīti. Taṃ sutvā thero anattamano ahosi. Balattho tiṭṭhantu bhante tayo sumana makula kumbhātattakāneva suvaṇṇapupphānipi etaṃ mattikapiṇḍaṃ nāgghanti. Cittaṃ pasādetha bhanteti pakkāmi.

At that time, the strong man approached the elder, paid homage, and said: "Venerable sir, the king makes it known that payment has been given for the unpaid lump of clay you gave to assist in the cetiya work, and he sends his respects to you." Hearing this, the elder became displeased. The strong man said: "Let it be, venerable sir. Not even that many golden flowers would be worth this lump of clay. Please gladden your mind, venerable sir," and then he departed.

Tadā koṭṭhivālajanapade piyaṅgalla vihāravāsī eko thero iṭṭhakavaḍḍhakissa ñātako ahosi. So āgantvā vaḍḍhakinā saddhiṃ mantetvā dīgha bahala kiriyato iṭṭhakappamāṇaṃ jānitvā gantvā sahattheneva sakkaccaṃ mattikaṃ madditvā iṭṭhakaṃ katvā pacitvā pattatthavikāya pakkhipitvā paccāgantvā ekena hatthena rañño iṭṭhakaṃ ekena pupphaṃ gahetvā attano iṭṭhakāsa saddhiṃ rañño iṭṭhakaṃ adāsi. Vaḍḍhakī gahetvā kamme upanesi.

At that time, in the district of Koṭṭhivāla, an elder residing in the Piyaṅgalla monastery was a relative of a brick-builder. He came and consulted with the builder, and having learned the required dimensions for the bricks, he went and with his own hands carefully kneaded the clay, made a brick, and fired it. Placing it in his bowl-bag, he returned. Then, taking one of the king's bricks in one hand and a flower in the other, he gave the king's brick to the builder along with his own brick. The builder took them and used them in the work.

Thero sañjāta pīti somanasso mahācetiye kammaṃ karonto iṭṭha kasālapariveṇe vasati tassa taṃ kammaṃ pākaṭaṃ ahosi. Rājā vaḍḍhakiṃ pucchi-bhaṇe ekena kira ayyena amūlika iṭṭhakā dinnāti. Saccaṃ deva, ekena ayyena dinna iṭṭhakā amhākaṃ iṭṭhakāya sadisāti kamme upanesinti āha. Puna taṃ iṭṭhakaṃ sañjānāsīti raññā vutte ñātakānuggahena na jānāmīti āha.

The elder, filled with joy and delight, stayed in the brick-hall cell while working on the great cetiya. His deed became known. The king asked the builder: "My good man, is it true that an elder gave bricks without payment?" "It is true, Your Majesty," he replied. "The bricks given by an elder were similar to our bricks, so I used them in the work." When the king asked, "Do you recognize that brick?" he replied, out of favor for his relative, "I do not know."

Rājā yadi evaṃ taṃ imassa dannehīti balatthaṃ ṭhapesi so taṃ pubbe viya balatthassa dassesi. Balattho pariveṇaṃ gantvā santike nisīditvā paṭisaṇthāraṃ katvā bhante tumhe āgantukā nevāsikāti pucchi. Āgantukomhī upāsakāti. Katara raṭṭhavāsiko bhanteti koṭṭhivālajanapade piyaṅgalla vihāravāsimhi upāsakāti idheva vasatha gacchathāti. Idha na vasāma asukadivase gacchāmāti āha. Balatthopi ahampi tumhehi saddhiṃ āgamissāmi. Mayhampi gāmo etasmiṃyeva janapade asuka gāmo nāmāti āha. Thero sādhūti sampaṭicchi. Balattho taṃ pavattiṃ rañño nivedesi.

The king said, "If so, then point him out to this man," and he appointed the strong man. The builder, as before, pointed the elder out to the strong man. The strong man went to the cell, sat down nearby, exchanged friendly greetings, and asked, "Venerable sir, are you a visitor or a resident?" "I am a visitor, lay follower." "From which district are you, venerable sir?" "I am a resident of the Piyaṅgalla monastery in the Koṭṭhivāla district, lay follower." "Will you reside here or will you leave?" "We will not reside here; we will leave on such-and-such a day," he said. The strong man replied, "I too will come with you. My village, named so-and-so, is also in this same district." The elder agreed, saying, "Very well." The strong man reported this matter to the king.

Rājā balatthassa sahassagghanakaṃ vatthayugalaṃ mahagghaṃ rattakambalaṃ upāhanayugaṃ sugaṇdhatelanāḷiṃ aññca bahuṃ samaṇaparikkhāraṃ therassa dehīti dāpesi sopi parikkhāraṃ gahetvā pariveṇaṃ gantvā therena saddhiṃ rattiṃ vasitvā pāto saddhiṃyeva nikkhamitvā anupubbena gantvā piyaṅgalla vihārassa dissamāne ṭhāne sītacchāyāya theraṃ nisīdāpetvā pāde dhovitvā gaṇdhatelena makkhetvā guḷodakaṃ pāyetvā upāhanaṃ paṭimuñcitvā idaṃ me parikkhāraṃ kulūpagatherassatthāya gahitaṃ, idāni tumhākaṃ dammi, idaṃ pana sāṭakayugaṃ mama puttassa maṅgalatthāya gahitaṃ, [O 0.0068] tumhe cīvaraṃ katvā pārupathāti vatvā therassa pādamūle ṭhapesi.

The king had a pair of cloths worth a thousand, a valuable red blanket, a pair of sandals, a measure of fragrant oil, and many other monastic requisites given to the elder. The strong man took the requisites, went to the cell, stayed the night with the elder, and in the morning departed together. Traveling in due course, at a place where the Piyaṅgalla monastery came into sight, he had the elder sit in a cool, shaded spot, washed his feet, anointed them with fragrant oil, gave him jaggery-water to drink, and put the sandals on him. He then said, "These requisites I obtained for our family's patron elder, but now I give them to you. This pair of cloths was obtained for my son’s auspicious ceremony. Please make it into a robe and wear it." Having said this, he placed them at the elder’s feet.

Thero sāṭakayugaṃ pattatthavikāya pakkhipitvā sesaparikkhāraṃ bhaṇḍikaṃ katvā upāhanaṃ āruyhaṃ kattayaṭṭhiṃ gahetvā maggaṃ paṭpajji. Balattho tena saddhiṃ thokaṃ gantvā “tiṭṭhatha bhante, mayhaṃ ayaṃ maggo“ti vatvā pubbe vuttanayeneva rañño sāsanaṃ therassa ārocesi

The elder placed the pair of cloths into his bowl-bag, made the remaining requisites into a bundle, put on the sandals, took his walking staff, and set out on the road. The strong man went a short distance with him and then said, "Stop, venerable sir, this is my path." Then, in the same manner as before, he delivered the king's message to the elder.

Thero taṃ sutvā mahantena parakkamena katakammaṃ akataṃ viya jātanti domanassappatto hutvā assudhāraṃ pavattetvā’upāsaka tava parikkhāraṃ tvameva gaṇhāhī“ti ṭhitakova sabbāparikkhāraṃ chaḍḍesi balattho kiṃ nāma bhante vadatha esa rājā tuyhaṃ bhavaggappamāṇaṃ katvā paccayaṃ dentopi tava iṭṭhakānurūpaṃ kātuṃ na sakkoti. Kevalaṃ pana mahācetiye kammaṃ aññesaṃ apattakaṃ katvā karomīti adhippāyena evaṃ kāreti tumhe pana bhante attanā laddhaparikkhāraṃ gahetvā cittaṃ pasādethāti vatvā theraṃ saññāpetvā pakkāmi. Imasmiṃ pana cetiye bhatiyā kammaṃ katvā cittaṃ pasādetvā sagge nibbatta sattānaṃ pamāṇaṃ natthi.

The elder, hearing this, was overcome with sorrow, thinking that his great effort had become as if undone. Shedding tears, he stood there and said, 'Lay follower, take back your requisites yourself.' Then he discarded all the requisites. The official said, 'What are you saying, venerable sir? Even if this king were to offer you requisites extending to the peak of existence, he could not match the worth of your brick. However, he merely intends to perform work at the great cetiya, thinking, "I will do something unprecedented." But you, venerable sir, should accept the requisites you have received and gladden your mind.' Having thus persuaded the elder, he departed. Indeed, there is no measure to the beings who, having performed work for hire at this cetiya with a gladdened mind, are reborn in heaven.

Tāvatiṃsa bhavane kira nibbatta devadhītaro attano sampattiṃ oloketvā kena nu ko kammena imaṃ sampattiṃ labhimhāti āvajjamānā mahācetiye bhatiyā kammaṃ katvā laddhabhāvaṃ ñatvā bhatiyā katakammassāpi phalaṃ īdisaṃ, attano santakena kammaphalaṃ saddahitvā katakammassa phalaṃ kīdisaṃ bhavissatīti cintetvā dibbagaṇdhamālaṃ ādāya rattibhāge āgantvā pūjetvā cetiyaṃ vandanti.

In the Tāvatiṃsa heaven, it is said that divine daughters, having been reborn there, reflected upon their own prosperity, wondering, 'By what deed did we attain this prosperity?' Realizing that they had gained it by performing work for hire at the Great Cetiya, they thought, 'If even the fruit of work done for hire is such, what will be the fruit of deeds done with faith using one's own possessions?' Taking divine perfumes and garlands, they came at night to worship and pay homage to the cetiya.

Tasmiṃ khaṇe bhātivaṅkavāsī mahāsīvatthero nāma cetiyaṃ vandanatthāya gato, tā vandantiyo disvā mahāsattapaṇṇirukkhasamīpe ṭhito tāsaṃ yathāruciṃ vanditvā gamanakāle pucchi? Tumhākaṃ sarīrālokena sakalatambapaṇṇidipo ekāloko, kiṃ kammaṃ karitthāti bhante amhākaṃ santake katakammaṃ nāma natthi imasmiṃ cetiye manaṃ pasādetvā bhatiyā kammaṃ karimhāti āhaṃsu evaṃ buddhasāsane pasannacittena bhatiyā katakammampi mahapphalaṃ hoti. Tasmā-

At that moment, a great elder named Mahāsīvatthera from Bhātivaṅka had gone to pay homage at the cetiya. Seeing them worshipping, he stood near the great sattapaṇṇi tree. When they were leaving, after they had worshipped as they pleased, he asked, 'By the light of your bodies, the entire island of Tambapaṇṇi is illuminated as one. What deed have you performed?' They replied, 'Venerable sir, we have performed no deed, so to speak, from our own possessions. We performed work for hire at this cetiya with gladdened minds.' Thus, even work done for hire with a gladdened mind in the Buddha's dispensation bears great fruit. Therefore—

“Cittappasādamattena-sugate gati uttamā,

Labbhatīti viditvāna-thūpapūjaṃ kare buddho”ti;
