Knowing that by the mere gladdening of the mind towards the Well-Gone One, the highest destination is attained, a wise person should perform worship of the stupa.

Evaṃ rājā cetiyakammaṃ kārāpento pupphadhānattayaṃ niṭṭhāpesi. Taṃ khīṇāsava thirabhāvatthāya bhūmisamaṃ katvā osīdāpesuṃ, evaṃ navavāre citaṃ citaṃ osīdāpesuṃ. Rājā kāraṇaṃ ajānanto anattamano [O 0.0069] hutvā bhikkhusaṅghaṃ sannipātesi. Asīti bhikkhusahassāni sannipatiṃsu rājā bhikkhusaṅghaṃ gaṇdhamālādīhi pūjetvā vanditvā pucchi’bhante mahācetiye pupphadhānattayaṃ navavāre na jānāmī’ti bhikkhusaṅgho āha mahārāja tuyhaṃ kammassa vā jīvitassa vā antarāyo natthi. Anāgate thirabhāvatthāya iddhimantehi osīdāpitaṃ, ito paṭṭhāya na osīdāpessanti. Tvaṃ aññathattaṃ akatvā mahāthūpaṃ samāpehīti. Taṃ sutvā haṭṭho rājā thūpakammaṃ kāresi.

Thus, while having the work on the cetiya done, the king completed the three flower-receptacles. For the sake of its stability, the Arahants leveled it with the ground and caused it to sink; thus, they caused it to sink nine times as it was being built. The king, not knowing the reason, became displeased and assembled the community of monks. Eighty thousand monks gathered. The king, having honored the Sangha with perfumes, garlands, and so forth, paid homage and asked, 'Venerable sirs, I do not know the reason for the nine sinkings of the three flower-receptacles at the Great Cetiya.' The community of monks replied, 'Great king, there is no danger to your work or your life. For the sake of future stability, it was caused to sink by those with psychic powers. From now on, they will not cause it to sink. Without being troubled, complete the Great Stupa.' Hearing this, the delighted king had the work on the stupa carried on.

Dasapupphadhānāni dasahi iṭṭhakākoṭīhi niṭṭhanaṃ gamiṃsu puna pupphadhānattaye niṭṭhite bhikkhusaṅgho uttara-sumana nāmake dve khīṇāsava sāmaṇere āṇāpesi. Tumhe samacaturassaṃ aṭṭharatana bahalaṃ ekeka passato asīti asiti hatthappamāṇaṃ chamedaka vaṇṇapāsāṇe āharathāti. Te sādhūti sampaṭicchitvā uttarakuruṃ gantvā cuttappakārappamāṇe bhaṇḍipupphanihecha medavaṇṇapāsāṇe āharitvā ekaṃ pāsāṇaṃ dhātugabbhassa bhūmiyaṃ attharitvā cattāro pāsāṇe catusu passesu saṃvidhāya aparaṃ dhātugabbhaṃ pidahanatthāya pācīna disābhāge vāluka pākāra samīpe adissamānaṃ katvā ṭhapesuṃ.

The ten flower-receptacles were completed with one hundred million bricks. When the three flower-receptacles were completed, the community of monks instructed two Arahant novices named Uttara and Sumana: 'You two, bring stones the color of fresh butter, perfectly square, eighty cubits on each side, and eight ratanas thick.' They agreed, saying 'Very well,' went to Uttarakuru, and brought back stones the color of fresh butter of the aforesaid type and size, resembling a heap of bhaṇḍi flowers. They laid one stone on the floor of the relic chamber, arranged four stones on the four sides, and placed another one for covering the relic chamber on the eastern side near the sand rampart, making it invisible.

22. Tato rājā dhātugabbhassa majjhe sabbaratanamayaṃ sabbākārasampannaṃ manoharaṃ bodhirukkhaṃ kāresi so hi iṇdanīlamaṇibhūmiyaṃ patiṭṭhito, tassamūlāni pacāḷamayāni, khaṇdho sirivacchādīhi aṭṭhamaṅgalikehi pupphapanti latāpanti catuppada haṃsapantihi ca vicitto aṭṭhārasa hatthubbedho rajatamayo ahosi.

22. Then the king had a delightful Bodhi tree made in the middle of the relic chamber, made of all kinds of jewels and complete in every aspect. It was established on a ground of sapphire. Its roots were made of coral. Its trunk, made of silver and eighteen cubits high, was adorned with the eight auspicious symbols such as the Srivatsa, and with rows of flowers, rows of creepers, quadrupeds, and rows of geese.

Pañcamahāsākhāpi aṭṭhārasahatthāca, pattāpi maṇimayāni, paṇḍupattāni, hemamayāni, phalā pavāḷamayāni. Tathā aṅkuropari celavitānaṃ baṇdhāpesi. Tassa ante samannato muttamaya kiṅkiṇikajālaṃ olambati. Svaṇṇaghaṇṭāpanti ca suvaṇṇadāmāni ca tahiṃ tahiṃ olambanti vitānassa catusu kaṇṇesu navasatasahassagghanako ekeko muttākalāpo olambati. Tattha yathānurūpaṃ nānāratana katāneva caṇda-sūriya-tārakārūpāni padumāni ca appitāni ahesuṃ. Mahagghāni anekavaṇṇāni aṭṭhuttara sahassāni vatthāni olambiṃsu.

The five great branches were also eighteen cubits long. The leaves were made of gems, the yellowed leaves of gold, and the fruits of coral. Likewise, he had a cloth canopy fastened over the new shoots. At its edge hung a net of pearl bells. Rows of golden bells and golden garlands also hung here and there. At the four corners of the canopy, a cluster of pearls, each worth nine hundred thousand, hung down. There, representations of the moon, sun, and stars, and lotuses, crafted from various suitable jewels, were affixed. One thousand and eight expensive cloths of various colors were hung.

Tato bodhirukkhassa samantato satta ratanamaya vedikā kāretvā mahāmalaka muttā attharāpesi muttā vedikānaṃ antare gaṇdhodaka puṇṇa sattaratanamaya puṇṇaghaṭa pantiyo ṭhapāpesi. Tāsu suvaṇṇaghaṭe pavāḷamayāni pupphāni ahesuṃ. Pavāḷaghaṭe suvaṇṇamayāni pupphāni. Maṇighaṭe rajatamayāni pupphāni [O 0.0070] rajataghaṭe maṇimayānipupphāti. Sattaratanaghaṭe sattaratanamayāni pupphāni ahesuṃ.

Then, around the Bodhi tree, he had railings made of the seven kinds of jewels constructed, and had pearls strewn on the great terrace. Between the jeweled railings, he had rows of full pitchers made of the seven kinds of jewels and filled with fragrant water placed. In them, the golden pitchers had flowers made of coral. The coral pitchers had flowers made of gold. The gem pitchers had flowers made of silver, and the silver pitchers had flowers made of gems. The pitchers made of the seven kinds of jewels had flowers made of the seven kinds of jewels.

Bodhirukkhassa pācīna disābhāge ratanamaye koṭiagghanake pallaṅke ghanakoṭṭima suvaṇṇamayaṃ buddhapaṭimaṃ nisīdāpesi. Tassā paṭimāya vīsati nakhā akkhinaṃ setaṭṭhānāni ca phaḷakamayāni. Hatthatala pādatala dantāvaraṇāni akkhīnaṃ rattaṭṭhātāni ca pavāḷamayāni kesa bhamukāni akkhinaṃ kāḷakaṭṭhānāni ca iṇdanīla maṇimayāni uṇṇālomaṃ panaṃ rajatamayaṃ ahosi. Tato sahampati mahā brahmānaṃ rajatacchattaṃ dhāretvā ṭhinaṃ kāresi. Tatā dvīsu devalokesu devatāhi saddhiṃ vijayuttarasaṅkhaṃ gahetvā abhisekaṃ dadamānaṃ sakkaṃ devarājānaṃ pañcasikha devaputtaṃ beḷuva paṇḍu vīṇamādāya gaṇdhabbaṃ kurumānaṃ. Mahākāḷa nāgarājānaṃ nāgakaññāparivutaṃ nānāvidhenathutighosena tathāgataṃ vaṇṇentaṃ kāresi.

On the eastern side of the Bodhi tree, he had a golden Buddha image seated on a jeweled couch worth a koṭi, with a solid paved base. On that image, the twenty nails and the white parts of the eyes were made of crystal. The palms of the hands, the soles of the feet, the covering of the teeth, and the red parts of the eyes were made of coral. The hair, eyebrows, and the black parts of the eyes were made of indranīla gems. The ūrṇā-hair, however, was made of silver. Then, he had Mahābrahmā Sahampati depicted standing and holding a silver parasol. He also had Sakka, king of the devas, depicted with deities from the two deva worlds, holding the victory conch and performing the consecration; the devaputta Pañcasikha playing music on his pale beluva-wood lute; and the great Nāga king Mahākāḷa, surrounded by nāga maidens, praising the Tathāgata with various sounds of praise.

Vasavattimārampana bāhusahassaṃ māpetvā tisūla muggarādi nānāvudhāni gahetvā sahassakumbhaṃ girimekhala hatthikkhaṇdha māruyha mārabalaṃ parivāretvā bodhimaṇḍaṃ āgantvā aneka bhiṃsanakaṃ kurumānaṃ kāresi. Sesāsu disāsu pācīna disābhāge pallaṅkasadise koṭi koṭi agghanake tayo pallaṅke attharāpetvā dantamayaṃ daṇḍaṃ pavāḷavījaniṃ ṭhapāpesi. Bodhikkhaṇdhaṃ ussīsake katvā nānāratanaṃ maṇḍitaṃ koṭiagghanakaṃ rajata sayanaṃ attharāpesi.

He had Vasavatti Māra depicted, having created a thousand arms and taken up various weapons such as tridents and maces. Mounting the back of the elephant Girimekhala, and surrounded by his army, he was shown approaching the Bodhi-terrace, creating various terrifying displays. In the remaining directions and on the eastern side, three couches, resembling the throne and each worth a koṭi of koṭis, were spread out, and an ivory staff and a coral fan were placed. Making the trunk of the Bodhi tree its headboard, a silver bed decorated with various jewels and worth a koṭi was spread out.

Dasabalassa abhisambodhimpatvā animisena cakkhunā bodhipallaṅkaṃ olokitaṭṭhānaṃ sattāhameva ratanacaṅkame caṅkamitaṭṭhānaṃ olokitaṭṭhānaṃ sattāhameva ratanacaṅkame caṅkamitaṭṭhānaṃ ratanagharaṃ pavisitvā dhammasammasitaṭṭhānaṃ mucaliṇdamūlaṃ gantvā nisinnassa mucaliṇdena nāgena sattakkhattuṃ bhogehi parikkhipitvā upari phaṇaṃ katvā ṭhitaṭṭhānaṃ, tato ajapāla nigrodha mūlaṃ gantvā nisinnaṭṭhānaṃ, tato rājāyatanaṃ bhojane upanīte catumahārājehi upanīta pattapaṭiggahaṇaṃ kāresi.

After the Ten-Powered One attained supreme enlightenment, he had depicted the place where he gazed at the Bodhi-throne with unblinking eyes; the place where he walked on the jeweled promenade for seven days; the place where he entered the jeweled house and contemplated the Dhamma; the place where, having gone to the foot of the Mucalinda tree and sat down, the Nāga Mucalinda encircled him seven times with his coils and stood with his hood held above; then, the place where he went to the foot of the Ajapāla banyan tree and sat; and then, at Rājāyatana, when food was offered, the receiving of the alms bowl brought by the Four Great Kings.

Tato brahmāyācanaṃ dhammacakkappavattanaṃ sasapabbajjaṃ, bhaddavaggiya pabbajjaṃ, tebhātika jaṭila damanaṃ, laṭṭhivanuyyāne bimbisāropagamaṇaṃ, rājagahappavesanaṃ, veḷuvana paṭiggahaṇaṃ asīti mahāsāvake ca kāresi tato kapilavatthugamanaṃ ratanacaṅkame ṭhataṭṭhānaṃ, rāhulapabbajjaṃ, naṇdrapabbajjaṃ jetavanapaṭiggahaṇaṃ, gaṇḍambamūle yamaka pāṭihārikaṃ devaloke.

Then he had depicted the request of Brahmā, the turning of the Wheel of Dhamma, the ordination of Sasa, the ordination of the Bhaddavaggiyas, the taming of the three Jaṭila brothers, the approach of Bimbisāra to the Laṭṭhivana grove, the entry into Rājagaha, the acceptance of the Veḷuvana, and the eighty great disciples. Then, the journey to Kapilavatthu, the standing place on the jeweled promenade, the ordination of Rāhula, the ordination of Nanda, the acceptance of the Jetavana, the twin miracle at the foot of the Gaṇḍamba tree, and the sojourn in the world of the devas.

“Nisinne”ti katthaci.

“Seated,” in some places.

Abhidhamma [O 0.0071] desanā devorohaṇa pāṭīhīraṃ therapañha samāgamañca kāresi.

He also had depicted the teaching of the Abhidhamma, the miracle of the descent from the deva world, and the assembly for the Elder's questions.

Tathā mahāsamayasutta-rāhulovāda sutta-maṅgasuttapārāyana sutta samāgamaṃ dhanapāla-āḷavaka-aṅgulimāla-apalāla damanaṃ āyusaṅkhāra vossajjanaṃ sūkaramaddava pariggahaṇaṃ siṅgivaṇṇa vatthayuga paṭiggahaṇaṃ pasannodakapānaṃ parinibbānaṃ devamanussa paridevanaṃ mahākassapattherassabhagavatopādavaṇdanaṃ sarīra ṅahanaṃ agginibbānaṃ āḷāhanasakkāraṃ deṇena brāhmaṇena katadhātu vibhāgañca kāresi.

Likewise, he had depicted the assembly for the Mahāsamaya Sutta, Rāhulovāda Sutta, Maṅgala Sutta, and Pārāyana Sutta; the taming of Dhanapāla, Āḷavaka, Aṅgulimāla, and Apalāla; the relinquishing of the life-sustaining force; the acceptance of the sūkaramaddava; the receiving of the pair of gold-colored cloths; the drinking of clear water; the final passing away; the lamentation of devas and humans; the veneration of the Blessed One's feet by the great elder Mahākassapa; the cremation of the body; the extinguishing of the pyre; the honoring at the cremation site; and the division of the relics by the brahmin Doṇa.

Tathā addhacchaṭṭhāni jātakasatāni kāresi. Vessantarajātakaṃ pana kārento sañjaya mahārājaṃ phusatīdeviṃ maddideviṃ jāliya kumāraṃ kaṇhājinañca kāresi. Tato paṇḍava hatthidānaṃ satta sataka mahādānaṃ nagara vilokanaṃ sindhavadānaṃ devatāhi rohitatha vaṇṇena rathassa vahanaṃ rathadānaṃ sayamevoṇata dumato phalaṃ gahetvā dārakānaṃ madhumaṃsa dinna nesādassa suvaṇṇa sūcidānaṃ vaṅkapabbatakucchimhi pabbajjāvesena vasitaṭṭhānaṃ pūjakassa dārakadānaṃ sakkabrāhmaṇassa bhariyaṃdānaṃ pūjakassa devatānubhāveka dārake gahetvā gantvā sañjaya nariṇdassa purato gataṭṭhānaṃ tato vaṅkapabbatakucchiyaṃ channaṃ khattiyānaṃ samāgamaṃ vessantarassa maddiyā ca abhisekaṃ pattaṭṭhānaṃ nakarampaviṭṭhe sattaratanavassaṃ vassitaṭṭhānaṃ tato cavitvā tusitapure nibbattaṭṭhānañca sabbaṃ vitthārena kāresi.

Likewise, he had five hundred and fifty Jātakas depicted. While enacting the Vessantara Jātaka, he portrayed King Sañjaya, Queen Phusatī, Queen Maddī, Prince Jāliya, and Kaṇhājina. Then he had depicted the gift of the elephant to the Paṇḍavas, the great gift of seven hundred, the viewing of the city, the gift of the Sindhava horse, the carrying of the chariot by devas in the guise of red steeds, the gift of the chariot, taking fruit for the children from a tree that bent down of its own accord, the gift of a golden needle to the hunter who had given honey and meat, the dwelling place in the Vaṅka mountain cave in the guise of an ascetic, the gift of the children to the supplicant brahmin, the gift of his wife to Sakka disguised as a brahmin, the place where the supplicant brahmin took the children and went before King Sañjaya by the power of the devas, then the gathering of the six khattiyas in the Vaṅka mountain cave, the place where Vessantara and Maddī received their consecration, the raining of the seven treasures upon entering the city, and after passing away from there, the place of rebirth in the Tusita heaven—all were depicted in detail.

Tato dasa sahassa cakkavāḷadevatāhi buddha bhāvāya āyācitaṭṭhānaṃ apunarāvattanaṃ mātukucchiokkamanaṃ mahāmāyādeviṃ suddhodana mahārājaṃ lumbinīvane jātaṭṭhānaṃ antalikkhato dvinnaṃ udakadhārānaṃ patanaṃ uttarābhimukhaṃ sattapadavītihāra gamanaṃ kāḷadevaḷassa jaṭāmatthake mahāpurisassa pādapatiṭṭhāna anativattamānāya jambucchāyāya dhātīnaṃ pamādaṃ disvā sirisayane pallaṅke nisīditvā jhānasamāpannaṭṭhānañca kāresi.

Then he had depicted the place where he was requested to strive for Buddhahood by the deities of ten thousand world-systems, the 'non-returning' from Tusita heaven, the conception in his mother's womb, Queen Mahāmāyā and King Suddhodana, the birthplace in the Lumbinī Grove, the falling of two streams of water from the sky, the taking of seven steps facing north, the placing of the Great Being's feet upon the matted hair of Kāḷadevala, the unmoving shadow of the Jambu tree, and the place where, seeing the negligence of his nurses, he sat cross-legged on the royal couch and attained jhāna.

Tato rāhula mātaraṃ rāhula bhaddakañca kāresi tato ekūna tiṃsavassakāle uyyāne kīḷatatthāya gamana samaye jiṇṇa vyādhita mata saṅkhyāte tayo devadūte disvā nivattanaṭṭhānaṃ catutthavāre pabbajitarūpaṃ disvā sādhupabbajjāti cittaṃ uppādetvā uyyānaṃ gantvā uyyānasiriṃ anubhavitvā sāyaṇha samaye nahātvā maṅgala silāpaṭṭe nisinnamatta vissakaṭṭhānā alaṅkaraṇaṭṭhāṇaṃ tato majjhimarattiyaṃ nāṭakānaṃ vippakāraṃ disvā kaṇthaka haya varamāruyha) mahābhinikkhamaṇaṃ nikkhamitaṭṭhānaṃ dasa sahassa) cakkavāḷa devatāhi kata pūjāvidhiṃ kaṇthakanivattana cetiyaṭṭhānaṃ [O 0.0072] anomā nadītīre pabbajjaṃ rājagahappavesanaṃ paṇḍava pabbataccha yāya rañño bimbisāramma rajjakaraṇatthāya āyācanaṃ sujātāya dinna khīrapāyāsa paṭiggahaṇaṃ nerañjarāya nadiyā tīre pāyāsa paribhogaṃ nadiyā pātivissaṭṭhaṃ pāṭihāriyaṃ sālavane divāvihāra gataṭṭhānaṃ sotthiyena dinna kusatiṇapaṭiggahaṇaṃ bodhimaṇḍaṃ āruyha nisinnaṭṭhānañca sabbaṃ vitthārena kāresi.

Then, he had depicted Rāhula’s mother and the good Rāhula. Then, at the age of twenty-nine, the place where, while going to the park for amusement, he turned back after seeing the three divine messengers—old age, sickness, and death; on the fourth occasion, seeing the form of a renunciant and giving rise to the thought, 'Excellent is renunciation!'; going to the park and enjoying its splendor; in the evening, after bathing, being divinely adorned as soon as he sat on the auspicious stone slab; then at midnight, seeing the disarray of the female musicians and mounting the excellent horse Kaṇṭhaka to set out on the Great Renunciation; the place of the Great Renunciation; the worship offered by the deities of ten thousand world-systems; the Kaṇṭhaka-return shrine; the renunciation on the bank of the Anomā River; the entry into Rājagaha; the request from King Bimbisāra in the shadow of Paṇḍava mountain to accept the kingdom; the acceptance of the milk-rice offered by Sujātā; the partaking of the milk-rice on the bank of the Nerañjarā River; the miracle of the bowl sent forth into the river; the place of spending the day in the Sāla Grove; the acceptance of the kusa grass offered by Sotthiya; and the place of ascending and sitting at the Bodhimaṇḍa—all these he had depicted in detail.

Tato mahiṇdatthera pamukhe satta saha āgate ca kārāpesi. Catusu disāsu khaggahatthe cattāro mahārājāno kāresi tato dvattiṃsa devaputte tato suvaṇṇa daṇḍa dīpakadhārā dvattiṃsa devakumāriyo tato aṭṭhavīsati yakkhasenāpatino tato añjaliṃ paggayha ṭhita devatāyo tato ratanamaya puppakalāpe gahetvā ṭhita devatāyo tato suvaṇṇaghaṭe gahetvā ṭhiti devatāyo tato naccanaka devatāyo tato turiyavādaka devatāyo tato pacceka satasahassagghanake dasahatthappamāṇe ādāse gahetvā ṭhita devatāyo tato tatheva satasahassagghanaka puppha sākhāyo gahetvā ṭhitadevatāyo tato caṇdamaṇḍale gahetvā ṭhitadevatāyo sūriyamaṇḍale gahetvā ṭhitadevatāyo tato padumāni gahetvā ṭhita devatāyo tato chattāni gahetvā ṭhitadevatāyo tato vicittavesadhare malladevaputte tato dussapoṭhana devatāyo tato ratanagghike gahetvā ṭhitadevatāyo tato dhammacakkāki gahetvā ṭhitadevatāyo tato khaggadharā devatāyo tato pañcahatthappamāṇa gaṇdhitelapūritā dukūlavaṭṭiyaṃ pajjalita dīpakañcanana pātiyo sīsehi dhāretvā ṭhitadevatāyo ca kārāpesi.

Then, he had figures made of the seven thousand who had come, led by the elder Mahinda. He had the four great kings made, each holding a sword in the four directions. Then, thirty-two devaputras; then, thirty-two celestial maidens holding golden staffs and lamps; then, twenty-eight yakkha generals; then, deities standing with hands folded in reverence; then, deities holding jeweled flower clusters; then, deities holding golden pots; then, dancing deities; then, deities playing musical instruments; then, deities holding mirrors, each worth a hundred thousand and measuring ten hands; then, deities holding flower branches, likewise worth a hundred thousand; then, deities holding moon discs and deities holding sun discs; then, deities holding lotuses; then, deities holding parasols; then, heroic devaputras adorned in colorful garments; then, deities snapping their robes in applause; then, deities holding jeweled offering vessels; then, deities holding Dharma wheels; then, deities holding swords; and deities holding on their heads burning golden lamp-bowls, five hands in measure, filled with fragrant oil and fitted with wicks of fine cloth.

Tato catusukaṇṇesuphaḷikamayaagghiya matthake cattāromahāmaṇa ṭhapāpesi catusu kaṇṇesu suvaṇṇa-maṇi-mutta-vajirānaṃ cattāro rāsiyo kāresi tato meghavaṇṇa pāsāṇabhittiyaṃ vijjullatā kāresi tato ratana latāyo tato cāḷavijatiyo tato niluppale gahetvā ṭhita nāgamaṇavikāyo kāresi.

Then, at the four corners, he had crystal offering vessels placed, and on the summit, he placed four great jewels. At the four corners, he made four heaps of gold, jewels, pearls, and diamonds. Then, on the dark-cloud-colored stone wall, he created lightning-creepers. Next, he made jeweled vines. Then, he made decorative friezes. Finally, he crafted nāga maidens standing holding blue lotuses.

Rājā ektikāni rūpakāni ghanakoṭṭima suvaṇṇeheva kāresi. Avasesampi pūjāvidhiṃ sattarataneheva kāresīti. Ettha ca vuttappakārampana pūjanīyabhaṇḍaṃ anantamaparimāṇaṃ hoti tathā hi ambapāsānavāsī cittaguttattheronāma heṭṭhā lohapāsāde sannipatitānaṃ dvādasannaṃ bhikkhusahassānaṃ dhammaṃ kathento rathavinītasuttaṃ ārabhitvā mahā dhātunidhānaṃ vaṇṇento ekacce na saddahissantīti maññamāno osakkitvā kathesi. Tasmiṃ khaṇe koṭapabbatavāsī mahātissatthero [O 0.0073] nāma khīṇāsavo avidūre nisīditvā dhammaṃ suṇanto āvuso dhammakathika tava kathāto parihīnampi atthi. Apacco sakakitvā vitthārena kathehīti āha. Atha imasmiṃyeva dīpe bhātiya mahārājā nāma saddo pasanno ahosi so sāyaṃ pātaṃ mahacetiyaṃ vanditvāva bhuñjati. Ekadivasaṃ vinicchaye nisīditva dubbīnicchitaṃ aṭṭaṃ vinicchinanto atisāyaṃ vuṭṭhito thūpavaṇdanaṃ vissaritvā bhojane upanīte hatthaṃ otāretvā manusse pucchiajja mayā ayyako vandito na vanditoti. Porāṇaka rājāno hi satthāraṃ ayyakoti vadanti. Manussā na vandito devāti āhaṃsu.

The king had single images made of solid gold. The rest of the worship arrangements he had made with the seven precious gems. Here, the items for worship, as described, were endless and immeasurable. For instance, the elder Cittagutta, who resided at Ambapāsāna, while teaching the Dhamma to twelve thousand monks gathered in the lower Lohapāsāda, began with the Rathavinīta Sutta and described the great relic-treasure. Fearing that some might not believe, he spoke with hesitation. At that moment, the elder Mahātissa, an arahant dwelling at Koṭapabbata, who was listening to the Dhamma nearby, said, "Friend, Dhamma-preacher, there is even something lacking in your talk. Speak without hesitation and in detail." Now, on this very island, there was a great king named Bhātiya, who was faithful and devoted. He would eat only after paying homage to the Great Cetiya in the evening and the morning. One day, while sitting in judgment and deciding a difficult case, he rose very late in the evening. Having forgotten the homage to the stupa, when the food was brought, he lowered his hand and asked his men, "Today, have I paid homage to the Venerable One or not?" For the ancient kings referred to the Teacher as the 'Venerable One.' The men replied, "You have not paid homage, Your Majesty."

Tasmiṃ khaṇe rājā hatthena gahita bhattapiṇḍaṃ pātiyaṃ pātetvā uṭṭhāya dakkhiṇadvāraṃ vivarāpetvā āgantvā pācīna dvārena mahācetiyaṅgaṇaṃ āruyha vandanto anto dhātugabbhe khīṇāsavānaṃ dhammaṃ osāraṇasaddaṃ sutvā dakkhiṇadvāreti maññamāno tattha gantvā adisvā eteneva nayena itarānipi dvārāni gantvā tatthāpi adisvā ayyā dhammaṃ osārento vicarantīti maññamāno olokanatthāya catusu dvāresu manusse ṭhapetvā sayaṃ puna vicaritvā apassanto manusse pucchitvā bahiddhā natthi bhāvaṃ ñatvā anto dhātugabbhe bhavissatiti sanniṭṭhānaṃ katvā pācīnadvāre āsannatare mahā cetiyābhimukho hatthapāde pasāretvā jīvituṃ pariccajitvā daḷhasamādānaṃ katvā nipajji. Sace mā ayyā dhātugabbhaṃ na olokāpenti-sattāhaṃ nirāhāro hutvā sussamāno bhūsamuṭṭhi viya vippakiriyamānopi na uṭṭhahissāmīti. Tassa guṇānubhāvena sakkassa bhavanaṃ uṇhākāraṃ dassesi.

At that moment, the king dropped the lump of food he had taken in his hand into the bowl, rose, had the southern gate opened, and went. Entering through the eastern gate, he ascended to the great cetiya courtyard and paid homage. Hearing the sound of the arahants reciting the Dhamma inside the relic chamber, he thought it was at the southern gate. Going there and not seeing them, he proceeded in the same manner to the other gates, but did not see them there either. Thinking, "The noble ones are reciting the Dhamma while moving about," he stationed men at the four gates to watch. Having searched again himself and not seeing them, he asked the men. Learning that there was no one outside, he concluded that they must be inside the relic chamber. Near the eastern gate, facing the great cetiya, he stretched out his hands and feet, gave up on life, made a firm resolution, and lay down, thinking: "If the noble ones do not let me see the relic chamber, I will not rise, even if after seven days without food I wither away and am scattered like a handful of chaff." By the power of his virtue, Sakka's abode showed a sign of heat.

Sakko āvajjanto taṃ kāraṇaṃ ñatvā agantvā dhammaṃ osārentānaṃ therānaṃ evamāha. Ayaṃ bhante rājā dhammiko buddhasāsane pasanno imasmiṃ ṭhāne sajjhāyana saddaṃ sutvā dhātugabbhaṃ apassitvā na uṭṭhahissāmīti daḷhasamādānaṃ katvā nipanno sace dhātugabbhaṃ na passati tattheva marissati. Taṃ pavesetvā dhātu gabbhaṃ olokāpethāti. Therāpi tassa anukampāya dhātugabbhaṃ dassetuṃ ekaṃ theraṃ āṇāpesuṃ rājānaṃ ānetvā dhātugabbhā olokāpetvā pesehīti. So rañño hatthe gahetvā dhātugabbhaṃ pavesetvā yathāruciṃ vandāpetvā sabbaṃ sallakkhitakāle pesesi rājā nagaraṃ gantvā aparena samayena dhātugabbhe attanā diṭṭharūpakesu ekadesāni suvaṇṇakhacitāni kāretvā rājaṅgaṇe mahantaṃ maṇḍapaṃ kāretvā tasmiṃ maṇḍape [O 0.0074] tāni rūpakāni saṃvidahāpetvā nagare sannipātetvā dhātugabbhe mayā diṭṭhāni suvaṇṇarūpakāni īdisāni āha. Tesaṃ rūpakānaṃ niyāmena katattā niyāmaka rūpakāni nāma jātāni.

Sakka, reflecting and knowing the reason, approached the elders who were reciting the Dhamma and spoke thus: "Venerable sirs, this righteous king, devoted to the Buddha's dispensation, having heard the sound of chanting at this place, is lying down, having made a firm resolution, 'I will not rise until I see the relic chamber.' If he does not see the relic chamber, he will die right there. Let him enter and see the relic chamber." The elders, out of compassion for him, ordered one elder to show him the relic chamber, saying: "Bring the king, let him see the relic chamber, and send him away." He took the king by the hand, led him into the relic chamber, had him pay homage as he wished, and when he had observed everything, sent him away. The king, having gone to the city, at a later time had some of the images he had seen in the relic chamber made, inlaid with gold. He had a great pavilion built in the royal courtyard, had those images arranged in that pavilion, assembled the townspeople, and said, "The golden images I saw in the relic chamber are like these." Because those images were made according to a model, they became known as 'model images'.

Rājā saṃvacchare saṃvacchare tāni rūpakāni nīharāpetvā nāgarānaṃ dassesi paṭhamaṃ dassitakāle nāgarā pasīditvā ekeka kulato ekekaṃ dārakaṃ nīharitvā pabbājesuṃ. Puna rājā ayyā etaṃ pakāraṃ ajānanakā bahū tesampi ārocessāmīti vihāraṃ gantvā heṭṭhā lohapāsāde bhikkhusaṅghaṃ sannipātetvā sayaṃ dhammāsanagato tiyāma rattiṃ dhātugabbhe adhikāraṃ kathetvā pariyosāpetumasakkāntoseva uṭṭhāsi. Tattheko bhikkhu rājānaṃ pucchi-mahārāja tvaṃ pātarāsabhattaṃ bhūtvā āgatosi. Tiyāma rattiṃ vaṇṇento dhātugabbhe pūjāvidhimpi pariyosāpetuṃ nāsakkhi aññampi bahuṃ atthiti.

Year after year, the king had those images brought out and shown to the citizens. At the time of the first showing, the citizens were inspired, and from each family, they brought out one child and had them ordained. Later, the king thought, "Many of the noble ones are unaware of this matter; I shall announce it to them also." He went to the monastery, assembled the Sangha of monks in the lower Lohapāsāda, went to the Dhamma seat himself, and for the three watches of the night spoke on the matter of the relic chamber, but rose up unable to finish. There, a certain monk asked the king, "Great king, you came after eating your morning meal. Though describing it for the whole night, you were not able to finish even the arrangements for worship in the relic chamber. Is there still much more?"

Rājā kiṃ katheta bhante, tumhākaṃ mayā kathitaṃ dasabhāgesu ekabhāgampi nappahoti. Ahampana mayā sallakkhita mattameva kathesiṃ. Anantaṃ bhante dhātugabbhe pūjāvidhānanti āha evaṃ anantaṃ pūjanīyabhaṇḍaṃ samacaturasse ekeka passato asīti asīti hatthappamāṇe dhātugabbhe nirantaraṃ katvā puretumpi na sukaraṃ, pageva yathārahaṃ saṃvidhātu tiṭṭhatu tāva dhātugabbho yāva mahācetiye vāluka pākāra paricchedā nirantaraṃ puretumpi na sakkā. Tasmā taṃ sabbaṃ pūjanīyabhaṇḍaṃ tattha kathaṃ gaṇhīti yadettha vattabbaṃ porāṇehi vuttameva.

The king replied, "What can I say, venerable sir? What I have told you does not amount to even one part in ten. I, however, spoke only of what I myself observed. The arrangements for worship in the relic chamber are endless, venerable sir. Thus, it is not easy to completely fill the relic chamber—which is a perfect square, with each side measuring eighty cubits—with endless items for worship, let alone to arrange them appropriately. Let the relic chamber be for now; it is not even possible to completely fill the enclosure of the sand-wall at the great cetiya. Therefore, how did all those items for worship fit in there? Whatever is to be said about this has already been said by the ancients."

Nigro dhapiṭṭhi tepiṭaka mahāsīvatthero kira rājagehe nisiditvā rañño dasabala sīhanādasutataṃ kathento dhātunidhānaṃ vaṇṇetvā suttantaṃ vinivaṭṭesi. Rājā therassa evamāha-ayaṃ bhante dhātugabbhā samacaturasso, ekekapassato asīti asīti hatthappamāṇe ettakāni pūjanīya bhaṇḍāni ettha ṭṭhitānīti ko saddahessatiti. Thero āha. Iṇdasālaguhākittakappamāṇāti tayā sutapubbāti. Rājā khuddaka mañcakappamāṇā bhanteti āha. Tato thero āha, mahārāja amhākaṃ satthārā sakkasasa sakka pañhasuttantaṃ kathanadivase guhāya kittakā parisā osaṭāti sutapubbāti. Rājā dvīsu bhante devalokesu devatāti āha. Evaṃ sante tampi asaddaheyyaṃ nu mahārājāti therena vutte rājā taṃ pana devānaṃ deviddhiyā ahosi deviddhi nāma acinteyyā bhanteti āha.

The Elder Mahāsīva, the Tipiṭaka master from Nigrodhapiṭṭhi, it is said, sat in the royal palace and, while expounding to the king the Dasabalasīhanāda Sutta, described the relic-deposit and concluded the discourse. The king said to the elder, "Venerable sir, this relic chamber is a perfect square, with each side measuring eighty cubits. Who would believe that so many items for worship are placed here?" The elder said, "Have you heard before what the size of the Indasāla Cave is?" The king said, "Venerable sir, it is the size of a small cot." Then the elder said, "Great king, have you heard before how large an assembly fit into the cave on the day our Teacher taught the Sakkapañha Sutta to Sakka?" The king said, "Venerable sir, the deities from two deva worlds." When the elder said, "This being so, that too would be unbelievable, would it not, great king?" the king replied, "But that was due to the divine power of the devas. Divine power, venerable sir, is inconceivable."

Tato thero mahārāja taṃ ekāyayeva deviddhiyā ahosi. Idaṃ pana rañño rājiddhiyā devānaṃ deviddhiyā ariyānaṃ ariyiddhiyāti imāhi [O 0.0075] tīhi iddhīhi jātanti avoca. Rājā therassa vacanaṃ sādhūti sampaṭicchitvā theraṃ setacchattena pūjetvā matthake chattaṃ dhārento mahā vihāraṃ ānetvā puna mahācetiyassa sattāhaṃ chattaṃ datvā jāti sumanapuppha pūjaṃ akāsīti. Etassatthassa sādhanatthameva aññānipi bahūni vatthūni dassitāni tāni kintehīti amhe upekkhitāni ettha ca rājā mahesakkho mahānubhāvo pūritapārami katābhinīhāroti tassavasena rājiddhiveditabbā sakkena āṇattena vissakammunā devaputtena ādito paṭṭhāya āvisitvā katattā tassa vasena devidadhi veditabbā, kammādhiṭṭhāyaka iṇdaguttatthero khuddānukhuddakaṃ kammaṃ anuvidhāyanto kāresi na kevalaṃtheroyeva, sabbepi ariyā attanā attanā kattabba kiccesu ussukkamāpannāyeva ahesunti imāhi tīhi iddhīhi katanti veditabbaṃ.

Then the elder said, "Great king, that was due to a single divine power. But this was born from three powers: the king’s royal power, the divine power of the devas, and the noble power of the noble ones." The king, accepting the elder’s words with "Good," honored him with a white parasol and, holding the parasol over his head, led him to the Great Monastery. Then, for seven days, he offered the parasol to the Great Cetiya and performed an offering of jasmine flowers. To establish this point, many other stories have been shown, but what of them? We have disregarded them. Here, the king, being of great influence and great power, had fulfilled the perfections and made his aspiration; by virtue of him, the royal power should be understood. Because it was accomplished by the devaputta Vissakamma, commanded by Sakka, who entered into the project from the very beginning, by virtue of him, the divine power should be understood. The elder Indagutta, the overseer of the work, directed even the most minor and secondary tasks. Not only the elder, but all the noble ones were diligently engaged in their respective duties. Thus, it should be understood that this was accomplished by these three powers.

Vuttañhetaṃ mahāvaṃso.

This was stated in the Mahāvaṃsa.

Iṇdagutta mahāthero-chaḷabhiñño mahāmati,

Kammādhiṭṭhāyako ettha-sabbaṃ saṃvidahi imaṃ;

Sabbaṃ rājiddhiyā etaṃ devatānañca iddhiyā;

Iddhiyā ariyānañca-asambādhā patiṭṭhita”nti;

"The great elder Indagutta, possessing the six supernormal knowledges, of great wisdom, the overseer of the work here, arranged all this. All this was established by the royal power, by the power of the deities, and by the power of the noble ones—unobstructed."

Dhātugabba rūpavaṇṇanākathā

The Account of the Description of the Relic-Chamber's Appearance

23. Evaṃ rājā dhātugabbhe kattabba kammaṃ niṭṭhāpetvā cātuddasi divase vihāraṃ gantvā bhikkhusaṅghaṃ sannipātesi, sannipatti bhikkhū tiṃsa sahassāni ahesuṃ rājā bhikkhusaṅghaṃ vanditvā evamāha. Dhātugabbhe mayā kattabbakammaṃ niṭṭhāpitaṃ, sve āsāḷabhimuposatha divase uttarasāḷha nakkhattena dhātunidhānaṃ bhavissati dhātuyo janātha bhanteti bhikkhusaṅghassa bhāraṃ katvā nagaramevāgañji.

23. Thus the king, having had the work to be done in the relic-chamber completed, went to the monastery on the fourteenth day and assembled the community of monks. The assembled monks numbered thirty thousand. The king, having paid homage to the community of monks, said thus: "The work to be done by me in the relic-chamber has been completed. Tomorrow, on the Āsāḷha uposatha day, under the constellation of Uttarāsāḷha, the enshrinement of the relics will take place. Venerable sirs, please procure the relics." Having entrusted this responsibility to the community of monks, he returned to the city.

Atha bhikkhusaṅgho dhātu āharaṇakaṃ bhikkhuṃ gavesanto pūjā pariveṇa vāsikaṃ soḷasa vassuddesikaṃ chaḷabhiññaṃ soṇuttaraṃ nāma sāmaṇeraṃ disvā taṃ pakkosāpetvā āvuso soṇuttara rājā dhātugabbhaṃ niṭṭhāpetvā dhātu ābharaṇaṃ bhikkhusaṅghassa bhāramakāsi. Tasmā tayā dhātuyo āharitabbāti āharāmi bhante dhātuyo kuto lacchāmīti pucchi tassa bhikkhusaṅgho evamāha. Āvuso soṇuttara tatāgato maraṇamañce nipanno sakkaṃ devarājānaṃ āmantetvā mayhaṃ aṭṭhadoṇappamāṇesu sārīrika dhātusu ekaṃ doṇaṃ koḷiyarājuhi sakkataṃ, anāgate tambapaṇṇi dīpe mahācetiye patiṭṭhahissatīti āha.

Then the community of monks, seeking a monk to bring the relics, saw the novice named Soṇuttara, a sixteen-year-old resident of the Pūjā Pariveṇa, who possessed the six supernormal knowledges. They had him summoned and said, "Friend Soṇuttara, the king has completed the relic-chamber and has made the bringing of the relics the responsibility of the community of monks. Therefore, you must bring the relics." "I will bring the relics, venerable sirs, but from where shall I obtain them?" he asked. The community of monks replied to him thus: "Friend Soṇuttara, when the Tathāgata was lying on his deathbed, he addressed Sakka, the king of the devas, and said, 'Of my bodily relics amounting to eight doṇas, one doṇa was honored by the Koḷiya kings. In the future, it will be enshrined in the Great Cetiya on the island of Tambapaṇṇi.'"

Atha [O 0.0076] bhagavati parinibbute doṇa brāhmaṇo dhātuyo aṭṭha koṭṭhāse katvā aṭṭhannaṃ nagaravāsīnaṃ adāsi te attano attano nagare cetiyaṃ kāretvā parihariṃsu tesu rāmagāme koḷiyehi katacetiye mahoghena bhinne dhātukaraṇḍako samuddaṃ pavisitvā ratanavālukā piṭṭhe chabbaṇṇaraṃsi samākiṇṇo aṭṭhāsi.

Then, after the Blessed One had attained Parinibbāna, the brahmin Doṇa divided the relics into eight portions and gave them to the inhabitants of eight cities. They each had a cetiya built in their own city and paid homage to them. Among these, when the cetiya built by the Koḷiyas of Rāmagāma was broken by a great flood, the relic casket entered the ocean. There it stood on a bed of jeweled sand, suffused with six-colored rays.

Nāgā disvā mañjerika nāga bhavanaṃ gantvā mahākāḷa nāga rañño ārocesuṃ so dasakoṭi nāga sahassa parivuto āgantvā gaṇdhamālādīhi pūjetvā suvaṇṇa-pavāḷa-miṇi-rajatadhaje ussāpetvā pañcaṅgika turiya paggahīta nānāvidha nāga nāṭakānaṃ majjhahato dhākukaraṇḍaṃ maṇicaṅgoṭake ṭhapetvā sīsenādāya mahāsakkāra sammānaṃ karonto nāgabhavanaṃ netvā channavuti koṭidhane pūjetvā sabbaratanehi cetiyañca cetiyagharañca māpetvā dhātuyo pariharati. Mahākassapatthero ajātasattuno dhātunidhānaṃ karonto rāmagāme dhātuyo ṭhapetvā sesa dhātuyo āharitvā adāsi rājā rāmagāme dhātuyo kasmā nāhaṭāti pucchi.

The nāgas, having seen it, went to the abode of the Mañjerika nāgas and informed the nāga king Mahākāḷa. He, surrounded by ten crores of thousands of nāgas, arrived and honored it with perfumes, garlands, and so on. They raised golden, coral, jeweled, and silver banners and, in the midst of various nāga dramas accompanied by the five kinds of musical instruments, they placed the relic casket in a jeweled receptacle. Carrying it on their heads, paying great homage and honor, they brought it to the nāga abode. They honored it with ninety-six crores of wealth and had a cetiya and a cetiya-house built with all kinds of jewels, and they protected the relics. The Elder Mahākassapa, while arranging the relic-deposit for Ajātasattu, left aside the relics in Rāmagāma and brought the remaining relics. The king asked, "Why were the relics in Rāmagāma not brought?"

Thero mahārāja tāsaṃ antarāyo natthi anāgate tambapaṇṇidīpe mahācetiye patiṭṭhahissantīti āha. Asoko dhammarājāpi dhātunidhānaṃ ugghāṭetvā olokento aṭṭhamaṃ dhātudoṇaṃ adisvā aparaṃ dhātuṃ doṇaṃ kattha bhanteti pucchi mahārāja taṃ koḷiyehi gaṅgātīre kata cetiye patiṭṭhitaṃ, mahoghena cetiye bhinne mahāsamuddaṃ pāvisi.

The Elder said, "Great King, there is no danger for them; in the future, they will be enshrined in the Great Cetiya on the island of Tambapaṇṇi." King Asoka, the righteous ruler, also opened the relic repository and, upon looking, did not see the eighth doṇa of relics. He asked, "Venerable sirs, where is the other doṇa of relics?" "Great King, it was enshrined in a cetiya built by the Koḷiyas on the bank of the Ganges. When the cetiya was broken by a great flood, it entered the great ocean."

Taṃ nāgā disvā attano nāgabhavanaṃ netvā pariharantīti khīṇāsavā āhaṃsu rājā nāgabhavanaṃ nāma mama āṇāpavattanaṭṭhānaṃ, tampi āharāmi bhanteti āha. Mahārāja tā dhātuyo anāgate tambapaṇṇidīpe mahācetiye patiṭṭhahissantiti nivāresuṃ. Tasmā tvaṃ mañcherika nāga bhavanaṃ gantvā taṃ pavattiṃ nāga rañño nivedetvā dhātuyo āhara, sve dhātu nidhānaṃ bhavissatīti. Soṇuttaro sādhūti sampaṭicchitvā attano pariveṇaṃ agamāsi.

The Arahants said: "The nāgas, having seen it, took it to their own nāga abode and are protecting it." The king said, "Venerable sirs, the nāga abode is a place where my command prevails. I will bring that as well." They restrained him, saying, "Great King, those relics will be enshrined in the future in the Great Cetiya on the island of Tambapaṇṇi. Therefore, you go to the abode of the Mañjerika nāgas, inform the nāga king of this matter, and bring the relics. Tomorrow, the enshrinement of relics will take place." Soṇuttara accepted, saying, "Very well," and went to his residence.

Rājāpi nagaraṃ antvā nagare bheriṃ carāpesi- sve dhātunidhānaṃ bhavissati nāgarā attano attano vibhavānurūpena alaṅkaritvā gaṇdhamālādīni gahetvā mahācetiyaṅgaṇaṃ otarantuti. Sakkopi vissakammaṃ āṇāpesi-sve mahācetiye dhātunidhānaṃ bhavissati. Sakala tampapaṇṇidīpaṃ alaṅkarohīti. So punadivase ekūnayojanasatikaṃ tambapaṇṇidīpaṃ kasīṇamaṇḍalaṃ viya samaṃ katvā rajatapaṭṭasadisaṃ vālukākiṇṇaṃpañcavaṇṇapupphasamākūlaṃ katvā [O 0.0077] samantato puṇṇaghaṭapantiyo ṭhapāpetvā sāṇīhi parikkhipitvā upari celavitānaṃ baṇdhitvā pathavitale thalapadumāni ākāse olambaka padumāni dassetvā alaṅkata devasabhaṃ viya sajjesi. Mahāsamuddañca sannisinnaṃ pañcavidha padumasañcannaṃ akāsi.

The king, having entered the city, had a proclamation made by drum throughout the city: "Tomorrow the enshrinement of relics will take place. Let the citizens, having adorned themselves according to their means, take perfumes, garlands, and other offerings, and descend to the courtyard of the Great Cetiya." Sakka also gave orders to Vissakamma: "Tomorrow, the enshrinement of relics will take place at the Great Cetiya. Adorn the entire island of Tambapaṇṇi." The next day, he made the island of Tambapaṇṇi, extending ninety-nine yojanas, as level as a kasiṇa-disc, covered with sand like a silver cloth, and filled with flowers of five colors. He had rows of full pots set up all around, enclosed it with screens, fastened a cloth canopy above, displayed terrestrial lotuses on the ground and hanging lotuses in the sky, and prepared it like a decorated assembly hall of the devas. He also made the great ocean calm and covered with five kinds of lotuses.

Dhātu ānubhāvena sakala cakkavāḷaṃ gabbhokkamanābhisambodhikālādisu viya sajjitaṃ ahosi nāgarāpa nagara vīthiyo sammajjitvā muttāphala sadisaṃ vālukaṃ okiritvā lājapañcamaka pupphāni samokiritvā nānāvirāga dhajapaṭākāyo ussāpetvā suvaṇṇaghaṭa kadalitoraṇa mālagaghikādīhi alaṅkaritvā nagaraṃ sajjesu. Rājā nagarassa catusu dvāresu anāthānaṃ manussānaṃ paribhotthāya nānāppakāra khādanīya bhojanīya gaṇdhamāla vatthābharaṇa pañcavidhi mudhavāsa sahita tāmbulāni ca ṭhapāpesi.

By the power of the relics, the entire world-system was adorned as if at the times of the conception, the full enlightenment, and so on. The citizens swept the city streets, scattered sand resembling pearls, strewed flowers with parched grain as the fifth, raised banners and flags of various colors, and adorned the city with golden pots, banana-tree arches, garlands, festoons, and other decorations. At the four gates of the city, the king had various kinds of hard and soft foods, perfumes, garlands, clothes, ornaments, and betel with the five kinds of mouth-perfumes placed for the use of the destitute.

Atha rājā sabbābharaṇa vibhūsito kumuda pannavaṇṇa catusiṇdhavayutta rathavaramāruyha alaṅkataṃ kaṇḍulaṃ hatthiṃ purato katvā suvaṇṇacaṅgoṭakaṃ sīse katvā setacchattassa heṭṭhā aṭṭhāsi. Tasmiṃ khaṇe sakkaṃ devarājānaṃ devaccharā viya nānābharaṇavibhūsitā devakaññupamā aneka sahassa nāṭakitthiyo ceva dasamahāyodhā ca caturaṅginī senā ca rājānaṃ parivāresuṃ.

Then the king, adorned with all ornaments, mounted an excellent chariot yoked to four Sindh horses the color of a white lotus petal. Placing the decorated elephant Kaṇḍula in front and having placed a golden casket on his head, he stood beneath a white parasol. At that moment, just as celestial nymphs surround Sakka, the king of the gods, so too did many thousands of dancing women adorned with various ornaments—resembling divine maidens—the ten great warriors, and the fourfold army attend upon the king.

Tathā aṭṭhuttara sahassa itthiyo ca puṇṇaghaṭe garahatvā parivāresuṃ. Aṭṭhuttara sahassa aṭṭhuttara sarassappamāṇeyeva purisā ceva itthiyo ca pupphasamuggāni daṇḍadīpikā nānāvaṇṇa dhaje ca gahetvā parivāresuṃ. Evaṃ rājā mahantena rājānubhāvena naṇdanavanaṃ nikkhanta devarājā viya nikkhami. Tadā nānāvidha turiya ghosehi ceva hatthassa ratha saddehi ca mahāpathavibhijjanākārappattā viya ahosi.

Likewise, one thousand and eight women, having taken full water pots, surrounded him. One thousand and eight men and women, holding caskets of flowers, staff-torches, and multicolored flags, surrounded him. Thus, the king, with great royal power, departed like the king of gods leaving Nandana Grove. Then, with the sounds of various musical instruments and the noises of elephants and chariots, it was as if the great earth was about to split apart.

Tasmiṃ khaṇe soṇuttaro attano pariveṇeyeva nisinno turiya ghosena rañño nikkhantabhāvaṃ ñatvā abhiññāpādakaṃ catutthajjhānaṃ samāpajjitvā adhiṭṭhayā pathaviyaṃ nimujjitvā mañjerika nāgabhavane mahākāḷānāgarañño purato pāturahosi. Nāgarājā soṇuttaraṃ disvā uṭṭhāyāsanā abhivādetvā gaṇdhodakena pāde dhovitvā vaṇṇa gaṇdha sampanna kusumehi pūjetvā ekāmantaṃ nisīditvā kuto āgatattha bhanteti pucchi tambapaṇṇi dīpato āgatamhāhi vutte kimatthāyāti pucchi.

At that moment, Soṇuttara, seated in his own dwelling, having known of the king's departure through the sound of instruments, attained the fourth jhāna, the basis for the abhiññās, and, resolving, submerged into the earth, appearing before the Nāga king Mahākāḷa in the Mañjerika Nāga palace. The Nāga king, seeing Soṇuttara, rose from his seat, paid homage, washed his feet with scented water, honored him with fragrant and colorful flowers, and sat to one side. He asked, "From where have you come, venerable sir?" When told, "We have come from the island of Tambapaṇṇi," he inquired, "For what purpose?"

Mahārāja tambapaṇṇidīpe duṭṭhagāmaṇi abhaya mahārājā mahā cetiyaṃ kārento dhātuyo bhikkhusaṅghassa bhāramakāsi. Mahāvihāre tiṃsamattāni bhikkhusahassāni sannipatitvā mahāthūpatthāya ṭhapitadhātuyo [O 0.0078] mahākāḷa nāga rañño santike ṭhitā, tassa taṃ pavattiṃ kathetvā dhātuyo āharāti maṃ pesesuṃ, tasmā idhāgatomhīti āha.

"Great king, in the island of Tambapaṇṇi, King Duṭṭhagāmaṇi Abhaya, while constructing a great cetiya, entrusted the relics to the community of monks. Thirty thousand monks assembled in the Mahāvihāra. They knew that the relics intended for the Great Thūpa were with the Nāga King Mahākāḷa. Explaining that matter, they sent me to fetch the relics. That is why I have come here," he said.

Taṃ sutvā nāgarājā pabbatena viya ajjhotthaṭo mahantena madāmanassena abhibhūto evaṃ cintesi mayaṃ pana imā dhātuyo pūjetvā apāyato muñcitvā sagge nibbattissamāti amaññimha. Ayaṃ pana bhikkhu mahiddhiko mahānubhāvo, sace imā dhātuyo imasmiṃ ṭhāne ṭhito bhaveyyuṃ, amhe abhibhavitvāpi gaṇhituṃ sakkuṇeyya. Dhātuyo apanetuṃ vaṭṭatīti cintetvā parisaṃ olokento parisa pariyante ṭhitaṃ vāsuladattaṃ nāma attano bhāgineyyaṃ disvā tassa saññamadāsi so mātulassa adhippāyaṃ ñatvā cetiyagharaṃ gantvā dhātukaraṇḍakaṃ ādāya gilitvā sinerupabbata pādamūlaṃ gantvā-

Hearing that, the Nāga king, overwhelmed by great intoxication and arrogance as if covered by a mountain, thought thus: "We, having worshipped these relics, imagined we would be freed from the lower realms and reborn in heaven. But this monk is of great power and might; if these relics remained in this place, he could overpower us and take them. It is fitting to remove the relics." Having thought thus, while looking around at his assembly, he saw his nephew named Vāsuladatta standing at the edge of the assembly. He gave him a sign. Understanding his uncle's intention, the nephew went to the relic chamber, took the relic casket, swallowed it, and went to the foot of Mount Sineru.

Yojanasatāmāvaṭṭaṃ dīghaṃ tisata yojanaṃ,

Phaṇānekasahassāni-māpisitvā mahiddhiko,

Sineru pādamūlamhi-dhumāyanto ca pajjalaṃ,

Ābhujitvāna so bhoge-nipajji vālukātale;

A hundred yojanas in circumference, three hundred yojanas in length, with many thousands of hoods, the mighty one, having created them, at the foot of Mount Sineru, smoking and blazing, having coiled his body, he lay down on the sandy ground.

“Anekāni sahassāni-attanā sadise ahi,

Māpayitvā sayāpesi-samantā parivārite,

Bahū devā ca nāgā ca-osariṃsu tahiṃ tadā,

Yuddhaṃ ubhinnaṃ nāgānaṃ-sassissāma mayaṃ iti;”

Many thousands of snakes like himself he created, and had them lie down, surrounding him on all sides. Many devas and nāgas came down there at that time, thinking, 'We shall witness the battle between these two great beings.'

Tato nāgarājā bhāgineyyena dhātuyo apanītabhāvaṃ ñatvā evamāha- mama santike dhātuyo natthi, tumhe idha papañca akatvā sīghaṃ gantvā bhikkhusaṅghassa taṃ pavattiṃ ārocetha bhikkhusaṅgho aññato dhātuṃ pariyesissatīti sāmaṇero ādite paṭṭhāya dhātu āgamanaṃ vatvā dhātuyo tava santikeyeva, papañcaṃ akatvā dehīti codesi.

Then the Nāga king, knowing that the relics had been removed by his nephew, said thus: 'The relics are not in my presence. You, without making any complication here, should go quickly and inform the community of monks of this matter, so that the community of monks may search for relics from elsewhere.' But the novice, recounting the history of the relics from the beginning, pressed him, saying: 'The relics are indeed in your presence. Without making any complication, give them.'

Tato nāgarājā sāmaṇerena mūlamhi gahitabhāvaṃ ñatvā yena kenaci pariyāyena dhātuyo adatvāva pesituṃ vaṭṭatīti cintetvā sāmaṇeraṃ dhātugharaṃ netvā cetiyañca cetiyagharañca dassesi. Taṃ pana cetiyañca cetiyagharañca sabbaratanamayameva ahosi.

Then the Nāga king, knowing that the novice had grasped the root of the matter, thought that he should by some means send him away without giving the relics. He took the novice to the relic chamber and showed him both the cetiya and the cetiya-house. Now, that cetiya and cetiya-house were entirely made of all kinds of precious gems.

Vuttañhetaṃ mahāvaṃse

For this was said in the Mahāvaṃsa:

“Anekadhā anekehi ratanehi susaṅkhataṃ,

Cetiyaṃ cetiyagharaṃ-passa bhikkhu sunimmita”nti;

"Behold, O monk, the cetiya and the cetiya-house, well-created, and well-fashioned in many ways with various jewels."

Dassetvā ca pana cetiyagharato oruyha addhacaṇdakapāsāṇe pavāḷapadumamhi ṭhatvā imassa cetiyassa cetiyagharassa ca agghaṃ karohi [O 0.0079] bhanteti āha sāmaṇero na sakkoma mahārāja agghaṃ kātuṃ, sakalepi tambhapaṇṇidīpe ratanāni imaṃ addhacaṇdaka pāsāṇaṃ nāgghatīti āha.

And having shown him, he descended from the cetiya-house, stood on the half-moon stone adorned with coral lotuses, and said, "Venerable sir, assess the value of this cetiya and the cetiya-house." The novice replied, "Great king, we cannot assess its value. All the jewels in the entire island of Tambapaṇṇi are not worth this half-moon stone."

Nāgarājā evaṃ sante mahāsakkāraṭṭhānato appasakkāraṭṭhānaṃ dhātūnaṃ nayanaṃ ayuttaṃ nanu bhikkhūti āha. Sāmaṇero evamāhamahārāja buddhā nāma dhammagarukā, na āmisa garukaṃ, tumhesu cakkavāḷappamāṇaṃ ratanagharaṃ māpetvā sabbaratanassa pūretvā dhātuyo pariharantesupi ekanāgopi dhammābhisamayaṃ kātuṃ samattho nāma natthi yasmā-

The Nāga King said, "If this is so, then is it not improper, O monk, to take the relics from a place of great honor to a place of lesser honor?" The novice replied, "Great King, Buddhas by nature esteem the Dhamma, not material offerings. Even if you were to build a jewel-house the size of a world-system, fill it with all manner of jewels, and so care for the relics, not even one Nāga would be capable of attaining a breakthrough to the Dhamma, because—"

“Saccābhisamayo nāga-tumhākampi na vijjati. Saccābhisamayaṭṭhānaṃ-netuṃ yuttañhi dhātuyo. Saṃsāradukkhamokkhāya-uppajjanta tathāgatā, buddhassa vetthādīppāyo-tena nessāmi dhātuyo. Dhātunidhānaṃ ajjeva-so hi rājā karissati.

"A breakthrough to the truth, O Nāga, is not found among you. It is indeed fitting to take the relics to a place where a breakthrough to the truth can occur. For the sake of liberation from the suffering of saṃsāra do Tathāgatas arise. The Buddha's intention is for this purpose; therefore, I shall take the relics. For that king will perform the enshrinement of the relics this very day.

Tasmā papañcamakaritvā-lahuṃ me dehi dhātuye”ti-āha. Evaṃ vutte nāgarājā appaṭihāno yutvā attano bhāgineyyena dhātuyo gopitoti maññamāno evamāha. Tumhe bhante cetiye dhātūnaṃ atthibhāvaṃ vā natthibhāvaṃ vā ajānantā dehi dehīti vadatha. Ahaṃ natthīti vadāmi. Sace passatha gahetvā gacchathāti. Gaṇhāmi mahārājāti, gaṇha bhikkhūti. Gaṇhāmi mahārājāti, gaṇha bhikkhūti tikkhattuṃ paṭiññaṃ gahetvā.

Therefore, without making any delay, give me the relics,' he said. When this was said, the Nāga king, unable to refuse and thinking that the relics were guarded by his nephew, replied thus: "Venerable sir, not knowing whether the relics exist in the cetiya or not, you say, 'Give, give.' I say they do not exist. If you see them, take them and go." "I take them, great king." "Take them, monk." "I take them, great king." "Take them, monk." Having taken the pledge three times,

“Sukhumaṃ karaṃ māpayitvā-thikkhū tatra ṭhitoca so,

Bhāgineyyassa vadane-hatthampakkhippa tāvade,

Dhātukaraṇḍamadāya-tiṭṭha nāgāti bhāsiha,

Nimujjitvā pathaviyaṃ-pariveṇamhi uṭṭhahi;”

Having created a subtle hand, the monk, while standing there, at that very moment placed that hand inside the nephew's mouth, took the relic casket, and said, 'Stay, O Nāga!' Then, having submerged into the earth, he arose in his dwelling.

Tadā sāmaṇerassa nāgena saddhiṃ yuddhaṃ passissāmāti samāgatā deva-nāga parisāpa bhikkhunāgassa vijayaṃ disvā haṭṭhā pamodito dhātuyo pūjayantāva teneva saha āgamuṃ, nāgarājā sāmaṇerassa gata kāle bhikkhuṃ vañcetvā pesitomhīti haṭṭhatuṭṭho dhātuyo gahetvā āgamatthāya bhāgineyyassa sāsanaṃ pesesi.

Then, the assembly of devas and nāgas that had gathered, thinking, "We will witness the battle between the novice and the nāga," seeing the victory of the noble bhikkhu, were delighted and joyful. Venerating the relics, they came along with him. When the novice had departed, the Nāga king, overjoyed and thinking, "I have deceived the bhikkhu and sent him away," sent a message to his nephew to come and bring the relics.

“Bhāgineyyo’tha kucchimhi-apassitvā karaṇḍakaṃ,

Paridevamāno āgantvā-mātulassa nivedayi;

Tadā so nāgarājāpi-vañcitamha mayaṃ iti,

Paridevi nāgā sabbepi-parideviṃsu piṇḍitā;”

"Then the nephew, not seeing the casket in his belly, lamenting, came and informed his uncle. Then that Nāga king too—'We have been deceived!'—lamented, and all the Nāgas, gathered together, lamented."

Tato [O 0.0080] nāgabhavane sabbe nāgā samāgantvā kese muñcitvā ubhohi hatthehi hadaye gahetvā nīluppala sadisehi nettehi vilīna sokamiva assudhāraṃ pavattayamānā-

Then, all the nāgas, having gathered in the nāga palace, loosened their hair, and clutching their hearts with both hands, with eyes like blue lotuses, as if with melting sorrow, they shed streams of tears—

“Paridevamānā āgantvā-nāgā saṅghassa santike,

Bahudhā parideviṃsa-dhātāharaṇa dukkhitā”ti;

Lamenting, the Nāgas came into the presence of the Saṅgha, grieving in many ways, afflicted by the removal of the relics.

Paridevitvā ca bhikkhusaṅghassa evamāhaṃsu- bhante kassaci pīḷaṃ akatvā amhākaṃ puññānubhāvena labhitvā cīraparihaṭa dhātuyo kasmā anavasesaṃ katvā aharāpetha amhākaṃ saggamokkhantarāyaṃ karothāti.

And having lamented, they said thus to the Saṅgha of bhikkhus: 'Venerable sirs, without causing harm to anyone, we obtained these long-possessed relics through the power of our merit. Why have you taken them all away, leaving none? You are creating an obstacle to our heaven and liberation.'

’Tesaṃ saṅgho’nukampāya-thokaṃ dhātumadāpayi,

Te tena tuṭṭhā gantvāna-pūjābhaṇḍāni āharuṃ;

Out of compassion for them, the Saṅgha gave a small portion of the relics. Pleased with that, they went and brought articles for offerings.

Tato sakko devānamiṇdo vissakammaṃ āmantetvā sāmaṇerassa uṭṭhitaṭṭhāne sattaratanamayaṃ maṇḍapaṃ māpehīti āha. So tasmiyeva khaṇe maṇḍapaṃ māpesi atha sakko dvīsu devalokesu devaparisāya parivuto suvaṇṇacaṅgoṭakena saddhiṃ ratana pallaṅkamādāya āgantvā tasmiṃ maṇḍape patiṭṭhāpetvā sāmaṇerassa hatthato dhātukaraṇḍakaṃ gahetvā tasmiṃ pallaṅke patiṭṭhāpesi. Tadā-

Then Sakka, lord of the devas, summoned Vissakamma and said, 'Build a pavilion made of the seven precious gems in the place where the novice stood.' In that very moment, he had the pavilion built. Then Sakka, surrounded by the assembly of devas from the two deva worlds, came, bringing a jeweled throne together with a golden casket. Having established it in that pavilion, he took the relic casket from the novice's hand and placed it upon the throne. At that time—

’Brahmā chattamadhāresi-santusito vālavijaniṃ,

Maṇitālavaṇṭaṃ suyāmo-sakko saṅkhantu sodakaṃ;

Brahmā held the parasol, Santusita the yak-tail fan, Suyāma the jeweled palm-leaf fan, and Sakka the conch filled with water.

Cattāro tu mahārāja-aṭṭhaṃsu khaggapāṇino,

Samuggahatthā dvattiṃsā-devaputtā mahiddhikā;

The Four Great Kings stood with swords in hand; thirty-two devaputtas of great power stood with caskets in hand;

Pāricchattaka pupphehi pūjayantā tahiṃ ṭhitā,

Kumāriyopi dvattiṃsā-daṇḍidīpadharā ṭhitā;

They stood there, worshipping with Pāricchattaka flowers; and thirty-two maidens also stood, holding staff-lamps.

Palāpetvā duṭṭhayakkhe-yakkhasenāpati pana

Aṭṭhavīsati aṭṭhaṃsu-ārakkhaṃ kurumānakā;

Having driven away the wicked yakkhas, the twenty-eight generals of the yakkhas then stood providing protection.

Vīṇaṃ vādayamānova-aṭṭhā pañcasikho tahiṃ,

Raṅgabhūmiṃ māpayitvā-timbarū turiyaghosavā;

Pañcasikha stood there, playing the lute; Timbarū, having prepared the stage, made the musical instruments resound.

Anekā devaputtā ca-sādhugītappayojakā,

Mahākālo nāgarājā-thūyamāno anekadhā;

And many devaputtas, performers of beautiful songs; the great Nāga king, Mahākāla, being praised in many ways;

Dibbaturiyāni vajjanti-dibbasaṅgiti vattati,

Dibbagaṇdhā ca vassāni-vassāpenti ca devatā;

Divine musical instruments played, a divine concert proceeded; and the deities caused divine fragrances and rains to fall.

Tadā iṇdaguttatthero mārassa paṭibāhanatthāya cakkavāḷapariyantaṃ katvā ākāse lohachattaṃ māpesi. Pañcanikāyika therā [O 0.0081] dhātuyo parivāretvā pañcasu ṭhānesu nisīditvā gaṇasajjhāyamakaṃsu. Tasmiṃ kāle rājā taṃ ṭhanaṃ āgantvā sisato suvaṇṇa caṅgoṭakaṃ otāretvā dhātu caṅgoṭakaṃ attano caṅgoṭake ṭhapetvā pallaṅke patiṭṭhāpetvā gaṇdhamālādīhi pūjetvā pañcapatiṭṭhitena vanditvā sirasi añjaliṃ paggayha akkhīni ummīletvā olokento aṭṭhāsi. Tasmiṃ khaṇe dhātumatthake setacchattaṃ dissati chattagāhaka brahmā na dissati. Tathā tālavaṇṭa vijani ādayo dissanti gāhakā na dissanti dibbaturiyaghosa saṅgitiyo suyyanti gaṇdhabba devatā na dissanti. Rājā etaṃ acchariyaṃ disvā iṇdaguttattheraṃ evamāha-devatā dibbachattena pūjesuṃ ahaṃ mānusa kacchattena pūjemi bhanneti. Thero yuttaṃ mahārājāti āha. Rājā attano suvaṇṇapiṇḍike setacchattena pūjetvā suvaṇṇabhiṃkāraṃ gahetvā abhisekodakaṃ datvā taṃ divasaṃ sakalatambapaṇṇi dīpe rajjaṃ adāsi.

Then, the Elder Indagutta, to ward off Māra, caused a metal canopy to be created in the sky, extending to the edge of the universe. The Elders of the five Nikāyas, surrounding the relics, sat in five places and performed a group recitation. At that time, the king came to that place, lowered the golden casket from his head, placed the relic casket into his own casket, established it upon the throne, and honored it with perfumes, garlands, and other offerings. He paid homage with the five-point prostration, raised his joined hands to his head, and stood gazing with eyes wide open. At that moment, a white parasol was seen above the relics, but the Brahmā holding the parasol was not seen. Likewise, palm-leaf fans, whisks, and so on were seen, but their bearers were not seen. The sounds of divine musical instruments and songs were heard, yet the Gandhabba devas were not seen. The king, seeing this marvel, said thus to the Elder Indagutta: 'Venerable sir, the devas have honored the relics with a divine parasol; I honor them with a human parasol.' The Elder replied, 'It is fitting, great king.' The king then honored them with a white parasol on his own golden stand, took a golden pitcher, offered consecration water, and on that day, gave the kingdom of the entire island of Tambapaṇṇi.

Tato sabbaturiyāni paggaṇhiṃsu, gaṇdhamālādīhi pūjetva mahantaṃ sakkāramakaṃsu puna rājā theraṃ pucchi - amhākaṃ satthā dibbamānusakāni dve chattāni dhāresi bhante. Na dve chattāni tīṇi chattāni mahārājāti. Aññaṃ chattaṃ napassāmi bhanneti. Sīlapatiṭṭhaṃ samādhidaṇḍakaṃ iṇdriyasalākaṃ balamālaṃ maggaphalapatta sañchannaṃ vimuttivarasetacchattaṃ ussāpetvā ñāṇābhisekampatto dhammaratana cakkaṃ pavattetvā dasasahassa cakkavāḷesu buddharajjaṃ hatthagataṃ katvā rajjaṃ kāresīti rājā tīṇicchatta dhārakassa satthuno tikkhattuṃ rajjaṃ dammīti tikkhattuṃ dhātuyo rajjena pūjesi.

Then all the musical instruments were played, and honoring the relics with perfumes, garlands, and so on, they paid great reverence. Again, the king asked the Elder: 'Venerable sir, did our Teacher bear two parasols, the divine and the human?' 'Not two parasols, great king, but three.' 'I do not see the other parasol, venerable sir.' 'Having raised the excellent white parasol of liberation—which is founded on virtue, has concentration as its staff, the spiritual faculties as its ribs, the powers as its garland, and is covered with the leaves of the paths and fruits—and having attained the consecration of knowledge, he set rolling the wheel of the jewel of the Dhamma, and having brought the Buddha-kingdom in the ten-thousand world-systems into his hand, he reigned.' Thus the king, thinking, 'I shall give the kingdom three times to the Teacher, the bearer of three parasols,' honored the relics three times with the kingdom.

Tato rājā devamanussesu dibbagaṇdhamālādīhi pūjentesu anekesu turiyaghosa saṅgitesu vattamānesu dhātukaraṇḍakaṃ sīsenādāya ratanamaṇḍapato nikkhamitvā bhikkhusaṅgha parivuto mahācetiyaṃ padakkhiṇaṃ katvā pācīnadvārenāruyha dhātugabbhaṃ otari. Tato mahācetiyaṃ parivāretvā channavuti koṭippamāṇa arahanto aṭṭhaṃsu.

Then, while devas and humans were offering honor with divine perfumes, garlands, and so on, and while the sounds of many musical instruments and songs were proceeding, the king, taking the relic casket on his head, departed from the jeweled pavilion. Surrounded by the Saṅgha of bhikkhus, he circumambulated the Great Cetiya, ascended by the eastern gate, and descended into the relic chamber. Then, ninety-six crores of Arahants stood surrounding the Great Cetiya.

Rājā sīsato dhātukaraṇḍakaṃ otāretvā mahārahe sayanapiṭṭhe ṭhapessāmīti cintesi tasmiṃ khaṇe dhātukaraṇḍako rañño sīsato satta tālappamāṇe ṭhāne gantvā sayameva vicari dhātuyo ākāsamuggantvā dvattiṃsa mahā purisalakkhaṇa asiti anubyañjanā byāmappabhā patimaṇḍitaṃ ketumālopa sobhitaṃ nila-pīta-lohitādi bheda vicitra raṃsijālā samujjalaṃ buddhavesaṃ gahetvā gaṇḍambamūle [O 0.0082] yamaka pāṭihāriyaṃ sadisa yamaka pāṭihāriyaṃ akaṃsu. Taṃ dhātupāṭihāriyaṃ disvā pasīditvā arahattampattā devamanussā dvādasakoṭiyo ahesuṃ. Sesaphalattayaṃ pattā gaṇanapathamatītā ahesuṃ. Evaṃ dhātuyo anekadhā pāṭihāriyaṃ dassetvā buddhavesaṃ vissajjetvā karaṇḍakaṃ pavisitvā tena saddhiṃ otaritvā rañño sīse patiṭṭhahiṃsu.

The king thought, 'I will lower the relic casket from my head and place it on the precious couch.' At that moment, the relic casket rose from the king's head to a height of seven palm-trees and moved about by itself. The relics, ascending into the sky, assumed the form of the Buddha, adorned with the thirty-two marks of a great man and the eighty minor characteristics, radiant with a fathom-wide aura, resplendent with a glorious top-knot, and blazing with a net of variegated rays of blue, yellow, red, and other colors. They performed a Twin Miracle similar to the one at the foot of the Gaṇḍamba tree. Seeing this miracle of the relics and becoming serene, twelve crores of devas and humans attained Arahantship. Those who attained the other three fruits were beyond counting. Thus, the relics, having displayed miracles in many ways, relinquished the Buddha's form, re-entered the casket, and descending with it, settled upon the king's head.

Rājā amatena viya abhisitto saphalaṃ manussatta paṭilābhaṃ maññamāno ubhohi hatthehi dhātukaraṇḍakaṃ gahetvā nāṭaka parivuto alaṅkata sayana samīpaṃ gantvā dhātucaṅgoṭakaṃ ratanapallaṅke ṭhapetvā gaṇdhavāsitodakena hatthe dhovitvā vatujātiya gaṇdhena ubbaṭṭetvā ratanakaraṇḍakaṃ vicaritvā dhātuyo gahetvā evaṃ cintesi.

The king, as if anointed with the deathless, considering his attainment of a human existence to be fruitful, took the relic casket with both hands. Surrounded by dancers, he approached the decorated couch. Having placed the relic casket on the jeweled throne, he washed his hands with perfumed water, anointed them with the finest perfumes, opened the jeweled casket, took the relics, and thought thus:

“Anākulā kehicipi yadi hessasti dhātuyo,

Janassa saraṇaṃ hutvā yadi ṭhassanti dhātuyo;

'If the relics are to be undisturbed by anyone, and if the relics are to endure, having become a refuge for the people,

Satthunipannā kārena parinibbāna mañcake,

Nipajjantu supaññatte-sayanamhi mahārahe”ti;

let them lie down on this well-prepared and precious couch in the manner of the Teacher lying on his couch of Parinibbāna.'

Evaṃ cintetvā pana varasayanapiṭṭhe dhātuyo ṭhapesi tasmiṃ khaṇe dhātuyo raññā cintita niyāmeneva mahārahe sayane buddhavesena sayiṃsu.

Having thought thus, he placed the relics on the excellent couch. At that moment, the relics, precisely in the manner conceived by the king, lay down on the precious couch in the form of the Buddha.

“Āsāḷahi sukkapakkhassa pannarasa uposathe,

Uttarāsāḷha nakkhatte evaṃ dhātu patiṭṭhitā;

On the Uposatha day, the fifteenth of the bright fortnight of Āsāḷha, under the Uttarāsāḷha constellation, thus were the relics enshrined.

Saha dhātu patiṭṭhānā akampittha mahāmahī, pāṭihīrāni nekāni pavattiṃsu anekadhā.”

With the enshrinement of the relics, the great earth trembled; and many miracles occurred in various ways.

Tadahi udakapariyantaṃ katvā ayaṃ mahāpathavī saṅkampi sampakampi sampavedhi, mahāsamuddo saṅkhūbhi, ākāse vijjullatā nicchariṃsu. Khaṇikavassaṃ vassi, cha devalokā ekakolāhala mahosi rājā etaṃ acchariyaṃ disvā pasanno attano kañcana mālika setacchattena dhātuyo pūjetvā tambapaṇṇidipe rajjaṃ sattāhaṃ datvā tiṃsa satasahassagghanakaṃ alaṅkāra bhaṇḍaṃ omuñcitvā pūjesi. Tathā sabbāpi nāṭakitthiyo amaccā sesa mahājano devā ca sabbābharaṇāni pūjesuṃ tasmā-

Then this great earth, with water as its boundary, trembled, shook, and quaked. The great ocean surged, and flashes of lightning shot forth in the sky. A sudden rain fell, and the six heavenly realms were in a great uproar. The king, seeing this marvel, was filled with faith. He honored the relics with his golden garland and white parasol, then granted sovereignty over the island of Tambapaṇṇi for seven days, and taking off his ornaments worth thirty hundred thousand, he paid homage. Likewise, all the dancing women, ministers, the rest of the great assembly, and the gods offered all their ornaments in worship. Therefore—

Tiṭṭhantaṃ sugataṃ tilokamahitaṃ yo pūjaye sādaraṃ,

Yo vā sāsapa bījamattampi taṃ dhātuṃ naro pūjaye;

He who reverently honors the Sugata, revered in the three worlds, while he is still living, or a person who honors a relic of him, even one as small as a mustard seed;

Tesaṃ puññaphalaṃ samānamīti taṃ cittappasāde same,

Ñatvā taṃ parinibbutepi sugate dhātuṃ budho pūjaye’ti;

The fruit of their merit is the same, when the confidence of mind is the same. Knowing this, a wise person should revere the relics even when the Sugata has attained final Nibbāna.

Tato [O 0.0083] rājā cīvaravatthāni ceva guḷa-sappi ādi bhesajjāni ca saṅghassa datvā sabbarattiṃ gaṇasajjhāyaṃ kāresi puna divase nagare bheriṃ carāpesi mahājano imaṃ sattāhaṃ gaṇdhamālādīni ādiya gantvā dhātuyo vandatūti iṇdaguttattheropi sakala tambapaṇṇi dīpe manussā dhātuyo vanditukāmā, taṃ khaṇaṃyeva āgantvā vanditvā yathāṭṭhānaṃ gacchantūti adhiṭṭhāsi.

Then the king gave robes and cloths, as well as medicines such as jaggery and ghee, to the Sangha and had a communal recitation performed throughout the whole night. The next day, he had a drum beaten in the city, announcing, "Let the great multitude take perfumes, garlands, and other offerings, and go and venerate the relics for these seven days." The Elder Indagutta also resolved, "May all the people in the island of Tambapaṇṇi who desire to venerate the relics arrive at this very moment, venerate them, and then return to their respective places."

Te tatheva dhātuyo vanditvā gamiṃsu rājā sattāhaṃ saṅghassa mahādānaṃ pavattetvā sattāhassa accayena dhātugabbhe mayā kattabbakiccaṃ niṭṭhāpitaṃ, dhātugabbhaṃ pidahatha bhanteti saṅghassa ārocesi. Saṅgho uttarasumana sāmaṇere āmantetvā tumhehi pubbe āhaṭa medavaṇṇa pāsāṇena dhātu gabbhaṃ pidahathāti āha. Te sādhūti sampaṭicchitvā dhātugabbhaṃ pidahiṃsu.

They paid homage to the relics in that very way and departed. The king, after conducting a great almsgiving for the Sangha for seven days, at the end of the seven days announced to the Sangha, "The duty to be done by me regarding the relic chamber has been completed. Close the relic chamber, venerable sirs." The Sangha, summoning the novices Uttara and Sumana, said, "You should close the relic chamber with the meda-colored stone that was brought by you earlier." They accepted with "Very well" and closed the relic chamber.

Tato khīṇāsavā dhātugabbhe gaṇdhā mā susantu mālā mā milāyantu. Dīpā mā nibbāyantu ratanāni mā vivaṇṇāni hontu pūjanīyabhaṇḍāni mā nassantu. Medavaṇṇa pāsāṇā sandhīyantu, paccatthikānaṃ okāso mā hotūti adhiṭṭhahiṃsu.

Then the Arahants, whose taints were destroyed, resolved: "In the relic chamber, let scents not fade, let garlands not wither, let lamps not be extinguished, let jewels not lose their color, let objects of veneration not perish, let the meda-colored stones be joined, and let there be no opportunity for adversaries."

Evaṃ rājā dhātu nidhāpetvā puna nagare bheriṃ carāpesi mahā cetiye dhātuṃ nidhāpetvā dhātuṃ āharitvā nidhānaṃ karontūti. Mahājano attano attano balānurūpena suvaṇṇa rajatādi karaṇḍe kārāpetvā tattha dhātuyo patiṭṭhāpetvā dhātunidhānassupari medavaṇṇa pāsāṇa piṭṭhiyaṃ nidahiṃsu. Sabbehi sannihita dhātuyo sahassamattā ahesunti.

Thus, the king, having had the relics enshrined, again had a drum proclaimed in the city: "Now that relics have been enshrined in the Great Cetiya, let others bring relics and perform the enshrinement." The people, according to their own ability, had caskets of gold, silver, and so on made, placed the relics therein, and deposited them upon the surface of the meda-colored stone above the relic enshrinement. All the relics thus gathered amounted to about a thousand.

Iti sādhujana manopasādanatthāya kate thūpavaṃse dhātunidhāna kathā niṭṭhitā.

Thus ends the account of the relic enshrinement in the Thūpavaṃsa, composed for the inspiration of the minds of good people.

24. Tato rājā taṃ sabbaṃ pidahetvā cetiyaṃ karonto udarena saddhiṃ caturassa koṭṭhakaṃ niṭṭhāpesi atha chattakamme sudhākamme ca aniṭṭhiteyeva māraṇantika rogena gilāno hutvā dīghavāpito kaniṭṭhabhātaraṃ pakkosāpetvā cetiye aniṭṭhitaṃ chattakammaṃ sudhākammañca sīghaṃ niṭṭhāpetvā maṃ tosehi tātāti āha. So rañño dubbalabhāvaṃ ñatvā antare aniṭṭhitakammaṃ kātuṃ na sakkāti suddhavatthehi kañcukaṃ kāretvā cetiye paṭimuñcāpetvā cittakārehi kañcuka matthake vedikā ca puṇṇaghaṭa pañcaṅguli pantiyo ca kārāpesi.

24. Then the king, having covered all that, while constructing the cetiya, completed the square chamber along with the interior. However, before the work on the umbrella and the plastering was finished, he fell ill with a mortal disease. He summoned his younger brother from Dīghavāpi and said, "Dear one, quickly complete the unfinished work on the cetiya's umbrella and plastering and bring me joy." He, knowing the king's weakness and that it was not possible to complete the unfinished work in time, had a covering made of white cloth and had it fastened over the cetiya. He then had painters create on the surface of the covering a vedikā railing, full vases, and rows of five-finger prints.

Naḷakārehi veḷumaya chattaṃ kāretvā kharapattamaye caṇdasūriyamaṇḍale muddhani vedikā kāretvā lākhākukuṭṭhakehi taṃ vicittaṃ katvā thūpakammaṃ niṭṭhitanti rañño arocesi rājā tena hi maṃ [O 0.0084] mahācetiyaṃ dassehīti vatvā sivikāya nipajjitvā cetiyā padakkhiṇaṃ katvā dakkhiṇadvāre bhūmisayanaṃ paññāpetvā tattha nipanno dakkhiṇena passena sayitvā mahāthūpaṃ vāmapassena sayitvā lohapāsādaṃ olokento pasanna citto ahosi. Tadā rañño sāsanassa bahūpakārabhāvaṃ sallakkhetvā gilāna pucchanatthāya tato tato āgatā bhikkhu channavuti koṭiyo rājānaṃ parivāretvā aṭṭhaṃsu. Tato saṅgho vagga vaggā hutvā gaṇasajjhāyaṃ akāsi.

He had reed-workers make a bamboo parasol, and on its top, a vedikā railing and sun-and-moon discs of rough leaves, and had it decorated with lacquer and vermilion. He then announced to the king that the work on the thūpa was completed. The king said, "Then show me the Great Cetiya," and lying down on a palanquin, he circumambulated the cetiya. At the southern gate, he had a couch prepared on the ground and lay there. Gazing at the Great Thūpa while lying on his right side, and at the Lohapāsāda while lying on his left side, his mind was filled with joy. At that time, recognizing that the king was a great benefactor to the Dispensation, ninety-six crores of monks who had come from various places to inquire about his health surrounded the king. Then the Sangha, having formed into groups, performed a communal recitation.

Rājā tasmiṃ samāgame theraputtābhayattheraṃ adisvā evaṃ cintesi. So mayi damiḷehi saddhiṃ aṭṭhavīsati mahāyuddhe kayiramāse apaccosakakitvā idāni maraṇayuddhe vattamāṇe mayhaṃ parājayaṃ disvā maññe nāgacchatīti. Tadā thero kariṇda nadī sīse pajjalita pabbate vasanto rañño parivitakkaṃ ñatvā pañcasata khīṇāsava parivusetā ākāsenāgantvā rañño purato pāturahosi.

The king, not seeing the Elder Theraputtābhaya in that assembly, thought thus: "He who did not retreat when I was fighting twenty-eight great battles with the Tamils, now, seeing my defeat in this present battle with death, I suppose he does not come." Then the Elder, who was dwelling on the Pajjalita mountain at the source of the Kariṇda river, knowing the king's thought, came through the air, attended by five hundred Arahants, and appeared before the king.

Rājā theraṃ disvā attano purato nisīdāpetvā evamāha bhante tumhehi saddhiṃ dasamahā yodhe gahetvā damiḷehi saddhi yujjhiṃ. Idāni ekakova maccūnā saddhiṃ yujjhituṃ ārabhiṃ. Maccu sattumpana parājetuṃ na sakkomīti. Tato-

The king, seeing the Elder, had him seated before him and said: "Venerable sir, taking the ten great warriors with you, I fought with the Tamils. Now, all alone, I have begun to fight with death. But I am unable to defeat the enemy, death." Then—

Theraputtābhayatthero mā bhāyi manujādhipa,

Kilesa sattuṃ ajinitvā ajeyyo maccūsattuko;

The Elder Theraputtābhaya said: "Fear not, O lord of men. Without conquering the enemy of the defilements, the enemy death is unconquerable."

Iti vatvā evaṃ anusāsi. Mahārāja sabboyeva loka sannivāse jātiyā anugato, jarāya anusaṭo, vyādhinā abhibhūto, maraṇena abbhāhato. Tetāha-

Having spoken thus, he gave this instruction: "Great king, every being in the abode of this world is followed by birth, pursued by aging, overwhelmed by disease, and struck down by death. Therefore it is said—"

“Yathāpi selā vipulā-nahaṃ āhacca pabbatā,

Samantā anupariyeyyuṃ-nippothentā catuddisaṃ;

"Just as vast mountains of rock, touching the sky, would advance from all sides, crushing the four quarters;

Evaṃ jarā ca maccu ca-adhivattanti pāṇino,

Khattiyo brāhmaṇe vesse sudde caṇḍāla pukkuse,

Na kiñci parivajjeti-sabbamevābhimaddati;

So too aging and death overwhelm living beings— Khattiyas, brahmins, vessas, suddas, caṇḍālas, and pukkusas— it spares nothing; it crushes everything.

Na tattha hatthinaṃ bhūmi-na rathānaṃ na pattiyā,

Na cāpi mantayuddhena-sakkā jetuṃ dhanena vā”ti;

There is no place there for elephants, nor for chariots, nor for infantry; it cannot be conquered by stratagem of war, nor by wealth."

Tasmā idaṃ maraṇaṃ nāma mahāyasānaṃ mahāsammatādīnaṃ mahā puññānaṃ jotiyādīnaṃ mahāthāmānaṃ baladevādīnaṃ iddhimantānaṃ mahāmoggallānādīnaṃ paññāvantānaṃ sāriputtādīnaṃ sayambhūñāṇena adhigata saccānaṃ, paccekabuddhānaṃ sabbaguṇasamannāgatānaṃ sammāsambuddhānampi upari nirāsaṅkameva patati kimaṅgapanaññesu sattesu.

Therefore, this thing called death falls without hesitation even upon the greatly renowned, beginning with Mahāsammata; the greatly meritorious, beginning with Jotiya; the greatly powerful, beginning with Baladeva; those with psychic powers, beginning with Mahāmoggallāna; the wise, beginning with Sāriputta; the Paccekabuddhas, who have realized the truth by their own knowledge; and even the Perfectly Self-Enlightened Buddhas, endowed with all virtues—what then to say of other beings?

Tasmā- [O 0.0085]

Therefore—

Mahāyasā rājavarā gatā te sabbe mahāsammata ādayopi,

Aniccabhāvaṃ baladeva ādi mahābalā ceva tathā gamiṃsu;

Those greatly renowned, foremost kings have all gone, even those beginning with Mahāsammata. The mighty ones, beginning with Baladeva, also went thus to impermanence.

Ye puññavantāni gatā pasiddhiṃ mahaddhanaṃ jotiyameṇḍakādī,

Upāvisiṃ vaccumukhaṃ sabhogā sabbepi te rāhumukhaṃ sasīva;

Those meritorious ones who attained fame, possessing great wealth, beginning with Jotiya and Meṇḍaka— all of them, together with their possessions, entered the mouth of death, just as the moon enters the mouth of Rāhu.

Yo iddhimantesu tathāgatassa puttesuseṭṭho iti vissutopi,

Thero mahārājasaheva iddhibalena so maccumukhaṃ paviṭṭho;

Even he who was renowned as the foremost among the Tathāgata's disciples in psychic powers— that Elder, O great king, even with his psychic power, entered the mouth of death.

Sabbesu sattesu jinaṃ ṭhapetvā nevatthi paññāya samopi yena,

So dhammasenāpati sāvakopi gato mahārāja aniccataṃ’ca;

Among all beings, excepting the Conqueror, there is none equal to him in wisdom— that disciple, the general of the Dhamma, O great king, has also gone to impermanence.

Sayambhūñāṇassa balena santiṃ gatā mahārāja sayambhūnopi,

Sabbepi te ñāṇabalūpa pannā aniccataṃ neva atikkamiṃsu;

By the power of self-arisen wisdom they attained peace, O great king, even the self-arisen ones; all those endowed with the power of knowledge did not overcome impermanence.

Tilokanātho purisuttamo so aniccabhāvaṃ samatikkamitvā,

Nāsakkhi gantuṃ sugatopi rāja aññesu sattesu kathāva natthi;

The Lord of the three worlds, the supreme among men, the Sugata, O king, was not able to transcend the state of impermanence; what then to say of other beings?

Tasmā mahārāja bhavesu sattā sabbepi nāsuṃ maraṇā vimuttā,

Sabbampi saṅkhāragataṃ aniccaṃ dūkkhaṃ anattāti vicintayassu;

Therefore, great king, all beings in these existences are not freed from death. Reflect that all which is conditioned is impermanent, suffering, and not-self.

“Dutiye attabhāvepi dhammacchaṇdo mahā hi te,

Upaṭṭhite devaloke hitvā dibbasukhaṃ tuvaṃ;

In your second existence, your delight in the Dhamma was great indeed; when the heavenly realm was present, you abandoned divine pleasures.

Idhāgamma bahuṃ puññaṃ akāsi ca anekadhā,

Karaṇampeka rajjassa sāsanajjotanāya te;

Having come here, you made much merit in many ways, and also established a single kingdom for the illumination of the Teaching.

Mahā rāja kataṃ puññaṃ yāvajja divasā tayā,

Sabbaṃ anussaretheva sukhaṃ sajju bhavissati;

Great king, all the merit you have made up to this day—just recollect it, and happiness will instantly arise.

Taṃ sutvā tuṭṭhamānaso rājā bhante tumhe maccuyuddhepi apassayāti vatvā laddhassāso puññapotthakaṃ vācetuṃ āṇāpesi lekhako puññapotthakaṃ evaṃ vācesi.

Hearing this, the king, with a delighted mind, said, "Venerable sir, I did not see you even in the battle with death." Having gained reassurance, he ordered the scribe to recite from the Book of Merit. The scribe then recited from the Book of Merit thus:

“Ekūnasatavihārā mahārājena kāritā,

Ekūnasatakoṭīhi vihāro maricaṭṭi ca;

Ninety-nine monasteries were caused to be made by the great king; and the Maricavaṭṭi monastery with ninety-nine crores.
