Uttamo lohapāsādo tiṃsakoṭīhi kārito,

Mahāthūpe anagghāni kāritā catuvīsati;

The excellent Lohapāsāda was caused to be made with thirty crores; at the Great Thūpa, twenty-four priceless offerings were made.

Mahāthūpamhi sesāni karitāni subuddhinā,

Koṭisahassaṃ agghanti mahārāja tayā puna;

At the Great Stupa, the remaining works were done by the one of good wisdom; they are worth a thousand crores, O great king, done by you as well.

Koḷamba nāma maleya akkhakkhāyika chātake,

Kuṇḍalāni mahagghāni duve datvāna gaṇhiya;

In a time of famine, as a Maleya named Koḷamba, a professional gambler, having given two very costly earrings and having received food in return;

Khīṇāsavānaṃ [O 0.0086] pañcannaṃ mahātherānamuttamo,

Dinno pasannacittena kaṅgu ambila piṇḍako;

To the foremost of five great elders who had destroyed the cankers, a morsel of millet and sour gruel was given with a serene mind.

Cūḷaṅgaṇiya yuddhamhi parajjhitvā palāyatā,

Kālaṃ ghosāpayitvāna āgatassa vihāyasā;

In the battle of Cūḷaṅgaṇiya, while defeated and fleeing, to one who came through the sky, having had the time announced,

Khīṇāsavassa yatino attānamanapekkhiya,

Dinnaṃ saraka bhattanti puññapotthaṃ avācayi;

To the ascetic who had destroyed the cankers, not regarding himself, a bowl of rice was given.' Thus he recited from the Book of Merit.

25. Taṃ sutvā rājā tussitvā ṭhapehi ṭhapehi bhaṇeti vatvā evamāha. Maricavaṭṭi vihāramahasattāhe thūpāramhasattāhe ca cātuddisa ubhato saṅghassa mahārahaṃ mahādānaṃ pavattesiṃ. Catuvīsati mahāvisākhapūjā kāresiṃ tambapaṇṇidīpe mahābhikkhusaṅghassa tikkhattuṃ cīvaramadāsiṃ satta satta dīnāni laṅkā rajjaṃ sāsanassa pañcakkhattuṃ adāsiṃ sappi sannitta suparisuddha vaṭṭiyā dvādasaṭhānesu, sattaṃ dīpasahassaṃ jālesiṃ aṭṭhārassu ṭhānesu gilānānaṃ vejjehi bhesajjañca bhattañca niccaṃ dāpesi. Catucattālīsa ṭhānesu telullopakañca adāsiṃ. Tattakesuyeva ṭhānesu ghatapakkajālapūve bhattena saddhiṃ niccaṃ dāpesiṃ.

25. Hearing this, the king was pleased and said, "Stop, stop!" and then spoke thus: "At the seven-day festival of the Maricavaṭṭi Vihāra and at the seven-day festival of the Thūpārāma, I offered a great and worthy donation to the Sangha of both communities from the four directions. I performed twenty-four great Visākha pūjās. In the island of Tambapaṇṇi, I gave robes three times to the great community of monks. Five times I gave the kingdom of Laṅkā to the Dispensation, for seven days each time. With clarified butter, honey, and perfectly pure wicks, I lit seven thousand lamps at twelve places. At eighteen places, I constantly provided medicine and food for the sick through physicians. At forty-four places, I gave oil for anointing. In that same number of places, I regularly offered ghee-cooked net-cakes along with meals."

Māse māse aṭṭhasu uposathadivasesu laṅkādīpe sabbavihāresu dīpatelaṃ dāpesiṃ. Āmisa dānato dhammadānaṃ mahantanti sutvā heṭṭhaṃ lohapāsāde dhammāsane nisīditvā maṅgala suttaṃ osāretuṃ ārabhitvāpi saṅgha gāravena osāretuṃ nāsakkhiṃ. Tato paṭṭhāya dhammadesake sakkaritvā sabbavihāresu dhammakathaṃ kathāpesiṃ ekekassa dhammakathikassa nāḷa nāḷippamāṇāni sappiphāṇita sakkharāni caturaṅgula muṭṭhippamāṇaṃ yaṭṭhimadhukaṃ sāṭakadvayañca māsassa aṭṭhasu uposatha disesu dāpesiṃ. Etaṃ sabbampi issariye ṭhatvā dinnattā mama cittaṃ sa ārādheti. Jīvitaṃ pana anapekkhitvā duggatena mayā dinnadānadvayameva ārādhetīti.

Month after month, on the eight Uposatha days, I provided lamp oil to all the monasteries in the island of Laṅkā. Having heard that the gift of the Dhamma is greater than material gifts, I sat on the Dhamma seat in the lower Lohapāsāda and began to recite the Maṅgala Sutta, but out of reverence for the Sangha, I could not complete it. From then on, honoring the Dhamma preachers, I arranged for Dhamma talks in all the monasteries. To each Dhamma preacher, I provided a nāḷi measure of ghee, molasses, and sugar, a handful of licorice four fingers in size, and two cloths on the eight Uposatha days of the month. All this, having been given while I was established in sovereignty, does not fully satisfy my mind. But, disregarding my own life, only the two gifts given by me while in destitution truly bring me satisfaction.

Taṃ sutvā abhayatthero mahārāja pasādanīyaṭṭhāneyeva pasādaṃ akāsi taṃ pana piṇḍapātadvayaṃ parissa pīḷaṃ akatvā laddha dhammika paccayattā attānaṃ anavaloketvā asajjamānena dinnattā paṭiggāhakānaṃ yāvadatthaṃ katvā dinnattā pītipāmojjaṃjanayitvā balava saddhāya dinnattā deyyadhammassa niravasesaṃ paribhogaṃ gatattāti imehi pañcahi kāraṇehi mahantatti vatvā mahārāja kaṅgu ambili piṇḍagāhakattheresu maliyamahādevatthero samantakūṭe pañcannaṃ bhikkhusatānaṃ datvā paribhuñji pathavi cālanaka dhammaguttatthero kalyāṇīya vihāre pañcannaṃ bhikkhusatānaṃ datvā paribhuñji.

Having heard that, the Elder Abhaya said: "Great king, you have shown confidence in what is truly worthy of confidence. That twofold offering of almsfood is said to be great for these five reasons: because it was a righteous requisite obtained without oppressing others, because it was given without regard for oneself and without clinging, because it was given having satisfied the recipients' needs, because it was given with strong faith which generated joy and gladness, and because the gift was consumed without remainder. O great king, among the elders who received the morsel of millet and sour gruel, the Elder Maliyamahādeva at Samantakūṭa gave it to five hundred monks and then partook of it; the Elder Dhammagutta, the earth-shaker, at the Kalyāṇīya monastery gave it to five hundred monks and then partook of it."

Talaṅgaravāsī [O 0.0087] dhammaguttattheropi piyaṅgudīpe dvādasannaṃ bhikkhusahassānaṃ datvā paribhuñji. Maṅgaṇavāsī cuḷatissatthero kelāsakūṭe vihāre saṭṭhisahassānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ datvā paribhuñji mahābhaggattheropi ukkānagara vihāre sattasatānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ datvā paribhuñji saraka bhattagāhakatthero pana piyaṅgudīpe dvādasannaṃ bhikkhusahassānaṃ datvā paribhogamakāsīti vatvā rañño cittaṃ hāsesi.

The Elder Dhammagutta, residing at Talaṅgara, also gave it to twelve thousand monks on the island of Piyangudīpa and then partook of it. The Elder Cūḷatissa, residing at Maṅgaṇa, gave it to sixty thousand monks at the Kelāsakūṭa monastery and then partook of it. The Elder Mahābhagga also gave it to seven hundred monks at the Ukkānagara monastery and then partook of it. Furthermore, the elder who received the bowl of rice gave it to twelve thousand monks on the island of Piyangudīpa and then partook of it.' Having said this, he gladdened the king's mind.

Rājā cittaṃ pasādetvā evamāha-ahambhante catuvīsati vassāni rajjaṃ kārento bhikkhusaṅghassa pahūpakāro ahosiṃ. Kāyopi me saṅghassa upakārako hotu saṅghadāsassa me sarīraṃ mahācetiyassa dassanaṭṭhāne saṅghassa kammamālake jhāpethāti. Tato kaniṭṭhaṃ āmantetvā’tāta! Tissa! Mahāthūpe aniṭṭhitaṃ kammaṃ sādhukaṃ niṭṭhāpesi. Sāyaṃ pāto ca mahāthūpe pupphapūjaṃ kāretvā tikkhattuṃ upahāraṃ kārehi. Mayā ṭhapitaṃ dānavaṭṭaṃ sabbaṃ aparihāpetvā saṅghassa kattabbakiccesu sadā appamatto hohī’ti anusāyitvā tuṇhi ahosi.

The king, having gladdened his mind, spoke thus: "Venerable sirs, for twenty-four years while ruling, I have been of great service to the community of monks. May my body also be of service to the Sangha. Let my body, a servant of the Sangha, be cremated at the Sangha's terrace for formal acts, in a place with a view of the great cetiya." Then, summoning his younger brother, he said, "My dear Tissa! Complete well the unfinished work at the Great Thūpa. Morning and evening, have flower offerings made at the Great Thūpa and have the triple offering made. Do not neglect any of the regular alms-giving I established, and always be diligent in the duties to be performed for the Sangha." Having exhorted him thus, he became silent.

Tasmiṃ khaṇe bhikkhu gaṇasajjhāyaṃ ārabhiṃsu devatā pana chadeva lokato cha rathe gahetvā ādāya paṭipāṭiyā ṭhapetvā mahārāja amhākaṃ devaloko ramaṇīyo, amhākaṃ devaloko ramaṇīyoti vatvā attano attano devalokaṃ āgamanatthāya yāciṃsu rājā tesaṃ vacanaṃ sutvā yāvāhaṃ dhammaṃ suṇāmi- tāva adhivāsethāti te hatthasaññāya nivāresi saṅgho gaṇasajjhāyaṃ nivāresīti maññitvā sajjhāyaṃ ṭhapāpesi.

At that moment, the community of monks began a group recitation. Meanwhile, deities from the six deva worlds, taking six chariots, brought them and arranged them in order. Saying, "Great king, our deva world is delightful, our deva world is delightful!" they requested him to come to their respective deva worlds. Hearing their words, the king said, "Wait so long as I am listening to the Dhamma," and restrained them with a hand gesture. The Sangha, thinking that he was stopping the group recitation, had the recitation halted.

Rājā kasmā bhante gaṇasajjhāyaṃ ṭhapethāti āha. Mahārāja tayā hatthasaññāya nivāritattāti. Bhante tumhākaṃ saññaṃ nādāsiṃ. Devatā chadevalokato cha rathe ānetvā attano attano devalokaṃ gantuṃ yācanti tasmā tesaṃ yāvāhaṃ dhammaṃ suṇāmi tāva āgamethāti saññaṃ adāsinti taṃ sutvā keci ayaṃ rājā maraṇabhayabhito vippalapati, maraṇato abhāyanaka satto nāma natthīti maññiṃsu.

The king asked, "Venerable sirs, why did you halt the group recitation?" "Great king," he was told, "because you restrained it with a hand gesture." "Venerable sirs, I did not give a sign to you. Deities from the six deva worlds have brought six chariots and are requesting that I go to their respective deva worlds. Therefore, I gave them a sign, saying, 'Wait so long as I am listening to the Dhamma.'" Hearing this, some thought, "This king, terrified by the fear of death, is raving. There is no being who is not afraid of death."

Tato abhayatthero āha kathaṃ mahārāja saddahituṃ sakkā cha devalokato cha rathā ānītāti taṃ sutvā rājā ākāse pupphadāmāni khipāpesi. Tāni gantvā visuṃ rathadhure olambiṃsu mahājano ākāse olambantāni pupphadāmāni disvā nikkaṅkho ahosi.

Then the Elder Abhaya said, "How, great king, can one believe that six chariots were brought from the six deva worlds?" Hearing this, the king had garlands of flowers thrown into the sky. Those garlands went and hung separately on the yokes of the chariots. The great multitude, seeing the garlands hanging in the sky, became free from doubt.

Tato rājā theraṃ pucchi-katamo pana bhante devaloko ramaṇīyoti. Tusitabhavanaṃ pana mahārāja ramaṇīyaṃ, buddhabhāvāya samayaṃ olokento metteyyo bodhisattopi tasmiṃyeva vasatīti āha.

Then the king asked the elder, "But venerable sir, which deva-world is delightful?" "Great king, the Tusita abode is delightful. The Bodhisatta Metteyya, observing the time for Buddhahood, also dwells there," he replied.

Taṃ [O 0.0088] sutvā rājā tasmiṃ ālayaṃ katvā mahāthūpaṃ olokento nipannova cavitvā suttappabuddho viya tusita bhavanato āhaṭa. Rathe nibbattitvā attano katapuññassa thalaṃ mahājanassa pākaṭaṃ kātuṃ ratheyeva ṭhatvā dibbābharaṇa vibhūsito mahājanassa passantasseva tikkhattuṃ mahāthūpaṃ padakkhiṇaṃ katvā bhikkhusaṅghañca vanditvā tusitabhavanaṃ agamāsi.

Hearing this, the king, having formed an aspiration for that realm, passed away while lying down and gazing at the great stūpa. As if waking from sleep, he was reborn in a chariot brought from the Tusita abode. In order to make the fruit of his merit manifest to the great multitude, he stood in that very chariot, adorned with divine ornaments. While the great multitude was watching, he circumambulated the great stūpa three times, paid homage to the community of bhikkhus, and then went to the Tusita abode.

Evaṃ asāre nicaye dhanānaṃ aniccasaṅghaṃ sattaṃ sapaññā,

Katvana cāgaṃ ratanattayamahi ādāya sāraṃ sugatiṃ vajanti;

Thus, the wise, seeing the accumulation of wealth as insubstantial and beings as an impermanent aggregate, having practiced generosity towards the Triple Gem, take the essence and go to a happy destiny.

Rañño nāṭakatthiyo matabhāvaṃ ñatvā yattha ṭhitā makuḷaṃ mocayiṃsu tatthaṃ ṭhāne katasālā makuḷamuttasālānāmajātā. Rañño sarīrasmiṃ citakaṃ āropite yattha mahājano hatthe paggahetvā viraci. Tattha katasālā viracitthasālā nāma jātā. Rañño sarīraṃ yattha jhāpesuṃ - so sīmāmālako rājamālako nāma jāto. Atha rañño kaniṭṭhabhātā saddhātissamahārājā nāma hutvā cetiye aniṭṭhataṃ jattakammaṃ sudhākammañca niṭṭhāpetvā thūpamakāsīti

The king's dancing women, knowing of his death, cast off their hair ornaments where they stood, and in that place a hall was built called the Hall of Cast-off Ornaments. When the king's body was placed on the funeral pyre, the great multitude raised their hands and lamented, and a hall built there was named the Hall of Lamentation. The place where the king's body was cremated became known as the Boundary Terrace or the Royal Terrace. Then the king's younger brother, becoming King Saddhātissa, had the unfinished brickwork and plasterwork on the cetiya completed, and thus finished the stūpa.

Iti sādhujana manopasādanatthāya kate thūpavaṃse mahācetiye katā niṭṭhitā.

Thus, in the Thūpavaṃsa, composed for the gladdening of the minds of good people, the account concerning the Great Cetiya is completed.

26. Etarahi duṭṭhagāmaṇi abhaya mahārājassa pitā kākavaṇṇatissa rājā metteyyassa bhagavato pitā bhavissati. Vihāramahādevī mātā bhavissati. Duṭṭhagāmiṇi abhayo aggasāvako bhavissati. Kaniṭṭho dutiya sāvako bhavisasati. Rañño pitucchā anuḷādevī aggamahesī bhavissati. Rañño putto sāli rājakumāro putto bhavissati. Bhaṇḍāgārika saṅghāmacco aggupaṭṭhāko bhavissati tassāmaccassa dhītā aggupaṭṭhāyikā bhavissatīti evaṃ sabbepi katādhikārā hetu sampannā tassa bhagavato dhammaṃ sutvā dukkhassantaṃ karitvā anupādisesāya nibbānadhātuyā parinibbāyissantīti.

26. Now, King Kākavaṇṇatissa, the father of the great King Duṭṭhagāmaṇi Abhaya, will become the father of the Blessed One Metteyya. Vihāramahādevī will be his mother. Duṭṭhagāmiṇi Abhaya will be his chief disciple. The younger brother will be his second disciple. The king’s paternal aunt, Anuḷādevī, will be his chief queen. The king’s son, Prince Sāli, will be his son. The treasurer, the minister Saṅgha, will be his chief attendant, and that minister’s daughter will be his chief female attendant. Thus, all these individuals, having made their aspirations and possessing the necessary conditions, will hear the Dhamma of that Blessed One, make an end of suffering, and attain the Nibbāna-element without residue remaining.

Ettāvatā ca

And thus far.

27.

27.

Mahiṇdasena nāmamhi vasanto pariveṇake,

Pattacīvarapādo? Yo piṭakattaya pārago;

Living in the Mahiṇdasena Pariveṇa, who possessed bowl and robe, he who had mastered the three Piṭakas;

Saddhāsilaguṇupeto sabbasattahite rato,

Tena sādhu samajjhiṭṭho yamahaṃ kātumārabhiṃ;

Endowed with the qualities of faith and virtue, delighting in the welfare of all beings, urged on by that good person, I began to compose this work.

Sodāni niṭṭhaṃ sampatto thūpavaṃso anākulo,

Paripuṇṇo saṅkhathā sādhu paṇḍitehi pasaṃsito;

Now that Thūpavaṃsa has reached completion, being clear, complete in its account, and well praised by the wise.

Yaṃ [O 0.0089] pattaṃ kusalaṃ kammaṃ karontena imaṃ mayaṃ,

Tena etena puññena sattā gacchantu nibbutiṃ;

By the wholesome kamma we have attained in composing this, by this merit, may all beings attain Nibbāna.

Anantarāyena yathā ca siddhiṃ

Mūpāgato thūpavarassa vaṃso,

Tatheva saddhammasitā janānaṃ

Mano rathā sīghamupentu siddhiṃ;

Just as the chronicle of the excellent stūpa attained success without hindrance, so too may the heart's desires of people devoted to the true Dhamma swiftly reach fulfillment.

Parisambhidāmaggassa yena līlattha dīpani

Ṭīkā viracitā sādhu saddhammodaya kāminā;

The sub-commentary on the Paṭisambhidāmagga, which illuminates the subtle meaning, was well composed by him, who desires the arising of the true Dhamma.

Tathā pakaraṇe saccasaṅkhepe atthadīpanā,

Dhīmatā sukatā yena suṭṭhu sīhaḷa bhāsato

Likewise, an elucidation of the meaning in the treatise Saccasaṅkhepa was well made by that wise one, excellently from the Sinhala language.

Visuddhimagga saṅkhepe yena atthappakāsanā,

Yogī namupakārāya katā sīhaḷabhāsato;

An elucidation of the meaning in the summary of the Visuddhimagga was made by him for the benefit of yogis, from the Sinhala language.

Parakkama nariṇdassa sabbabhūpāna ketuno,

Dhammāgāre niyutto yo piṭakattaya pārago;

He who was appointed to the Hall of Dhamma of King Parakkama, the banner of all kings, a master of the three Piṭakas;

Sāsanaṃ suṭṭhitaṃ yassa antevāsika bhikkhusu,

Tena vācissaratthera pādena likhito ayanti;

Whose teaching is well-established among his resident-pupil bhikkhus; by the venerable Elder Vācissara, this was written.

Thūpavaṃso niṭṭhito.

The Thūpavaṃsa is completed.
